 viii

 1 Introduction

1.1 Purpose and Scope of the Chemical Stockpile Emergency
 Preparedness Program History Project

The History Project of the Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program (CSEPP or
CSEP Program) was initiated in 2010 to document CSEPP’s extraordinary efforts and
achievements. CSEPP was implemented to improve emergency preparedness at U.S. Army
installations and their surrounding communities as the Army’s stockpile of chemical weapons
was being destroyed. As noted in an earlier program retrospective,

 When Congress mandated destruction of the U.S. chemical stockpile, it ordered
 that this be carried out with “maximum protection” for the public until the
 chemical agents are completely gone. Early studies of the communities near the
 stockpiles showed the need to improve existing emergency plans, training,
 equipment, and facilities. CSEPP was created to enhance the emergency response
 capabilities in these communities, recognizing that each site has its own particular
 needs for emergency preparedness. Program goals and metrics were established
 and tools were created to work more efficiently. These breakthroughs were the
 result of all the program stakeholders collaborating to identify program needs,
 solve problems, and advance program goals. Since CSEPP began, state and local
 emergency management officials have teamed with the U.S. Army and the
 Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA) to protect the public in the
 unlikely event of a stockpile accident. This partnership among multiple Federal
 agencies, 10 states, 40 counties, one tribal nation, volunteer agencies, and the
 private sector presented challenges to the newly formed program... [Over the
 program’s history, however] many emergency management innovations have
 been discovered, planned, and implemented in CSEPP communities. These best
 practices and lessons learned should be collected and shared with all the CSEPP
 sites, as well as with other emergency management agencies that may benefit
 from CSEPP’s experience.1

As the installations completed disposal operations and their chemical weapons were eliminated,
CSEPP was no longer necessary, and the program was shut down at those sites. The History
Project captured and documented the experiences and lessons learned at those locations before
the relevant personnel, institutions, and documentation dispersed. Destruction of the chemical
stockpile was completed in June 2023.

The CSEPP History Project report has been completed in two volumes. The first volume presents
a narrative history of the program including its development, administration, and
accomplishments in various emergency management fields, along with perspectives from
program staff and a short set of conclusions. The second volume contains the 37 lessons that
were identified and documented by CSEPP staff and support personnel. Each of these lessons is

1
 CSEPP: A Legacy of Innovation, brochure produced by FEMA, 2009.

 1

presented with the idea and hope that they may be of value to other Army installations and the
wider emergency management community. Material from these lessons was shared, as
appropriate, with the U.S. Army through the Center for Army Lessons Learned (CALL) and with
the civilian emergency management community through the FEMA Lessons Learned
Information System (LLIS), later the Homeland Security Digital Library (HSDL). Several
lessons also became the basis of articles published in emergency management or public
administration journals; those publications are noted for the relevant lessons.

The following lessons learned were developed from 2012 to 2025. They are presented here as
they were written at the time – so there may be some anachronisms in terminology and there are
references to CSEPP as an ongoing program, which is no longer the case. In addition, because
they were initially produced as stand-alone documents, there is some redundancy in the material;
for example, each lesson briefly introduces the CSEP Program.

1.2 Organization of This Report
The lessons learned in this report are organized into four categories: program management,
planning and protective action, exercises, and emergency public information. Each lesson
learned contains a summary, a brief description of the context (background), the key issue
identified, the action taken, and a brief analysis of how the lesson can be applied moving
forward. Lessons learned from this report may be applicable to other hazardous material
emergency preparedness programs, or to preparedness for any type of emergency; the scope of
potential application depends on the particular lesson. Each lesson also lists applicable
references.

Note: some referenced documents, particularly CSEPP reports and documents cited to the
website CSEPP Portal, may be available from the Disaster Research Center at the University of
Delaware, which holds an archive of CSEPP documents.

 2

 2 CSEPP Program Management

This section includes lessons learned pertaining to program management, including interagency
cooperation, the program funding process, guidance development, and specific examples of
successful interagency working groups and the results they achieved.

2.1 Use of IPTs2
CSEPP used Integrated Process Teams (IPTs) at the local and national level to break through
inter-jurisdictional logjams and implement consensus-based management practices.

2.1.1 Summary

The CSEP Program involved multiple federal agencies, multiple states, and numerous local units
of government. The involvement of diverse agencies at different levels of government led to
conflict and difficulty in resolving issues. That, in turn, led to slow progress in achieving
program goals. Program management was criticized repeatedly by the U.S. General Accounting
Office (GAO). After other mechanisms for interagency coordination had failed to give the
desired results, FEMA and the Army agreed to form site-specific and national-level IPTs. The
IPTs worked well to break the logjam and accelerate implementation of the program. Key
elements of the IPTs’ success included constant interagency communication, solving problems at
the lowest level, and obtaining buy-in from higher authority. The IPT technique could be applied
to other emergency management and homeland security programs that require interagency
coordination.

2.1.2 Background

The Defense Authorization Act of 1986 (P.L. 99-145) provided for destruction of the Nation’s
stockpile of lethal unitary chemical weapons, and it mandated, among other things, that
maximum protection be provided to the public during the destruction process. During
preparation of the environmental impact statement for the program, studies showed a small but
finite possibility of harmful impact to the public near the storage sites. The hazard to the public
would apply both during continued storage and during the disposal process. Based on these
studies and on comments received during public hearings, it was determined that the
communities near the storage sites should upgrade emergency preparedness for a chemical
accident.

The Army sought FEMA’s assistance because FEMA already had the infrastructure and
experience needed to provide federal emergency response funds and assistance to states and
counties. In an August 3, 1988, memorandum of understanding (MOU) between the Army and
FEMA, the agencies agreed that the Army would be responsible for providing technical
assistance and required resources in developing emergency response plans and related

2
 Lesson written in 2012. An article based on this lesson was published in The Public Manager, December 2013.

 3

capabilities; integrating the on-site and off-site planning processes; ensuring that all emergency
plans are adequate and can be readily implemented; and conducting site-specific hazard analyses
for planning. FEMA would be responsible for administering CSEPP funding to the states and
localities, taking the lead in working with state and local governments in upgrading community
response capabilities and conducting training.3

A successful program would require coordinated efforts on the part of the main participants in
the program, the Army, FEMA, and affected state and local governments. The parties had
interests that were partially congruent and partially in conflict. The Army’s main interest was in
fulfilling its obligation to destroy the weapons, and to do so safely but expeditiously and at the
lowest possible cost. The state and local governments were charged with protecting their
populations. To varying degrees, they also sought to maximize the funding that could be
obtained to hire personnel, build facilities, and purchase equipment and training that they would
otherwise not be able to afford. Because the Army would require licenses from the states to
operate the chemical weapons disposal facilities, the states had a certain degree of leverage to
obtain what they wanted. FEMA was caught in the middle but was generally supportive of the
concept of upgrading state and local preparedness, its agency mission.

Conflicting interests, management styles, planning schedules, and cultures led to problems
administering the program. For the first five years, CSEPP had difficulty finding an effective
modus operandi. The Army-FEMA agreement was changed in 1994 to establish new
coordination mechanisms.4 Nevertheless, the program drew criticism from the GAO and
Congress for being slow to achieve its goals. As characterized by the GAO, “CSEPP’s slow
progress has been due largely to long-standing management weaknesses, including disagreement
between the Army and FEMA over their respective roles and responsibilities.”5

2.1.3 Issue

Progress on the CSEP Program appeared to be impeded by interagency conflict and
disagreement. Issues included adequacy of the budget, accounting for funds spent, selection and
procurement of equipment (such as warning systems and personal protective equipment), and
standards for measuring preparedness.6 Congress was threatening to drastically restructure the

    GAO/NSIAD-97-9 (note 5).

 4

program unless a more cooperative and efficient process could be established.7 Most importantly,
slow progress on building response capabilities meant that the Army workforce and public were
not being protected as well as they could be.

2.1.4 Solution

The program addressed issues of accountability, interagency conflict, and inter-jurisdictional
conflict by using Integrated Process Teams (IPTs). An IPT is a project management tool used in
both government and industry to address complex projects involving multiple stakeholders and
cross-disciplinary issues by assembling a team of experts.8 The IPT concept was adopted by the
U.S. Department of Defense (DoD) for major acquisition programs.9 DoD defines an IPT (here
referring to a closely related tool, the integrated product team) as follows:

 The Integrated Product Team (IPT) is composed of representatives from all
 appropriate functional disciplines working together with a Team Leader to build
 successful and balanced programs, identify and resolve issues, and make sound
 and timely recommendations to facilitate decision-making.” (DoD Directive
 5000.1.)

and also:

 An Integrated Product Team (IPT) is a multidisciplinary group of people who are
 collectively responsible for delivering a defined product or process.” (DoD
 Integrated Product and Process Development Handbook.)

FEMA and the Army created a Financial IPT in 1996. With the CSEPP State Directors’
concurrence, the Financial IPT was formed with representatives from five of the ten CSEPP
States, three of the seven FEMA CSEPP Regions, the CSEPP Core Team (a headquarters-level
interagency team formed to manage CSEPP), and budget specialists from FEMA Headquarters
and the Army Cost and Economic Analysis Center. It was led by a state emergency management
executive. The purpose of the Financial IPT was to establish guidelines and a methodology for
preparing state and local CSEPP budget submissions to FEMA (known as Cooperative
Agreements). The Financial IPT was a significant step forward in building trust between State
and Federal government participants in the budgeting process.

7
 In 1993, the Senate Committee on Appropriations reported that CSEPP’s cost growth and program delays were
 unacceptable and directed the Army to take full control of the program (Senate Report No. 103-158, at 368-369).
 The Army continued to work with FEMA. In the 1997 National Defense Authorization Act, Congress directed
 the Secretary of the Army to submit a report within 120 days assessing the implementation and success of the
 site-specific integrated process teams. The Act further states that if the Army and FEMA were unsuccessful in
 implementing the IPTs within 120 days, the Secretary of the Army was to assume full control and responsibility
 for the program and FEMA’s management role would be eliminated.
8
 There is a suite of related team concepts in the same vein, going by similar names: integrated product teams and
 integrated project teams, to name two. See Integrated Project Team (IPT) Start-up Guide, MITRE Corp.,
    October 2008.
9
 See DoD Integrated Product and Process Development Handbook, Office of the Under Secretary of Defense
 (Acquisition and Technology), July 6, 1998.

 5

The guidelines and worksheets developed by the IPT were subsequently approved by Army and
FEMA CSEPP management and the CSEPP State Directors. The Financial IPT also oversaw
development of a CSEPP Cooperative Agreement software application—the CSEPP CA
Database Module. The Module was first used for the submission of the fiscal year 1997 CSEPP
Cooperative Agreement applications.

The process developed by the Financial IPT allowed funding requests to be developed
interactively and in a transparent manner. Accountability was built in through a requirement to
link budget items to specific response capabilities that were needed for chemical accident
response. All reviewers and approvers could make narrative comments in the software, providing
an open process that involved all parties. State and local governments were able to know the
status of their requests—something they didn’t necessarily know before the Financial IPT
developed the new process. Those in the review and approval chain could be identified and held
accountable for their approvals or disapprovals. The process developed by the Financial IPT was
widely credited with solving the program’s budget and accountability impasse.

The success of the Financial IPT laid the groundwork for expanded use of the concept within
CSEPP. In May 1998, Army and FEMA CSEPP executives approved a Joint Memorandum for
the Record (JMFR) on the use of IPTs within the program.10 The JMFR provided for three types
of IPTs: (1) site-specific IPTs, with representatives of the Army chemical storage installation,
county and state emergency management staff, and FEMA regional offices; (2) functional IPTs,
established on an as-needed basis to allow Federal, state, and local stakeholders to make
recommendations and develop proposed solutions on functional issues that affect national
operational or programmatic policy; and (3) a national-level overarching IPT, also convened on
an as-needed basis to provide a forum for national decision-makers to discuss national CSEPP
policy.

The IPTs established forums for pulling local, state, and national-level stakeholders together so
they could understand each other’s perspectives, work through issues, and build consensus-based
solutions. These benefits were especially evident in the site-specific IPTs where issues could be
worked through at the local level without first raising them to the Army or FEMA or expending
political capital with Congressional representatives or staff. Functional IPTs were established to
oversee programmatic policy and guidance development on planning, exercises, training,
automation, public information, medical preparedness, protective actions, post-accident chemical
monitoring, post-accident recovery and restoration, and program closeout. Over the next dozen
years, these IPTs issued numerous guidebooks and oversaw development and refinement of
software tools for CSEPP. These products set direction for the program and in many cases
advanced the art of emergency preparedness; national standards such as the Homeland Security
Exercise and Evaluation Program (HSEEP) and the National Incident Management System
(NIMS) have significant roots in CSEPP.

10
 U.S. Army and FEMA, 1998, Joint Memorandum for the Record, Subject: Use of Integrated Process Teams as a
 Management Tool Within the Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program, May 21.

 6

What Sets IPTs Apart

Why were the IPTs more successful than previous efforts, including other interagency
committees? A U.S. Department of Energy (DOE) guide summarized the role of an IPT:

 The IPT is the primary tool for breaking down the walls that can exist between
 different organizations, different professions, and different levels within the
 different organizations’ command structures. A successful IPT brings these
 diverse elements together to form a unit that willingly shares information,
 balances conflicting priorities and ideologies, and jointly plans and executes the
 project mission.11

Use of IPTs in the CSEP Program was extensively studied by Kunz and Company, Inc., in 2005.
According to their report,

 IPTs bring together stakeholders, staff, and other experts needed to design and
 implement new processes and to create new products. The heart of the approach is
 this concentration of interest, capability, and knowledge, which allow IPTs to
 efficiently make decisions that cross organizational and disciplinary lines.”12

As identified in the Kunz report and elsewhere, factors that set the IPT concept apart included:
 • Resolving issues at the lowest appropriate organizational level. IPTs include experts who
 are familiar with the problem to be solved and can discuss the details of how the product,
 policy, or procedure will be implemented in practice.
 • Representation of key organizations. The IPT should include representatives of all the
 organizations who will implement, or have to live with, the results of the project to be
 completed. Each organization (e.g., agency or jurisdiction) brings its own requirements,
 goals, and limitations to the table; one organization often will not be familiar with
 another organization’s imperatives. For example, if the IPT is discussing acquisition of
 equipment (radios, personal protective gear, etc.), one organization may have issues of
 compatibility with other equipment, another may have a requirement to buy U.S.-made
 items only, and another may have issues with cost. The sooner each organization is aware
 of the others’ concerns, the better.
 • Operation by consensus. Operating by consensus forces discussion until everyone is
 satisfied that they have been heard and that they can live with whatever compromises
 were reached. The back-and-forth involved in IPT discussions contrasts with alternative
 approaches where a product design, budget, or policy is developed by one organization
 and then “thrown over the fence” to the next one.
 • Empowerment. Because the purpose of the IPT is to resolve interagency issues at the
 lowest possible level, the members must be able to speak, to the extent feasible, for their
 organizations. That involves a two-way trust relationship: the IPT member must be

11
 DOE G 413.3-18, Integrated Projects Team Guide, Sept. 24, 2008.
12
 CSEPP’s Teams: Best Practices and Lessons Learned, Kunz and Company, Inc., Sept. 2005.

 7

 familiar enough with his or her organization’s positions and constraints to be able to
 represent the organization’s interests effectively. Similarly, higher authorities in the
 organization must allow the IPT member latitude to make at least tentative commitments.
 In the CSEP Program, IPT products generally had to be approved by higher authorities in
 the respective organizations; however, through trust and regular communication, most
 often the products were able to be approved without undue delay.

Getting the Most Out of Your IPT

The Kunz and Company report on CSEPP IPTs identified 24 best practices that contributed to
IPT success in CSEPP. The practices summarized below draw from those practices and from
other “how-to” IPT guides:
 • Effective leadership. Effective leadership is that which facilitates the efforts of the team
 members. A team leader should encourage free expression of views but also guide the
 group to consensus.
 • Team charter. A formal team charter may be appropriate to spell out items such as the
 purpose of the group, duration (temporary group to develop a particular product, or
 standing team to take on new issues as they occur), membership selection process,
 decision making process, meeting schedule, and other items. Annual workplans may also
 be useful.
 • Training. An initial training session can be useful, particularly for team formation tasks
 such as defining the mission, setting operating rules, task planning, and the like.
 • Operational support. CSEPP IPTs often benefitted from contract support ranging from
 administrative tasks such as setting up conference calls and drafting minutes to expert
 tasks such as developing software, research papers, and guidance documents.
 • Sub-IPTs. At the local level, the IPT can appoint subcommittees (working IPTs or
 WIPTs) to address specific issues and report back to the IPT with recommendations. The
 WIPT should include IPT members and can also bring in subject matter experts from
 staff or support organizations. For example, a WIPT might be established to address
 selection or development of software to support particular response functions. The WIPT
 would include emergency managers (future users of the software) plus information
 technology experts to evaluate the suitability of different programs and assist with
 adapting them to meet the community’s needs.

2.1.5 Potential Application

The preamble of the Army-FEMA JMFR on IPTs stated:

 The Integrated Process Team management process is designed to encourage
 innovative problem solving at the management level closest to the issues or
 concern. Using data (not opinion and guesswork) gathered from all available
 sources, IPTs seek to monitor, control, and constantly improve the emergency
 preparedness process by pushing decision making downward, within legal
 limitations of authority. In the CSEPP, the focus is on improving how emergency

 8

 preparedness is accomplished in a storage community within established resource
 limitations. Gains are made in the emergency preparedness process by pooling the
 skills, talents, and knowledge of the people assigned to an IPT to resolve issues in
 executing a preparedness function. IPTs are formed at management levels
 appropriate for resolving the issue(s). This approach allows for inclusion of
 installation and local and State government representatives at all levels of
 programmatic decision making.13

The preamble incorporated the general principles of an IPT: open discussion with no secrets;
qualified, empowered team members; consistent, success-oriented, proactive participation;
continuous up-the-line communications; reasonable disagreement; and issues raised and resolved
early. This model for a problem-solving and decision-making proved to be very effective for
management of the CSEP Program. CSEPP experience indicates that use of this management
tool should be considered early when diverse and apparently conflicting organizations are trying
to reach a common goal.

Potential applications include interagency emergency management programs such as the
Radiological Emergency Preparedness Program, inter-jurisdictional projects such as regional
preparedness initiatives, and resilience initiatives that involve public agencies such as emergency
management and law enforcement along with private entities such as infrastructure owners.

2.2 Funding Process14
The CSEP Program developed mechanisms to allocate and prioritize funding to achieve
consistent preparedness goals across a variety of sites and circumstances.

2.2.1 Summary

The CSEP Program involved development of state and local capabilities to respond to an
accident or incident involving chemical weapons at any of the Army’s eight chemical weapons
storage facilities. With funding originating from the Army, passing through FEMA and then
going to state and local governments, budgeting and accountability were problematic. Where
cooperation and program-building were needed, often there was controversy and tension instead.

CSEPP needed to develop a standard format and process for State, local and tribal governments
to use in requesting funding to develop and implement the needed capabilities. Two elements
contributed to the solution: a set of national, substantive capabilities standards; and a budget-
development process that involved all parties in a transparent, consensus-building approach.
Capabilities standards were adopted as “benchmarks” that all CSEPP communities should meet.
The budget-development process involved use of the Integrated Process Team (IPT) mechanism.
A national financial IPT developed and refined the process, and site-specific IPTs implemented
it. Software was developed and later expanded to facilitate the process.

This model was used for the life of the CSEP Program and should be considered for other
programs that have similar structure and dynamics.

2.2.2 Background

The CSEP Program involved development of state and local capabilities to respond to an
accident or incident involving chemical weapons at any of the Army’s eight chemical weapons
storage facilities. Building this capability took place at the Army installations and local and state
response agencies. It was overseen by FEMA and funded by the Army. With funding originating
from the Army, passing through FEMA and then distributed to state and local governments,
budgeting and accountability were problematic at first.

In August 1988, the Army and FEMA signed a Memorandum of Understanding that stated,
among other things, that the Army and FEMA would cooperate in determining the funding
requirements to develop and implement emergency preparedness programs to facilitate the
Army’s request for necessary funding in each fiscal year.

In the early years, all CSEPP funding requirements were considered and approved in the Office
of the Deputy for Chemical Demilitarization, Assistant Secretary of the Army for Installations,
Logistics, and Environment in the Pentagon. Requests for CSEPP funding were submitted to that
office from the Army stockpile installations and agencies, through their respective commands,
and from FEMA on behalf of itself and the participating state and local governments. Requests
14

 11

were communicated through memos that listed requirements and the desired amounts of funding.
If the Office of the Deputy for Chemical Demilitarization approved the request, it would send a
memo to the Chemical Stockpile Disposal Program Resource Management Office, which would
complete a Military Interdepartmental Purchase Request (MIPR) for the approved amount of
funding to the requesting organization. All MIPRs for off-post requirements went to FEMA,
which, in turn, would enter into cooperative agreements with the CSEPP states.

In the first few years of the program, when CSEPP was relatively small in scope and required
relatively modest amounts of funding, managing the program funding in this top-down fashion
was feasible, although it was never considered totally satisfactory by FEMA or the CSEPP states.
As the program started to expand rapidly, the top-down approach to managing the funding
became problematic, especially for managing off-post funding where the greatest expansion was
occurring. As the program expanded, particularly in the building of local and state capabilities,
the challenge was to meet readiness requirements while containing the cost of the program. The
Army and FEMA did not always agree on which CSEPP off-post requirements were valid, but
the Army had the final decisions on what would be funded. Many CSEPP states began to push
back on the denials of some of their stated requirements. Because the states had permitting
authority over the planned incinerators, the stage was set for conflict, with both sides having
some degree of leverage. The top-down, non-negotiating process for approving funding requests
was cumbersome, slow, and did not lend itself to the establishment of preparedness standards
across the eight sites. Participating agencies and jurisdictions were frustrated, and the
U.S. Government Accounting Office (GAO) was critical of the program’s slow progress.

2.2.3 Issue

There were two main issues with the funding process, one procedural and one substantive. The
procedural issue was a stovepiped, sequential review process. Requests were made by state and
local governments and forwarded to FEMA Regions, then FEMA Headquarters, and then the
Army. Decisions about the requests were made by the Army and FEMA and then passed back to
the states and then on to the local governments. This process was slow and prone to
miscommunications and misunderstandings that impeded the search for solutions to emergency
preparedness problems. The substantive issue was a lack of standards and basis for determining
an adequate state of readiness at each site. Without standards, decisions on whether particular
items would be funded could appear arbitrary.

2.2.4 Solution

The solution was likewise twofold: first, the program developed a set of agreed-upon standards
for capabilities that should be present and would be funded. Second, the program built a process
for developing budgets and approving funding that involved interagency communication and
buy-in from the ground up and that incorporated both long-term and annual budget planning.

 12

Substantive Standards (Benchmarks)

The capabilities standards were termed “benchmarks.” A set of nine national benchmarks were
adopted in 1993, later expanded to 12. The goal was to achieve full compliance within each
benchmark and to sustain this capability throughout the program. CSEPP Program Managers
tracked the obligation of their CSEPP resources by these national benchmarks. Assessment of
benchmark capabilities was governed by the principle of “functional equivalency”—that is, it is
not necessary to provide every jurisdiction with identical assets, as long as their basic emergency
capabilities met CSEPP guidance. The benchmarks were:
 1. Administrative support for each installation, state, Immediate Response Zone
 (IRZ) county, and Protective Action Zone (PAZ) county.
 2. A functioning alert and notification (A&N) system for each installation, IRZ,
 and transition zone.
 3. A functioning automated data processing (ADP) system connecting critical
 installation facilities/on-post emergency operations center (EOC)/off-post
 EOC/Joint Information Center (JIC)/State EOC.
 4. Functioning communications systems (COM) for IRZ, installations, and between
 EOCs/Military Installation/JIC/State.
 5. Coordinated plans (COP) in conformance with established CSEPP guidance for
 each installation, state, IRZ county, and PAZ county.
 6. Functioning EOCs (EOC) for each installation and IRZ county.
 7. An exercise (EX) program that is consistent with the Exercise Integrated Process
 Team (IPT)-approved exercise policy.
 8. A medical (MED) program for off-post medical preparation and response to a
 CSEPP incident/accident.
 9. Personnel (PER), such as CSEPP coordinators, public information/public affairs
 officers, planners, and ADP specialists to support CSEPP activities on the
 installation, in the state, and in IRZ counties.
 10. Protective action (PRO) strategy[ies] in conformance with established CSEPP
 guidance for each jurisdiction.
 11. A public outreach/education (POE) program for public information and
 education.
 12. Training (TNG) programs, which are consistent with the FEMA State Training
 Plan (for off-post jurisdictions) and Army certification requirements (for on-post
 installations) and which maintain proficiency of emergency services
 providers/responders and CSEPP staff, as defined and measured by CSEPP
 guidance.

The functional equivalency concept allowed benchmarks to be achieved in different ways in
different locations. It was not necessary for each location to use the same equipment, approach,
or strategies as the others; rather, there were many paths to success. The program worked from
the existing infrastructure with a bottom-up approach that, with technical support from the Army

 13

and FEMA, allowed the jurisdictions to define whether each benchmark capability had been
achieved.

Long-Term and Annual Budgeting

The long-term budget planning process consisted of Life Cycle Cost Estimates (LCCEs). In
December 1994, the Chemical Demilitarization Program was declared an Acquisition Category I
Defense Acquisition Board Program. This designation required by law that life cycle cost
estimates be developed for all Chemical Demilitarization projects including CSEPP. The LCCE
process was initiated in 1995, and the first set of LCCEs covered the period 1996–2004. Long-
term program and cost plans were determined by the states and counties on the basis of their
current capabilities, experience, jurisdictional requirements, and CSEPP policy and guidance.
The LCCEs provided a baseline and predictability for annual budget requirements; although
from 1996 through 2000, a lot of disparities existed between what the states and counties thought
were required and what FEMA and the Army were willing to support. These disparities were
reconciled when new program management on both sides became more willing to negotiate and
initiated a “re-baselining” of CSEPP in early 2000. The re-baselining included development of a
new standardized CSEPP LCCE process where CSEPP state agencies and counties took their
annual cooperative agreement (CA) detailed line-item budget requests and expanded them into
multi-year life cycle cost estimates. Once these multi-year life cycle cost estimates were
established and validated by FEMA and the Army, the annual budget process became much
easier to negotiate.

The annual budgeting process was developed by the CSEPP Financial IPT and refined over
several years. The Financial IPT was formed in 1995 and included representatives from five of
the ten CSEPP States, FEMA Headquarters, three of the seven FEMA CSEPP Regions, the
CSEPP Core Team, and the Army Cost and Economic Analysis Center. It was led by the Deputy
Director for the Utah Division of Comprehensive Emergency Management.

The IPT developed a set of guidelines and worksheets for budget planning and oversaw
development of software for managing the process. The software allowed states to build their
CSEPP CA requests on worksheets by entering their funding requirements by line item and
explaining in narrative blocks how the line items would help meet their program objectives. Each
line item had a unique, computer-generated identification (ID) number. The software would
group the line items under functional areas (benchmark capabilities) and provide a summary of
the total funding required by each county or State agency requesting CSEPP funding. For line
items that were annual and recurring requirements, there only had to be an initial negotiation
when the requirement was first requested. Once that item was approved by FEMA and the Army,
additional approval was not required in subsequent budget years unless the scope or basic cost
changed. These items were known as Base Operating Cost (BOC) items and were also used in
the LCCEs. Using BOC items in the LCCE and budget requests saved a lot of negotiating time.

The funding process for off-post agencies was initiated at the county or state level. Local
requests were passed up to the state for review and approval, on to the FEMA Region for review
and recommendation, then to FEMA HQ and the Army for approval. FEMA HQ and the Army
would approve the amount of funding for each item, and after all the line items were considered,

 14

send the approved budget request back to the FEMA Region and CSEPP State for completion of
the CA. Narrative comments could be made in the software by all the reviewers and approvers.

Transparency and Dialogue

The automated process insured that funding requests were initiated by counties, consolidated at
the state level, and forwarded to FEMA and the Army for approval. Local and state governments
were able to track the status of their requests throughout the process—something they could not
do before. Those in the review and approval chain could be identified and held accountable for
their approvals or disapprovals. Also, the ability to insert narrative comments at all levels
allowed the focus of discussion to shift from simple up or down decisions to the reasoning
behind them and reinforce the link between what would be funded and what was needed to
establish the benchmark capabilities.

The budgeting process was later refined both procedurally and through expansion of the software
tool. Whereas the original software tool was designed only for development of cooperative
agreement applications, the expanded software tool provided more information and
accountability through the development of work plans for CSEPP personnel and reports used for
managing the funding after awards had been made. The main thrust of the refinements was to
encourage more dialogue among the involved organizations—state and local government,
FEMA, and the Army—at the lowest level, when budget requests were at the beginning stages of
formulation. That led to organizational buy-in during budget development and avoided later
confrontations over what would be funded.

Dialogue at the local level was accomplished mainly through expansion of the IPT concept. On
May 21, 1998, the Army and FEMA signed a Joint Memorandum for the Record that established
Integrated Process Teams as a management tool within CSEPP. Through the Joint Memorandum,
the program established site-specific IPTs with the primary goal of identifying site-specific
operational issues, proposing solutions to those issues to the appropriate level of decision-
makers, and implementing programmatic and operational decisions. In turn, the site-specific
IPTs could establish Working IPTs to resolve distinct technical issues affecting the community.
Put another way, the site-specific IPTs became venues for discussing what was needed at each
site to build the benchmark capabilities. This substantive capability discussion set the stage for
more productive discussion of funding issues. As stated in the preamble to the Joint
Memorandum,

 The Integrated Process Team management process is designed to encourage
 innovative problem solving at the management level closest to the issues or
 concern. Using data (not opinion and guesswork) gathered from all available
 sources, IPTs seek to monitor, control, and constantly improve the emergency
 preparedness process by pushing decision making downward, within legal
 limitations of authority. In the CSEPP, the focus is on improving how emergency
 preparedness is accomplished in a storage community within established resource
 limitations. Gains are made in the emergency preparedness process by pooling the

 15

 skills, talents, and knowledge of the people assigned to an IPT to resolve issues in
 executing a preparedness function.15

Implementation of site-specific IPTs did not solve all of the problems, but it set the stage for
effective negotiations among the parties, which led to resolution of most funding disputes.

2.2.5 Potential Application

The combination of substantive benchmarks, and local discussion leading to agreement over
funding to achieve those benchmarks, could be applied to any emergency management program
where funds are distributed from one organization to another with the goal of increasing
preparedness. Compared to a block-grant approach, it is probably more time-consuming and
certainly more constrained, in the sense that the grantees have less latitude as to how to spend the
money. However, FEMA and the Army were accountable to Congress for CSEPP funding and
had to maintain more oversight than required for a block-grant. This approach has been effective
in building functionally equivalent programs at diverse locations.

Taking a wider focus, this approach could be considered for any program where federal funds are
distributed to state or local entities (or state funds distributed to local entities) to achieve a
specific set of capabilities, whether it is for emergency preparedness, homeland security,
environmental response, or any other purpose.

2.3 Reentry and Recovery Guidance Development16
CSEPP pioneered guidance (and planning) for reentry and recovery following an emergency.

2.3.1 Summary

As the CSEP Program matured, more attention was paid to post-emergency recovery issues, and
a working group was formed to develop guidance on reentry and recovery issues. The group
developed a workbook containing a sample recovery plan, along with relevant background and
reference information. The sample plan addressed topics such as hazard assessment, access to
restricted areas, protection of food and water, medical services, relocation, social services, public
information, financial assistance, and environmental remediation. It also laid out recovery
responsibilities by Incident Command System (ICS) position. The approach and structure of the
workbook can be adapted to planning for recovery from any event or hazard.

2.3.2 Background

Reentry and recovery were recognized as an element of chemical stockpile emergency
preparedness early on in the program. However, recovery received relatively little emphasis
compared to response-phase preparedness, which was seen as more directly related to public
health and safety.

Early CSEPP activities included a series of studies performed by Oak Ridge National Laboratory
to lay the technical groundwork for reentry and recovery decisions:
 • A 1990 study provided information for emergency planners on the safety of reentry to
 areas affected by a chemical agent release and on possible contamination of agricultural
 products, livestock, and water supplies.17
 • A 1992 study developed working estimates for control limits for media that offer the
 potential for human exposure, including drinking water, milk, soil, and unprocessed
 food.18
 • Existing chemical agent exposure guidelines were compiled and presented in a 1997
 paper.19

16

17
 See, for example, Oak Ridge National Laboratory (ORNL), 1990, Reentry Planning: The Technical Basis for
 Offsite Recovery Following Warfare Agent Contamination, ORNL-6628 (April), and CDC, 1990, Results of a
 Workshop Meeting to Discuss Protection of Public Health and Safety During Reentry into Areas Potentially
 Contaminated with a Lethal Chemical Agent (GB, VX, or Mustard Agent), availability announced in Federal
     Register, 55 FR 28940, July 16, 1990.
18
 Watson, A.P., et al., 1992, Estimated General Population Control Limits for Unitary Agents in Drinking Water,
 Milk, Soil, and Unprocessed Food Items, ORNL/TM-12035.
19
 Cheri Bandy Foust, 1998, Compilation of Existing Chemical Agent Guidelines Table as of September 1997,
     ORNL/TM-13649.

 18

Technical studies and guidance regarding the health effects of chemical agent exposure also have
been conducted by the Army, U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA),20 Occupational
Safety and Health Administration (OSHA),21 and Centers for Disease Control and Prevention
(CDC).22 The U.S. Army Center for Health Promotion and Preventive Medicine (USACHPPM)
(now the U.S. Army Public Health Command) at Aberdeen Proving Ground, Maryland, has had
a continuing mission to determine safe levels of exposure to chemical agents for both military
and civilian applications.23

As time went on and some of the more urgent response-phase issues were addressed to the
satisfaction of those in the program, reentry and recovery gained attention. Recovery was
identified as an area needing attention at the 2000 planning workshop, leading to formation of a
working group that oversaw development of reentry and recovery guidance.

2.3.3 Issue

Reentry and recovery planning was lagging behind planning for other aspects of response.
Recovery planning was identified as a priority during a planning conference in December 2000.
Of particular concern was reentry decision making, which had been discussed in several national
workshops without progressing toward resolution. Off-post communities wanted to know what
monitoring data would be available and when, and the Army wanted to know what data the off-
post communities were looking for. In addition, information was needed about other aspects of
community recovery, including sources of assistance that would be available and how to handle
public information during recovery, among others. A reentry and recovery working group was
formed under the leadership of Steve Douglas, Pueblo County, Colorado Emergency Director.

2.3.4 Solution

Recovery and reentry had been addressed in previous CSEPP planning guidance, in Appendix M
to the 1996 CSEPP planning guidance. The appendix addressed recovery-phase decontamination,
reentry, and restoration. It incorporated a concept of phased reentry in which selective reentry by
specially trained and equipped personnel might be needed, for example, to perform a rescue
operation or to repair critical infrastructure, followed later by public reentry. Topics suggested
for reentry and recovery planning included assessment of residual hazards, control of access to
the affected area, collection of extensive data on land use in the affected area, public information
needs during recovery, and provision for long-term relocation including provision of social and
medical services for displaced persons.

20
 See Acute Exposure Guideline Levels for Selected Airborne Chemicals, Volume 3, National Academies Press
 (2003), accessed August 2025 at https://nap.nationalacademies.org/catalog/10672/acute-exposure-guideline-
 levels-for-selected-airborne-chemicals-volume-3.
21
 See the OSHA/National Institute for Occupational Safety and Health (NIOSH) webpage on emergency
 preparedness standards, accessed August 2025 at https://www.osha.gov/emergency-preparedness#Introduction.
22
 For a historical overview of CDC activities in support of CSEPP, see the CDC webpage on Chemical Weapons
 Elimination. Accessed August 2025 at https://www.cdc.gov/chemical-weapons-
 elimination/?CDC_AAref_Val=https://www.cdc.gov/nceh/demil/pdfs/EPHRB_oversight_summary.pdf.
23
 See, for example, Derivation of Health-based Environmental Screening Levels For Chemical Warfare Agents: A
 Technical Evaluation (USACHPPM March 1999).

 19

The recovery working group formed in 2000 expanded on previous guidance and developed a
workbook that could serve as a template and reference for recovery planning by CSEPP
communities. The workbook went through several drafts and was issued in final form in May
2003. It recommended developing a community-wide CSEPP recovery plan, incorporating the
installation and off-post jurisdictions, in advance of any emergency. It also described the
conditions and concerns that could be expected in the wake of a chemical agent emergency. The
workbook’s main component was a model recovery plan with chapters on hazard assessment,
access control, food and water protection, medical services, relocation, social services, public
information, claims and disaster assistance, and environmental remediation. The model plan was
made available in a simple electronic format, accessible to the most common word-processing
software, to enable planners to customize it for local application. The workbook also included
appendices with background information on topics relevant to recovery planning including
chemical agent exposure standards, listings of resources for sample analysis and other technical
services, and discussions of legal procedures associated with environmental cleanup and disaster
assistance. The workbook was used by multiple sites to develop local recovery plans.

2.3.5 Potential Application

The recovery workbook advocated advance planning for disaster recovery. FEMA recovery
doctrine agrees. The National Disaster Recovery Framework (FEMA, Sept. 2011) says, “Both
pre- and post-disaster recovery planning are critical for communities to develop resilience and
for successful and timely recovery.”24

While the workbook was developed and designed specifically for CSEPP, several of its
components could be adapted to recovery planning for other hazards or all-hazards scenarios. In
particular:
 • The workbook’s basic design elements—an introduction, model recovery plan, and
 informational appendices—can function as templates.
 • A recovery context description is included in the introduction. This section describes the
 situation that would be expected in the aftermath of the emergency, including elements
 such as what has occurred, what emergency response steps were taken, and some of the
 particular concerns and issues that the community would be facing after the situation has
 stabilized. It helps set the tone for the model recovery plan.
 • The first-level structure of the model plan provides a reasonable starting point for
 developing other kinds of recovery plans. Topics include the basic plan, hazard
 assessment, access to restricted areas, ingestion pathway protection (i.e., protection of
 food and water), medical services, relocation, social services, public information, disaster
 assistance, and environmental remediation. Arguably these or similar topics would be
 applicable in many types of disaster.
 • The model plan contains general allocations of ICS responsibility for various functions to
 particular ICS positions.

24
 FEMA, 2011, National Disaster Recovery Framework, p. 63.

 20

 • Appendices are included that contain additional information and references. Appendix
 topics include health-based exposure standards, protective equipment, sources of external
 support, lists of volunteer organizations, and explanations of roles, responsibilities, and
 procedures under applicable law. While relevant as of the time of publication, this
 material tends to be perishable as standards, guides, regulations, organizations, and
 practices change. Unless regular updates are anticipated, it might be better to have
 references to places to find the information, rather than summaries of the information
 itself.

Thus, the CSEPP recovery workbook may inform future recovery planning efforts for various
specific hazards or all-hazards scenarios.

2.4 Alabama Hazard Analysis Working Group25
A small working group incorporating both technical and operational personnel proved effective
in developing procedures and agreements to make the best use of available technology.

2.4.1 Summary

The Alabama Hazard Analysis Working Group (HAWG) was instituted early in the CSEP
Program. It was tasked with developing common procedures and agreements across the entire
Alabama CSEPP community to make the best use of available technology and operational
practices and thus provide the quickest and safest decisions to protect the general public. As the
technology in CSEPP evolved, so did the role of the HAWG to ensure that the technology was
used to the best advantage. The HAWG informally joined with the Alabama CSEPP Automation
Working IPT to ensure tight integration between technology and operational procedures.

This approach, using a small working group of both technical and operational personnel to solve
specific issues, can be applied in any new, or even existing, program that involves a number of
organizations that must develop and agree upon how to make the best use of technology and
procedures in a complex operating environment.

2.4.2 Background

The CSEP Program was established in 1988. Serious attention to this program began in Alabama
around 1990 when the state began to receive CSEPP funding and authorization to hire CSEPP
personnel at both the state and local county levels. The installation also began efforts to develop
plans in accordance with the new requirements of the CSEP Program at this time. In the early
years, there were no CSEPP automation systems, communication systems, or procedures to
communicate accident information off-post in the event of an accident. The installation did have
stand-alone D2PC (a plume modeling program) to make elementary plume projections. But the
county emergency management agencies (EMAs) and the state EMA were not well versed in the
threat and were not sure what information they even needed. The whole concept of a coordinated
alert-and-notification system among the installation, the CSEPP counties, and the state EMA was
brand new territory, wide open to development of a process to assess a hazard, alert the public,
and provide emergency notification information.

2.4.3 Issue

How can a diverse community consisting of the Army installation, six CSEPP counties, and the
State of Alabama come together to:
 a. Become educated about the nature of the hazard?
 b. Assess the risks involved?

25

 22

 c. Determine the needs and capabilities of each stakeholder regarding the technical
 aspect of the potential hazard?
 d. Develop procedures to address the needs and portray the hazard?
 e. Make a protective action recommendation (PAR) and protective action decision
 (PAD)?
 f. Develop mechanisms to transmit the information as quickly and consistently as
 possible from the installation to the off-post community?
 g. Have a process to continually review, refine, and revise these procedures as
 stakeholder needs changed and/or the technology improved?

2.4.4 Solution

The Alabama HAWG was established to address these issues.26 It became a standing body that
convened as needed throughout the entire CSEP Program in Alabama. When the Alabama
CSEPP IPT formed in 1995, the HAWG was incorporated into the IPT as a working IPT, but the
name was retained.

The purpose of the HAWG was to look at the technical issues and develop plans and procedures
to address them. Early on in the program, the solutions consisted of a large number of
documents, named Forms, to address the output from the D2PC model and turn it into
procedures, actions, and communication tools. Each Form was numbered, from 1 to 20. A Form
could consist of as little a single page, such as Form 18, which was used by the Depot to provide
notification of the chemical accident to off post; or it could be as large as a 3” binder, such as
Form 19, which contained numerous tables based on type of agent, wind direction, and speed to
enable the manual creation of PARs and PADs.

As automation tools improved, many of these forms were abandoned as the functions they
performed were incorporated into the automated system. Form 18 was the last one abandoned
when WebPuffTM was released in 2003.

The role of the HAWG also evolved as the automation tools developed, first from the stand-
alone D2PC, to D2PCw, D2Puff, to WebPuff (see the Lesson Learned on the Alabama CSEPP
Automation Working IPT for more information on these systems). As the automation systems
evolved, they began to come with more “bells and whistles” and options that could be used. The
role of the HAWG became focused on reviewing the new features and functions of the
automation systems and making a determination on how best to use each one, or not use it, to
meet the operational needs of the Alabama CSEPP community. Once consensus was reached
among its members, the HAWG would make a recommendation to the Alabama CSEPP IPT for
a decision, and the memorandum of agreement (MOA) and operating procedures were revised to
accommodate the IPT’s decision.

26
 Note: other CSEPP sites also established similar groups; this one is described to illustrate their value.

 23

During the latter part of the program, from about 2005 to close out, it became apparent that many
of the functions of the HAWG were overlapping those of the Automation Working IPT. Thus,
the two groups were informally combined to work together to:
 1. Make recommended improvements to the automations system, such as automating the
 off-post notification form.
 2. Perform operational testing of new versions of the automation system.
 3. Look at the best ways to use new systems operationally.
 4. Make recommendations to the IPT for implementation or a rationale for not fielding new
 versions of the automation systems.
 5. Recommend the best time, both technically and operationally, to change over to a new
 version of the automation systems and the best timing for changeovers.

2.4.5 Potential Application

The HAWG allowed a cross section of stakeholders from various diverse organizations to come
together to look at both technical and operational issues involved in a very complex program.
The consensus-building process, followed by formal presentation to the decision making body
(the IPT), resulted in the best practices and procedures for using the ever-evolving tools of the
CSEP Program. This process also provided for common procedures, terminology, and practices
to be implemented throughout the entire Alabama CSEPP community, thus providing the best
protection to the public using the available technology and experience of the operations staff.
This approach can be applied in any new or existing program that involves a number of
organizations that must develop and agree upon how to make the best use of technology and
procedures in a complex operating environment.

2.4.6 References

Lesson content derived from correspondence and conversations with Len Motz, Argonne
technical support to Anniston Army Depot.

2.5 Alabama Automation IPT27
The Alabama CSEPP Automation Working Integrated Process Team (AWIPT) demonstrated the
importance and value of local testing for automation systems.

2.5.1 Summary

The Alabama CSEPP AWIPT was created shortly after the advent of the IPT process in the
Alabama CSEP Program. The AWIPT consisted of automation experts, hazard analysts, and
operations personnel representing a cross section of the Alabama CSEPP community. The goal
of the AWIPT was to monitor developing automation systems, make recommendations for
27

 24

improvements and new features, and thoroughly test newly fielded automation systems before
recommending their operational use to the Alabama CSEPP IPT. The Alabama AWIPT worked
closely with the system developer and the National CSEPP Automation IPT to ensure that the
Alabama CSEPP community’s requirements were met. Extensive testing protocols were
developed and used to evaluate software for use in Alabama. As automation issues became
increasingly interwoven with operational issues, the Alabama CSEPP AWIPT combined with the
Alabama Hazard Analysis Working Group (HAWG) to jointly resolve issues from about 2005 to
the end of the program in Alabama in 2011. The methods employed to test systems in Alabama
were essentially embraced by the software developer, Innovative Emergency Management, Inc.
(IEM), in 2011 to be employed at the remaining two CSEPP sites in Kentucky and Colorado.
Key elements that made the AWIPT effective as a test bed were the diverse skills of the group
members, the group’s autonomy within the national program, communication with local and
national program elements, and the systematic methods that the group developed.

2.5.2 Background

When the CSEP Program was established in Alabama around 1990, the program needed a quick,
reliable way for the Army to notify and inform off-post authorities in the event of a chemical-
weapons emergency on an installation. An automation system, with computers on the installation
linked to computers in the off-post emergency operations centers, offered a way to communicate
information quickly. However, there were very few automation tools (software to accomplish
this goal) available. No one was certain at that time what tools would be available or when they
would be developed. Even in the early stages of the CSEP Program, the requirement to have
state-of-the-art automation tools was well understood. The development task was taken on by the
National CSEPP Automation Sub-Committee. As the national program developed automation
tools (first the Emergency Management Information System [EMIS] and then the unsuccessful
Federal Emergency Management Information System [FEMIS]), it was apparent that the
Alabama CSEPP community would need a multidisciplinary group to monitor these
developments and determine whether they would meet the needs of the Alabama CSEPP
community. The group would need automation technicians, hazard analysts, and operations
personnel in order to test the nationally developed systems and recommend whether to adopt
them locally. The tools developed would need to:
 • Meet the critical five-minute time limit to provide a hazard assessment and transmit
 notification to the local off-post emergency management agencies,
 • Provide tools to the responders to help make response decisions, and
 • Provide a suitable method to log and review actions taken.

Thus, when the Alabama CSEPP IPT was formed in 1995, one of the original Working IPTs
established within the IPT was the Alabama CSEPP AWIPT.

 25

2.5.3 Issue

Any automation tools developed for the CSEP Program in Alabama were required to meet the
general CSEPP requirements as well as any Alabama-specific requirements. The questions
facing the Alabama CSEPP community were how to:
 a. Identify Alabama-specific requirements that were part of the operational plan, such as
 integration of Form 18 (Anniston Army Depot [ANAD] Chemical Accident Notification
 Form) and the Alabama Reference Number System into the automation system for use in
 Alabama.
 b. Develop a methodology for testing new automation systems within the Alabama CSEPP
 community.
 c. Help the National CSEPP Automation IPT identify general requirements of an
 automation system to meet CSEPP standards.
 d. Determine the schedule and timing of fielding new automation systems so as to avoid
 interfering with operational schedules.
 e. Ensure adequate training was provided to all users of the automation tools, not just
 hazard analysts.
 f. Make recommendations to the Alabama CSEPP IPT on emerging CSEPP automation
 tools.

2.5.4 Solution

It was recognized that a standing group of automation experts, hazard analysts, and operational
planners was required to look at these issues, solve them, test potential systems, and make
recommendations to both the Alabama CSEPP IPT and, when appropriate, the National CSEPP
Automation IPT. One of the first tasks of the Alabama CSEPP AWIPT was to review the
operational procedures that had already been established in the Alabama CSEPP community to
determine which elements could feasibly be automated and which ones could not. This
information was then conveyed to the National CSEPP Automation IPT as a request for
consideration in developing automation systems.

Once automation systems were ready for field trials and/or testing, the Automation IPT would
work with the system developer to ensure that adequate training was provided. After training on
the system, the Alabama CSEPP AWIPT developed protocols and test exercises in the
community to ensure that the systems not only operated as advertised but also met all of the
requirements to provide timely and accurate information. These tests would be conducted in
individual jurisdictions, as well as through a series of exercises conducted over a period of 30 to
90 days, depending on the system being tested. This protocol was applied to EMIS, FEMIS,
D2Puff, and WebPuff for each version fielded. Any problem detected or new functions that
needed to meet requirements, etc., would be reported back to the developer and the National
CSEPP Automation IPT. This process worked well, gave each system a thorough shake-down,
and provided feedback to the developer. Except for FEMIS, which was rejected in Alabama, it

 26

resulted in improvements to the systems before they went into operation. The Alabama CSEPP
Automation IPT also rejected one version of D2Puff and one version of WebPuff.

When the Alabama CSEPP AWIPT decided a system was suitable, a recommendation would be
made to the Alabama CSEPP IPT. If the recommendation was accepted, the AWIPT would work
with the developer for final installation and the training contractors to ensure proper training
before the system would become operational on a date established by the IPT.

Automation and operational procedures became more tightly integrated when WebPuff was
developed. The Alabama CSEPP AWIPT combined with the HAWG to jointly work through
issues, develop best practices and procedures for use of the more complex systems, and make
joint reports and recommendations to the Alabama CSEPP IPT. This joint effort proved most
beneficial in determining how to best switch from the old, well-understood system of “no
effects,” “no deaths,” and “1% lethality,” to the Acute Exposure Guideline Level (AEGL) system
in 2003. This process continued to the end of CSEPP’s operational requirements for automation
in September 2011.

2.5.5 Potential Application

The approach taken by the Alabama CSEPP AWIPT proved to be most beneficial in finding
issues with developing systems, reporting them, getting them fixed, and making new feature
requests for future versions of the system. It is fair to say that as the program progressed to
D2Puff and particularly WebPuff, the developer probably dreaded the almost daily phone call
they would receive from the AWIPT during one of the operational testing phases. Yet they came
to appreciate, over time, the issues that the group would find through operational testing, which
they could not always create in their more limited laboratory testing environment. Indeed, the
approach developed in Alabama was essentially adopted by IEM in 2011 and used at the two
remaining CSEPP sites (Pueblo and Blue Grass) for the remainder of the program.

The AWIPT helped develop and adapt automation systems to perform rather technical
analyses—hazardous material dispersion modeling, including hazardous vapor concentrations
and dose levels over time for particular areas near the potential source. The system also
generated recommendations for protective actions for the public. The system had to be fast,
reliable, and user-friendly in order to support local officials in determining the hazard and
issuing warnings to protect the public. Key elements that made the AWIPT an effective user
group were:
 • Composition of the group. The group had a mix of emergency managers, hazard analysts,
 and information technology (IT) experts, all serving at the local level. It had the benefits
 of cross-disciplinary communication and multiple perspectives, but all at the local level.
 • Autonomy. The group’s mission was to vet potential systems for local use. The national-
 level program developed the systems, but the Alabama community had the option as to
 whether to adopt them. Thus, the AWIPT served as a group of empowered customers—
 they would adopt the national program’s product only if they felt it would meet local
 needs.

 27

 • Feedback. The group regularly communicated both with local users and with the
 technical staff who developed the systems. This three-way communication provided
 useful feedback for developing and refining the automation systems.
 • Systematic methodology. The group served as an independent test bed for the software
 developers, since the members of AWIPT were not linked to the developers and produced
 their own methods and protocols for testing. The key elements of the methodology
 included multiple simple and complex scenario exercises on a planned schedule which
 involved the entire local CSEPP community. Thus, the system and communication links
 were placed under a full operational load. Problems were identified and communicated to
 the developer. If the problem identified as a “showstopper” type of issue, the exercise
 schedule would be suspended until the developer could fix it.

Any program to develop software for emergency management functions would be well served by
incorporating these elements to ensure effective testing by an independent user group. The level
of “stress” placed on the system, both the software and hardware, involving the whole local user
community cannot be fully replicated in the developer’s testing environment.

2.5.6 References

Lesson content derived from correspondence and conversations with Len Motz, Argonne
technical support to Anniston Army Depot.

 28

2.6 Alabama Reentry/Recovery Planning Guide28
Recovery planning was enhanced by the use of a small working group that developed a locally
adapted recovery planning guide. The guide was tested and refined through tabletop exercises.

2.6.1 Summary

Early CSEPP planning efforts in Alabama concentrated on the development of response-phase
planning and the acquisition of equipment. Once these efforts were well established, the
Alabama CSEPP community began to take a serious look at recovery-phase planning. The
Alabama CSEPP Integrated Process Team (IPT) decided to develop planning guidance that
would enable recovery plans to be developed quickly after an accident to meet the specific
scenario needs. A Reentry and Restoration Working Group (RRWG) was established to develop
just such a recovery planning guide. The RRWG included representatives of the U.S. Army and
local, state, and federal stakeholders. A draft guide was developed, tested, and refined over the
course of a few tabletop exercises (TTXs). As completed, the recovery planning guide outlined
organizational roles for recovery planning and covered elements of reentry and restoration such
as planning section staffing, recovery planning assumptions, post-emergency monitoring and
sampling, a phased reentry approach, recovery-phase public information, and procedures for
developing and approving a reentry/restoration plan.

Key elements of the success of this project included (a) the interagency and interdisciplinary
composition of the planning team, and (b) the concept of developing a planning guide that could
be adapted to expedite recovery planning for a spectrum of possible post-emergency scenarios.
This approach and concept may prove useful as communities implement new recovery guidance
and doctrine as represented by the National Disaster Recovery Framework (NDRF).

2.6.2 Background

In the spring of 1993, the Alabama CSEPP community conducted some basic recovery-phase
operations as part of a Service Response Force Exercise (SRFX). A draft recovery plan was
outlined as a result of this exercise. No further work was pursued on this issue until the Alabama
CSEPP community began to plan for the 1998 Anniston Community CSEPP Exercise (ACE-98).
A decision was made then to explore recovery-phase issues again.

As a follow-on to ACE-98, a tabletop discussion was held among the various federal, state, and
local agencies in the Alabama CSEPP community. The outcome of this discussion was a
conceptual outline of how to approach recovery-phase operations. The concept employed a
Federal Response Center to coordinate and control reentry and limited restoration operations
after a chemical accident or incident. The Army On-Scene Coordinator (OSC), pursuant to the
provisions of the National Contingency Plan (NCP), would be in charge of activities in the
Federal Response Center, with representatives of other Federal, State, and local agencies
participating as required.

28

 29

A working group was formed, headed by Anniston Chemical Activity, with representatives from
Anniston Army Depot, Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA) Region IV,
U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) Region IV, the Alabama Department of
Environmental Management (ADEM), and the Alabama Emergency Management Agency
(AEMA). The working group developed a general Anniston Chemical Stockpile Emergency
Preparedness Program (CSEPP) Reentry and Restoration Planning Guide for the Alabama
CSEPP community. The draft planning guide was developed by 2003, then tested and refined
over the course of the next few years in a series of recovery TTXs.

Further adjustments were made to the planning guide due to changes in national incident
management philosophy as the National Incident Management System (NIMS) was
implemented. The need for change was further amplified by the 2006 SRFX (sometimes referred
to as the Chemical Incident Management Exercise or CIMX), which was specifically designed to
look at the interface of CSEPP response mechanisms, the NCP, and the National Response Plan.
A group consisting of members from Anniston Chemical Activity, Anniston Army Depot,
U.S. Department of Homeland Security (DHS)/FEMA Region 4, Alabama Emergency
Management Agency, and select Alabama CSEPP counties was formed to revise the planning
guide. The final revision of the Anniston Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program
(CSEPP) Reentry and Restoration Planning Guide was published in September 2007.

2.6.3 Issue

How to ensure that response operations would smoothly transition to recovery operations, and
that recovery-phase decisions such as reentry into restricted areas would be well supported and
implemented.

2.6.4 Solution

In 1998, the RRWG was established and functioned like a Working IPT of the Alabama CSEPP
IPT. Early on, it was decided that the initial effort would not focus on writing an actual Recovery
Plan, since actual recovery plans would necessarily be very accident-specific. The approach
taken was to write an umbrella planning guide that would provide the guidance, information,
organizational structure, and process to write an accident-specific plan when and if needed. The
concepts developed by the Off-Post Monitoring Integrated Product Team2 were incorporated into
the planning guide, as well as a detailed planning process chart to follow in developing accident-
specific plans and making Recovery Phase decisions. As noted above, the planning guide, which
underwent a number of tests and revisions over the years, was finalized in September 2007. The
RRWG disbanded in 2008.

Key elements of the success of this project included (a) the interagency and interdisciplinary
composition of the planning team, and (b) the concept of developing a planning guide that could
be adapted to expedite recovery planning for a spectrum of possible post-emergency scenarios.

 30

Composition of the Working Group

The composition of the working group was important in at least two ways. First, the presence of
representatives of the Army installation, DHS/FEMA, and the state and local jurisdictions
allowed for informed discussion of the roles and responsibilities for each organization. The
Army OSC would be directing operations at the accident scene, and generally directing
environmental assessment and cleanup operations, with the assistance of other Army units and
various other federal assets, including the EPA Regional Response Team (RRT). DHS/FEMA
would implement disaster assistance under the Stafford Act and the National Response Plan
(now the National Response Framework [NRF]). State and local officials would be the key
decision makers for managing public protective actions and allowing reentry into restricted
areas. Coordinating the different organizational roles was a focus of the recovery planning guide.

Second, the planning team consisted of emergency management specialists and technical
specialists in hazard assessment. The combination of skills allowed important communication to
take place regarding the role of data in supporting recovery decision making. The planning guide
describes the process of gathering data about where a chemical contaminant plume has been and
whether there is any residual contamination. Post-emergency, decision makers want to know
which areas are safe for reentry; it is important for them to understand the limitations on the
monitoring and sampling process and how long it may take to complete an assessment. Likewise,
it is important for hazard analysts to understand the pressure to make decisions and to allow
people back into their homes if it is safe to do so.

Development of Recovery Planning Guide

The recovery planning guide developed by the RRWG included planning for those elements of
the process that could be planned ahead, while leaving open the items that would need to be
developed post-emergency to respond to the specific scenario. For example, the planning guide
described the post-emergency Reentry and Restoration Planning Team (RRPT), including its
place in the ICS structure, workspace, leadership, and sources for staffing. However, it did not
prescribe a particular staffing level since that would depend on the severity of the situation at the
time. Similarly, the planning guide described a process for the team to develop a phased
reentry/restoration plan—but the particulars of the plan, including the areas affected, the types of
monitoring and sampling that would need to be carried out, and other elements, would be
specific to the situation. Similar to the National Recovery Framework in concept, the planning
guide described a framework for decision making and implementation without prescribing
specific courses of action that would need to be determined at the time.

2.6.5 Potential Application

As noted in the NDRF,

 The speed and success of recovery can be greatly enhanced by establishment of
 the process and protocols prior to a disaster for coordinated post-disaster recovery
 planning and implementation. All stakeholders should be involved to ensure a
 coordinated and comprehensive planning process, and develop relationships that

 31

 increase post-disaster collaboration and unified decisionmaking. . . Partnerships
 and collaboration across groups, sectors and governments promote a successful
 recovery process. Partnerships and inclusiveness are vital for ensuring that all
 voices are heard from all parties involved in disaster recovery and that all
 available resources are brought to the table. This is especially critical at the
 community level where nongovernmental partners in the private and nonprofit
 sectors play a critical role in meeting local needs.29

Recovery planning is a complex issue that requires a large number of stakeholders to ensure that
all aspects are considered and that a holistic approach is taken to the recovery planning process.
In the CSEP Program, recovery planning for the remaining sites in Colorado and Kentucky
continued for a number of years, employing approaches and techniques pioneered in Alabama.
These approaches and techniques could serve as a useful model for recovery planning in any
community for any hazard.
