2.7 Program Closeout30
CSEPP shut down at each site as the chemical destruction process was completed. Knowing that
shutdown would occur, each community took its own approach to planning for and executing
program closure. National-level guidance on program closeout was developed to help with this
process.

2.7.1 Summary

Emergency management programs, like any government program, may suffer a reduction in
funding due to changes in hazards or demographics, budget issues, or any number of other
factors. In the Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program (CSEPP), local
communities were able to build substantial emergency management programs with the support of
federal funding and technical assistance. However, once the hazard (chemical weapons stored at
U.S. Army installations) was eliminated, CSEPP funding ended; and then those communities had
to adjust and streamline their programs to more sustainable levels. The adjustments could be
anticipated in advance and, as a result, were planned for and guided. This lesson summarizes
lessons learned from the first six CSEPP to close (the closed sites as of 2018). The goal is to
provide useful advice for any community facing a potential reduction in funding for emergency
management. Based on the CSEPP experience, a community’s approach to such a transition
should include advance planning and should consider community size, reuse of equipment and
facilities, and lasting benefits with regard to personnel development and interagency
coordination.

2.7.2 Background

In 1985, Congress mandated the destruction of the U.S. stockpile of chemical weapons to
comply with international treaty obligations. The mandate to destroy the chemical weapons came
with a provision requiring “maximum protection for the environment, the general public, and the
personnel involved.”31 From that simple but powerful phrase sprang the CSEP Program, created
to assist communities near the eight Army chemical-weapons storage sites in preparing for any
emergencies involving those weapons.

The Army and the Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA) worked with local and
state emergency management agencies to upgrade their capabilities for responding to a potential
chemical weapons emergency. Upgrades included building and improving facilities, plans,
staffing, training, and equipment, and they were tested regularly with drills and exercises. The
communities, many of which were sparsely populated rural counties with few resources for
emergency management, were able to build substantial programs with CSEPP funding and
technical assistance.

However, while the CSEP Program was helping build community preparedness, officials also
knew from the beginning that the hazard would eventually disappear and federal program

30
 Lesson written in 2018.
31
     Public Law 99-145, DoD Authorization Act, 1986, Section 1412(c)(1)(A).

 33

funding would come to an end. While building up local community response capabilities, at the
same time the Army was building incinerators and chemical processing facilities to destroy the
chemical weapons (referred to as “demilitarization”) and eliminate the hazard. Logically and
through Congressional mandate, CSEPP was linked to the existence of the chemical weapons
stockpile: at each storage site, CSEPP assistance and funding remained in place only as long as
the hazard it was designed to address. Once the weapons were destroyed at a given site, the
program came to an end for that community.

At each site, the end of the program—referred to as closeout—was anticipated and planned for.
The Maryland site (Edgewood community), being the first to close, more or less had to invent
the process. Later-closing sites had the opportunity to benefit from the experience of earlier sites.

Guidance on how to plan for and execute closeout was developed at the national level and then
executed at the local level by state and local governments with the assistance of FEMA. Closeout
of CSEPP occurred at six sites located in Alabama, Arkansas, Indiana, Maryland, Oregon, and
Utah over the period 2005–2012. At each of these sites, the program had been in place for many
years before closeout—more than 20 years at some of them.

Each site had its own unique circumstances to deal with. To collect data for analysis, staff from
Argonne National Laboratory reviewed closeout planning documents (national guidance and
individual site plans), interviewed personnel from the closed sites, scanned closeout reports
produced by the Chemical Materials Agency, and gathered information from CSEPP reports to
Congress, local news coverage, and other sources. A few themes emerged and are discussed in
the analysis below, followed by conclusions and potential applications.

2.7.3 Issue

The CSEPP communities needed to address how to adapt to the loss of CSEPP funding,
including retention of CSEPP-funded personnel and capabilities, and avoidance of waste.

2.7.4 Solution

After completion of closeout at six sites, sufficient experience existed to analyze the process that
was followed and the results that were obtained. Themes emerged relating to planning,
community size, reuse of equipment, and lasting benefits to personnel and interagency
coordination.

National Planning/Guidance Effort

In 2003, anticipating the end of the program at several sites, CSEPP formed a program-wide
integrated process team (IPT) to help guide the closeout process. The mission of the Closeout
IPT was to:
 • Identify DHS and DoD regulatory, policy, and guidance materials relevant to program
 closeout.

 34

 • Identify specific program closeout issues for the U.S. Army, FEMA, and state and local
 CSEPP organizations and, for each issue, identify requirements, necessary research,
 potential approaches, and real-world solutions.
 • Identify program activities and suggested approaches to support the transition from a
 CSEPP-funded program to an all-hazards emergency management program.
 • Identify activities to reduce the complexity, length, and cost of the closeout process.
 • Develop program closeout guidance that incorporates the IPT’s work in the areas listed
 above and provide this information to CSEPP personnel in an easily accessible manner.32

Program closeout was highlighted in plenary and/or breakout sessions at each of the CSEPP
National Workshops from 2003–2010. In addition, in 2008 a two-day state and local forum was
held in Denver, Colorado, focusing exclusively on closeout issues.

The IPT developed a closeout guidebook to serve as a resource for CSEPP communities
approaching the end of the program. The guidebook, last revised in 2010, addressed:
 • The process of planning for closeout;
 • Life-Cycle Cost Estimate (LCCE) and budget considerations for closeout;
 • How to deal with program components such as personnel, facilities, leases, equipment,
 and systems;
 • How to maintain strong capabilities through the end of demilitarization operations; and
 • How to close out FEMA grant requirements.33

The guidebook also contained annexes with information on public affairs planning for closeout
(developed by the Public Affairs IPT), alternative funding sources (to help identify emergency
management funding opportunities after CSEPP), a closeout task list, and a description of a
spreadsheet-based tool for closeout planning, the Closeout Planning Matrix. The individual sites
used these tools to varying degrees during their closeout processes.

Effect of Community Size

The size of the communities was a significant factor determining the impact of the loss of
CSEPP funding. The Edgewood Chemical Activity (ECA) in Maryland was in a densely
populated metropolitan area with robust local government. This allowed personnel and resources
no longer needed for CSEPP to be absorbed into the larger whole. The Harford County
emergency management director, interviewed a few years after closeout, described a process of
planning and budgeting to find alternate funding to maintain key items after the CSEPP money
ran out. He was proud to report that 100% of his CSEPP personnel were retained by the County.
Similarly the Kent County Director of Emergency Services remarked that, “all of our plans and

32
 CSEPP Program Closeout IPT Charter, 2003. Accessed on the CSEPP Portal website,
 https://www.cseppportal.net.
33
 U.S. Army and FEMA, CSEPP Closeout Guidance, August 2010, p. 5. Accessed on the CSEPP Portal website,
 https://www.cseppportal.net.

 35

all of our equipment [including] decontamination equipment that was purchased through the
CSEPP program [are] still being used” (as of 2009, four years after CSEPP closeout in
Maryland). COL Gladney, ECA commander during the closure process, said in his interview that
while the community appreciated the CSEPP funding and used it wisely, “the community wasn’t
really fretting” over its ability to maintain a high level of preparedness post-CSEPP.34

In contrast, the Newport Chemical Depot in Indiana was located in a remote, very rural area. The
Newport CSEP Program included five counties in two States (Indiana and Illinois). Of the five
counties, four had populations between 16,000 and 19,000 in the 2010 census; the fifth had a
population of 81,625. Prior to CSEPP, the Newport area county emergency management
agencies had very limited capabilities. CSEPP enabled the communities to build robust
capabilities that included expanded staffing, emergency operations centers (EOCs), public alert
and notification (A&N) systems, communications systems, and public outreach programs.
However, after closeout, most of the CSEPP-funded personnel were lost (retired, moved, or
found other positions), and the facilities and equipment were largely repurposed or reallocated.
EOCs funded by CSEPP were still in use for emergency management purposes; however, with
reduced emergency management staffing, the facilities also acquired other uses. In one county
the County Commission started meeting in the EOC conference room, freeing space in the
Courthouse so that the County Auditor could have a real office instead of (literally) working out
of a coat closet.35

Reuse of Resources

With the loss of CSEPP funding—and the reduction of risk—at the closed sites, in many cases
the decision was made to reuse CSEPP-acquired resources for other purposes and sometimes in
other locations.

In Indiana, one county had a mobile command unit that was left behind in good condition and
was still in use as of 2015. Also as of 2015, the county had retained three CSEPP-funded
decontamination (decon) trailers of which one was actively in use for training. Other trailers
were in use by regional (district) hazardous materials (hazmat) teams, for decon, training, or as a
field shower unit for response personnel.36

Public warning systems, in particular, were often reallocated. At the Anniston, Alabama, site,
some counties agreed to take over maintenance of sirens erected by CSEPP — severe storms and
tornadoes are a significant threat there, and the sirens can be useful warning devices for those
situations. However, maintenance costs for the sirens are considerable, and some counties could
not afford the upkeep; those sirens were moved to other communities or stored for spare parts.37
In Oregon, one county transferred its sirens to coastal counties to be used for tsunami warnings.38

 36

Also in Oregon, highway message boards were transferred to the state department of
transportation for continued use. In Utah, sirens and highway message boards located in more
densely populated areas were left in place, but others were transferred to more populated areas.

Retention or reallocation of radio communication systems (including handheld units, consoles,
towers, and repeaters) also became an issue at some sites where maintenance costs were a
concern. At least two sites transferred responsibility for radio systems to multi-jurisdiction
entities. In Arkansas, radio maintenance was taken over by the Metropolitan Emergency
Communications Association, funded jointly by city and county jurisdictions. In Alabama, the
state legislature created a special Regional Communications System board, with members
nominated by the two main CSEPP counties. Operating and maintenance costs were allocated on
a per-radio basis among many organizations (county and municipal police and fire departments).
However, the Regional Communication System was unable to raise funds when the system was
determined to require updating in 2013. As a result, the system was turned over to county control
with each county taking over the resources within its boundaries.39

Some resources were not reused due to limited shelf life or their specialized, hazard-specific
nature. For example, at some CSEPP sites, local schools had been outfitted with specialized
ventilation equipment to allow safe, positive-pressure sheltering in place. This equipment was
removed after the hazard was eliminated to avoid future maintenance costs; the cost of removal
became part of the closeout plan at these sites. Also, personal protective equipment (PPE)
acquired for use by first responders was eventually discarded or relegated to training use after
exceeding its expiration date, as reported by officials in Indiana and Utah.40

The Soft Power of Training and Agreements

Officials involved in the closeout process often focus on facilities and equipment and whether it
can be reused, repurposed, or transferred. There is a natural concern to ensure that valuable items
do not go to waste. However, some of the most lasting benefits of the program may lie in the
softer realms of personnel development and intergovernmental relationships.

A number of officials interviewed were grateful for the opportunities that CSEPP provided their
staff to acquire training and experience that could be carried over to future duties or future
positions. A Maryland county director characterized the CSEPP training as “invaluable.”41 In
Arkansas, a CSEPP recap noted that “[m]ore than 2,800 emergency responders were trained
through the CSEPP. These trained professionals include emergency managers, firefighters,
police officers, medical teams and hazardous material technicians.”42 In Indiana, one county EM
director reported that because of his CSEPP experience, he was often consulted by surrounding

39
 Anderson, Brian, 2013, Radio system begins $4.1 million upgrade, Anniston Star, December 25, accessed
 October 2016 at http://www.annistonstar.com/news/local/radio-system-begins-million-
     upgrade/article_75e61add-18ca-5bf2-a9cd-30a9080d82e2.html.
40
 Interview with Mark O’Heir, Director, Vermillion County Indiana Emergency Management Agency, August 24,
 2015. Report on Deseret Chemical Depot closeout process prepared by Wade Mathews, Argonne National
 Laboratory, 2015.

 Interview with Sue Willits, Director of Emergency Services for Kent County, Maryland, 2009.

 U.S. Army Chemical Materials Agency, 2011, Pine Bluff Chemical Weapons Stockpile Elimination End of
 Operations: Our Noble Mission Safely Completed, 2005–2010, February.

 37

jurisdictions for planning and preparedness advice. On one occasion, a much larger, more
populous county asked him how to get started on building an emergency plan. Years after
CSEPP he was still considered the local “go-to person.”43

CSEPP also occasioned a lot of contact among neighboring jurisdictions and response
organizations through participation in IPTs, planning meetings, workshops, joint projects, and
exercises, as well as more directly through negotiation of mutual aid agreements. In Arkansas, a
network of mutual aid agreements was established among the 10 CSEPP counties, along with
agreements with resource/service providers. A hospital emergency preparedness coordinator in
Maryland commented on the value of making contact with other medical providers in the region
via CSEPP and planned to maintain those networks after the program ended.44

Conclusions

CSEPP was able to anticipate its own mortality and took advantage of that foreknowledge to
plan for and implement the closeout process in an organized way. The experiences of the six
sites that had closed out as of 2018 were been quite varied, and inevitably there were some
disruptions and displacement when funding came to an end. However, it appears that on the
whole, the planning process helped to ease the transition and avoid unnecessary adverse impacts.
Thus, the first lesson that can be drawn is that planning pays off—not a surprising conclusion in
the world of emergency management. Additional lessons include:
 • Organizational size matters. Larger, more robust emergency management organizations,
 typically found in more populous, urbanized areas, are better able to reallocate or
 reabsorb both personnel and equipment internally. In a more rural setting, personnel and
 equipment may need to be reallocated on a regional or statewide basis, requiring
 intergovernmental cooperation and/or coordination at a higher (i.e., state) level.
 • Plan for reusing equipment. Equipment such as radios, public warning devices, PPE,
 decon trailers, computers, office equipment, and the like may have been acquired to meet
 specific needs or address specific hazards that are no longer present. To minimize the
 potential for waste, the sites were urged to do the following:
 o Consider versatility when acquiring equipment, particularly equipment that is
 expensive and/or has a long useful life. For example, a mobile hazmat trailer is a
 major investment and should be adaptable to various uses.
 o Consider maintenance costs. Items that require specialized maintenance may not
 be able to be supported in future when resources are more limited.
 o Look for regional or statewide options for equipment reuse. Items such as sirens
 or highway reader boards may not be sustainable in a sparse rural area but may be
 reallocated successfully to areas with greater concentrations of population and/or
 greater hazards.
 • Invest in people and relationships. While items such as PPE, radios, and computers may
 soon become obsolete or unusable, training, certification, and the experience of personnel

43
 Interview with Joe W. Whitaker, EMA Director, Fountain County, Indiana, August28, 2015.
44
 CSEPP Legacy interviews with Sue Willits and Christina Hughes, 2009.

 38

 can have a long-lasting (and portable) benefit. In addition, inter-organizational
 relationships can also build lasting capabilities. Just knowing the people and resources
 available in neighboring areas is a step up; memorializing the relationship in a mutual aid
 agreement is even better, because it establishes a connection that can extend beyond the
 tenure of specific personnel.

2.7.5 Potential Application

Any situation where a reduction in program funding is anticipated would be a potential
application for the recommendations above. Funding reductions can occur for a variety of
reasons—budget crunches, tax base reductions, or changing budget priorities.

Emergency management funding may also suffer when there is a perception of reduced risk.
Natural hazards don’t really change—wherever there is a risk of severe storms, floods, wildfires,
or earthquakes, that risk tends to persist; and the potential cost in human terms and property
damage only rises over time. However, technical hazards can change when major sources of risk
are removed. The most direct parallel to the CSEPP closeouts would be the shutdown of a
nuclear power plant. When a nuclear power plant shuts down, a specific, unusual technical
hazard goes away—and likely a source of local emergency preparedness funding as well. As a
highly regulated industry, the shutdown process takes some time, and so there would likely be
some advance warning allowing emergency managers to plan ahead.

Shutdowns or changes to other types of industrial facilities such as chemical plants, refineries,
mines, or other operations that use or produce hazardous materials may also reduce community
risks and resources, leading to a budgetary reduction for emergency managers.

 3 Exercises

Exercises have been a major component of CSEPP and have driven many advances in chemical
weapons emergency preparedness. In turn, the resources and effort dedicated to exercising by
CSEPP advanced the art of large-scale exercise planning, control, and evaluation. As noted in
Volume I of the CSEPP History, exercise practices developed by CSEPP were later adopted
nationwide in the Homeland Security Exercise Evaluation Program.

This section includes lessons learned pertaining to exercise design and execution, including
overall exercise design, and several individual components of the exercise process: extent-of-
play agreements, scenario design, control systems, Simulation Cells (SimCells), and significant
events timelines.

3.1 Exercise Evaluation Design and Guidance45
The program developed a performance-oriented evaluation methodology that works across a
spectrum of jurisdictions and tasks, producing evaluations that are perceived as well grounded.

3.1.1 Summary

The CSEP Program needed an evaluation program that would bridge the differences among the
involved agencies and would produce defensible results. Both the Army and FEMA had well-
developed exercise programs but with somewhat differing approaches and philosophies. Through
cooperative processes, exercise policies and practices evolved over time, and the program
developed highly integrated systems for managing and gauging readiness. The CSEPP exercise
evaluation methodology emphasized evaluator observation and assessment of the outcome of
play rather than simply checking off a list of expected actions. The performance-oriented
approach relied on the expertise of the evaluator to determine whether the functions and purposes
of emergency response were being demonstrated successfully. The techniques developed for
CSEPP drew from both Army and FEMA roots and provided a basis for reports to Congress,
budget allocation, and facility permitting. CSEPP techniques were later adopted on a national
scale for the Homeland Security Exercise Evaluation Program (HSEEP).

3.1.2 Background

From CSEPP’s inception, exercises were a key element in determining how effectively the Army
and State and local organizations could perform and coordinate emergency response functions.
Each CSEPP site conducted exercises annually from 1992 until closeout. Each exercise required
several months of planning and coordination among the Army, FEMA, State and local response
agencies, and support contractors. A full-scale CSEPP functional exercise involved hundreds of
individuals and thousands of person-hours’ of effort. Approximately 100 people would travel to
the exercise site to serve as evaluators and controllers and to provide administrative support.

45

 41

Prior to CSEPP, the Army already had a chemical response exercise program and a developed
body of doctrine on military exercises in general. FEMA’s principal technological exercise
program, the Radiological Emergency Preparedness Program (REPP), in existence since 1980,
was developed to promote and measure off-site preparedness for communities near nuclear
power plants. Both agencies used independent evaluators to assess exercise performance.

3.1.3 Issue

The program needed an evaluation methodology that would be (a) acceptable to all involved
organizations, despite some differences in exercise philosophy; and (b) defensible as an
evaluation of capabilities and a means to improve them. Exercise evaluations were reflected in
annual progress reports to Congress; were part of the budget planning and justification process;
and, in some cases, helped fulfill regulatory requirements for preparedness to allow a permit to
be issued for operation of the demilitarization facility.

3.1.4 Solution

In order to merge the Army and FEMA philosophies and protocols, a national-level Exercise
Integrated Process Team (IPT) was established to engage Army, FEMA, and state and local
program staff in developing CSEPP exercise policy. The evaluation approach adopted by the IPT
combined elements of both agency perspectives. It adopted a standard set of objectives, later
modified as outcomes and tasks, to be demonstrated by the participant organizations. Evaluation
of these objectives/tasks was supported by evaluation forms, essentially questionnaires
identifying the usual components of the task and also providing references. While practices and
terminology varied over time, in one way or another the evaluators provided a narrative
summary of exercise play and identified particular strengths and weaknesses for later follow-up.
Precedent for much of the foregoing was set by the REP Program.

From the Army side, the IPT adopted a performance orientation. A performance-oriented
evaluation focuses on the demonstrated response to the exercise scenario and assesses it based on
its effectiveness, rather than fidelity to plans and procedures per se. The performance-oriented
approach relies on the expertise of the evaluator to determine whether the functions and purposes
of emergency response are being demonstrated successfully. While this type of evaluation can be
supported by evaluation forms, the forms are not used as a ‘checklist’, where a missed box must
be explained.

To promote a uniform set of practices and schedules across the program, the IPT issued exercise
policy and guidance through a document commonly referred to within the Program as the “Blue
Book”46 because of its blue cover. The Exercise IPT published several versions of the Blue Book
between 1992 and 2019. It contained guidance on exercise scheduling, exercise planning
including pre- and post-exercise tasks, expected participation levels, and detailed evaluation
criteria. A number of significant policy and practice innovations were developed and

46
 U.S. Department of the Army, Office of the Assistant Secretary of the Army and Department of Homeland
 Security, and Federal Emergency Management Agency, 2009, Exercise Policy and Guidance for the Chemical
 Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program, June 19.

 42

implemented via the Blue Book, including use of an extent-of-play agreement (XPA), simulation
cell (SimCell), Master Scenario Events List (MSEL, pronounced ‘measle’), and Integrated
Performance Evaluation (IPE). These techniques assured implementation of a state-of-the-art
exercise program that thoroughly tested capabilities and documented the results. That, in turn,
provided a solid foundation for reports, permits, etc.
 • Extent-of-Play Agreements. As the scale of an exercise and the number of organizations
 and players increased, the process of setting goals and determining which parts of the
 response will be demonstrated versus simulated became increasingly complicated. In
 addition to the greater number of participants, the links between them often affected other
 organizations’ ability to demonstrate or simulate their response actions. To address these
 issues, the CSEPP exercise program emphasized that representatives from each
 participating jurisdiction should clearly identify what they wanted to learn from it. This
 information was recorded in an XPA that served as a kind of contract between the
 jurisdiction and the exercise co-directors. The XPA also stated what capabilities each
 jurisdiction intended to demonstrate during the exercise and how they intended to do it.
 The use of XPAs facilitated accountability and efficient exercise design.
 • Simulation Cell. A SimCell is a group of exercise controllers in a central location,
 interacting with exercise players via telephone and serving as a hub for exercise
 monitoring and control. In CSEPP exercises, SimCell controllers performed two key
 functions: calling in exercise injects, and portraying non-playing organizations, both of
 which involved role-playing. To call in an inject, the controller assumed the identity of a
 simulated person such as, for example, a concerned resident (“is my child’s school in the
 affected area?”) or a local Congressional staffer asking for a status report. Controllers
 portraying non-playing organizations had a more passive role of answering calls that in a
 real event would be directed to organizations that were not playing in the exercise. For
 example, if an exercise player’s standard operating procedure (SOP) said to notify the
 senators’ offices but they were not playing, the player would call the SimCell to make the
 notifications instead.
 SimCells also provided a centralized location for exercise control. SimCell controllers
 tracked the status of exercise actions and identified unusual trends or circumstances.
 They provided situational awareness for the exercise directors. That awareness, in turn,
 allowed for control of CSEPP exercises to be adjusted “on the fly” as exercise play
 unfolded.
 • Master Scenario Event List. A MSEL is a chronological timeline of expected actions
 and scripted injects to stimulate exercise play developed as part of the exercise planning
 process and agreed to among the participating organizations at a MSEL conference. The
 CSEPP exercise MSEL was, in a sense, a master script for the exercise that mapped out
 what scenario events and information would be provided to the players, where, and when.
 The MSEL also outlined what the expected player responses would be. A typical CSEPP
 exercise MSEL contained a couple of hundred entries. Each MSEL record contains a
 designated scenario time; an event synopsis; the name of the controller responsible for
 delivering the inject; and, if applicable, special delivery instructions, the task and
 objective to be demonstrated, the expected action, the intended player, and a note-taking
 section.

 43

 • Integrated Performance Evaluation. From 2000 on, exercise evaluation came to be
 organized around seven “response streams” which evolved into eight “emergency
 response outcomes” (EROs), each representing a broad functional area of response (e.g.,
 hazard assessment, protective action, public information, medical response). Appendix C
 to the Blue Book contained guides for exercise evaluators that were organized around the
 ERO framework. Each ERO was broken down into a set of component tasks. Exercise
 evaluation guides (EEGs) were included for each task. The EEGs, in turn, broke down
 the tasks into component steps and provided references and other information about the
 task.

3.1.5 Potential Application

One of the greatest challenges in CSEPP was to develop an exercise program based on a standard
set of objectives and requirements derived from different programs, cultures, and perspectives. In
the early 2000s, the federal government began an initiative to integrate federal, state, and local
emergency response resources and develop a unifying set of doctrines, plans, and tools with
which to coordinate response to disasters.47 Policy direction was set by Homeland Security
Presidential Directives (HSPDs) 548 and 8,49 and implementation measures included
development of several DHS documents: the NIMS,50 NRF,51 and HSEEP.52 The latter was in
many ways modeled on the CSEPP exercise program. HSEEP tools and concepts including the
MSEL, SimCell, exercise outcomes, and EEGs were all adapted from CSEPP precedents. The
basic concept of integrated performance evaluation — that an exercise should be measured by
the demonstrated public safety outcome, given the exercise scenario — can be adapted as
emergency management doctrine evolves. The exercise policies and tools developed for CSEPP
can be applied to any large-scale exercise program.53

3.2 Exercise Extent-of-Play Agreements54
An extent-of-play agreement (XPA) ensures that expectations are clear regarding the levels of
play and demonstrations to be carried out during the exercise. This lesson explains how they
were implemented in CSEPP and provides an example.

3.2.1 Summary

The scale of CSEPP exercises and the number of organizations and personnel involved
challenged exercise planners. Additional complications would be introduced when participating
organizations wavered on their degree of participation. Uncertainty about one organization’s
participation would create difficulties for other participating organizations, as well as for those
planning the exercise control and evaluation. The program learned that early development of an
XPA among the participating jurisdictions was essential to the success of an exercise. An XPA is
a formal commitment that shapes the scope of the exercise (scenario and related demonstrations,
simulation requirements, and resource requirements) and subsequent planning for its conduct and
evaluation. An XPA sets a firm footing for exercise planning by establishing what activities the
participating organizations will demonstrate. An XPA requires effort to negotiate but makes for a
more organized exercise and likely saves effort in the long run. An XPA can be a useful part of
planning for any multi-organization exercise; the larger the exercise, the more valuable the XPA
will be.

3.2.2 Background

Exercises that involve multiple jurisdictions, dozens of organizations, and hundreds of
participants were challenging to plan and conduct because there were so many differing priorities
and agendas, levels of capability, and resource constraints. The CSEP Program faced challenges,
trying to organize an effort where every organization in every jurisdiction participated at a
comparable level of effort consistent with their mission, with minimal gaps in the continuity of
exercise activities.

In the early years of CSEPP, some exercises fell short of expectations because exercise planning
and scenario design were completed before planners were assured of which organizations and
what capabilities would be represented in the exercise. It was necessary to continually revise
exercise plans and scenario injects, sometimes right up until the day before the exercise, as
participants vacillated on the scope of their involvement. In some instances, despite repeated
planning meetings to attempt to keep up with changing player lineups, these differences were
never resolved. Exercises were plagued by disconnects in player actions and disruptions in the
flow of interaction among participants, thus relying heavily on simulations in lieu of
demonstrations of capability.

As the CSEPP exercise program matured at each site, exercise planning and operations became a
little easier because personal relationships bridged some of the formidable differences among

54
 Lesson written in 2012. An article based on this lesson was published in Domestic Preparedness Journal,
 February 2014.

 46

participants. But only when formal definitive XPAs from all jurisdictions were negotiated prior
to the commencement of detailed planning did exercises begin to reach their full potential.

Even when the CSEPP exercise program initiated the concept of formal XPAs for an exercise,55
some of the negotiations were carried out at the action officer level (e.g., exercise coordinator) in
a jurisdiction without the commitment of the lawful authority in each of the organizations in the
jurisdiction. After exercise managers initiated planning based on this soft commitment, they were
blindsided when the organization leader did not support the original draft XPA.

3.2.3 Issue

When there are multiple organizations involved, exercise planners need to know in advance the
extent of play for each organization: what functions the organization will demonstrate and what
resources (personnel, facilities, response assets) it will commit to the exercise. The reasons are
(a) interdependencies among organizations, and (b) planning for exercise control and evaluation.

Interdependencies

The need for an early commitment of participation in an exercise is most compelling where
multiple organizations operate joint facilities or processes. For example:
 • A Joint Information Center (JIC) or Joint Information System (JIS) requires participation
 by all key organizations to test their ability to coordinate public information with one
 another.
 • Set-up and operation of a reception center and shelter complex at a school might require
 the integrated actions of several law enforcement agencies, fire departments, the
 American Red Cross, county Emergency Operations Center (EOC) staff, and medical
 volunteers, in addition to some school staff and the use of the facilities. Reception and
 shelter staff would also coordinate with public information staff. If one organization
 involved in demonstrating the reception/shelter function is absent or is not playing at the
 same level of effort as the others, all other participants in this venue will be impacted to
 some degree by the inability to interact with the absent organization.

Likewise, if an EOC in a core jurisdiction does not fully staff to demonstrate all functions, any
other playing organization that would feed information into or take direction from that EOC will
be affected in some way.

55
 Note that after CSEPP initiated use of XPAs, the XPA concept was adopted by the Homeland Security Exercise
 Evaluation Program (HSEEP). See HSEEP Volume II, p. 18.

 47

Exercise Control and Evaluation

It is also challenging to field a team of controllers and evaluators appropriate for the scope of an
exercise when there are uncertainties about the level of play. A mismatch can mean some
controllers and evaluators are not properly prepared for their assignments, some activities are not
adequately covered, and/or some controllers and evaluators are not used to best advantage.

3.2.4 Solution

The above issue was addressed in CSEPP by negotiating a formal detailed XPA to define the
commitment of each jurisdiction well in advance of the development of detailed exercise
planning and scenario design. Key elements of the XPA and associated negotiations include
these elements.

Detail

An XPA must include sufficient detail about the expected play by every organization so that
exercise managers can design scenario inputs to meet expectations and then recruit and prepare
controllers and evaluators. This effort means some negotiations within and among jurisdictions
will be needed to ensure a balance in time, space, activities, and numbers of participants so that
all get an appropriate opportunity to meet their individual objectives as well as the overall
objectives of the exercise. The attached sample XPA illustrates the type of detail needed.

Executive Approval

An XPA must have the unqualified support of the appropriate authorities in each jurisdiction
before it can be used for exercise planning. It should be a signed document such that all
participants can rely on its being executed.

Advance Planning

In order to make this work well, XPAs should be drafted at least nine months before the planned
exercise and signed soon afterward. Allowance should be made for true emergencies that may
arise and preclude a jurisdiction from meeting its commitment, as happened in CSEPP in a few
instances (e.g., an exercise in Utah was cancelled by the events of 9/11/2001 just prior to the
scheduled exercise; exercises were modified to accommodate the Covid-19 pandemic). However,
such allowances should very much be the exception. CSEPP guidance called for exercise dates to
be confirmed at least one year in advance, and for negotiations on the XPAs of participating
jurisdictions to begin 310 days before the exercise date, be completed 270 days before the
exercise date, and signed not later than 150 days before the exercise date. This schedule
encouraged commitment to certain demonstration levels before exercise planners invested too
much time in inject development and other planning details. The long period allowed for XPA
negotiation reflected program experience; in practice, the negotiations could be protracted,
although the time and effort expended prove to be worthwhile.

 48

It should be noted that negotiation of the XPA does not merely serve those who are “running”
the exercise; rather, every participant organization stands to gain. The added certainty means that
each organization can be confident of a robust opportunity to test their capabilities.

In addition, an XPA can be used to specify various exercise parameters besides expected player
actions. It can also be used to set ground rules for other aspects of the exercise such as evaluation
standards, protocols for controllers and evaluators, precedence for real-world emergencies, and
agreed-upon simulations.

A sample CSEPP XPA is included below to illustrate XPA contents and format.

 SAMPLE EXTENT-OF-PLAY AGREEMENT
The following illustrates the content and level of detail recommended in an XPA. An actual
CSEPP XPA would include more entries than this sample. The sample consists of a main
agreement with general exercise parameters, followed by an attachment with function-by-
function specifics on expected play. Details in the enclosure are organized according to
Emergency Response Outcomes (EROs) and tasks used in CSEPP. Tasks are subparts of EROs.
CSEPP EROs and tasks correspond roughly to the capabilities and activities identified in
HSEEP. The sample uses generic terms for the key organizations: [Depot], [County], [State], and
so on. Note: the sample agreement uses the term “Exercise Co-Director.” In CSEPP, each
exercise was overseen by co-directors from the Army and FEMA. While each jurisdiction
controlled its own participation, the co-directors coordinated overall exercise planning and
direction.

 [County]
 Extent-of-Play Agreement (XPA)
 Community Exercise on [date]

PURPOSE: This Extent-of-Play Agreement (XPA) identifies the conditions that will be used to
develop, conduct, control, and evaluate the Community Exercise, as agreed to by the Exercise
Co-Director and [County Executive].

STANDARDS & REFERENCES: The play by [County] will be based on the editions of the
following as current 60 days before the exercise:
 • The County Emergency Operations Plan implementing procedures and checklists,
 including the Annex L dated [date].
 • JIC SOP dated [date].
 • MOA between [County] and [other County] concerning the Emergency Alert System
 (EAS) message and Tone Alert Radio (TAR) announcement after notification of an
 accident/incident at [Depot], dated [date].

 49

 • MOA between [Depot] and [County] dated [date] regarding mutual support for an
 accident/incident at the facility.

EXERCISE PARAMETERS: The scenario will be based on events occurring where toxic
chemicals are stored at the [Depot]. Real-world weather will be used. These events cause
hazardous liquid agent contamination in the vicinity of the storage site and vapor hazards
downwind. The hazards may extend beyond the facility boundary and may require protective
actions and other emergency responses to be taken in the protective action zones round the
facility. The type of chemical hazard released, the area of ground contamination, the vapor plume
path, and the number and condition of casualties will be within a range to achieve the objectives
for each jurisdiction, and will be consistent with responders demonstrating their capabilities at
the locations listed in this agreement.

Exercise play will begin no earlier than 0800 on [date] and will continue uninterrupted for a
minimum of 6.5 hours. Some responders may play beyond 6.5 hours. The enclosure to this
agreement describes the extent to which organizations in this jurisdiction will participate in this
exercise, to include the times and places when responders will play and the specific capabilities
to be demonstrated. Simulations needed to account for any designed shortfalls in demonstrations
are also included.

For this exercise, a JIC will be activated and operated jointly by all participating jurisdictions
according to existing plans. [County] will demonstrate its relationship with [Depot] as described
in the MOA for an accident at the facility.

EXERCISE PARTICIPANTS: All County offices that have direction and control
responsibilities in the event of a chemical accident at [Depot] will play in the County EOC and
the JIC consistent with the exercise objectives and scenario. Field responses will be
demonstrated at traffic and access control points, reception centers, shelters, schools, and
decontamination centers as described in this agreement. Medical responses will be demonstrated
at the [Hospital] from StartEx until StartEx + 5 hours.

SIGNATURES: The following agree to support this exercise as described herein.

3.2.5 Potential Application

Using formal XPAs to drive planning for any multi-jurisdiction exercise will pay dividends in
terms of exercise commitment and certainty. The larger the scope of the exercise, the more
valuable this tool will be.

3.3 Scenario Design56
A well-crafted scenario lays the foundation for a safe and productive exercise.

3.3.1 Summary

Exercises of community capabilities to respond to large-scale hazardous materials disasters can
be helpful to ensure that community preparedness meets expectations.

The accident site scenario is a crucial component of such an exercise, because it determines the
extent to which the community can demonstrate its capabilities and thus is fundamental to the
credibility of the assessment. In this regard, a successful scenario design requires considerable
forethought and represents the culmination of substantial planning and extent-of-play
negotiations.

Over the years, the CSEPP exercise directors experimented with different techniques to
incorporate realism in scenario design. The most successful approaches are described here and
could be useful for any organization conducting large-scale emergency response exercises where
realism and real-world safety and security are major considerations.

3.3.2 Background

CSEPP exercises simulated accidents involving large quantities of highly toxic chemicals, with
hundreds of trained responders with a variety of skill sets performing complex and
technologically sophisticated procedures at multiple locations simultaneously. These responders,
observed by a cadre of dozens of subject matter expert controllers and evaluators, demonstrated
their capabilities in and around the actual facility where these chemicals were stored, and at
supporting facilities such as emergency operations centers, medical facilities, information
centers, traffic control points, and reception centers. Activities by responders at these locations
were driven by scenario injects that were as realistic as possible. Scenario setup and injects
included use of physical props; visual and sound effects; actors representing affected persons and
media reporters; and print, voice, and video injects from the SimCell and mock media. All
scenario injects were introduced on a common timeline that kept the play integrated across
jurisdictions. These scenario design features, along with the fact that responders dd not have
prior knowledge of scenario injects, provided realism and excitement to the experience, and
added to the credibility of the after-action report.
Scenario design should be integrated with other aspects of exercise planning such as determining
what organizations will participate in the exercise, setting exercise objectives, and developing an
extent-of-play agreement. In particular, the scenario must be sufficiently robust to drive
demonstration of each organization’s chosen objectives consistent with the extent-of-play
agreement. The scenario design must also take into consideration the following two factors:

56

 54

 • Probability. The scenario for any large-scale exercise, whether a natural hazard,
 technological hazard, or terrorist act, will be an event that is in the tail of the distribution
 curve; something that is possible but is expected to be rare. Organizations and people
 who deal with smaller-scale emergencies on a routine basis may be skeptical about
 focusing on such an unusual event. On the other hand, only a scenario severe enough to
 cause large-scale consequences will drive demonstration of the coordination and
 resource-adequacy considerations involved in disaster response. Finding scenarios that
 are plausible but severe enough to drive the desired level of play is part of the art of
 scenario design.
 • “It can’t happen here.” Technological hazard scenarios such as a large-scale hazardous
 material event (e.g., CSEPP) or radiological event (e.g., nuclear power plant accident)
 may be perceived as implying the failure of safety mechanisms and practices. That, in
 turn, can lead to two sources of concern at the involved facility. First, there may be
 concern about the public relations aspects of portraying a hazard to the community. The
 facility’s owner likely has a community relations program already, the purpose of which
 is to highlight the safety of the facility and its benefit to the community; an exercise
 scenario portraying a disaster may be seen as undermining these efforts. Second, it is
 natural for facility personnel to feel that they can handle any situation that comes up.

3.3.3 Issue

Good exercise scenario design offers major training value, real-time meaningful feedback to
players about their performance, and credibility to the after-action report. Conversely, unrealistic
or vague injects, disconnects in the timeline, and other scenario flaws can confuse and frustrate
players and lead to results that are inconclusive or incorrect in the after-action report. In addition,
elements of the scenario design and execution of simulations can impact the safety of exercise
participants responding on-scene to perform technically and physically challenging work. The
processes and guidelines below are intended to help exercise planners develop a scenario that can
be executed safely and will allow a full demonstration of player capabilities.

3.3.4 Solution

Scenario Design Team and Tasking

An exercise scenario design team (ESDT) is established including trusted agents from each
participating jurisdiction (trusted agents should not play in the exercise), experts on the technical
or industrial operations or processes involved in the scenario, and other subject matter experts as
needed, such as medical, meteorological, or hazard analysis experts.

The ESDT is tasked to create a scenario that is realistic and comprehensive, incorporating
opportunities for players from all organizations to participate interactively. Care must be taken to
avoid driving exercise play that might jeopardize the safety of the participants. At the same time,
the scenario must be sufficiently robust to drive full-scale play and address all desired exercise
objectives.

 55

Scenario Design Team Products

CSEPP ESDTs typically produced the following products:
 • A copy of the latest approved procedures or a narrative description of the technical or
 industrial operation or process that players were engaged in when the scenario initiating
 event occurs.
 • A list of safety gear to be worn by controllers who set up and operate the accident site.
 • An information package to brief players about any extant precursor conditions prior to
 the scenario initiating event, and information about the use of simulations
 (e.g., meteorology) and props (e.g., simulants to represent hazardous materials, smoke
 generators) unique to the scenario.
 • A list of required props and simulations (e.g., video injects, noisemakers, simulated
 hazardous materials, damaged vehicles, etc.). This list would also identify who would
 obtain or prepare the props and arrange for positioning props when needed.
 • Scripted injects to be introduced from the SimCell to support the scenario. These would
 be produced in the appropriate MSEL Implementer format.
 • A scenario description that included all the details about ground truth that controllers
 need to set up and operate the scenario. This would include details about how many
 hazardous materials containers were damaged or destroyed, when, to what extent, where,
 and how; the mechanisms by which the material was released; the size and location of
 debris fields if explosions are involved; what is burning and for how long if fire is
 involved; the size and location of hazardous liquid puddles and areas of aerosol
 deposition; and the number and initial condition of victims by time and location. The
 scenario description would also include:
 o A description of the operation just before the simulated accident (a diagram
 showing important facility features, work crew composition, and location of
 vehicles and equipment).
 o Meteorological data for before, during, and after the scenario initiating event. (In
 CSEPP, the Army installation maintained meteorological instrumentation and
 performed plume modeling for emergency preparedness; during exercises, the
 participants could use “real weather” (actual conditions at the time of the
 exercise) but more often used “canned weather,” where pre-scripted data was
 input to the modeling program to achieve the desired plume characteristics and
 direction.
 o A description of initiating events, supplemental events, and separate
 demonstrations.
 o Probable initial player hazard assessment(s).
 o Ground truth hazard assessment(s).
 • Simulated documentation, mock news reports, mock internal memos, mock photographs
 (e.g., a photo showing structures damaged by an earthquake), hazardous materials storage
 diagrams, and information about any pre-positioned responders agreed to in the extent-of-

 56

 play agreement. These would be provided to players in a timely fashion to help them
 understand the conditions and circumstances leading up to the scenario accident.
 • A brief summary of the scenario for publication in exercise planning documents and the
 after-action report.
 • A summary of scripted trauma injuries for the medical moulage controllers so they could
 arrange necessary moulage.
 • Instructions to controllers for use of video injects.
 • Inclement weather plans should severe weather impact outdoor exercise venues.

Scenario Design Guidelines

(1) Consider safety in the design and staging of on-scene operations. In CSEPP exercises, the
exercise scenario typically involved an event that occurred when chemical munitions were being
repackaged or transported. Other scenarios involved external events that occur in static storage,
like a partial structural collapse. Regardless, actual munitions were not used in exercises.
Nonetheless, the response scene could be a challenging environment from a safety standpoint
with responders wearing cumbersome personal protective equipment (PPE) and dealing with
moving vehicles, heavy equipment, slippery surfaces, electrical hazards, and the like. In addition,
exercise simulations might involve use of smoke generators, heat sources, noisemakers, and
other distractions. Any exercise featuring on-scene response to a hazmat or adversarial situation
may include similar features and thus will require careful safety planning. A safety environment
can be served by:
 • Using normal procedures when the scenario is built on an operation in progress. Players
 should not be called upon to use an unfamiliar or unapproved operational arrangement. In
 CSEPP, this meant using only chemical operations covered by a current SOP as the
 premise for scenario events.
 • Considering how to set up and operate the scenario props and simulations in the safest
 possible way. For example, avoid an arrangement that requires players wearing PPE to
 work in locations where rough terrain or tripping hazards increase the risk of actual
 injury, and avoid staging simulated unconscious or incapacitated players on roadways or
 on moving vehicles. Also consider what safety gear may be needed by controllers or
 evaluators who will be setting up the props.
 • Documenting planned procedures and submitting them through normal channels for
 workplace safety review. For example, obtain local safety permits for the use of heat- or
 flame-producing props.

(2) Minimize use of simulations. There is a correlation between scenario realism and player
performance. Since the quality of player performance will be reflected in the evaluation, it is
imperative that exercise play be realistic so that the evaluation will be valid. The most credible
exercises, the best training, and the fairest evaluations are obtained when players respond just
like they would to an actual emergency. When simulations are introduced, they should be so
realistic that players do instinctively what they would do if the situation were real. Players should
not be distracted from demonstrating appropriate and timely responses by unrealistic conditions

 57

or events. This level of planning puts a burden on scenario design and control, but the effort will
generate more reliable data for the report and give the players a more valuable training
experience. Confusion about conditions, materials, events, and actions in the field will compound
as others respond to flawed reports from the field. This confusion could compromise exercise
objectives.

(3) Avoid use of an artificially accelerated timeline. Interruptions or accelerations in time (“time
jumps”) are extremely difficult to control, can cause confusion among players, and can
compromise the evaluation. Artificial timelines put an undue burden on controllers to ensure not
only that every player knows what happened in the interim, but also what the players would have
done during this interval. (An artificial timeline may be unavoidable, however, when exercising
functions that take place much later in a response, such as recovery and reentry measures.)

(4) Consider exempting key personnel. Negotiate with participating organizations to simulate that
one or two key officials, supervisors, or emergency responders are not available during the
exercise, as there is no guarantee that all key personnel will be in a duty status when an accident
actually occurs. This exemption also allows deputies and alternates to obtain the experience of
having a larger role in the response. The exempted personnel can also serve as trusted agents for
the exercise planning process.

(5) Substitute video for hard-to-simulate items. Video can be used as a scenario component to
represent events that that cannot otherwise easily be demonstrated to players (e.g., smoke coming
from a building, a helicopter crashing, or a lightning strike).

(6) Use moulage and acting to depict injuries. Consider the need for moulage to represent
injuries, as well as also the time and place where this moulage can be applied. Scenario design
can also include brief instructions for controllers to give to players to “act out” the physical
manifestations of their simulated injury.

(7) Carefully plan timing of special effects such as explosions, smoke, and fire. Timing of
explosions, smoke, and fire must be built into scenario design to address, for example, how long
a fire burns, the amount of time between explosions, and important and related what-ifs based on
possible player actions.

3.3.5 Potential Application

The scenario design techniques used in CSEPP exercises could apply to any exercise of
capabilities to respond to a large-scale disaster.

 58

3.3.6 References

DHS (U.S. Department of Homeland Security), undated, HSEEP Policy and Guidance webpage,
available at https://preptoolkit.fema.gov/web/hseep-resources/policy-and-guidance, accessed
January 2024.

Policy and Guidance, December.

 59

3.4 Control of Complex Exercises57
Effective exercise control leads to a safe and productive exercise.

3.4.1 Summary

CSEPP exercises simulated accidents involving large quantities of highly toxic chemicals, with
dozens of trained responders with a variety of skill sets performing complex and technologically
sophisticated procedures at multiple locations on-post simultaneously. These responders,
observed by a cadre of 30–50 subject-matter expert controllers and evaluators, demonstrated
their capabilities in and around the actual facility where these chemicals were stored. Locating
exercise activities at this venue enabled the use and test of the actual facility response
infrastructure, provided realism and excitement to the experience, and added to the credibility of
the after-action report. An exercise where activity was occurring at multiple locations, combined
with core exercise activities in a location where real-world safety and security consideration
were paramount, posed special control challenges.

These factors together necessitated exceptional control by the exercise staff to sustain play on the
trajectory needed to meet exercise objectives, because player actions at these on-post locations
drove the scope and timing of exercise events in nearby off-post communities. Also, because of
the immediate proximity of the simulated accident site to the chemical stockpile, exceptional
safety and security measures had to be sustained. Over the years the Army exercise directors
experimented with different arrangements to obtain tight control of these exercises. The best
solution found involved a structured control team, especially including one exercise controller
dedicated to tracking all critical on-post player activity in real time, including both field play and
the installation Emergency Operations Center (EOC). The Army’s successes with this exercise
control arrangement could be a useful lesson for any organization conducting exercises involving
complex player activities in multiple venues and/or when real-world safety and security are
major considerations.

3.4.2 Background

CSEPP exercises were annual high-visibility exercises conducted by the Department of Army
and FEMA and involving the participation of multiple state and local response agencies and
assets. The Army component of these joint exercises included responses in the actual facility at
the installation where the chemicals were stored, using all of the various safety, security, and
emergency response personnel and infrastructure in the facility and at the installation. In order to
control exercise activity this complex and within the finite time available for an exercise, while
simultaneously maintaining real-world safety and security, the Army maintained close oversight
of all critical exercise control and response operations in real time.

The control arrangement employed for CSEPP exercises involved lead controllers in the field, in
the various EOCs, and at medical venues, supported by sufficient subject matter expert
controllers and evaluators to cover all player activities, including a handful of controllers in the
57

 60

Simulation Cell (SimCell). A typical Army exercise staff included about a dozen controllers
overall.

This basic arrangement was enhanced by the assignment of one controller who was dedicated to
proactively ensuring the integrity of the overall scenario and the accomplishment of the critical
exercise objectives without compromising safety or security. This controller was called the
EOC/Field Liaison (EFL). He or she knew the scenario details, monitored player and controller
communications channels, and had direct communications with all lead controllers and the
exercise director. The EFL was tasked with promptly recognizing any unexpected or
inappropriate event or condition and initiating a remedy to limit the impact of the problem and
sustain the balance of exercise play with minimum interruption.

3.4.3 Issue

Experience had shown that any exercise with the complexity of a CSEPP exercise, especially
when the hazard facility itself was a venue, could have unplanned events occur that would
distract or confuse players. Such distractions could interfere with the accomplishment of exercise
objectives, and more importantly could potentially reduce safety and security at the site. A well-
organized control team and effort could minimize any detrimental effects.

3.4.4 Solution

The basic control structure used for CSEPP exercises was adequate as long as the players’
responses were as expected and controllers at the various locations could address any issues that
arise. The EFL position was instituted by Army CSEPP exercise directors to monitor exercise
progress and address situations with potential safety or security implications or which threatened
to derail exercise demonstrations. The EFL was expected to take the lead to identify and fix these
problems, so that lead controllers could continue to focus on their basic exercise control duties,
although they might need to work with the EFL on the resolution of problems that impacted their
area of responsibility.

Another benefit of establishing an EFL position was that all critical player and controller
communications could be monitored at one location, enabling the EFL to identify a potential
problem before anyone else.

The EFL tracked the pace and content of exercise injects, simulations, and player actions across
all exercise venues on-post to ensure the integrity of the scenario and the accomplishment of
exercise objectives. The EFL would also be especially alert for safety or security issues. To
perform this mission, the EFL had to be knowledgeable about the exercise objectives and the
scenario, familiar with player plans and capabilities, and understand how the exercises were
structured and operated. The EFL had to be able to assess the quality and impact of exercise
injects and simulations as they were used during the exercise, be alert for problems with exercise
control communications systems and directories, and be quick to identify when the scenario
became internally inconsistent or incurred delays impacting achievement of objectives. Most
importantly, the EFL had to be proactive in initiating, coordinating, and confirming corrections
with exercise leaders if the scenario got off-track, if safety or security became compromised, or

 61

if exercise communication systems broke down. During the exercise, the EFL operated in the
background, interacting primarily with (and providing assistance to) the lead controllers. The
EFL also assisted with the post-exercise analyses of player actions, paying particular attention to
instances when lack of scenario realism might have contributed to deficient player responses.

Following are some examples of issues that developed during exercises and what EFLs did to fix
them:
 • Player reports from the field to the EOC were contradictory to such a degree that
 continuing player responses to the conflicting information began to jeopardize exercise
 objectives. The EFL picked up on this situation and, in consultation with the EOC lead
 controller, personally worked with field controllers to identify the cause of the problem.
 (In this case, the cause was a misunderstanding of the simulation about the involvement
 of chemical munitions in a fire. Some contradiction in player reporting is tolerable
 because this really can happen, and players must deal with it, but not in this case.) The
 EFL then followed up until player reporting was improved sufficiently to allow the
 exercise to resume on track.
 • An exercise simulation was misunderstood by players who were not being observed by
 controllers at their location. In this case, the simulation had to do with the status of a
 security barrier to an actual munitions storage structure. As a result, players halted
 response to the exercise scenario in order to address what they thought was an actual
 security emergency. The EFL quickly noted this situation and immediately provided
 information to field controllers to regain control of the exercise.
 • Medical players in the installation medical clinic stopped playing in an exercise and
 began to break down their triage facility after processing a simulated victim because
 medical evaluators departed to another venue for a period of time. Players assumed they
 would have no further play in the exercise, not realizing that they had to remain in play
 until the end of the exercise. The EFL recognized that many injects with medical triage
 implications were still pending, and the absence of medical responders would have
 precluded several important planned scenario vignettes involving victim care. The EFL
 was able to direct the return of medical players to the exercise in time to preclude these
 lapses.
 • An ambulance service that was to transport injured workers to an off-post medical
 treatment facility canceled their participation in the exercise at the last minute, and
 exercise players assumed that meant that demonstration of victim transport was no longer
 required. The off-post medical treatment facility also assumed they were no longer
 required to demonstrate victim care. The EFL, in consultation with the lead Army
 medical controller and a senior off-post medical controller, arranged for alternative
 patient transport to sustain the demonstration and evaluation of victim care.

 62

 • A report from the field to the installation EOC concerning an injured worker was not
 identified either as an exercise message or as a real-world emergency. The EFL
 immediately checked with field controllers to ascertain if the injury was real (it was) and
 followed up to ensure immediate medical care to the injured worker without impacting
 the flow of any other exercise activities.
 • A simulation was granted by the EOC Leader allowing a group of workers to access a
 location within the exercise area but downwind of the hazard area to perform an urgent
 real-world maintenance task that was not related to the exercise. However, this decision
 had the potential to distract and confuse emergency responders in the field. The EFL
 contacted appropriate field controllers to arrange for the necessary access without
 interfering with exercise play.
 • The approach of real-world inclement weather (lightning) jeopardized the continued safe
 operation of accident response activities in the open. The EFL took the lead to implement
 the exercise’s inclement weather plan in time to temporarily halt all exercise play
 simultaneously in all venues, then orchestrated prompt resumption of play when the
 threat passed with minimal discontinuity among player actions.
 • A real-world chemical alarm activated during the initial response to a simulated accident
 in the storage facility. The EFL was able to coordinate an immediate temporary halt in
 scenario injects (to include real-world protective actions by players and controllers) until
 the alarm was cleared. It was a false alarm, and the exercise resumed with little time and
 no objectives lost. Had there been an actual chemical release, all persons in the facility
 would have been protected.
 • A report of an unauthorized entry to a restricted area was made during the initial response
 to a simulated accident in the restricted area. The EFL was able to coordinate an
 immediate temporary halt in scenario injects in all venues and then helped account for all
 controllers in the area until the security condition was resolved. The cause of the problem
 was an administrative error in access control logs, and the exercise resumed with little
 time and no objectives lost. Had there been an actual security breach, exercise controllers
 would have been able to be accounted for quickly and relocated to avoid interfering with
 the security response.

CSEPP’s exercise experience revealed that in order to be effective, the EFL needed to make
certain preparations before the exercise, including the following:
 • Reviewing all exercise planning documents and all core player response plans, to include
 procedures relevant to the operations that players would be engaged in. Also, the EFL
 examined scenario injects so that any deviations from planned exercise control could be
 noted and considered for action. The information in these documents provided an
 understanding of the scenario and exercise objectives, as well as an appreciation of where
 and when critical controller input and player responses should occur.
 • Attending controller meetings and player briefings (to include safety and security
 briefings), with close attention paid to discussions about the scenario, exercise objectives,
 extent-of-play agreements, and exercise safety and security contingency plans. A visit to

 63

 the planned accident site was also helpful in understanding player and controller actions
 during the exercise.
 • Discussing the role of the EFL with the lead controllers to ensure they understood this
 feature of exercise control.
 • Visiting the location assigned to the EFL for the exercise (usually the EOC) to make final
 arrangements for the workspace in order to be effective without being a distraction.
 • Becoming familiar with the exercise and player communications systems the EFL would
 be using during the exercise. The EFL would perform communications checks from the
 assigned EFL workspace to confirm the capability to communicate with lead controllers
 and player points of contact via multiple modes (e.g., land line, radio, or cell phone).

While the exercise was in progress, the EFL did the following:
 • Monitored the pace and content of exercise injects, simulations, and player actions across
 all venues, with particular attention to locations that his or her experience suggested
 would require close attention. The EFL coordinated proactively with the lead controllers
 as necessary to keep the exercise on track, especially with regard to unexpected player
 actions and the need for ad hoc implementers to keep the exercise consistent with
 objectives and extent-of-play agreements. The expectation was that the EFL would do the
 majority of the work needed to fix problems in order to free the lead controllers to sustain
 their overall supervision of exercise operations. The EFL was alert for problems that
 exercise leaders might not have been aware of, and either addressed them directly or
 elevated them to the leaders for action.
 • Monitored exercise control communications and took immediate action to remedy any
 communications problems that might arise. If present, communications support staff were
 enlisted to help solve communication problems.
 • Served as the clearinghouse for information regarding interruptions in exercise play.
 Experience suggested that in a large multi-venue exercise, interruptions might occur at
 particular locations for a variety of reasons. The EFL assessed the extent of and reason
 for the interruption and passed this information along to appropriate exercise leaders.
 • Took notes as time permitted to help with post-exercise analyses.

After the exercise, the EFL did the following:
 • Attended the after-action review meetings and contributed to the analysis of player
 performance, especially in regard to impacts that exercise interruptions or simulations
 might have had on player performance.
 • Informed the Army exercise director about any exercise control problems that emerged
 during the exercise, how they were resolved, and recommended improvements for future
 EFL operations.

 64

3.4.5 Potential Application

This lesson may be applied to any exercise that either has significant real-world safety or
security dimensions, or that is large and interconnected enough that disruptions in one operation
may affect others downstream. Exercise controllers should be aware of the possibility of
disruption. Where staffing permits, use of a controller dedicated to ensuring the integrity of the
exercise scenario and the accomplishment of core exercise objectives can help protect the
investment of time and money in the exercise.

3.4.6 References

DHS (U.S. Department of Homeland Security), 2007a, HSEEP Vol. I, Overview and Exercise
Program Management, February.

DHS, 2007b, HSEEP Vol. II, Exercise Planning and Conduct, February.

Policy & Guidance, December.
