3.5 Using a SimCell for Exercise Realism58
A simulation cell is an effective and flexible tool for exercise control and evaluation. It allows
players to interact, via simulation, with a wide variety of non-playing organizations and officials.

3.5.1 Summary

A Simulation Cell or SimCell is a physical or virtual location where controllers generate and
deliver injects during the exercise. SimCell controllers simulate the outside world for purposes of
interaction with exercise participants. In addition, a SimCell helps provide a centralized
clearinghouse for exercise control, and generates information for exercise evaluation. Use of a
SimCell requires considerable advance preparation and effort to address logistics (staffing and
workplace) and to generate scripts for controller role-playing. Adopted from prior Army practice,
SimCells in the CSEP Program were used to upgrade exercise realism and allow play of
functions that would not be possible otherwise.

3.5.2 Background

Full-scale CSEPP exercises involved many months of planning and thousands of person-hours of
effort. In addition to the extensive efforts of exercise participants, considerable resources were
expended in exercise control and evaluation. One component of the exercise control and
evaluation process was a SimCell. A SimCell as defined in the 2020 DHS HSEEP document is:

 “A location from which controllers deliver scenario messages representing the
 actions, activities, and conversations of an individual, agency, or organization that
 is not participating in the exercise.”59

Predating the CSEP Program, the Army used simulation cells for large command and control
exercises. The practice was adopted by the CSEP Program and later incorporated into FEMA
HSEEP doctrine.

3.5.3 Issue

Responding to a complex emergency involves interaction with a variety of other people and
organizations. For example, coordination or communication may be needed with numerous other
response agencies at the local, state, and federal level; response resource providers; affected
businesses; schools; elected officials; news organizations; and members of the public. Any given
exercise contains a limited number of participants, so interaction with these other entities is
simulated or simply ignored.

58
 Lesson written in 2012. An article based on this lesson was published in the Journal of Emergency Management,
 October 2013 (see Section 3.5.6, References).
59
 DHS, 2020, Homeland Security Exercise and Evaluation Program (HSEEP), January, page Glossary-9, available
 at https://www.fema.gov/sites/default/files/2020-04/Homeland-Security-Exercise-and-Evaluation-Program-
 Doctrine-2020-Revision-2-2-25.pdf, accessed January 2024.

 66

The lack of interaction can reduce the value and realism of the exercise with respect to both
proactive and reactive player actions. Proactive player actions that involve “reaching out” to
other organizations are not played and/or not documented. For example, a state EMA director
might determine during the exercise that it’s time to request a declaration of emergency by the
Governor – but if the Governor’s office is not playing in the exercise, the director is unable to
complete the process of contacting the office, answering their questions, justifying the request,
and so on. Similarly, reactive player actions that involve answering inquiries from outside
entities may not be played or documented. EOC personnel will reside in an unnatural calm
without calls from elected officials, school principals, parents, bloggers, and other concerned
members of the public.

3.5.4 Solution

CSEPP exercises featured use of a SimCell to increase realism of exercise play. A SimCell is a
group of exercise controllers in a central location, interacting with exercise participants via
telephone and serving as a hub for exercise monitoring and control. SimCell controllers perform
two key functions: calling in exercise injects and portraying non-playing organizations. Both
functions involve role-playing by the controller. To call in an inject, the controller assumes the
identity of a simulated person; for example, a concerned parent (“is Washington School in the
affected area?”), or a local Congressional staffer calling for a status report. Portraying non-
playing organizations is a more passive role and involves fielding calls that in a real event would
go to organizations that are not playing in the exercise. For example, if an exercise player’s SOP
says to notify the National Response Center’s chemical release hotline but that organization is
not a part of the exercise, the player would call the SimCell to make the notification instead.

A SimCell also provides a centralized location for exercise control. SimCell controllers track the
status of exercise actions and identify unusual trends or circumstances. They provide situational
awareness for the exercise directors. That awareness, in turn, allows for control of exercises to be
adjusted “on the fly” as exercise play unfolds.

Controllers in the SimCell should take notes on their interaction with players for use in exercise
feedback. Information and impressions from their interactions can be reported during hotwashes
or other player interactions after the exercise. In addition, SimCell controllers should interact
with exercise evaluators. In the course of playing their role(s), a SimCell controller likely will
have many interactions with players. The SimCell controller knows what was reported to their
simulated character; the “Governor” or “NY Times reporter,” or “distraught parent.” The
information collected should be forwarded to exercise evaluators, or alternatively the controller
can serve as an exercise evaluator in addition to controller duties.

3.5.5 Potential Application

A SimCell can be part of the control effort for any functional exercise. It relies on simple
resources — people and telephones — but requires considerable advance planning to implement.
To reap the benefits of using a SimCell, key steps include:

 67

 • Developing injects to be called in. Injects should be designed to test the various functions
 or objectives to be demonstrated for the exercise. For example, if rumor control is an
 objective for the exercise, include injects representing people who have heard rumors.
 The number of injects should be reasonable given the number of controllers available, the
 number of participants, and the duration of the exercise. Timing of injects should be
 addressed, both to space them out over the course of the exercise, and because certain
 injects may depend on the occurrence of events in the exercise: for example, a call
 requesting assistance to evacuate would only be injected after an evacuation order has
 been given to the public. Injects should be drafted realistically with actual names of local
 officials, towns, landmarks, etc. Injects can be part of a Master Scenario Events List
 (MSEL).
 • Building SimCell information into player and controller training. Participants should be
 aware that they may receive calls from SimCell controllers playing a role. They also need
 to know that the SimCell is there to receive calls to non-participating organizations. Each
 player who might be in a position to make such a call must be provided with the relevant
 SimCell phone number(s).
 • Establishing protocols for SimCell calls. Controllers should have specific scripts to
 ensure that SimCell calls are identified as exercise calls and to clarify who/what is being
 simulated. For example, for incoming calls, if there is only one SimCell phone number,
 callers will have to identify whom they are calling. (“This is Springfield County calling
 the National Response Center Chemical Spill Hotline.”) Similarly for outgoing calls from
 the SimCell, controllers should always identify themselves.
 • Even if using the SimCell for injects only, the SimCell should have at least one line open
 for incoming calls for call-backs. Participants may offer to call back with further
 information and should have the opportunity to do so.

The information above is couched in terms of using telephones for communications during a live
functional exercise. The SimCell concept is flexible and can be adapted to other communication
modes (email, texting, social media) and other types of exercises. For example, a distance
exercise with multiple web sessions over several days or weeks can incorporate an email version
of a SimCell, where participants can email questions, notifications, requests, and the like to a
specified email address.

Evaluation Program (HSEEP), January, page Glossary-9, available at
https://www.fema.gov/sites/default/files/2020-04/Homeland-Security-Exercise-and-Evaluation-
Program-Doctrine-2020-Revision-2-2-25.pdf, accessed January 2024.

Lerner, K., 2013, “Using a Simulation Cell for Exercise Realism,” Journal of Emergency
Management, vol. 11, no. 5, Sept., pp. 338–44, doi:10.5055/jem.2013.0149. Available at

3.6 Student Staffing in an Exercise SimCell60
The Blue Grass CSEPP Community worked with Eastern Kentucky University for staffing the
SimCell during the annual CSEPP exercise, which worked to the mutual benefit of CSEPP and
the participating university students.

3.6.1 Summary

CSEPP used a simulation cell (SimCell) to enhance the exercise experience by simulating
interactions with local residents, media, public officials, and non-participating agencies. SimCell
staff interacted with exercise participants primarily through phone calls that were scripted ahead
of time. In a large, complex CSEPP exercise with multiple jurisdictions, there would be hundreds
of such scripted calls; SimCell staffing requirements would become considerable and were
expensive to fulfill using contractors. Using local emergency management students under the
supervision of experienced staff proved to be a cost-effective method to supplement SimCell
staffing. Over the 15 years this practice was employed, CSEPP learned how to use the students
effectively, through training and preparation, support systems, and supervision. This practice had
the additional benefits of providing useful training and experience for the participating students
and helped establish a good relationship between CSEPP, local emergency management services,
and a central Kentucky university that offered programs in support of emergency management.

3.6.2 Background

In an effort to simulate conditions during an emergency, CSEPP used a SimCell to place and
receive phone calls with exercise participants. In the SimCell, exercise controllers portrayed
news reporters, concerned citizens, public officials, and others who might be expected to call in
to emergency managers during a chemical emergency. They also received calls from exercise
participants, who were instructed to call the SimCell instead when they needed to speak with an
organization that was not participating in the exercise. For example, if an exercise participant
needed to report a hazmat spill to the regulatory agency hotline or update the Governor’s office
or request assistance from a nonparticipant agency, they would call the SimCell instead. Making
this resource part of the exercise gave the participants an opportunity to work their way through
their checklists and procedures and allowed them to get a sense of just how busy they would be
during a chemical emergency response. Particularly for staff who interacted with the public, such
as public information officers and human relations (HR) staff, calls from the SimCell provided a
sense that the emergency and response did not take place in a vacuum and that in a real event,
they should expect there to be intense interest from the public and media.

CSEPP exercises at the Blue Grass site in Kentucky were particularly large and complex due to
the number of organizations involved. Major participants included the Army installation,
Madison County where the installation was located, the Commonwealth of Kentucky, and a ring
of nine surrounding counties that were either at risk of being affected or might be called upon to
host evacuees or provide other assistance.

60
 Lesson written in 2018. This lesson was used in subsequent Blue Grass CSEPP exercises for training student
 volunteers.

 69

Each jurisdiction generally received a few dozen calls during the exercise; for example in the
2016 exercise, the range was 15 to 109 scripted calls among the participating jurisdictions.61
Between scripted calls, received calls, follow-ups, and ad-hoc calls undertaken in reaction to
exercise events, a Blue Grass SimCell could easily receive more than 500 calls during the 4- to
5-hour duration of the exercise.

3.6.3 Issue

The SimCell process added realism to the exercise, but at a price: simulating the entirety of the
outside world involved a tremendous amount of labor! Dealing with hundreds of outgoing and
incoming calls created an acute need for staffing on exercise day. The SimCell was staffed with
35 to 40 people. Local emergency management staff were generally busy with the exercise as
participants and so were not available to assist in the SimCell. While agency staff and contractors
could be brought in, that option was expensive, especially when they had to travel to the
Lexington, Kentucky, area from elsewhere.

3.6.4 Solution

Staff supporting the Blue Grass community CSEPP program made arrangements with a local
university that had multiple programs in emergency management-related fields. Students from
these programs staffed SimCell positions during annual CSEPP exercises. This approach filled a
need for the CSEPP program in an inexpensive way, while also providing a learning experience
for the students.

Eastern Kentucky University and the Homeland Security Program

In 2018, Eastern Kentucky University (EKU) in Richmond, Kentucky, had about
16,000 students, with 4,300 residing on campus. The College of Justice and Safety, one of six
colleges at EKU, offered several programs relating to disaster services.

Students from a variety of EKU programs participated in the exercise each year, including
students enrolled in on-campus programs leading to a Bachelor of Science degree in Homeland
Security or a Master of Science degree in Safety, Security, and Emergency Management, plus
students from fire protection administration and occupational safety programs. The Homeland
Security program was established in 2007 within the College of Justice and Safety. It provided
students with a broad understanding of safety and security issues, plus a specific focus on three
pillars: Security Operations, Disaster Management, and Intelligence Studies. It featured courses
on disaster preparedness and response, mitigation and disaster recovery, and disaster planning
and exercises, among many others.62

61
 The consulting firm DETech produced Master Scenario Event Lists (MSELs) summarizing calls to each
 jurisdiction. For the 2016 Blue Grass CSEPP exercise, there were 477 scripted calls in the MSELs.
62
 EKU College of Justice and Safety, Homeland Security Program Undergraduate Advising Guide 2016–2017,
 available at http://homelandsecurity.eku.edu/sites/homelandsecurity.eku.edu/files/files/HLS ADVISING GUIDE
 - 2016 2017 - FINAL.pdf, accessed January 12, 2017. See generally EKU Homeland Security webpage,

Student Role in Blue Grass CSEPP Exercises63

Student participation in the CSEPP Blue Grass Community exercise was initiated in 2005, when
John Simpson of FEMA approached EKU. Dave McCollum of D&E Technical, Inc. (DETech)64
coordinated the Blue Grass SimCells starting in 2010. He worked with Chad Foster, assistant
professor of homeland security at EKU.

Each year, about 20 to 25 students participated. Although the project was not tied to a specific
class, it was part of the University’s emphasis on “experiential learning,” which also included
other activities outside the traditional classroom environment. The students received a certificate
from Madison County for their assistance and could note this practical experience on their
resumes. Service to the region surrounding EKU was also a university goal; it aimed to “address
needs, challenges, and perspectives of our Region with innovative courses, research, service, and
program offerings utilizing effective delivery methods.”65

To prepare for the exercise, students received read-ahead materials, and the day before the
exercise they went through a four-hour training session that detailed the next day’s activities.
The training covered:
 • Background about CSEPP, with an emphasis on exercises. They learned how CSEPP
 exercises were managed and controlled, and they were introduced to the scale and
 complexity of a Blue Grass CSEPP Community exercise. These exercises involved
 several dozen evaluated locations, hundreds of evaluators/controllers, and thousands of
 participants. Extent-of-play documentation ran to hundreds of pages.66
 • The role of the SimCell in simulating non-playing organizations and people, and their
 specific roles and responsibilities as SimCell controllers.
 • The exercise scenario.
 • Specifics of SimCell protocols including logistics, schedule, phone etiquette, forms to
 use, and so on.

At the training session, the students received the specific forms corresponding to the calls they
would be making, so they could become familiar with the roles they would play during the
exercise. Each student generally was asked to make about 20 to 25 calls. They were encouraged
to throw themselves into their roles — so, for example, a parent inquiring about a school child
being sheltered in place would be very concerned and extremely persistent about getting answers
to their questions.

 It should be noted that in addition to the student role described in this paper, EKU participated in CSEPP as a
 potentially affected organization, including via plans, agreements, training, and participation in exercises. Also,
 students from the EKU School of Nursing participated in medical portions of the CSEPP exercises.

66
 See Ken Lerner and George Yantosik, 2014, “Exercise Extent-of-Play Agreements,” Domestic Preparedness
 Journal, Vol. 10 Issue 2, February. Available at https://www.domesticpreparedness.com/preparedness/exercise-
 extent-of-play-agreements/, accessed January 5, 2018.

 71

On exercise day, the students were transported by bus from campus to the location of the
SimCell in Lexington, about 30 miles away. Their professor accompanied them and also
participated in SimCell activities.

The students (and other SimCell controllers) received a one-page form for each call they were to
make. The form indicated who they were portraying in the call, who to call, and the approximate
time for the call. It provided a small amount of background (e.g., you are a construction worker
in such-and-such town, or you are from out of town, staying at a local motel), and there could be
other conditional instructions (e.g., do not call until after sirens have sounded). There was a short
script to read. The form also contained space for the student to record the details of the call as
made — when they called, who they spoke with, and the answer received.

A separate form was used for recording incoming calls, where a SimCell controller was playing
the role of an outside organization being contacted by an exercise participant. On these forms,
the student recorded who called, who they asked for, and the gist of the discussion.

Yet another form was used for ad-hoc calls. These could occur when exercise play took an
unexpected turn, and controllers reacted creatively to “go with the flow” of the response.

All completed forms were kept as a resource for evaluating the exercise — to help, for example,
with evaluating whether exercise participants answered calls promptly, gave correct answers,
faithfully returned calls as promised, and so on.

In the SimCell, the students were divided into groups and sat at tables according to the
jurisdiction they would interact with, so there would be an Army table, a Kentucky table, a
Madison County table, etc. Each table had an experienced emergency manager as “table lead,” to
answer questions and iron out any difficulties that came up. The table leads also collected verbal
feedback from the students and reviewed the completed inject forms to assist with the exercise
evaluation process.

Educational Benefit

The program proved popular and beneficial to the students. Apart from the certificate and the
opportunity to get out of the classroom for a while, the students were able to witness a well-run,
complex exercise from the inside and get a sense for the degree of preparation, organization,
coordination, and management involved in executing a successful exercise. They also benefited
from interaction with a variety of emergency management professionals at the local, state, and
federal levels. Their participation in the CSEPP exercise dovetailed with their classroom
instruction on preparedness and response activities, including the exercises (e.g., tabletop
exercises, drills) they did in class.67

67
 Telephone interview with Chad S. Foster, Ph.D., Assistant Professor, College of Justice & Safety, Eastern
 Kentucky University, December 16, 2016.

 72

3.6.5 Potential Application

A SimCell adds realism to any functional exercise.68 Without a SimCell, the participants are
arguably operating in a kind of bubble, a quiet zone where nothing and no one outside the
Emergency Operations Center is heard from. Operating a SimCell is labor intensive. However,
CSEPP showed that students can successfully fill many of the roles in a SimCell, with some
training and supervision.

At Blue Grass, the CSEPP program was fortunate that there was a local university with a
homeland security program that encompassed disaster management. However, there are — or
were — many such programs offered across the country. According to an August 2016 listing by
the FEMA Emergency Management Institute’s Higher Education Project, a total of 276 colleges
and universities had programs in homeland security, emergency management, or related fields,
with at least one in each state and the District of Columbia.69 So for any given location, there
would seem to be a reasonable chance that a school with a relevant program is nearby. It might
also be possible to access a more distant school by using a “virtual SimCell” or supplementing a
SimCell with virtual resources, although coordination and training might require some additional
effort. Because most SimCell interactions with exercise participants are by telephone, email,
social media, or other electronic form of communication, a SimCell can be set up at a location
remote from the exercise.

3.7 Virtual SimCell70
CSEPP developed software to implement a virtual SimCell for exercise support. Using the
software facilitated effective, cost-efficient SimCell exercise control.

3.7.1 Summary

A Simulation Cell (SimCell) is used to enhance exercise realism by having exercise controllers
simulate a variety of inquiries from elected officials, electronic and print media, businesses, and
the local public. SimCell controllers also receive player-initiated calls to organizations that are
not playing in the exercise (e.g., a notification call to higher headquarters). Controllers in the
SimCell interact with exercise participants via phone calls, email, and social media. A SimCell
provides practice for emergency responders who, in a real emergency, would be called upon to
communicate with the public, news media, higher headquarters, or other outside organizations.

While useful, a SimCell can be costly to implement if staff or contractors must travel to the site
of the exercise. Alternatively, local staff can be used, but that may take them away from
participating in the exercise, or from other duties.

To implement a SimCell without these drawbacks, a virtual SimCell can be used to provide
SimCell functions from one or more remote locations. This can provide the benefit of a SimCell
without diverting local staff from the exercise or requiring large numbers of staff or contractors
to travel. However, to be effective, the exercise controllers implementing the virtual SimCell
must be able to cross-communicate and share information before, during, and after the exercise.

CSEPP developed Virtual SimCell software to facilitate implementation of a virtual SimCell for
exercises. Based on the Virtual Community Platform (VCP), Virtual SimCell was intended to
support emergency preparedness exercises by allowing SimCells to be operated through an
online network. This approach was developed and tested at U.S. Army chemical storage
installations in Kentucky and Colorado to support Chemical Accident/Incident Response and
Assistance (CAIRA) exercises71 and then employed for CSEPP exercises, either as the main
SimCell tool or for the Army/federal component of the SimCell. It received positive reviews
from the Army installations and offers a model for effective and efficient exercise control.

70
 Lesson written in 2018.
71
 These were quarterly, medium-scale exercises primarily limited to Army participation (as opposed to the annual,
 full-scale, multi-jurisdiction CSEPP exercises); they were later renamed as Chemical Incident/Mishap Response
 and Assistance (CIMRA) exercises.

 75

3.7.2 Background

DHS defines simulation cells, or “SimCells,” as follows: “A location from which controllers
deliver scenario messages representing actions, activities, and conversations of an individual,
agency, or organization that is not participating in the exercise.”72

Participation is key to obtaining value from an exercise, and the more organizations and
personnel that can be involved, the more realistic the exercise will be. However, scheduling
conflicts, real-world events, or other competing requirements can limit the availability of key
organizations or players to participate in an exercise. When that happens, exercise designers can
simulate the decisions and actions of those participants through an exercise SimCell.

CSEPP emphasized the role of a SimCell in stimulating and controlling exercise play. As defined
in CSEPP exercise guidance, a SimCell was:

 “An exercise ‘control hub’ — a central location where exercise controllers work.
 Controllers track exercise events, inject information or simulated events into the
 exercise, and serve as a substitute for agencies and personnel not playing in the
 exercise. For example, a SimCell controller may pretend to be a member of the
 media calling in a question or may play the role of a Congressional office, taking
 a notification call from an exercise player. SimCell controllers actively manage
 the flow of exercise events in coordination with controllers in the field, Lead
 Controllers, and the Exercise Co-Directors.”73

A SimCell gave exercise participants the opportunity to work their way through their checklists
and procedures, and allowed them to get a sense of just how busy they might be during an actual
emergency response. Particularly for staff who interacted with outside entities such as the public,
higher headquarters, state or federal agencies, public officials, or the media, calls from the
SimCell (referred to as “injects”) provided a sense that the emergency and response did not take
place in a vacuum, and they should expect that in a real emergency, there would be intense
interest from these outside entities.74

However, the advantages of a SimCell came at a price: a full-scale CSEPP exercise involving
hundreds of participants required dozens of SimCell controllers. In a typical CAIRA exercise
involving several dozen response staff, at least a handful of staff had to be dedicated to making
and receiving SimCell messages. Thus, staffing a local SimCell “boiler room” operation meant
taking away several staff who might otherwise participate in the exercise themselves.
Alternatively, staff from another facility or contractor support staff could be brought in from

72
 DHS, 2020, Homeland Security Exercise and Evaluation Program (HSEEP), January, page Glossary-9, available
 at https://www.fema.gov/sites/default/files/2020-04/Homeland-Security-Exercise-and-Evaluation-Program-
 Doctrine-2020-Revision-2-2-25.pdf, accessed January 2024.
73
 U.S. Army and FEMA, 2019, Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program Exercise Policy and
 Guidance, December, p. H-6. For more information on SimCells, see Lerner, Ken, 2013, Using a Simulation
 Cell for Exercise Realism, Journal of Emergency Management, vol. 11, Issue 5 (September/October).
74
 Any response position may interact with the public or other outside entities; however, it is an especially
 important job function for public information officers, security officers, command staff, human relations staff,
 and environmental officers.

 76

elsewhere; however, this was costly as it involved travel. Also, unless they were experienced
SimCell controllers already, SimCell staff had to be trained on how to perform SimCell duties.

3.7.3 Issue

How to gain the advantages of using a SimCell to enhance exercise play, without either
dedicating local staff to this task, or incurring travel costs to bring in staff or contractors from
another location.

3.7.4 Solution

A virtual SimCell overcomes the need for a physical working location at the exercise site. Given
that most SimCell interactions with exercise participants are by telephone, email, or other
electronic form of communication, a SimCell can be set up at a location remote from the
exercise, or controllers can even work remotely at scattered locations, provided they have a way
to share information quickly and conveniently.

Virtual SimCell Concept

Argonne National Laboratory (Argonne) staff developed a software application, Virtual SimCell,
to facilitate operating a remote SimCell by allowing SimCell controllers to network through
software. The Virtual SimCell application was based on the Virtual Community Platform (VCP).
Users established accounts on VCP and used it to access the Virtual SimCell application.

The VCP was a web-based, virtual online collaboration tool built on Microsoft’s SharePoint®
software, Unified Communications Solution architectures, and commercial off-the-shelf
applications and then modified through the inclusion of “wizards” to facilitate collaboration,
coordination, and communication activities. The virtual community that was created was readily
accessible through a range of web-connected devices; the user was not tied to one computer and
required neither local software nor administrator rights in order to launch it.75

Opening Virtual SimCell, SimCell controllers would see a status board showing a spreadsheet-
like display of the injects to be used for the exercise. Much of the information could be pre-
loaded, such as inject text, schedules, and assignments. Other items were left blank, and the
controller was expected to fill in the information, such as when a particular call was actually
made and what the response was. For each inject, the status board showed:76
 • An inject ID number for reference.
 • Inject method (phone, email, etc.).
 • Name of the responsible controller (who is to inject this message).

75
 Dactelides, John, 2015, The Virtual Community Platform, Argonne National Laboratory Global Security
 Sciences Division Fact Sheet.
76
 For brevity and clarity of explanation, some fields have been omitted in the list below.

 77

 • Controller “persona,” i.e., for inject calls, who the caller is and that individual’s role. For
 example, the caller for a particular inject might be a local parent wanting to know if the
 school has been evacuated and where the children were taken.
 • The recipient player (i.e., who to call).
 • An overview of the message and the expected player response.
 • The full message script.
 • Scheduled inject time.
 • Actual inject time (to be filled in by controller).
 • Action summary (to be filled in by controller —brief description of how the call went).
 • Additional comments (to be filled in by controller, as needed).

All the controllers shared this status board, so SimCell controllers could see both their calls and
other calls being made by other controllers. During the exercise, controllers used the shared
status board to log outgoing inject calls, log incoming notification calls from players, and for
situational awareness of the progress of the exercise. Controllers and exercise managers could
track exercise progress in real time; they could quickly see what information was being received
from exercise participants, and determine any adjustments needed to put the exercise back on
track or fill in gaps in exercise play.

In addition to logging their scheduled calls, controllers had the ability to insert new, ad-hoc
injects. Ad-hoc injects were used for flexible control, especially when unexpected responses
occur. So, for example, if responders decided on a road closure that was not anticipated, ad-hoc
injects might be used to represent calls from people who live or work along the closed road.

Controllers also used the Virtual SimCell to log exercise events. Events were inserted to mark
exercise milestones that might affect the use or timing of injects. For example, when sirens were
sounded for public warning, injects were used to simulate the public calling in to ask what the
sirens meant — so controllers needed to know when the sirens were “sounded.” The siren-
activation event would be logged in to the Virtual SimCell status board and thus visible to all
controllers.

In order to log such key events in the status board, it was important to have one controller
actually present in the exercise emergency operations center (EOC) and logged in to Virtual
SimCell. This controller could input vital events such as exercise start time, public warning,
news releases, ambulance dispatch, etc. This controller also could answer any questions that
other controllers had pertaining to exercise events. After the exercise, this controller could also
provide feedback from the virtual SimCell to the exercise participants during the post-exercise
hot-wash session.

For many injects, the desired timing was a function of when the exercise started. For example, a
given inject might be scheduled for about 90 minutes after the exercise’s start. However, in
CSEPP exercises, the start time was a variable. The start time for an exercise typically depended
on a number of factors beyond the control of exercise controllers, so it had to be treated as

 78

externally determined. To accommodate variations in start times, the software had a function to
adjust inject times based on the exercise start time. A controller would input the exercise start
time when it happened, then the program automatically updated the scheduled inject times to
preserve the desired intervals.

Another important Virtual SimCell function was the chat sidebar. The chat function operated
alongside the status board and allowed people to check in and confirm that they were in
communication with the group, get assistance, compare notes, ask questions, and share
information on an immediate basis. The controller in the EOC could use the chat sidebar to
supplement postings about exercise events and to answer questions from the remotely located
controllers about the progress of exercise play.

The Virtual SimCell also linked to an email account used for exercise purposes. The email
account could be used for a variety of purposes; in CAIRA exercises, it was used to receive news
releases from player organizations as these communications were normally distributed via email.
(In other words, CSEPP jurisdictions had a list of news organizations that would receive emails
with news releases in a real emergency; for exercise purposes, the news releases were instead
sent to a SimCell email address.) All controllers using the status board could also receive and
send emails from this account.

Beta-Testing of Software

As noted above, the Virtual SimCell application was beta-tested at Army CAIRA exercises,
including both the Blue Grass Army Depot (BGAD) in Kentucky and the Pueblo Chemical
Depot (PCD) in Colorado. The following description is based on the November 2016 CAIRA
exercise at BGAD.

CAIRA exercises were medium-scale functional exercises undertaken quarterly at the Army
chemical depots to meet Army requirements, maintain emergency preparedness, and test new
techniques, plans, personnel, and equipment. They included field play within the Chemical
Limited Area, at forward staging areas, and at the installation Emergency Operations Center
(EOC). Coordination of Army response elements was tested including toxic material handlers,
fire, security, and medical functions, and sometimes support teams performing explosive
ordinance disposal (EOD). Notifications were made to local off-post authorities to test those
communication channels, but off-post participation was very limited – CAIRA exercises were
mainly an Army function.

CAIRA exercises used a small SimCell staffed by about a half-dozen people. Before beta-testing
of the Virtual SimCell began, SimCells for Blue Grass CAIRA exercises were staffed by Depot
personnel. In the Virtual SimCell tests, SimCell controllers were primarily Argonne support
staff, plus one staff person from Pueblo Chemical Depot.

To prepare for the November 2016 CAIRA, a few days in advance of the exercise, the virtual
SimCell controllers had a brief (2-hr.) training session to prepare for the exercise. The controllers
were instructed to bring the computer they would be using for the exercise, and the first order of
business was to ensure that they could log on to VCP and access the virtual SimCell functions.

 79

Once this connection was established, the task leaders went over how to use the software to
perform their controller duties and a few protocols for exercise day, including the time schedule
and how to obtain help, if needed.

Specific injects were assigned to particular controllers. The controllers included personnel with
varying levels of skill and experience with novel software applications. None were IT specialists;
all were emergency management or public affairs specialists. All were able to access and use the
Virtual SimCell application successfully on exercise day.

During the exercise, the controllers used the status board, chat function, and email to implement
and coordinate exercise injects. A controller at the Blue Grass EOC used the status board event
function to update the group on key events such as the start of the exercise. Controllers called in
their injects and recorded the responses on the status board as planned. Five controllers put in
28 injects altogether. SimCell controllers also fielded notification calls from exercise participants
(calls to non-playing elected officials and agencies).

SimCell controllers used the chat function heavily, with a total of 98 messages posted over the 5-
hour exercise span. The on-post controller used it to keep controllers up to date about on-post
announcements, EOC staffing, and posting of news releases. A task lead used the chat forum to
make sure that everyone was on track and to offer assistance if needed. Controllers also could
access technical assistance via the chat forum.

After the exercise, information from the status board was available immediately for exercise
evaluation purposes. The controller at the EOC was able to review the action summaries and
controller comments from the Virtual SimCell as input for the hotwash process. Later, the
recorded status board information served as a resource for compiling exercise actions and
performance records for the formal after-action report.

Implementation

Following beta-testing at the Blue Grass and Pueblo CAIRA exercises, Virtual SimCell was used
at subsequent CAIRA exercises and at CSEPP exercises as either the main SimCell tool or for
the Army/federal component of the SimCell. In particular, when the Covid-19 pandemic struck
in late 2019/early 2020, the advantages of avoiding a physical boiler room operation were
abundantly clear, making Virtual SimCell an obvious choice. The software received positive
reviews from users and exercise executive staff.

3.7.5 Potential Application

A SimCell can add realism to any functional exercise. Without a SimCell, arguably the
participants are operating in a kind of bubble, a quiet zone where no one hears anything from
outside the immediate exercise environment.

Using Virtual SimCell or similar software allows implementation of a SimCell in a way that
provides most of the advantages of an in-person operation but at potentially lower costs. Because
most SimCell interactions with exercise participants are by telephone, email, or other electronic

 80

form of communication, a SimCell can be set up at a location remote from the exercise or can be
operated remotely from numerous home or office workstations. Some initial effort is required to
teach controllers how to use the system; but after that, they will be familiar with it for future
exercises.

In addition, the system provides built-in documentation of interactions, allowing evaluators to
quickly access data, such as the number of injects that were handled and the quality of
interaction with exercise participants.

3.8 Significant Events Timeline77
A Significant Events Timeline allows evaluators to map the overall flow of information and
decision making during an exercise.

3.8.1 Summary

A significant events timeline is a consolidated list of the significant scenario events and player
responses in chronological order. After a large-scale exercise, constructing such a timeline helps
provide a sound basis for evaluation. By mapping the time sequence of major events, evaluators
can analyze the timeliness of the response and pin down when and where delays may have
occurred. Including a significant events timeline in the after-action report (AAR) will help the
reader visualize the sequence and timeliness of the events and responses that are discussed in the
report.

To construct a detailed timeline, it is helpful to plan well ahead, including identifying the key
times to be recorded and allocating responsibility for collecting those times. The process of
identifying the key times can deliver incidental benefits in exercise planning. The CSEPP
exercise program learned how to use timelines as an effective tool for all of these purposes.

3.8.2 Background

CSEPP exercises involved many jurisdictions, complex processes, and elaborate scenarios that
drove play within and among the jurisdictions at a very fast pace, including activities where time
was of the essence to save lives. Because time was such a critical component in determining the
effectiveness of the response, any analysis of player performance had to incorporate timeliness as
a factor. With play at many locations, no single evaluator or observer was in a position to see the
full flow of events during the exercise. It was therefore necessary to collect information about the
timing of key events in the exercise in an organized way, with data collected by evaluators at
various locations and then integrated to depict the overall flow of information and actions. Key
events included scenario injects, player decisions, and actions that affected the outcome of the
exercise. The integrated summary of time information served as a reference for evaluation
purposes and provided an overview of exercise play when incorporated in the AAR.

The inclusion of a timeline of significant events in the AAR was a requirement since CSEPP’s
inception, although in the early years of the program, it was little more than an information list
created after the analysis of player action was completed. It soon became apparent that a list of
significant events that could be sorted by time and jurisdiction could facilitate and discipline the
analysis of player actions. This realization put a premium on developing and distributing a
significant events timeline to the evaluation team almost immediately after the exercise was over.
Evaluators could have this data available in time to support their analyses.

77

 82

3.8.3 Issue

A timeline of exercise events can be a powerful tool for exercise evaluation. How can the
exercise evaluation/control team ensure that all key times are represented, and that the data is
available quickly after the exercise concludes?

3.8.4 Solution

The CSEPP process to develop and use timelines incorporated three key steps: identifying key
times to be recorded; allocating the times to be recorded among the exercise
evaluators/controllers; and collecting, consolidating, and distributing the time data immediately
after the exercise. Together, these steps ensured that the data collected was complete, accurate,
and available in a useful format. The steps are discussed in more detail below. Sample templates
used for data collection are also included.

Identify Key Events/Times during Exercise Planning

The first step was to identify key events that should be captured during the exercise. In theory, a
timeline could be constructed just by asking evaluators to write down the times of events that
seemed significant at their location. However, this would result in the collection of volumes of
information requiring a complex sorting and vetting process that would delay the availability of
the timeline for analysis purposes — and would still not assure that all significant events would
be captured. A better way was to set up the key events list ahead of time, and task evaluators
(and/or controllers) to fill in the times.

The key events of an exercise will depend on the particular scenario, players, and goals of the
exercise. It is not possible to construct a universal list. However, based on CSEPP experience,
the following items might be considered.

Note that not all events and actions are necessarily significant. Good judgment must be used in
assigning significance lest the master list of significant events become unmanageably large.
Significant items are generally those that are on the critical path to protection of workers, the
public or the environment.

 • Exercise envelope times – start and end of the exercise (StartEx and EndEx).
 • Initiating event (the accident, natural event, criminal act, or other event that initiates the
 scenario).
 • Initial arrivals and departures (e.g., dispatch of response teams, arrival of patient at
 hospital, arrival of first evacuee at reception center).
 • Facilities becoming operational (e.g., Incident Command Post, Emergency Operations
 Center, Joint Information Center, decontamination facility, mass care shelter).
 • Messages sent or received (e.g., initial alert/notification of emergency; protective action
 recommendations; notifications to higher headquarters, officials, or regulatory agencies;
 notification of next-of-kin).

 83

 • Player decisions (e.g., protective action decisions, decision to notify public, declaration of
 state of emergency).
 • Activation of public alert and notification systems (actual or simulated).
 • News releases and press conferences.
 • Significant player actions (e.g., sirens sounded, personnel accountability completed,
 traffic control point(s) set up, results of monitoring for contamination obtained).
 • Controller injects (injects of information or direction to players that are intended to cause
 a significant player action).

Identification of these key points in the exercise should be integrated with other aspects of
exercise planning, such as construction of the exercise scenario, Master Scenario Events List
(MSEL), and extent-of-play agreement (XPA). Considering the expected events and when they
should occur can help focus these other aspects of planning for the exercise.

Exercise evaluation guides (EEGs) can be a resource for identifying items to include in the time-
capture list. An EEG is essentially a generic description of how a particular response function is
expected to be carried out. The CSEP Program used a custom set of EEGs developed over years
of exercise experience; the HSEEP EEGs could be similarly used for a non-CSEPP exercise.

The output of this step is a master list of event times to be recorded.

Allocate Time-collection among Evaluators/Controllers

The next step is to allocate the times in the master list among the people who will collect the
data, based on who will be in position to observe and record the time of occurrence. Times are
best recorded based on direct observation rather than a player-reported time. For example, the
time of an event that takes place in the field should be recorded by an evaluator in the field,
rather than an EOC evaluator recording the time that the event was reported to happen.

In CSEPP, evaluators were provided a template (an extract from the master list) with anticipated
significant events listed, more or less in the order expected, with blank spots to record the time
they occurred. Evaluators were briefed beforehand on which times they would be expected to
collect, and were asked to synchronize watches to provide a one-minute or better agreement.

Collect, Validate, and Consolidate Data

The various timeline inputs (the completed template extracts from the master list) from each
evaluator were consolidated immediately after the exercise. The consolidated version was then
distributed to evaluators to assist them with their analysis of player action and the drafting of
evaluation narratives for the AAR. The timeline served as a baseline to ensure the accuracy and
completeness of narrative descriptions of exercise play. Sometimes the evaluation process led to
additions, corrections, or deletions from the timeline. The final version would be included in the
exercise AAR.

 84

Sample Templates

Following are abbreviated sample tables to illustrate templates that were developed for each
jurisdiction or location early in the planning for a CSEPP exercise. Entries during the planning
phase were adjusted to accommodate changes in scenario design and the XPA, leading up to a
draft template used to capture information during the exercise.

The examples below represent only a fraction of the total entries that might be associated with an
exercise. An actual comprehensive timeline in an AAR might run four to nine pages, depending
on the scenario, the complexity of the response, the number of jurisdictions participating, and the
extent to which each jurisdiction was participating.

The examples below cover actions at the accident site and the on-post EOC, at an off-post early
warning point/EOC, and at the Joint Information Center (JIC). In practice, CSEPP timeline
templates were made to cover all decisions and events deemed significant to the overall success
of the response in all participating jurisdictions, including medical treatment facilities, critical
traffic and access control points, decontamination stations, reception centers, shelters, and any
other significant locations.

The events listed in the timeline examples are arranged in the order they are expected to occur; in
practice, those responsible for recording times were aware that some of the listed events might
occur in a different order.

The templates above include a column to name the evaluator assigned to capture the time for that
entry, to assure that this information is documented. A column can also be added to include
“Expected Time” if planners believe it will help evaluators know when to expect certain events.
The Activity column contains a description of the event or action of significance that is brief, but
sufficient for the reader to understand what happened. In some instances an evaluator will need
to enter information in the draft timeline to complete the description.

Most significant events are an instant in time when something happens, or when a task is
completed, or a decision is made.

3.8.5 Potential Application

Any complex exercise, especially one involving multiple organizations and diverse locations,
may benefit from development of a significant events timeline. The benefits occur during
exercise planning and also during after-action assessment.

3.9 Exercising Recovery from a Chemical Weapons Accident78
The Colorado Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program (CSEPP) community held a
series of activities to enhance planning and preparedness for recovery from a chemical weapons
accident.

3.9.1 Summary

The Colorado CSEPP community held a series of activities in 2014 and 2015 that focused on the
process the community would follow in the unlikely event of a chemical accident or incident
(CAI) at the Pueblo Chemical Depot (PCD). Recovery plans were already in place; this series of
activities was designed to familiarize staff with existing plans, technical issues, and available
resources, and to assess planning and coordination through directed discussions and a tabletop
exercise.

The series consisted of five events, spanning a range of activity types as defined in the Homeland
Security Exercise and Evaluation Program (HSEEP): a seminar, workshops, and a tabletop
exercise. First, a seminar was held to familiarize staff with existing recovery plans and
terminology. Next, three workshops focused on particular aspects of recovery: management
through Unified Command, monitoring and sampling to assess residual hazard, and medical and
community services to address human impacts of the accident. Last, a tabletop exercise served to
draw all aspects of recovery together and give emergency managers the opportunity to walk
through the recovery process.

Participants found the series of activities to be valuable. They particularly appreciated the
extensive information provided and the opportunity to meet and coordinate with participants
from other jurisdictions and organizations. Some indicated that follow-up would be needed to
refine plans and discuss further measures to prepare for disaster recovery in southeast Colorado.

3.9.2 Background

Recovering from a CAI at PCD would have involved a number of challenges including
interagency coordination, hazard assessment, and finding resources to address the human
impacts of the event. The Colorado CSEPP community regarded recovery as part of its total
preparedness effort. Recovery-oriented planning and exercising at Pueblo dated back to 1992.

A Colorado CSEPP Community Recovery Plan was completed in 2012. The term “recovery,” as
defined in the plan, included measures to assess the hazard and perform other urgent tasks in the
area affected by the emergency; a controlled process for reentry, restoration, and remediation;
and provision of services to persons, businesses, and other organizations affected by the
emergency. The primary purpose of the recovery plan was to protect public health and safety
while returning the community to normal or near-normal conditions. In keeping with CSEPP
guidance and the program’s proactive approach to preparedness, the Colorado CSEPP

78
 Lesson written in 2018.

 89

community sought to follow-up the plan with workshops and exercises to ensure that it could be
implemented effectively if needed.

3.9.3 Issue

How to highlight recovery issues and get responders to focus on what post-emergency recovery
would require in terms of organization, coordination, expertise, services and resources.

3.9.4 Solution

Once the recovery plan was completed, the community sought to acquaint local jurisdictions and
recovery organizations with the plan; ensure a common understanding of the associated
technical, managerial, and human service aspects of recovery; and test the plan to assess its
completeness and workability. A series of activities was conducted from January 2014 to
August 2015 to fulfill these objectives. The series comprised five sessions:
 • Seminar (January 2014) (introduction to recovery planning)
 • Workshop (June 2014) (focusing on command and control)
 • Workshop (November 2014) (focusing on monitoring and sampling)
 • Workshop (June 2015) (focusing on medical and community services)
 • Tabletop Exercise (August 2015) (integration of recovery processes)

Planning Process

A core planning group was appointed by the Colorado CSEPP Integrated Process Team (IPT) in
August of 2012. Initially, planning group members came from the Pueblo Chemical Depot,
Federal Emergency Management Agency, Colorado Division of Homeland Security and
Emergency Management, Pueblo County Emergency Services Bureau, Pueblo City-County
Health Department, and Argonne National Laboratory. The core group was later expanded to
include representatives from the U.S. Army Chemical Materials Activity and U.S. Army Public
Health Command.

The core planning team sought assistance from Argonne National Laboratory and DETech for
internal and external project coordination, document development, and distribution, as well as
agenda development and facilitation of planning team calls and meetings.79 When planning each

79
 The following organizations participated in the activity series: American Medical Response, American Red
 Cross, Argonne National Laboratory, Boone Volunteer Fire Department, Center for Disabilities, Colorado
 Department of Agriculture, Colorado Division of Homeland Security and Emergency Management, Colorado
 Department of Local Affairs, Colorado Mental Health Institute at Pueblo, Colorado National Guard Civil
 Support Team, Colorado State Patrol, Colorado State University – Pueblo Extension Office, DE Tech,
 U.S. Environmental Protection Agency, Federal Emergency Management Agency, National Weather Service,
 Parkview Medical Center, Pueblo Animal Services, Pueblo City Fire Department, Pueblo City-County Health
 Department, Pueblo County Budget & Finance, Pueblo County Coroner, Pueblo County Department of Social
 Services, Pueblo County HazMat Committee, Pueblo County Housing & Human Services, Pueblo County
 Sheriff’s Office, Pueblo Police Department, Pueblo Rural Fire Department, Pueblo West Fire Department,

 90

individual activity, the core group invited subject matter experts to assist with topic-specific
aspects such as recruiting appropriate presenters.

The planning team and planning process were organized according to the Incident Command
System (ICS). A website was established for sharing reference materials including applicable
regulations and guidance, fact sheets, and exercise materials (presentations and handouts).

A dress rehearsal was conducted several weeks prior to each event. Speakers were asked to
provide their presentations a few days in advance and participate either in person or via
teleconference. Speakers and the planning work group found this practice to be very valuable as
it reduced duplication, built interagency awareness of capabilities, and improved the quality of
presentations.

Activity Descriptions

The five activities in the recovery series are detailed below.

Recovery Seminar

The purpose of the January 2014 Recovery Seminar was to provide participants a general
overview of the Colorado CSEPP Recovery Plan, setting the foundation for the subsequent
activities that would be focusing on particular aspects of recovery. It consisted of three modules:
an overview of CSEPP and the Pueblo Chemical Depot, an overview of the Colorado CSEPP
Recovery Plan, and facilitated group discussions and report-outs. For the group discussions,
participants were separated into groups along disciplinary lines: Emergency Management
personnel from local, state, and federal jurisdictions; Law Enforcement and Fire Service
personnel; and Public Health, Medical, and Social Services personnel.

The Recovery Plan module explained the four components of recovering from a CAI at PCD:
assessing the hazard; performing urgent tasks in the affected area; effecting controlled reentry,
restoration, and remediation; and providing services to people, businesses, and organizations. It
was noted that a CAI may or may not involve a release of chemical agent or protective actions
for the public; however, most planning and exercises assume a worst-case scenario to drive
activities. Participants were briefed on the structure of the plan and the topics it addressed.
Information was also provided about applicable regulatory requirements and the range of
potential agencies involved in recovery.

The group discussion module was designed to elicit discussion of agency roles and
responsibilities during the recovery process.

 Spanish Peaks Mental Health Center, St. Mary-Corwin Medical Center, U.S. Army Chemical Materials Activity,
 U.S. Army Public Health Command, U.S. Army Pueblo Chemical Depot, U.S. Department of Agriculture,
 U.S. Department of Health & Human Services, and West Park Volunteer Fire Department.

 91

Workshop: Command and Control

The June 2014 Recovery Workshop concentrated on Command and Control, including how each
organization would fit into the Unified Command System, how recovery objectives and priorities
would be set, and who is authorized to make reentry decisions.

The group received a briefing on the Unified Command concept under the National Incident
Management System (NIMS). In a Unified Command recovery operation, multiple jurisdictions
and organizations coordinate objectives, strategies, plans, resource allocations, and priorities; and
they work together to execute integrated incident operations and maximize the use of assigned
resources. An integrated Incident Action Plan is used to provide strategic direction.

A U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) on-scene coordinator (OSC) briefed the group
about the National Contingency Plan (NCP) and the role of an OSC. Environmental response is
authorized, and mandated, by the Comprehensive Environmental Response, Compensation, and
Liability Act (CERCLA) in the event of a hazardous materials (hazmat) release. The NCP is a
federal regulation that outlines roles, responsibilities, and procedures for hazmat response. The
U.S. Department of Defense (DoD) is responsible for providing an OSC to oversee response
when the hazmat release is on or from a DoD facility. The Regional Response Team (RRT) is a
region-level group composed of members from 15 federal agencies who are available to advise
and assist the OSC. At the state level, a State Emergency Response Commission (SERC) and
Local Emergency Planning Committee (LEPC) develop state and local hazmat response plans
and oversee their implementation in the event of a release.

The EPA has about 250 trained OSCs (as of this writing in 2018). In the event of a CAI, EPA
would offer advice and assistance on executing OSC responsibilities, and also offer assistance to
state and local governments through Public Health Act mechanisms.

The session concluded with a brief command-and-control exercise. As in the previous activity,
participants were separated into groups for discussion. They were presented with a brief situation
report based on a CAI scenario and then discussed questions relating to command and control
during the recovery period.

Workshop: Monitoring and Sampling

The purpose of the November 2014 Incident Recovery Workshop was to discuss monitoring and
sampling to assess damage and residual hazards in the affected area and protect public health and
safety during the recovery process. After a chemical event, determining the actual extent of any
chemical hazard would be a priority, including what areas were affected by the emergency, and
whether there is any possibility of residual hazard in those areas. Those determinations are a
prerequisite for key recovery activities such as reentry.

Participants were briefed on aspects of the Colorado CSEPP Community Recovery Plan
pertaining to monitoring and sampling. As noted in the plan, the initial hazard assessment after a
chemical accident or incident would come from plume modeling based on assumptions (and
later, on first-hand observation) about the quantity of agent released. Off-post monitoring and

 92

sampling would then be used to support: (1) reentry to the restricted area to perform vital
missions; (2) access to specific transportation corridors, utilities, and other critical infrastructure;
(3) progressive clearing of areas for unrestricted reentry; and (4) providing assurance of safety to
the public with respect to key locations. The plan outlined an outside-in reentry strategy where
areas would be cleared, starting with the outskirts of the potentially affected area and then
progressing inward in stages. The plan identified organizations that might contribute resources to
the monitoring and sampling process, including the U.S. Army and EPA, with support from the
Colorado National Guard Civil Support Team, U.S. Department of Agriculture, and local
agencies. Annex F of the plan outlined recovery sampling and analysis procedures in
considerable detail.

A representative from the Colorado Department of Public Health and Environment (CDPHE)
Hazardous Materials and Waste Management Division explained the State’s role in permitting
and regulating the mustard agent stored at PCD, which was designated as hazardous waste under
Colorado law. Immediate response actions to contain or mitigate a release were not subject to
permit conditions, but subsequent actions during recovery would require a permit. The Division
could issue an emergency permit but would be looking for a detailed sampling and analysis plan,
defined cleanup levels, and air monitoring to protect recovery personnel, as appropriate.

A Depot representative explained the various exposure limits that had been set for mustard agent
(short- and long-term limits for workplace and general public exposure) and outlined the Depot’s
capabilities for monitoring, sampling, and analysis. The Depot maintained mobile and fixed
laboratories for real-time monitoring of agent concentrations in the field and analysis of collected
samples.

An Army Chemical Materials Activity (CMA) representative explained some basics about the
risk of mustard agent exposure both to on-post personnel and off-post residents, including the
term “maximum credible event” and the even-more-unlikely catastrophic events that were
sometimes used in scenarios to drive exercise play. Under these extremely unlikely catastrophic
scenarios, the vapor plume would be expected to dissipate within several hours; nevertheless,
traces of vapor might linger in low-lying areas or interiors of buildings, and there could be
deposition in areas exposed to high plume vapor concentrations. Deposited material would be
very sparse and would not be a contact hazard nor offer an acute hazard from off-gassing. It
would eventually decompose or dissipate. Sampling would mainly be used to confirm the
absence of chemical agent rather than to measure its presence.

A U.S. Army Public Health Command representative spoke to basics of monitoring and
sampling, what each meant, and the objectives of each — the types of questions that could be
answered. He also addressed practicalities of monitoring and sampling for mustard agent
including establishing objectives for a group’s sampling plan and time constraints that affect
what can be accomplished within particular time periods.

An EPA representative described the agency’s Environmental Response Laboratory Network
(ERLN), which included 143 nationally accredited laboratories. The ERLN could provide surge
capacity for sample analysis during recovery. One of the mobile laboratories was stationed in
Castle Rock, Colorado, and could be dispatched in 24 to 48 hours. Examples of past ERLN

 93

activations included an incident in June 2010 when a fishing vessel, the E.S.S. Pursuit, dredged
up munitions containing mustard agent, and a site in Vermont where more than 250 air, soil, and
water samples were analyzed in about 4 days to expedite the cleanup process.

A U.S. Department of Agriculture (USDA) representative addressed food and agriculture
concerns. The USDA oversees the safety and marketability of livestock, poultry, crops, livestock
feed, and other agricultural products, plus the safety and sheltering of pets, service animals, and
working animals. The USDA would work with the state to establish controls, protocols, and
processes for clearing areas for reuse, along with appropriate public information.

The workshop participants were also presented with a visual display of capabilities and
equipment from different agencies, including a PCD mobile laboratory (Real Time Analytical
Platform, or RTAP), an EPA Region VIII mobile laboratory, and various vehicles from the
Colorado National Guard 8th Civil Support Team, including a mobile command vehicle,
communications vehicles, multiple hazmat entry vehicles, and other team equipment.

The workshop concluded with a panel discussion with all the presenters, allowing workshop
participants to ask questions to gain a better understanding of the intricacies of monitoring and
sampling as well as individual agency capabilities.

Workshop: Medical and Community Services

The purpose of the June 2015 Medical and Community Services Recovery Workshop was to
detail coordination between on-post and off-post medical services and provide information on
the public health, medical, and community services that would be available during a Colorado
CSEPP recovery effort. The workshop featured presentations by numerous state, local, and
national organizations with potential roles in providing medical and social services during
recovery, including the following:
 • Colorado Department of Homeland Security and Emergency Management (CDHSEM)
 • Colorado Department of Public Health and the Environment (CDPHE)
 • Pueblo County Department of Social Services
 • Pueblo City-County Health Department
 • Pueblo County Coroner
 • Red Cross of Southeastern Colorado
 • Pueblo Animal Services
 • Colorado Voluntary Organizations Active in Disaster (VOAD)
 • Center for Disabilities
 • Spanish Peaks Behavioral Health Centers (now Health Solutions)
 • Pueblo Chemical Depot
 • Battelle Memorial Institute

 94

The presentations addressed various aspects of community recovery and the local organizations
that supply recovery services. Available services included various kinds of assistance for persons
with disabilities, mental health services, emotional and spiritual care, mortuary services,
individual/household assistance with basic needs (housing, transportation, food, childcare,
finances), and animal and veterinary services including animal rescue and reuniting lost animals
with owners. The basic structure and available benefits of state and federal disaster-assistance
programs were explained, along with the many services available from private and volunteer
organizations.

Recovery Table-Top Exercise

The purpose of the August 2015 Colorado CSEPP Recovery Table-Top was to put the individual
components together and have the participants work through recovery from a simulated CAI at
PCD. For this activity, after a brief introduction, the participants were presented with a situation
report based on a hypothetical CAI and then broke into their discussion groups to work out
several aspects of the recovery effort: command and control, monitoring and sampling, re-entry,
and health and community support. As in the previous such exercises, the discussion groups each
reported out to the whole group at the conclusion of their work session.

Discussion was broken up into two modules, focusing first on “Day 2,” that is, activities 24 to
48 hours after the triggering event, and then on “Day 3,” activities beyond 48 hours after the
event. The discussion groups were provided with a set of questions to address for each module.

During the reports-out, a spokesperson from each group presented a synopsis of the group’s
prioritized issues, plan references, coordination requirements and solutions. Priority issues that
were identified, discussed, and briefed included:
 • People (responders and the public)
 • Emergency Public Information
 • Sheltering and Mass Care: Companion animals
 • Sampling and Monitoring: air, ground, water, population (short and long term)
 • Economic Recovery
 • Fatality Management
 • Behavioral Health
 • Surge Capacity (medical)
 • Patient Tracking
 • Large Animal Sheltering
 • Gaps: Alternate Care Facility, Reception Center

Participants in each of the five activities were asked to fill out feedback forms to rate the value of
the activity and make suggestions. The feedback was generally very positive. Participants gave
good marks to the structure of the activities, and their arrangement as a connected series. They

 95

found the activity as a whole valuable, and in particular they appreciated the opportunity for
inter-agency communication and coordination. Based on their participation, some participants
noted that additional work was needed to prepare for recovery efforts, including plan updates and
additional attention to particular functions such as reception of evacuees.

3.9.5 Potential Application

The broad concept of preparing for recovery from a disaster is applicable anywhere that a
disaster might occur, and for any type of disaster whether natural or human-caused. Of the
activities conducted in this particular project, most would be applicable to any kind of recovery
situation: planning, command and control, and medical and community service response would
be needed for any well-managed recovery effort, and exercising these functions would be of
value for preparedness. The session on hazard assessment (monitoring and sampling) related
mainly to hazardous materials incidents, although it should be noted that many natural disasters
such as hurricanes, earthquakes, or floods also acquire a hazardous material aspect when
industrial facilities, pipelines, or tanker vehicles are disrupted.
