3.10 Adapting CSEPP Exercises to a COVID-19 Environment80
The COVID-19 pandemic in the early 2020s necessitated actions to minimize public health risks,
referred to as “social distancing.” The Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program
(CSEPP) adapted its exercise program to retain its preparedness benefits while maintaining
social distancing to the extent possible.

3.10.1 Summary

As the Covid-19 pandemic began to affect the United States in March 2020, numerous measures
were put into place to control spread of the disease through restriction of travel and enforcement
of social distancing. To comply with these measures and protect staff, planning and execution of
CSEPP exercises had to be modified. These large-scale exercises included hundreds of
participants and staff and were previously executed with large in-person gatherings and a great
deal of interstate travel. At the same time, the hazard associated with storage and handling of
chemical weapons was still present and it was important to maintain an ongoing standard of
emergency preparedness. This lesson documents how the program adjusted and continued to
plan and execute CSEPP exercises in order to maintain a state of chemical emergency
preparedness under pandemic conditions.

3.10.2 Background

After most of the Army’s chemical storage sites completed chemical weapons disposal in 2012,
the CSEP Program held two major functional exercises per year, one each at the two remaining
active sites in Pueblo, Colorado, and Richmond, Kentucky. Each such exercise required about a
year of planning and involved the participation of hundreds of Army, State, and local emergency
management staff and volunteers, plus dozens of federal evaluators and controllers from the
Army and the Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA), along with supporting
contractors and volunteer organizations.

CSEPP exercise play normally took place in the Army installation weapons storage area and
emergency operations center (EOC), state and local EOCs, a joint information center (JIC), local
hospitals, local emergency shelter locations, and an exercise control location, along with several
outdoor field locations. These venues could be crowded places, with dozens of exercise
participants, evaluators, controllers, and observers working in close proximity.

During the exercise there would also be a gathering of support personnel to operate a simulation
cell (SimCell) and exercise control center. Evaluation of the exercise was performed
immediately after the exercise and generally involved another large gathering of evaluators and
support staff (at least several dozen), generally in one or two hotel ballrooms or other large
meeting area, working for the next couple of days to produce a draft report.

Beginning in March 2020, the United States began experiencing and responding to a pandemic
caused by the SARS-CoV-2 (aka COVID-19) virus. Efforts to control transmission of the virus
80
 Lesson written in 2022.

 97

and respond to the surge of COVID-19 patients were implemented at the local, State, and Federal
levels. For the first year of the pandemic, the absence of effective vaccines or treatments meant
that one of the few available methods to prevent widespread serious illness and death was social
distancing to reduce disease transmission. Many costly and disruptive measures were put into
place to create social distancing: schools, restaurants, theaters, concerts, parades, and many other
facilities and activities were closed or canceled. After vaccines became available in 2021, they
could be used to reduce the risk of serious illness and death, but social distancing was still an
important public health tool to protect unvaccinated persons and vulnerable populations and help
reduce spread of the virus among the general public. Mass vaccination centers were operated to
vaccinate as many people as quickly as possible (Figure 3.10-1).

 Figure 3.10-1 FEMA COVID-19 Mass Vaccination Site in California81

The pandemic situation affected the CSEPP exercise program in several ways. As noted in the
CSEPP Fiscal Year (FY) 2020 Report to Congress, “This year provided unique challenges in
how exercises are planned and executed during a pandemic. The two remaining CSEPP
communities in Colorado and Kentucky were forced to use alternate methods and means of
communicating, meeting, and engaging with stakeholders.”82 CSEPP’s efforts to adapt the
exercise program to this new situation are documented in this lesson.

81
 Photo credit: CNN, available at https://www.cnn.com/2021/02/16/us/california-fema-mass-vaccination-sites/,
 accessed August 2025.
82
 FEMA, 2021, CSEPP FY2020 Report to Congress, May 17, p. 25.

 98

3.10.3 Issue

The CSEP Program was important to reduce risks and reinforce the ability of the Army
installation and surrounding community to respond to a chemical weapons emergency. However,
during the pandemic those same installations and communities, like the rest of the nation, were
engaged in responding to a very real and deadly public health emergency. In addition, large
gatherings of people, particularly indoors, became inadvisable and many measures were put into
place to prohibit or avoid them. The pandemic situation caused CSEPP exercises to be delayed
and/or modified to address several issues. In particular, the program needed to:
 • Reduce the exercises’ impact on local responders to avoid interfering with ongoing
 pandemic response – especially medical providers who were already implementing health
 emergency plans and procedures.
 • Avoid having the exercise itself become a “spreader event” and pose a risk to exercise
 participants. This consideration involved changing the planning, evaluation, and control
 processes to protect personnel.
 • Recognize response agencies for ongoing pandemic response activities. CSEPP policy
 already provided for giving response agencies “credit” for recent actual responses;83 this
 provision was widely applied to acknowledge the capabilities demonstrated during the
 pandemic response.
 • Accommodate changes in the way agencies realistically would respond to a chemical
 emergency in a pandemic environment. For example, EOC staffing patterns were
 changed, and mass evacuation centers or shelters would be avoided to maintain social
 distancing among persons displaced by the emergency.

3.10.4 Solution

Reflecting the complexity and scale of CSEPP exercises, many adjustments had to be made to
adapt the exercise process to the new realities of the COVID-19 pandemic. There were federal
policies to consider, along with state and local requirements for Colorado and Kentucky. In
addition, exercise evaluators, controllers, and support staff might be traveling from other states,
so those states’ requirements also became relevant, along with internal policies among support
contractors. And all of these requirements and guidance were changing over time in response to
new developments in the pandemic and the growth of relevant scientific knowledge and public
policy.

CSEPP convened a national-level committee to develop a program-wide exercise health and
safety plan (HASP) in April 2021.84 The HASP provided a safety framework for planning
exercises. It also acknowledged that planning would have to adapt as conditions and
requirements changed over time.
The HASP provided guidelines for pre-exercise travel, exercise arrival and execution, and post-
exercise procedures. It identified Covid-related hazards and spelled out safeguards, reporting
83
 U.S. Army and FEMA, 2019, CSEPP Exercise Implementation Guidance, December, Change 1, February 2021,
 p. 15.
84
 U.S. Army and FEMA, 2021, 2021 CSEPP Exercise COVID-19 Health and Safety Plan, April.

 99

requirements, protocols for seeking treatment, and available resources for medical treatment in
the event personnel felt unwell during the activity. It included appendices with specific guidance
and resources for Colorado, Kentucky, and Pueblo.

A Chemical Materials Activity (CMA) Covid study group was established with staff from CMA,
the Army Public Health Center (APHC), and Argonne National Laboratory. The study group
collected information from academic and medical research, news and media sources, and Covid-
related community illness statistics to aid in decision-making. Their products supported Army
decisions regarding level of play and mission-critical travel authorizations for evaluation and
control staff. Study group products took the form of weekly “Covid Forecasts” and event-
specific analyses. For example, an analysis was prepared for the 2021 Kentucky Community
CSEPP Exercise summarizing such planning factors as local vaccination rates, infections,
hospitalizations, and post-infection immunity, as well as travel and program requirements; and
recommending measures to reduce risk to the exercise work force.

Exercise staff were provided personal protective equipment (PPE) (face masks, gloves, and hand
sanitizer) and required to use them along with other social distancing measures. In addition, each
person was to complete a self-screening questionnaire daily. Anyone feeling symptoms was to
self-isolate and report the presence of symptoms to their supervisor (for exercise management
purposes only, to maintain privacy).

The exercise after-action reports (AARs) detailed how exercise safety measures were
implemented. The text below summarizes adjustments made with respect to exercise planning
and execution, credit given for pandemic response measures, and adjustments to reflect response
procedures under pandemic conditions.

Exercise Planning and Execution to Minimize Risk to Participants

Adjustments were made to all aspects of the exercise process, from planning to execution to
evaluation. As many operations and meetings as possible were conducted virtually. In addition,
changes were made to the scheduling and scope of the exercises to reduce risk to participants and
to acknowledge relevant accomplishments from the jurisdictions’ Covid response
(i.e., participants were given exercise credit for actual recent responses). Travel restrictions
meant that evaluators could not travel to the exercise site at times, necessitating evaluation
through remote observation and/or use of local personnel for evaluation.

The 2020 Pueblo community exercise, previously scheduled for early May, was rescheduled for
August to accommodate the need for adjustments. As a result of the ongoing pandemic response,
off-post exercise participation included only very limited play by state and local emergency
management organizations within their EOCs;85 no off-post field play (e.g., decontamination,
sheltering, and medical treatment for members of the public) was conducted.86 For public
information functions, a Joint Information System (JIS) and JIC were established including local,
State, and Federal representatives, and were evaluated remotely.

 U.S. Army, 2020, PCD CAIRA Exercise 20-04 (12 August 2020) AAR, August.

 100

The 2020 Blue Grass Community exercise was conducted as regularly scheduled in September,
but with significant changes to the exercise scope and evaluation process. Like the 2020
Colorado exercise, no field play was conducted by the local jurisdictions. As described in the
CSEPP FY2020 Report to Congress:

 During the exercise, FEMA Region IV personnel served as facilitators of the
 virtual exercise tool and directed evaluators from across the United States to site-
 specific areas on the platform to conduct their evaluation. All CSEPP partners
 live-streamed from their emergency operations centers during the exercise,
 enabling evaluators to watch the operations. There were 10 live video streams and
 separate data streams including air dispersion models, social media feeds, and
 emergency operation center status boards. The simulation cell was also conducted
 virtually, and exercise injects were provided and tracked. Mock media performed
 remote calls and established closed exercise social media accounts for partners to
 use during the exercise. Emergency public information products and social media
 posts were likewise evaluated remotely.87

In 2021, as pandemic conditions improved, planning began for full-scale exercising in Colorado
and Kentucky. Again, adjustments were made to protect personnel during exercise planning and
execution. As noted in the CSEPP FY 2021 Report to Congress, “CSEPP’s priority is the health
and safety of our exercise participants and the communities we serve.”88

Exercise Planning

The extensive exercise planning process was conducted virtually to avoid in-person meetings. A
virtual platform was created to support planning meetings, provide for virtual evaluation, and
serve as a venue for after-action report writing. Planners consulted guidance from the CSEPP
Exercise HASP and the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), along with state and
local guidance.

Exercise Conduct and Evaluation

Numerous adjustments were made to the conduct and evaluation of the 2021 exercises to reduce
risk to participants and exercise staff. At the 2021 Pueblo Community exercise, off-post exercise
participation was primarily limited to state and local emergency management organizations
within their EOCs. However, the community also held separate, out-of-sequence activities to
evaluate protective actions at a local school, traffic and access control points, highway variable
message boards, and newly acquired decontamination tents. These activities were evaluated by a
team of CSEPP-trained local evaluators. Self-assessments of response capabilities at local
hospitals were also performed.89

The Installation EOC was operated on a modified basis:

 The EOC adapted its response operations to provide emergency management
 while meeting restrictions associated with Covid and social distancing. Many
 personnel normally in the EOC worked remotely from their office to reduce the
 density of people within the building, and they called in via a conference line or
 on MS Teams to communicate information and participate in briefings.90

The 2021 Blue Grass Community exercise was successfully conducted using a hybrid approach
that used both onsite evaluators and remote personnel to support the exercise. A virtual platform
was used for the pre-exercise meeting, eliminating the need for large in-person gatherings. The
platform was also used during the after-action report writing phase that enabled evaluators to
submit their analyses virtually for review, approval, and inclusion in the exercise report. Some
CSEPP partners had to postpone their annual exercise participation due to the pandemic.
However, 11 EOCs, the JIC, and a decontamination site were exercised on September 15,
2021.91

Credit for Actions Taken

All of the jurisdictions involved in CSEPP were, of course, also responding to the Covid-19
pandemic during 2020 and 2021. While the pandemic response centered around public health
and medical measures, it also incorporated other elements such as public information and traffic
control (e.g., around testing and vaccination centers). CSEPP exercise evaluations gave credit for
applicable demonstrations of capability from the pandemic response. The notion of giving credit
for actual responses accomplished two objectives simultaneously: documenting capabilities for
chemical weapons accident response and avoiding any interference with ongoing pandemic
response operations. For example,
 • As described in the 2020 Blue Grass Community CSEPP Exercise AAR, “Due to the

 should be noted that this was not a simple pass; hospital performance was evaluated
 through interviews and written up in an annex to the AAR, including identification of
 strengths and opportunities for improvement. Hospitals were found to have implemented

Adjustments to Reflect Pandemic Practices

Because the pandemic changed the way response would be carried out in an actual emergency,
exercise standards had to be changed to accommodate this change in underlying practice.
Exercises were evaluated against a combination of standards of effectiveness and fidelity to
existing plans and procedures. Adjustments to practices due to pandemic conditions may or may
not have been incorporated into written procedures but were accommodated as if they were.

As an example, the configuration of EOCs was altered from pre-pandemic times. Staff who
would normally have been stationed in an EOC were moved to nearby offices or other locations
(including working from home in some cases) to maintain social distancing.99

Public protective actions were also adjusted. As noted in the Blue Grass Community CSEPP
Exercise 2020 AAR:

 The Centers for Disease Control and the American Red Cross (ARC) determined
 that congregate sheltering will only be performed in extreme emergency situations
 and provided a framework for non-congregate sheltering to be used during this
 time. The Lexington Chapter of the ARC determined that they will not support
 congregate sheltering exercises during the pandemic. For these reasons, it was
 decided to remove all sheltering and reception sites from the 2020 exercise.100

In short, the exercise was adjusted to reflect a change – however temporary – in response
procedures.

Another adjustment involved the effect of the pandemic on responder training programs. CSEPP
exercises included a review of training records, to confirm the ability to field a cadre of
appropriately trained personnel. However, the pandemic seriously disrupted classroom and
hands-on training. Evaluation of training was adjusted to reflect what was possible:

 Madison County has had . . . to cancel or postpone all-hazards training, public
 information classes/courses, and other required ‘hands-on’ training. The EOC
 staff and others have taken the opportunity to take web-based training and FEMA
 independent study courses to help with their preparedness and response
 capabilities. Hospital staff training is conducted, on occasion, online using the
 Learning Management System technologies.101

Crisis communication and spokesperson training continued with adjustments to accommodate
the Covid circumstances. In addition to adopting a remote learning format, other adjustments
were made to lesson format and content in light of the pandemic response – shorter and less
formal sessions that could more easily be mixed into a busy responder’s duty schedule.102

3.10.5 Potential Application

There have been multiple pandemics in recent memory with global reach – the Asian Flu (H2N2,
1957–1958), Hong Kong flu (H3N2, 1968–1969), HIV/AIDS (1981–present), Severe Acute

Respiratory Syndrome (SARS) (2002–2003), and Swine Flu (H1N1, 2009–2010), among others.
It is only realistic to expect that there will be more emergent diseases and that social distancing
may be a necessary feature of our lives from time to time. In addition, as of this writing, the
COVID-19 pandemic is not yet over; and it is not clear when it will end, as new variants appear
and vaccination programs have uneven success. There may be waves of protective actions and
response yet to come.

At the same time, all other hazards remain. The advent of one disaster does not prevent others
from happening. Preparation for all other natural and technological disasters continues and
remains an important part of emergency management. Plans, procedures, and exercises should be
adjusted and continued to the extent feasible. The lessons learned in the CSEP Program can be
applied to any other large-scale emergency exercise programs such as the Radiological
Emergency Preparedness Program and other programs for natural or technological disasters.

 4 Planning and Protective Action

CSEPP advanced the art of certain aspects of emergency planning and protective action through
innovation and/or adaptation of existing techniques to new uses. This section captures these
aspects including use of a synchronization matrix for planning, a geographic zone approach to
protective action, identification of special needs populations, creating an inclusive service
environment, effective sheltering in place, planning for time-critical alert and notification,
Integrated Public Alert and Warning System (IPAWS) testing, sampling and analysis planning,
on-scene coordinator training, using a distributed Emergency Operations Center (EOC), and
emergency preparedness during site redevelopment.

4.1 Using a Synchronization Matrix for Planning103
The synchronization matrix technique allows emergency planners to map the sequence and
timing of emergency actions, identify interdependencies and bottlenecks, and link the efforts of
multiple organizations.

4.1.1 Summary

As chemical weapons disposal facilities approached the point of operational readiness, a review
was conducted of community emergency preparedness measures. The review found that response
plans were not well integrated across the community and did not necessarily reflect how
response would be conducted. Software and an implementation system were developed to
analyze response tasks, diagram them, and enable planners to integrate multiple agency and
jurisdictional plans. This proved to be a valuable planning tool and has been adopted by other
programs.

4.1.2 Background

In 1996, the Army’s Project Manager for Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness (PM
CSEP) tasked Argonne National Laboratory (Argonne) with conducting a series of assessments
of the CSEPP exercise program. Argonne presented the results of these assessments to Federal
Emergency Management Agency (FEMA) and PM CSEP program managers in early June 1997
and to the CSEPP Exercise Integrated Process Team (EIPT) in August 1997.

Argonne’s assessment identified several practical issues with preparedness activities in the
CSEPP communities: (1) response plans were not sufficiently coordinated and integrated;
(2) plans were written to meet guidance and did not reflect the way jurisdictions actually
responded; and (3) exercises were becoming more and more separated from the planning
process. One of Argonne’s recommendations to overcome these issues was to adapt the
military’s synchronization matrix concept to guide exercise design and to improve plan
integration. The EIPT tasked Argonne with conducting a pilot demonstration of the

103

 107

synchronization matrix concept for the 1998 Deseret Community CSEPP Exercise. The
evaluation component of this concept eventually became Integrated Performance Evaluation
(IPE), used extensively by CSEPP. “Sync Matrix,” as the concept came to be known, became
more focused on operational planning.

4.1.3 Issue

CSEPP community response organizations needed a more effective way to integrate response
efforts across the many involved agencies and jurisdictions.

4.1.4 Solution

Emergency Response Synchronization Matrix (ERSM) software was developed for CSEPP by
automating a military planning tool (the synchronization matrix) and adapting the tool to fit
existing emergency planning theory. As technology improved, ERSM evolved from a thick-
client application (i.e., software the client organization would install on its own computers to
use) to a web-accessible, virtual application called Sync Matrix Enterprise (SME).

A Sync Matrix graphically depicted the flow of a community’s response to a disaster in a
chronological, task-by-task fashion. In building a Sync Matrix, key events were “hung” on a
timeline, including (a) key points in the disaster itself, (b) key decision points for response
managers, and (c) critical response events, such as public warning or opening of mass care
shelters. Supporting and follow-on response tasks that would either precede or follow critical
response events were then filled in to make a more complete picture of the total response effort.
Response tasks were charted according to an organizational scheme based on six main response
operating systems (ROSs), each supported by a number of more specific functions. Table 4.1-1
shows the ROSs and functions that were used for CSEPP ERSMs.

Table 4.1-1 CSEPP Response Operating Systems and Functions
 Response Operating System Associated Functions
 Emergency Management Direction, Control, Coordination
 Public Information
 Resource Coordination
 Hazard Mitigation Incident Containment
 Site Mitigation
 Emergency Assessment Hazard Analysis
 Monitoring/Sampling
 Classification and Notification
 Protection Traffic and Access Control/Security
 Transportation
 Protective Action Recommendation/Decision/Implementation
 Population Warning
 Schools
 Special Populations/Facilities
 Worker Support

 108

 Response Operating System Associated Functions
 Victim Care Initial Treatment and Evacuation
 Treatment Facilities
 Decontamination
 Remains
 Evacuee Support Reception Centers
 Shelters
 Services
 Non-Government Support

A completed ERSM for a particular organization included which tasks would be accomplished,
by whom, and using what resources.

The Sync Matrix software also depicted task-dependency relationships. The beginning of one
task could be linked to the end of another task or to a decision point. For example, a task to call
in response staff to the operations center could be linked to receipt of notification of the
emergency, so that it would start when notification was received.

In addition to linking tasks and decisions within a jurisdiction’s response effort, multiple
jurisdictional sync matrixes could be linked together to show the interrelationships and
dependencies between them. Thus, ERSM/SME provided CSEPP communities a way to
integrate emergency operations plans across jurisdictions. The ERSM/SME software portrayed
emergency response operations by graphically depicting how a response was organized; how
response tasks were integrated across jurisdictions; and how tasks were synchronized in relation
to the hazard’s impact, response actions, and operational decisions. ERSM/SME enabled
planners and responders to easily view potential cross-jurisdictional conflicts, by response
function, over the course of an entire emergency.

Initially, the ERSM software and Sync Matrix process were used to support plan validation at
CSEPP locations that were nearing the start of chemical weapons destruction operations.
Because of its participation in the pilot project, the Deseret CSEPP community was the first
location to complete the Sync Matrix process. Having just certified their readiness to their
governor, the community used ERSM to validate how well they had integrated their plans. The
results were unexpected. In the words of the Tooele County EMA Director, “We thought we had
integrated plans . . . [we] found disconnects we didn’t know existed — mainly because we
couldn’t fully understand each participant’s processes until we saw the picture the ‘sync matrix’
provided.” The success of the pilot led to similar plan integration efforts at the Anniston, Blue
Grass, Pine Bluff, and Pueblo communities.

 109

4.1.5 Potential Application

By 2002, use of Sync Matrix had spread beyond CSEPP. The process was identified as a
National Governors Association “Best Practice” and was adopted by FEMA’s National
Preparedness Directorate as a technical assistance offering to Urban Areas Security Initiative and
Homeland Security Grant Program participants. Sync Matrix has been used by more than
20 communities to plan for hazards that include complex hazmat incidents, cascading
earthquake/tsunami events, and pandemic disease outbreaks. Lessons learned from employing
the Sync Matrix process were included in FEMA’s Comprehensive Preparedness Guide 101,
Developing and Maintaining Emergency Operations Plans.

emergency_052125.pdf, accessed August 2025. Note: the concept of synchronizing plans was
introduced in the first version of CPG 101 and carried through to subsequent versions.

4.2 Using an Execution Matrix for Remediation and Recovery104
An execution matrix provides an overview of response processes and facilitates multi-
jurisdiction emergency response coordination and planning. It can also help provide situational
awareness for responders if the plan had to be executed.

4.2.1 Summary

In 2018, a group of local, State, and Federal emergency managers gathered to develop a plan for
a specific aspect of hazardous materials response: sampling and analysis. As part of that effort,
the group created a situational awareness and synchronization tool known as an Execution
Matrix. The Execution Matrix provided an overview of participating
units/organizations/capabilities and their tasks over a span of time to better understand the
resources needed, linkages between activities, and how long particular tasks may take. It gave a
simplified overview of response actions and illustrated timing of tasks, critical paths, and key
decision points. This section describes the process of developing the Execution Matrix, which
was carried out using the expertise of those present and a simple paper sticky-note process
combined with standard office software.

4.2.2 Background

The Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program (CSEPP) enhanced and integrated
hazardous materials preparedness on the part of Army chemical-storage installations and their
surrounding communities. As of 2020, the two remaining installations of this type were in
Kentucky and Colorado. Each installation stored tons of chemical munitions, and the stockpile
included both blister (mustard) and nerve agents. The Army was in the process of destroying
these munitions, but until that process was completed, there remained a small possibility that an
incident could release chemical agent, posing a hazard to on-post workers and nearby residents.
The Army, FEMA, and local and state agencies in the affected communities regularly
collaborated on planning, training, and exercises to maintain and improve preparedness against a
chemical weapons accident.

In December 2018, representatives of the Army, Federal Emergency Management Agency
(FEMA), and several state and local agencies in the Kentucky CSEPP community held a focused
workshop to develop a plan for post-emergency sampling and analysis after a chemical weapons
accident.105 The sampling and analysis plan developed during the workshop described a process
for determining the presence, level, and extent of any residual contamination after the event. The
plan would be executed after initial emergency-phase protective actions, and the results of the

104
 Lesson written in 2020. An article based on this lesson was published in the International Association of
 Emergency Managers Bulletin, May 2019.
105
 “Sampling and analysis” refers to a process of assessing hazards by collecting environmental samples in the
 relevant area (soil, water, vegetation, etc.) and analyzing them in a laboratory to determine the presence and
 concentration of residual contamination. In the case of a chemical weapons accident, the task would likely
 involve looking for very small amounts of agent or agent breakdown products scattered over a wide area. It is a
 technically complex and challenging process involving specialized equipment and highly trained personnel.

 111

sampling and analysis would support decision making for reentry, cleanup, and other recovery
functions.

4.2.3 Issue

In the event a sampling and analysis effort is needed, it would be a complex undertaking with
many moving parts. A simple, high-level, visual overview of the sampling and analysis process
is needed to facilitate situational awareness and give future responders a sense of where they fit
in the overall scheme.

4.2.4 Solution

To provide a simple overview of the sampling and analysis process, the Kentucky CSEPP
community developed an Execution Matrix, based on an Excel spreadsheet. At its core, the
Matrix was a table of actions versus time. Operational periods were laid out across the top, and
organizations down the left side. The bulk of the table then was filled in with text descriptions of
what each organization would do during each operational period (Figure 4.2-1). Over the course
of a few printed pages, the total progression of tasks over the life of the sampling and analysis
effort was depicted.

Specific Design Elements

The top axis of the Matrix was the element of time; the columns were labeled Operational
Periods 1 through 10, with the first period being the initial emergency-phase response period.
After discussion of the nature of sampling and analysis, the Operational Periods were defined to
cover a range of time spans: first using standard ICS 12-hour shifts, then going to spans of days,
then weeks, and finally the last operational period was “Day 31 and beyond.” These spans
reflected the fact that there would be many short, initial tasks to set up operations, then executing
those operations would likely take somewhat longer. Many initial tasks such as securing
resources, developing an event-specific sampling scheme, and setting up sampling teams would
take hours to days, and then subsequent tasks such as sample collection, analysis, data quality
assurance (QA), and analysis of results would take days to weeks. Finally, depending on initial
results, there might be subsequent phases of sampling and analysis that go on for a longer period.

Just as the time element (top axis) had to be massaged to fit the tasks, so too did the
organizational element (side axis). Based on the organizations expected to take part in sampling
and analysis, the side axis of the Matrix consisted of a mix of ICS elements and specific
agencies. The ICS elements were Unified Command, Environmental Unit (a unit within the
Planning Section), Logistics, and Public Information (element of Command Staff). Specific
organizations called out included the Army installation (Blue Grass Army Depot), two counties,
Kentucky state agencies (Emergency Management, Environmental Protection, National Guard,
and Public Health), and FEMA.

 112

 Figure 4.2-1 Sample Execution Matrix Page
 113

In addition to these organizations, the side axis included callouts for three more functional areas
of the response: area control (referring to the process of managing access to any evacuated
areas), leadership decision points, and essential elements of information (EEIs). These rows
represented aspects of the sampling and analysis process that were considered important but did
not easily fit under one specific agency or ICS element.

The Matrix also included decision points and EEIs. As response evolves, conditions may change
or more complete information may become available, which, in turn, may necessitate a
reassessment of the operational approach. Those nodes in the operation where a leader will need
to make a decision are referred to as decision points. Identifying EEIs allows tracking of key
items of information that are expected to be significant to overall direction of the response. The
flexibility of the Excel platform and the mental flexibility of the planners in the workshop
allowed those aspects to be included in the Execution Matrix.

As a last design element, some tasks in the Matrix were labeled with a distinguishing background
color and font color to indicate they were nodes on the critical path: the key tasks necessary to
ensure mission accomplishment. Thus, tasks on the critical path to the sampling and analysis
endpoint (i.e., a confident assessment of the presence or absence of residual hazard) would be
readily distinguishable at a glance.

Construction Process

Construction of the Execution Matrix required gathering information from several agencies as to
their roles and responsibilities after a chemical weapons accident. Fortunately, during the
planning workshop, representatives of all of the key agencies were available for in-person
consultation. The information was gathered using a simple, paper-based, visual system,
reminiscent of some methods of task organization under ICS.

Large sheets of paper were posted along the walls in a room dedicated to Execution Matrix
development. The paper depicted a timeline after a chemical weapons accident – day 1, day 2,
and so on. Initially, workshop participants were writing directly on the paper, but it soon became
apparent that using sticky notes would allow more flexibility. Sometimes tasks were rearranged
as synchronization gaps were identified. Tasks to be completed were written on yellow sticky
notes and posted in the appropriate place on the timeline. The yellow sticky notes indicated the
agency and the task to be performed (Figure 4.2-2).

 114

 Figure 4.2-2 Sticky Note Process

Decision points and EEIs were identified by organizational lead planners and reviewed by
decision makers.

This system was simple, easy to understand, and flexible in the sense that tasks could easily be
rearranged if subsequent discussion resulted in changes to the anticipated timing or sequence.

The Execution Matrix task leader then input the handwritten information from the timelines and
sticky notes to an Excel spreadsheet and printed copies for the various agencies to review and
correct. Excel was chosen as a common platform as most people were familiar with it.

The draft Execution Matrix was presented to the workshop participants for review and comment.
After review, a slightly refined version was then appended to the draft Sampling and Analysis
Plan.

Matrices in Emergency Planning

Although the process leading to the Execution Matrix for sampling and analysis was innovative,
it was not a completely novel concept in emergency planning. The process followed at the
workshop was essentially an instance of the process described in FEMA’s comprehensive
preparedness guide CPG 101,106 section 1.4.1, under the heading “develop and analyze courses
of action.” As described in CPG 101,

106
 Federal Emergency Management Agency, 2021, Developing and Maintaining Emergency Operations Plans,
 Comprehensive Preparedness Guide (CPG) 101, Version 3.0, September, available at
 https://www.fema.gov/sites/default/files/documents/fema_npd_developing-and-maintaining-
 emergency_052125.pdf, accessed August 2025.

 115

 When developing courses of action, planners depict how an operation unfolds by
 building a portrait of the incident’s actions, decision points and participant
 activities. This process helps planners identify tasks that occur immediately at
 incident initiation, tasks that are focused mid-incident and tasks that affect long-
 term operations. The planning team should use tools that help members visualize
 operational flow, such as a whiteboard, sticky note chart or project management
 or planning software.107

An earlier version of CPG (Version 2.0 from November 2010) depicted a “sticky note” chart
similar to what was used during the Kentucky CSEPP Community sampling and analysis
workshop (Figure 4.2-3).

 Figure 4.2-3 Sticky Note Chart (Figure 4.3 from CPG 101)108

107
 Federal Emergency Management Agency, 2021, Developing and Maintaining Emergency Operations Plans,
 Comprehensive Preparedness Guide (CPG) 101, Version 3.0, September, p. 54, available at
 https://www.fema.gov/sites/default/files/documents/fema_npd_developing-and-maintaining-
 emergency_052125.pdf, accessed August 2025.
108
 Federal Emergency Management Agency, 2010, Developing and Maintaining Emergency Operations Plans,
 Comprehensive Preparedness Guide (CPG) 101, Version 2.0, November, available at
    https://www.fema.gov/sites/default/files/2020-05/CPG_101_V2_30NOV2010_FINAL_508.pdf, accessed

 116

The concept of a synchronization matrix to analyze simultaneous actions in operations planning
dates back to military guidance. For example, in 2015’s Military Decision Making Process
(MDMP) Handbook, use of a synchronization matrix is referenced as a component of analyzing
courses of action (also known as war-gaming).

Software to facilitate development of synchronization matrices was developed for CSEPP and
had been used for planning in CSEPP and other emergency management contexts. Similar to the
Execution Matrix, Sync Matrix software depicted emergency tasks grouped by category along
one axis, with time as the other axis, so each task was depicted with a beginning and end point
(or indefinite continuation). This software also allowed tasks to be linked to show dependency
relationships, and multiple matrices could be combined or “rolled up” to create one larger matrix
depicting an entire response scheme. Sync Matrix software allowed tasks to be displayed as a
simulated sticky-note panel (Figure 4.2-4) or as a Gantt-chart style diagram.

 Figure 4.2-4 Sync Matrix Software Simulated Sticky Note Chart

 117

Other task planning software, such as Microsoft Project, can also be adapted to emergency
planning.

4.2.5 Potential Application

An Execution Matrix provides a compact visual overview of a complex emergency management
task involving multiple organizations. The generalized, flexible nature of the Excel platform
allows planners to tailor the Matrix to the specific needs of this particular situation. Using the
Execution Matrix, planners can depict tasks by organization over time, decision points, and
essential elements of information needed for response.

An Execution Matrix can be a useful component of any emergency management planning
process. Particularly for large-scale or unusual disasters, constructing an Execution Matrix forces
planners to think through the process of response, the roles of various organizations, and how
various tasks interweave to achieve the desired end result. What tasks are dependent upon other
tasks? Which are simultaneous or sequential? Will they be competing for personnel and
resources? By incorporating an Execution Matrix into emergency operations plans, planners
ensure that these issues will be periodically revisited as part of the regular planning update cycle.

In addition, an Execution Matrix could be used during response to provide an overview of
operations and to track progress. It is a tool that shows what is supposed to happen, when, and by
whom. It can be used as a high-level check to see which tasks have been accomplished and
which are pending.

4.3 Organizing Community Protective Action by Zones109
CSEPP adapted a zone-based concept to allow for specific, prioritized protective action
instructions to be provided to the public.

4.3.1 Summary

Emergency Planning Zones (EPZs) in the CSEP Program were defined geographical areas
surrounding a facility that posed a potential threat of release of hazardous chemicals. EPZs
served two main functions in the program. First, the zones defined the area that was included in
the program. That had a number of implications in terms of funding and services provided.
Second, the zones were subdivided into subzones that would serve an important role in
protective action decision making and alert/notification in the event of an emergency. The size
and shape of these zones was critical because they directly impacted the effectiveness of
protective actions such as evacuation and sheltering in place (SIP).

The focus of this lesson learned is on the second function and how subzones were defined and
used in CSEPP to enhance protection of at-risk populations. This lesson can be useful to anyone
responsible for alerting and notifying a community about an airborne toxic chemical hazard.

4.3.2 Background

The hazard addressed in CSEPP came from stockpiles of chemical weapons stored at U.S. Army
installations within the continental U.S. At the beginning of CSEPP, there were eight such
locations; this number was reduced as weapon destruction operations were completed at
successive sites. The at-risk area around each stockpile storage and destruction location
essentially became the boundary for the emergency preparedness program. An emergency
response concept plan, prepared as part of the environmental impact statement for the stockpile
destruction program, defined a geographic zone-based emergency planning approach.

Recognizing that risk varied with distance from the source, emergency preparedness resources
were concentrated on geographic areas close to the installation. Two concentric zones were
defined around each chemical installation: the inner zone was the immediate response zone
(IRZ), and the outer zone was the protective action zone (PAZ). The IRZ encompassed the area
where less than one hour would be available to effect protective action under typical weather
conditions. The IRZ extended to approximately 10 to 15 km (6 to 9 miles) from the location of
the hazard (the chemical weapons storage and destruction areas). The PAZ extended from the
outer edge of the IRZ to approximately 15 to 50 km (10 to 30 miles) from the hazard location,
depending on the nature of the chemical and other factors. The PAZ was that area where public
protective actions might still be necessary in the event of a chemical release, but more time
would be available for implementation.

The sizes of the IRZ and PAZ were based on judgments as to possible hazard scenarios,
including how much chemical agent might be released and meteorological conditions that would
109

 119

affect the dispersal. The zone sizes also reflected the need for priorities in emergency
preparedness. Because planning and preparedness resources were finite, they were concentrated
on areas where there was at least a reasonable possibility of hazard to the public. From an
emergency planning standpoint, the IRZ and PAZ served as the basis for planning public
warning and instruction, evacuation, access control, and protection of special populations. The
zones determined the areas where public warning mechanisms such as sirens and tone-alert
radios were installed, which households received public-information mailings, which schools
were eligible for positive-pressure ventilation systems,110 and various other aspects of
preparedness. In a larger, programmatic sense, the zones determined which local units of
government participated in the program, making them eligible for funding to support planning,
equipment, personnel, exercising, and training.

4.3.3 Issue

Even though the risk of a significant release was very low, time would be of the essence if a
plume of hazardous vapors from a fire or explosion headed into populated areas, because of the
high lethality of chemical warfare agents and the relatively close proximity of at-risk populations
on the installation and in nearby communities. Therefore, it was important to be able to quickly
identify and communicate protective action instructions to the people who were most at risk.

The areas most at risk would depend on conditions at the time of the event (wind direction and
stability) and would change over time as the toxic plume drifted downwind and dispersed. In
addition, the optimum protective action might change over time for particular areas; for example,
areas that initially sheltered in place could be recommended for relocation later as the plume
passed and as toxic concentrations became lower. Therefore, protective action recommendations
would apply to specific areas at specific times; in order to best protect these at-risk populations,
the instructions had to be tailored to specific areas and communicated quickly. In addition, it was
necessary that the receiving population understood they were being targeted for specific
instructions.

4.3.4 Solution

To determine and communicate the best protective actions to populations at risk, it was useful to
subdivide the emergency planning zones (IRZ and PAZ) into smaller areas, for which
instructions could then be tailored. In an emergency, responders would use plume modeling to
predict where the hazardous vapors would be moving (progressively over time) and then
determine appropriate actions for people to take in each subdivided area. This modeling would
be carried out almost instantly on a computer system; the process of alert and notification that
followed would take a few minutes longer but could be completed within about 5 to 10 minutes,
as demonstrated at exercises.

110
 Positive-pressure systems operated to ensure that air pressure inside a building would always be higher than the
 pressure outside, preventing contaminated outside air from entering. Such systems would prolong the period that
 a shelter could be occupied safely.

 120

A number of factors entered into how the emergency planning zones should be subdivided for
purposes of planning, protective action decision making, and alert/notification.

Protective Action Zones on the Installation

Some of the chemical storage installations had a significant on-post population, including
military personnel, civilian employees, occupants of family housing, visitors, and people
associated with tenant activities on the installation. This population had the least time to take
protective action in the event of an accident.
 • All persons in or near the chemical facility itself were issued protective masks and had
 immediate access to transportation to evacuate to a safe location. Some workers who
 would be involved in the response to mitigate the hazard had immediate access to
 pressurized filtered air facilities (e.g., control and operations centers).
 • The larger restricted area surrounding a chemical storage and destruction facility
 constituted the next priority for protective action. Given that few persons were in this
 area at any one time, as well as its relative close proximity to the facility, this area in its
 entirety was considered a single zone for immediate evacuation regardless of the size of
 the release. Persons in this area were not routinely issued protective masks.
 • The rest of the installation was divided into several zones, such that persons within each
 zone could relocate to an assembly point and eventual evacuation while constantly
 moving in a direction away from the storage and disposal facility. Each of these zones
 was given a distinctive name or number and had primary and alternate evacuation routes.
 Alert and notification messages to persons on the installation were keyed to these zone
 identifiers. All persons on the installation were expected to know the zone they were in
 and the related evacuation routes and assembly points for that zone.

Protective Action Zones Outside the Installation

In addition to the IRZ and PAZ, CSEPP communities also had designated sub-zones that divided
the IRZ and PAZ into smaller units. The use of smaller sub-zones within the IRZ and PAZ
allowed instructions to the public to be directed to the specific affected area, while minimizing
the need for adjacent populations to take any actions. The concept is that minimizing the area
where protective actions are required frees up assets to assist with evacuation or relocation from
areas actually impacted. For example, in a situation requiring rapid evacuation, relatively smaller
zones allow authorities to direct evacuation of the most endangered areas first, reducing traffic
congestion and travel times. Similarly, if sheltering in place (SIP) is called for, smaller zones
allow authorities to direct exit from shelter at the best time to minimize harmful exposure from
any residual vapors that accumulate in shelters for each area.

The need to take wind direction and distance from the hazard into account suggested a certain
design for the sub-zones: a kind of dartboard design with radial lines from the hazard area,
intersected by concentric circles (Figure 4.3-1).

 121

 Figure 4.3-1 Zone Design with Radial Lines and Concentric Circles

However, another consideration came into play. In an emergency, responders would have to
describe the sub-zones to the affected public in a way they could understand. Therefore, the sub-
zones had to be defined in terms of landmarks and boundaries that the public was familiar with,
such as towns, major roads, rivers, and so on. In practice, the CSEPP communities generally
adopted a hybrid system in which sub-zones were defined in terms of familiar landmarks and
boundaries that approximated the dartboard pattern. Figure 4.3-2 shows an example.

 A

 Figure 4.3-2 Umatilla IRZ and PAZ with Letter-Designated Subzones

The number of designated zones varied among the CSEPP sites. Typically, there were at least
four to six zones adjacent to the installation boundary, and perhaps double that number in the
next circle of zones around the installation within the IRZ.

 122

The use of familiar landmarks and boundaries allowed emergency instructions such as
Emergency Alert System (EAS) messages to be couched in familiar terms, for example “if you
live in Springfield,” or “If you are north of Interstate 110.”

Other factors also influenced the sub-zone designs. Planners had to respect established political
jurisdictions, such as town or county boundaries, because so many protective action decisions
were made by elected officials in those jurisdictions. The next step was to factor into
consideration local topographical features that might affect chemical vapor dispersion. For
example, a valley or hill might affect how a plume would propagate, so zone boundaries were
extended in some places and shortened in others.

It should be noted that a similar radial-zone concept was employed by the Radiological
Emergency Preparedness Program (REPP) several years prior to CSEPP, and for similar reasons:
to allow quick, focused protective action recommendations to be made to the public to counter a
hazardous material plume (in that case, a potential radiological release from a nuclear power
plant).111

Using Protective Action Zones in an Emergency

Fortunately, the emergency response apparatus established by CSEPP was never tested in an
actual emergency since no major chemical weapons emergencies occurred. However, the
program held hundreds of exercises in which the protective action and alert/notification
processes were tested as realistically as possible. The material below describes the sequence of
events as planned and demonstrated during exercises.

A chemical accident or incident would trigger a sequence of actions both on the installation and
in the surrounding communities. When protective actions were needed, installation authorities
would initiate on-post notification to protect persons located on the installation and would also
notify off-post warning points and provide a protective action recommendation (PAR) for each
affected sub-zone. Off-post authorities were responsible for making a protective action decision
(PAD) and then alerting and notifying the public in affected areas. (In some cases, to expedite
the process, off-post authorities agreed to let the installation activate warning systems and make
recommendations to the nearby public in the event of an emergency.)

Emergency instructions to the public generally included three key elements: the sub-zone to
which the instruction applied; what protective action to take (e.g., shelter or evacuate); and how
or where to do it (e.g., instructions on taking shelter, ending shelter, or specific evacuation
routes).

The zone concept and maps showing the pre-designated sub-zones were included in public
education materials distributed to nearby residents and businesses.

111
 See U.S. Nuclear Regulatory Commission and Federal Emergency Management Agency, 2019, Criteria for
 Preparation and Evaluation of Radiological Emergency Response Plans and Preparedness, NUREG-
 0654/FEMA-REP-1, Revision 2, December, available at https://www.fema.gov/sites/default/files/2020-
      06/NUREG-0654_FEMA-REP-1_Rev_2_Dec_2019_Secure.pdf.accessed February 2024.

 123

4.3.5 Potential Application

The CSEPP experience in developing emergency planning zones on and around Army chemical
weapons storage and disposal sites could be of interest to planners and managers responsible for
any military, industrial, or commercial facilities where hazardous materials are present, and for
the communities near them. Local Emergency Planning Committees (LEPCs) for areas that
include facilities with significant hazardous material inventories might find some of these
principles applicable.

The Radiological Emergency Preparedness Program (REPP), a joint FEMA/U.S. Nuclear
Regulatory Commission program for communities surrounding nuclear power plants, has
policies and practices very similar to those described in this lesson learned.

Preparedness for most types of hazards relies on some type of geographic vulnerability
assessment: a 100-year flood plain map, fire vulnerability map, seismic vulnerability zones, and
so on. Whether it makes sense to divide the vulnerable areas into specific sub-zones for purposes
of response decisions and public warning will depend on the nature of the hazard and local
approaches to public protection.

4.4 Creating an Inclusive Service Environment112
The Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program (CSEPP) developed and
implemented policies for inclusion of people with disabilities and others with access and
functional needs, including updates as doctrine and requirements on this topic evolved.

4.4.1 Summary

CSEPP developed and implemented policies for inclusion of people with disabilities and others
with access and functional needs since the program’s inception in the 1980s. In the latter years of
the program, there was a renewed emphasis on inclusion as a result of program reviews by the
U.S. Department of Homeland Security (DHS) Office of Civil Rights and Civil Liberties
(CRCL) / Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA) Office of Equal Rights (OER).
CSEPP and its constituent jurisdictions pursued this goal through measures such as appointing
personnel to serve as access and functional needs (AFN) coordinators, creating an
interjurisdictional working group to review AFN policies, and developing tools to serve diverse
populations in the CSEPP communities. These measures covered the gamut of emergency
preparedness, response, and recovery including planning, exercising, public outreach, public
warning, implementation of protective actions, and recovery/remediation.

4.4.2 Background

During the CSEP Program, Federal law required inclusion of people with disabilities in
federally-funded programs. The primary legislative authorities were:
 • Title VI of the Civil Rights Act of 1964 (Title VI), which prohibited discrimination based
 on race, color, and national origin in federally assisted programs and activities and
 required recipients to take reasonable steps to provide persons with Limited English
 Proficiency (LEP) meaningful access to programs and services; and
 • Section 504 of the Rehabilitation Act of 1973 (Section 504), which prohibited
 discrimination based on disability in federally assisted programs and activities.

These authorities were implemented through a variety of federal regulations and guidance
documents (see the references for a sampling of documents relating to inclusive emergency
response).

In 2007, FEMA appointed a disability coordinator as required by the Post-Katrina Emergency
Management Reform Act of 2006.113 The disability coordinator “advises the FEMA
Administrator on issues concerning people with disabilities before, during and after disasters.
The Office of Disability Integration and Coordination was created in 2010 to support the work of

112
 Lesson written in 2022.
113
      Pub. L. 109-295, title VI, Oct. 4, 2006, 120 Stat. 1394.

 125

the disability coordinator.”114 FEMA had policies and guidance documents relating to inclusive
services on its website, https://www.fema.gov/about/offices/disability.

CSEPP had a long history of policies designed to serve diverse elements of the community. For
example, the CSEPP Program Guide115 included policies on design of public warning systems,
protective action implementation, sheltering, and public educational programs aimed at persons
with disabilities, limited English proficiency (LEP) or access and functional needs. The final
version of this guidance was promulgated in 2019, but antecedent documents with similar
policies date back to the beginning of the program.116

In 2017, DHS CRCL and FEMA OER conducted compliance reviews pertaining to inclusive
services at the two remaining CSEPP sites in Colorado and Kentucky. The reviews had “an
emphasis on the obligation to ensure meaningful access for persons with LEP and effective
communication, program access, and physical access for persons with disabilities and others with
access and functional needs.”117 The Office of CRCL found much to praise, while also making a
number of recommendations for improvements at both sites. Those recommendations combined
with other factors led to a renewed emphasis on diversity and inclusion in CSEPP.118

The modern approach to emergency management programs is that diversity and inclusion must
be “baked in” from the beginning so that they are incorporated into planning, training, and

exercising, rather than added as an afterthought to an otherwise mainstream-oriented program.119
CSEPP refined its approach over time as the program strove for continual improvement.

4.4.3 Issue

How to ensure that CSEPP was an inclusive program serving the entire community. Planning
and preparation to meet this goal had to be tailored to each community, but involved a number of
organizational and practical functions that can be described in general terms. Late-program
(2012 and beyond, when the program was down to two sites) CSEPP efforts to enhance diversity
and inclusion are described below.

4.4.4 Solution

The ultimate goal was a program that served the needs of the whole community. To achieve that
goal, a number of organizational and practical elements were addressed in CSEPP.

Organizational elements included:
 • Assignment of responsibility.
 • An inclusive working environment.
 • A policy of inclusion.

Practical elements included:
 • Information gathering.
 • Planning.
 • Communication and public warning.
 • Sheltering and recovery.
 • Public education/outreach.
 • Training and exercises.

CSEPP implementation of these elements is detailed below.

Organizational Elements

Assignment of Responsibility

Each site had a state-wide or CSEPP-wide coordinator and a working group that met regularly.

The State of Colorado had an AFN Coordinator within the Office of Emergency Management.
The AFN Coordinator served the entire state, primarily through providing information and
advice to county, municipal, and regional emergency management agencies. Among other
things, the AFN Coordinator provided a biweekly newsletter and held monthly virtual meetings
with agencies from across the state. The newsletter and meeting transcripts were made available

119
 Interview with Mark Shull, independent public relations practitioner, November 15, 2021.

 127

electronically, along with guidance and other information resources.120 The Pueblo County
CSEPP Coordinator was a regular participant in the monthly meetings.

The Colorado CSEPP IPT established a Civil Rights and Civil Liberties/Access and Functional
Needs Advisory Group, which developed a work plan with 22 specific tasks addressing AFN.
The workplan was adopted by the IPT.121

In Kentucky there was a CSEPP AFN Coordinator within the Lexington Division of Emergency
Management. The Lexington AFN Coordinator served all of the CSEPP jurisdictions in
Kentucky and worked independently with each county to address local needs and capabilities.
The AFN Coordinator developed a number of planning and public outreach products for use by
Kentucky CSEPP jurisdictions, which are described below under the Planning and Public
Outreach headings.

Inclusive Working Environment

The process of inclusive emergency planning and preparation includes all agencies and
organizations that serve the public in an emergency – not just emergency management (EM)
agencies but also public health, law enforcement, schools, nursing homes, American Red Cross,
and other organizations. In addition, the process should involve advocacy organizations that
serve relevant constituencies such as disability organizations and ethnic or immigrant service
organizations. CSEPP communities reached out to include these organizations. For example, in
Colorado, the statewide AFN working group included representatives of state and county EM,
human services, public health, and agencies on aging, plus representatives of organizations such
as Disability Law Colorado, the Center Toward Self Reliance (a not-for-profit organization
based in Pueblo), the Independence Center (a not-for-profit based in Colorado Springs), and the
American Red Cross.122

In Kentucky, CSEPP AFN efforts were coordinated with public health departments, public
libraries (which often had programs serving the AFN community), and service organizations
such as the Commonwealth Council on Developmental Disabilities, Kentucky Commission on
the Deaf and Hard of Hearing, Bluegrass Council of the Blind, Kentucky Autism Training
Center, Cedar Lake Foundation, and Bridges of Kentucky.123 As stated in the Kentucky CSEPP
AFN Annex (an emergency operations plan [EOP] annex devoted to AFN, described further
below), “[I]f included, individuals with AFN can:
 • Assist emergency managers in developing plans that take into consideration the access
 and functional needs issues within their community.
 • Include children and child congregate care settings such as school, childcare, and child
 welfare in exercises.

 • Identify weaknesses and gaps in plans that require further development.
 • Help develop solutions and resources within the community that can support the
 emergency management system.
 • Articulate emergency needs within their communities.
 • Encourage overall greater collaboration, coordination, and communication before, during,
 and after disasters.
 • Provide opportunities to build awareness about functional needs and emergency
 preparedness issues.”124

Policy of Inclusion

An important aspect of creating an inclusive emergency management program is the concept of
serving the entire community; an understanding that all elements of the emergency management
program – planning, preparation, facilities and equipment, training, and exercising – must be
inclusive as a matter of course. Assignment of responsibility and an inclusive working
environment are important, but all personnel involved in planning and executing emergency
management should have the orientation and consider themselves part of the process of serving
the whole community. Involving all staff and making clear that inclusiveness is a goal of the
organization is the best way to identify and proactively address potential barriers to effective
communication, protection, and recovery for persons with access and functional needs.125

AFN coordinators in Colorado and Kentucky used CMIST (Communication, Maintaining
Health, Independence, Support and Safety, Transportation) as an organizing principle for efforts
to serve the AFN community. Addressing the CMIST topic areas during preparedness, response,
and recovery went a long way toward addressing the needs of vulnerable populations and the
whole community.

Practical Elements

Information Gathering

Knowing the community is an important foundation for inclusive emergency preparedness. It’s
important to identify the vulnerable populations and their needs within the community. One
component is residential or day-use institutions of all kinds: hospitals, nursing homes, schools
and daycare centers, care centers for the elderly, and even jails and prisons. Another component
is individuals and families living independently with disabilities or functional needs such as
having limited English proficiency, perceptual issues, cognitive issues, mobility issues, durable
medical care needs, or simply a lack of resources that might impair their ability to self-help in an
emergency (e.g., shelter in place or evacuate).

124
 Kentucky CSEPP AFN Annex, Version 1.5, April 26, 2021, Section 6.8, Training and Exercises.
125
 This point was made in all interviews conducted in developing this lesson, including interviews with Mark Shull,
 independent public relations practitioner, November 15, 2021; Nikesha Kersey, FEMA HQ, December 3, 2021;
 Maria Church, Lexington CSEPP AFN Coordinator, December 21, 2021; and Sadie Martinez, Tiffaney Brooks,
 and Gabby Roldan, December 29, 2021.

 129

The CSEPP jurisdictions in Colorado and Kentucky developed techniques to identify people with
access and functional needs, together with their specific resource needs, in the at-risk area. In
smaller jurisdictions, emergency managers often were aware of the institutions and residents
with access and functional needs. Local school districts had data on students’ native languages
and participation in English as a second language (ESL) programs. Local service agencies, such
as meals-on-wheels and visiting nurse programs, had information on disabled individuals in their
service areas. General information from Census data on the prevalence of disabilities and
indicators such as age, language, living alone, and poverty was provided on the Colorado
Community Inclusion webpage.126

Planning

Part of “baking in inclusivity” is to ensure that serving all components of the community is
incorporated into plans and procedures. As noted earlier, CSEPP policy had long called for
inclusion in CSEPP emergency planning.

The Kentucky CSEPP community, which included 10 counties of varying levels of population
and resources, created a template for developing an AFN annex to EOPs.127 Originally developed
as part of the Lexington-Fayette County CSEPP Incident-Specific Plan, the AFN Annex template
was intended to foster consistent AFN planning among all of the Kentucky CSEPP counties. The
template addressed legal authority and background; planning assumptions; the CMIST
framework; concepts of operations for preparation, response, and recovery; roles and
responsibilities; and training and exercises. It also included a reference library of links for further
information and a shelter-intake questionnaire for use at mass shelters that was developed by the
American Red Cross and the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services.

Communication and Public Warning

As noted in the Kentucky county AFN annex template, “Individuals who have limitations that
interfere with the receipt of, and response to, information will need that information provided in
formats they can understand and use.”128 Preparation for this element included presentation of
public warning messages in both text and audio formats in public access channels and other alert
systems (e.g., weather radio and mobile phone systems), and translation to other languages as
identified during information-gathering efforts. During exercises, news conferences used
American Sign Language (ASL) translators, interviews in Spanish, and closed captioning as
available.129 Colorado acquired portable Assist2Hear “loop systems” that could be used at local
gatherings, for example, at a public education event or at a mass care shelter after an emergency,
to facilitate communication with hearing-impaired people. The loop system broadcast an audio
signal directly to telecoil- (or T-coil) enabled hearing aids, cochlear implants, or headsets with

loop receivers. Each Colorado Department of Homeland Security and Emergency Management
(CDHSEM) region had one of these systems.

Sheltering and Recovery

A CSEPP emergency could lead to the displacement of residents in certain off-post areas until
the areas could be evaluated for safe return. Displaced residents would be housed either in
situations of their own arrangement – with friends or relatives or other temporary lodging – or in
shelters arranged through service organizations such as the American Red Cross. Displacement
can be particularly disruptive for people who normally live with a network of support services
and care. Considerations for vulnerable populations during displacement and eventual return
include access to information about their options; access to suitable housing; support services
such as medical supplies, equipment, and personal care; transportation; and connecting with
family (e.g., reuniting children and parents).

To ensure inclusive services, CSEPP jurisdictions used an AFN coordinator in the EOC who,
among other things:
 • Assessed AFN needs and identified response priorities.
 • Coordinated with public health agencies, shelter operators, and other support
 organizations.
 • Executed or coordinated with Emergency Support Function (ESF) 6: Mass Care,
 Emergency Assistance, Housing, and Human Services; and ESF 8: Public Health and
 Medical Services.
 • Provided situational awareness of AFN needs and responses in EOC briefings and
 reports.
 • Coordinated with public information officers to ensure that crisis communications
 addressed AFN needs and were provided in appropriate formats and languages to serve
 diverse populations.
 • During recovery operations, coordinated to ensure that AFN needs were met and diverse
 populations had access to social services, financial assistance, and other benefits
 associated with disaster recovery.130

In the Kentucky CSEPP Community, the policy was as follows: “Every American Red Cross
shelter in the CSEPP footprint will have access to a Designated Integration Specialist (DIS). The
DIS will be responsible for assisting persons with functional needs, identifying and requesting
durable medical equipment (DME), consumable medical supplies (CMS), and any other
reasonable accommodations. In the absence of a DIS, the Shelter Manager shall manage any
requests for specialized assistance.”131

 Kentucky CSEPP AFN Annex, Version 1.5, April 26, 2021, Sections 6.5, Response, and 6.6, Recovery.

 Ibid., Section 8, Shelter.

Public Education/Outreach

AFN Coordinators in Colorado and Kentucky cast a wide net to connect with the AFN
community and distribute information regarding emergency preparedness. In Colorado, outreach
efforts included representatives of public health and AFN service organizations, as well as other
community groups such as Councils on Aging and the Colorado Latino Leadership, Advocacy &
Research Organization.132 The Google Drive resource page populated by the Colorado AFN
Coordinator had agendas and transcripts from the monthly statewide CMIST meetings, biweekly
newsletters from the Colorado Department of Public Health and the Environment (CDPHE), and
folders with resources from FEMA Region 8, CDPH, the University of Colorado, and the
American Red Cross.133

The Kentucky community CSEPP program developed a series of preparedness pamphlets
addressing AFN topics:
 • Cognitive Impairments,
 • Deaf and Hard of Hearing,
 • Limited-English Communities,
 • Mental Health Impairments,
 • Mobility Impairment, and
 • Vision Impairment.

The pamphlets, along with other accessible resources and information, were available on the
CSEPP Prepare Kentucky website, https://prepareky.com/accessible-resources/. The website also
had a Spanish language version (click on the Español tab in the above website).

In 2021, the Kentucky CSEPP AFN Coordinator represented Kentucky and FEMA Region IV in
a special Twitter Spaces podcast for National Preparedness Month’s segment on “Low Cost, No
Cost Preparedness” tips and recommendations. The broadcast was promoted via various social
media platforms to engage residents throughout the CSEPP footprint.134

It should also be noted that the CSEPP Portal, a FEMA-sponsored website to provide
information for and about CSEPP, had a webpage on whole community inclusion,
https://www.cseppportal.net/whole-community.html, with information on law and policy,
individual and community preparedness, evacuation, and sheltering and decontamination.

Training and Exercises

Training and exercises are a necessary component of any emergency preparedness program, and
CSEPP had an extensive and robust training and exercise program. Late-program initiatives in
training and exercises associated with AFN included a recommendation that all CSEPP-funded
positions take FEMA Independent Study course IS-368, Including People with Disabilities and

4.4.5 Potential Application

Service to the whole community is an integral part of any emergency management program, and
(currently) is a requirement for any programs that receive FEMA funding.136 CSEPP included a
diverse level of local EM organizations, from populous counties with robust EM staffs and
spacious EOCs, to smaller counties and municipalities with one part-time person and a
conference room at the sheriff’s dispatch center. As CSEPP demonstrated, elements of diversity
and inclusion can be implemented with a wide variety of resources and effort. Essential elements
are outreach to service agencies and advocacy organizations as part of the planning process;
inclusion of AFN considerations throughout the program from planning, training, and exercising
to response and recovery; and public education/outreach programs with local partners.
