4.5 Identification of Special Needs Populations137
A multi-pronged effort was made to identify persons with special needs in a CSEPP community.
The effort paid off in terms of identifying persons who might need assistance in an emergency.

4.5.1 Summary

CSEPP was an early adopter of multiple approaches to help maximize the protection of persons
with special needs residing in the vicinity of Army installations where chemical weapons were
stockpiled. In order to provide assistance, the first step was to identify individuals and groups
who might require it.

As demonstrated in the Anniston, Alabama CSEPP community, using a multi-pronged approach
increased the rate of self-identification by persons with special needs and led to a more complete
and accurate database of special needs individuals. The self-identification initiative was
accompanied by a robust public information campaign, strong community involvement, data
verification, and stated protection of confidentiality. The lessons learned in Alabama could
improve the process for any community seeking to enhance preparedness for special needs
populations.

4.5.2 Background

As defined in CSEPP guidance, special needs individuals are, “persons who require assistance or
special equipment in order to receive notification of an emergency and/or to take action to
protect themselves. Examples include persons with perceptual, physical, or mental
disabilities.”138 Such individuals are present within every community.

The CSEP Program was an early implementer of measures designed to help residents who
needed special assistance in the event of a release of chemical agent. Service to special
populations was incorporated into CSEPP guidance as early as 1991.139 Guidance evolved as
awareness and requirements to serve AFN populations grew. In July 2004, President Bush
directed federal agencies to include persons with disabilities in emergency planning via
executive order (EO).140 A national report on emergency planning in 2006 recommended
increased attention to persons with disabilities in emergency preparations.141 Implementing the

137

138
 CSEPP Planning Guidance, June 2008, appendix B Glossary. Later versions of the guidance updated the
 terminology to refer to persons with access and functional needs (AFN). “For CSEPP purposes, individuals with
 access and functional needs include people with disabilities, older adults, and populations having limited English
 proficiency, limited access to transportation, and/or limited access to financial resources to prepare for, respond
 to, and recover from the emergency.” CSEPP Program Guidebook, March 2019, p. 58. Available at
 https://portalfiles.blob.core.usgovcloudapi.net/training/2019_Program_Guidebook_FINAL.pdf, accessed Feb.
 2024.
139
 The May 1996 Planning Guidance for CSEPP addressed measures for special populations; it was issued as an
 interim document in 1991.
140
 Executive Order 13347, Individuals with Disabilities in Emergency Preparedness.
141
 DHS, 2006, Nationwide Plan Review Phase 2 Report, June.

 137

Post-Katrina Emergency Management Reform Act of 2006,142 FEMA issued guidelines for
accommodating individuals with disabilities in disasters and established the role of disability
coordinator within FEMA management.143 In 2010, Comprehensive Preparedness Guide 101
(Developing and Maintaining Emergency Operations Plans) placed great emphasis on
representing and engaging the whole community—to include those with access and functional
needs, children, and those with household pets and service animals—under the reality of the
current operational planning environment.144 Last, in 2011 a federal district court held that the
City of Los Angeles was in violation of the Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA) due to
insufficient consideration of persons with disabilities in their emergency plans and
preparations.145

4.5.3 Issue

Planning to provide assistance to individuals with disabilities and others with access and
functional needs starts with simple but crucial information: who are they, where are they, how
can they be contacted, and what assistance might they require? A registry is the first step to
understanding the dimension of the planning effort and estimating the resources needed to meet a
capability requirement ahead of time. One simple identification method consists of a saturation
mailing asking residents with access and functional needs to self-identify using a mail-in card or
by visiting a website. However, this method will yield incomplete results; only a relatively small
percentage of such persons will register that way. In the Anniston CSEPP community, within the
main at-risk area (the immediate response zone or IRZ), annual mass mailings had yielded a total
of about 400 registrants out of a total population of 75,000 residents. This number was thought to
be too low, that is, not representative of the real number of persons who might need assistance in
an emergency based on U.S. Census data.

4.5.4 Solution

An aggressive program to assess and support the needs of persons with special needs was
implemented involving multiple search and self-registration methods, a public awareness
campaign, and community involvement. This program yielded a much more complete inventory
of those individuals with disabilities and others with access and functional needs residing in the
community.

Specific practices that contributed to the success of this program included a baseline survey to
estimate the number of households that might need assistance; use of multiple search and self-
registration methods; associated general and targeted public information campaigns; community

142
 Post-Katrina Emergency Management Reform Act of 2006, P.L. 109-295, Title VI.
143
 Brian S. Parsons, MUEP, MPA, and Debra Fulmer, MA, The Paradigm Shift in Planning for Special-Needs
 Populations, available at
 https://www.google.com/url?sa=t&source=web&rct=j&opi=89978449&url=http://www.jik.com/Paradigm2007
    %2520Parsons%2520and%2520Fulmer.doc, accessed August 2025.
144
 FEMA, 2010, Comprehensive Preparedness Guide (CPG) 101: Developing and Maintaining Emergency
 Operations Plans, November.
145
 Communities Actively Living Independent and Free, et al. v. City of Los Angeles, et al., United States District
    Court Case No. CV09-0287-CBM (RZx) (February 2011).

 138

involvement; data verification and de-confliction; and assurances of privacy protection. The
program increased the voluntary registry from 400 to more than 2,600 individuals.146

Baseline Survey. In order to estimate the number of households and persons with access and
functional needs who might need assistance, a 10 percent random-sample telephone survey was
conducted of the 31,000 households to establish the base case estimate that 9 percent of
households would require assistance.

Use of Multiple Search and Self-registration Methods. No single method will be perfect for
contacting and interacting with people in a given geographic area; using multiple search and self-
registration methods increases coverage given that contact gaps or hesitancy to respond in one
method may be filled in by another. For example, a mass mailing may result in few returned
forms for these reasons: a form provides personal distance from those seeking information and
thus can be ignored, some may be embarrassed to ask for assistance or acknowledge a disability,
and some recipients will perceive it as junk mail and discard it.

The multi-method system also leverages existing resources and contact points in the community
for identifying residents with disabilities and others with access or functional needs. Social-
service programs, such as Meals on Wheels and public health care, offer avenues to clients who
might be potential candidates for the voluntary registry; agency lists of clients in the area can be
a useful resource whereby the agency contacts clients on behalf of the registry. The CSEPP
registry program in Anniston used a total of five methods to search for and encourage those with
assistance needs to self-register:
 • Telephone survey. Ten percent of households in the IRZ were randomly surveyed by
 telephone, and each contact was asked whether they would require assistance in an
 emergency.
 • Saturation mailing. Self-registration materials were delivered to each residential and
 commercial mailbox and post office box address in the IRZ in order reach all
           31,000 households.
 • Targeted distribution of registration materials. Self-registration packets were distributed
 to support-service organizations and at locations frequented by special needs populations,
 including public paratransit systems, public housing authorities, public and private health
 care agencies, social service organizations, senior centers, religious institutions, and
 schools.
 • List acquisition. The program collected existing mailing lists from the following: social
 service and volunteer organizations, religious institutions, caregiver agencies, vocational
 rehabilitation centers, educational institutes for those with disabilities, and the state’s
 handicapped-driver registry; it then mailed self-registration packets to the addresses
 listed.

146
 William C. Metz, Paul L. Hewett, Jr., Julie Muzzarelli, and Edward Tanzman, 2002, “Identifying Special-Needs
 Households That Need Assistance for Emergency Planning,” International Journal of Mass Emergencies. See
 also William C. Metz, Edward Tanzman, Leslie Nieves, and Vanda Holt, 2005, “Preparing People with Special
 Needs for Emergencies: The Alabama Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program (CSEPP) Model,”
 Journal of Emergency Management, Vol. 3, No. 4 (July/August).

 139

 • Referrals. The program used “chain referral” and “snowballing” techniques, in which
 individuals being interviewed were asked if they could refer any individuals with special
 needs who might need assistance for follow-up contact.

Public Information Campaign. The annual efforts to register those with disabilities and other
access and functional needs were accompanied by public information campaigns to publicize the
program and encourage a voluntary response to the self-registration form. The campaign
included periodic local media coverage through newspaper ads, public service announcements
and paid advertisements, speakers at meetings, and information booths at community events.

Community Involvement. The CSEPP community conducted outreach to any organization,
agency, or institution that interacted with persons with special needs and educated them on the
benefits, utility, and expectations of a voluntary registry. Another term for this practice could be
“social networking,” although this effort was undertaken circa 2000, before the term and the
electronic technologies associated with it were common.

Data Verification. Because of the multiple methods used to contact individuals, registration data
initially contained duplicate entries. Duplicate entries occurred for a variety of reasons including
people sending in multiple forms, appearing on multiple lists, having a variety of address errors,
being registered under a nickname as well as a formal name, having maiden and married names,
and simple typos. Careful examination was required to recognize and eliminate duplicates, and
telephone calls were made to resolve disparate entries. In addition, data-verification forms were
mailed annually to all registrants, followed up by phone calls to those who did not respond to the
request for updates. Annual verification pared down the registry given that some individuals had
moved since the previous year’s effort, some were deceased, some felt that they no longer
needed assistance, and some no longer wanted to be on the list. In addition, area newspapers
were checked for obituaries.

Data Confidentiality. Information about individuals with physical or mental disabilities is
particularly sensitive from a personal privacy standpoint and because of the possibility that such
persons could be targeted by criminals if identified. The program developed database safeguards
to protect the confidentiality of collected data and emphasized in the packet’s cover letter that the
data was being collected under the authority of the State of Alabama and that the State would
protect privacy and all the confidential information provided.

4.5.5 Potential Application

The Anniston CSEPP community was concerned with a specific geographic region and with
response to a specific risk. Some of the protective actions of concern there, such as the ability to
use air-filtering equipment or to use plastic and tape to create a “safe room” for sheltering in
place, were specific to CSEPP. However, every community has residents with disabilities or
access and functional needs who might need particular assistance in an emergency. A rigorous
estimation of the number of residents with disabilities and other access and functional needs,
coupled with knowledge of their locations, enables emergency planners to match needs with
resources such as transportation and sheltering. The possibility of requiring assistance can apply
to many facets of a potential incident and its consequences; for example, the ability to receive a

 140

warning, take shelter, or evacuate is an important element of self-help in the event of severe
weather, flooding, or wildfire, apart from technological or human-caused hazards.

As recipients of CSEPP resource support, the Anniston community was able to conduct
extensive surveys, mailings, and public information campaigns that might be beyond the
capabilities of many local communities. However, concepts and techniques from the Anniston
program may be implemented at low cost in creative ways. The principles of using multiple
search and self-registration methods, public outreach, community involvement, and so on can be
carried out in many ways and at varying levels of intensity. Informational inserts and self-
registration materials might be included in community mailings such as utility bills, event
announcements, and newsletters. Information can also be distributed through local cable access;
informational speakers at meetings; attendance at community events; support service
organizations; announcements at televised meetings; and through social media. Elements such as
data confidentiality are a function of policies and practices – they require staff time to develop
and implement but do not necessarily require any new equipment or services.

4.6 Effective Sheltering in Place147
Understanding the protection offered by sheltering in place against a toxic-vapor hazard is
essential for development of an effective shelter-in-place protective action strategy.

4.6.1 Summary

In the event of an accidental release of hazardous vapors, ideally jurisdictions would like to
evacuate everyone in the path of the hazard. However, time does not always allow for doing so.
Sheltering in place (SIP) is a viable (and sometimes the only) alternative for those closest to the
point of release. The CSEP Program studied the protection offered by typical residential
structures and developed methods aimed at maximizing the effectiveness of SIP as a protective
action. A study revealed the importance of exiting shelter, or ventilating the shelter, at the right
time in order to minimize toxic exposure. The methods developed include using computer
modeling to (a) estimate when and where the hazards will migrate over time, and (b) determine
the best times to enter and exit shelter to minimize exposure to the hazardous vapor.

This lesson could be helpful to anyone responsible for protecting such a population, by learning
when and how to take advantage of temporary shelter-in-place in lieu of evacuation. While SIP
can be an effective protective action, its benefits may be reduced if the shelter is not exited or
ventilated at the right time.

4.6.2 Background

In the event of an accidental release of hazardous vapors from a fixed facility, the perfect
protective action strategy would be to evacuate all persons at risk to a safe area before they are
exposed to the hazard. This strategy was what the CSEP Program adopted initially to protect
persons who lived and worked near Army chemical stockpile storage sites. Huge investments in
time and effort were made to develop the plans and warning systems to make this strategy work;
and at the margins, the strategy could be implemented successfully. But evacuation modeling
and exercises demonstrated that the populations closest to the source, and hence at highest risk of
toxic exposure, could not be notified and evacuated in time to protect them. In some cases,
attempts at evacuation could have actually increased their exposure. In the meantime, real-life
experience with populations exposed to hazardous vapors from industrial and transportation
accidents suggested that even typical structures like homes and offices could provide some
temporary protection until the vapor plume passed. These gave rise to initiatives to calculate the
protection afforded by temporary sheltering, studies to gauge the effect of measures to enhance
shelter protection, and strategies to implement this concept for public protection.

4.6.3 Issue

The accidental release of a plume of hazardous vapor from a fixed facility such as a storage
depot, transportation hub, or manufacturing facility, is unpredictable, yet can pose an almost

147

 142

immediate threat to life and health in nearby populated areas.148 However, despite being
potentially lethal to unprotected persons, a vapor plume is often of a relatively short duration
(minutes to a few hours at most). Temporary shelter-in-place can be a life-saver if the population
at risk takes immediate temporary shelter, then enhances that shelter expediently. But even as the
vapor plume passes the shelter, some vapors will infiltrate structures (unless they are fitted with
special protective equipment to maintain indoor air pressure higher than outdoors). As the plume
dissipates outside, there will come a time when the air inside the shelter has a higher toxic
concentration than the air outside the shelter. Therefore, temporary shelters should be ventilated
or vacated as soon as the plume passes, when outside air is cleaner than the air inside the shelter.

The best time to ventilate or exit will depend in part on the rate of infiltration into the shelter. For
residential structures, the rate of air exchange between inside and outside (the structure’s
“leakiness”) is generally expressed in air changes per hour (ACH). A tighter, less leaky structure
will admit lower concentrations of toxic vapor from outside, but on the other hand it will also be
flushed out more slowly, leading to a longer exposure time for people within the structure.
Figure 4.6-1 illustrates the concept of vapor concentrations over time in different types of
structures.

 Figure 4.6-1 Benefit of Temporary Shelter-in-Place

4.6.4 Solution

Computer modeling can be used to predict the track of toxic vapors over time, given certain
information including the amount of material released, location of the release, and some basic
meteorological information such as wind direction, speed and stability. Air dispersion modeling
is not simple and in an emergency there is often uncertainty about the amount or rate of release.
However, at a fixed facility, preparations can be made to obtain the requisite meteorological
data, and to know or make reasonable assumptions about the other factors. In the CSEP Program,
meteorological towers were installed at the facilities to provide met data. If an accident were to

148
 See, for example, U.S. Chemical Safety Board, 2007, CSB Issues Safety Bulletin on Dangers of a Major
 Chlorine Release During Railcar Unloading, June 14, available at
 http://www.csb.gov/newsroom/detail.aspx?nid=260, accessed August 2025.

 143

occur, the location would be known, and the quantity of the release might be known from
eyewitness reports; if not, assumptions were made based on the quantity of chemical known to
be available at that location or for the operation that was being performed. CSEPP personnel
used a computer model to predict toxic vapor concentrations downwind over time.

The calculated concentrations were combined with guidelines about the effects of varying dose
levels to indicate where and when there would be health hazards, possibly including life-
threatening dose levels. The model used Acute Exposure Guideline Levels (AEGLs) from the
U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) to relate dose levels to potential harm. The model
incorporated a decision support tool that makes recommendations as to which particular areas
should shelter in place or evacuate.

The model could factor in the protection afforded by buildings used as temporary shelters to
effectively reduce the area in which a particular threshold of health effects was predicted,
provided that persons in shelters ventilated or exited the shelters promptly when the plume
passed. Argonne National Laboratory created an algorithm to calculate the optimum times to end
temporary shelter-in-place. The plume model tool used in CSEPP, WebPuffTM, contained a
variation of this algorithm. Thus, WebPuff could advise decision-makers on the optimum time
for persons to end temporary shelter-in-place to maximize the protection of the buildings in
which a given population had taken shelter.

4.6.5 Potential Application

At the most basic level, the lesson to be taken from the CSEPP experience is that sheltering in
place against a toxic vapor hazard should not be continued indefinitely, since the protection
offered by most structures erodes over time. Exposures can be minimized by exiting or
ventilating the shelter at the right time. Responders addressing any scenario involving hazardous
materials, whether from a fixed source or a transportation accident, should bear this in mind.

For hazards originating from a fixed facility, if resources are available, the end-shelter time can
be modeled using the same or similar software developed for the CSEP Program. Using such an
algorithm can help decision-makers know the best time to end temporary shelter-in-place for any
instance where plume modeling is used to generate protective action options for areas where the
population is at risk. This tool is most powerful when used to minimize fatalities in areas closest
to the release.

4.7 Planning for Immediate Alert and Notification149
The nature of the hazard in CSEPP led to extensive efforts to develop timely, accurate public
alert and notification capabilities. Planning, procedures, equipment, and training all combined to
allow essential instructions to be provided over a wide area within minutes.

4.7.1 Summary

The U.S. Army stored chemical warfare agents at installations within the continental
United States, close to places where civilians lived and worked. There was the potential for a
release that could harm persons located on the facility or in nearby areas. The single most
important function of the CSEP Program was to ensure that procedures and systems were in
place at these sites to provide timely alert and notification to the population at risk in the event of
an accident or incident. The Army, in partnership with FEMA and state and local governments,
developed protocols, procedures, and systems to do just that. Years of joint exercises confirmed
the efficacy of this program. The particulars can serve as a model for managers of any military,
commercial, or industrial facility handling lethal toxic chemicals and for the officials in nearby
jurisdictions where unprotected populations are at risk.

4.7.2 Background

While the Army’s safety record with regard to the storage and disposal of chemical weapons was
excellent, there was always the possibility of a low-probability, high-consequence event. When
these storage sites were established, generally nearby populations were relatively sparse, and
technologies to communicate emergency public information were crude at best. Over time,
population growth encroached on these sites. Fortunately, new technologies became available
that offered opportunities to better notify and protect those populations. With the impetus of a
Congressional mandate to provide maximum protection to the chemical workers and nearby
populations, the Army led the development of vapor plume models to help assess the
significance of an accidental release and installed meteorological instruments to collect detailed
local weather information to help with plume modeling. The Army also installed or funded
installation of infrastructure and communication systems to support the alert and notification
process. FEMA and nearby state and local jurisdictions partnered with the Army on design,
installation, training, and demonstration of these systems.

4.7.3 Issue

Because a chemical release could occur without advance warning, systems were needed to
recognize and evaluate the situation quickly, determine the best protective actions, and broadcast
this advice to nearby populations on- and off-post within minutes of the release.

149

 146

4.7.4 Solution

Solutions to this issue evolved over time and included interactions among different elements of
the program: policy development, hardware acquisition, software development, training, testing,
and negotiations among the multiple organizations involved. The key elements were:
 • Setting the program goal: disseminate alert signals and protective action instructions to
 affected populations within a certain time frame.
 • Developing technical capabilities to support the goal (communications, analytical
 capabilities, warning systems).
 • Identifying all of the steps in the alert and notification process.
 • Developing human capabilities to assess the significance of events and perform alert and
 notification (plans, procedures, agreements, training).
 • Preparing the at-risk population for alert and notification through a public-education
 program about the potential for an accident, how they would be notified, and what their
 options for protective action might be.
 • Conducting extensive drills and exercises to confirm capabilities and attain confidence in
 the processes.
 • Establishing a continuous cycle of maintenance, testing, and improvement.

The elements were interactive; for example, determination of the alert and notification time and
content goal (first bullet) was based in part on what was considered feasible in light of hardware
and practices at the time (next three bullets). The process was complex and did not always take
the shortest path. The key elements are described below. Note that none of these processes were
ever implemented for an actual emergency, but they were exercised regularly with close
evaluation.

Setting the Goal: Alert and Notification within a Set Time Frame

Program managers determined that it should be feasible to for the Army to assess initial reports
about a chemical release, determine protective actions for the installation, and recommend
protective actions for off-post populations within 5 minutes (10 minutes at more remote sites).
Operators at off-post warning points were then expected to assess the Army recommendations,
decide on the best protective action for the community, and alert and instruct the at-risk
population on protective actions within 8 minutes after receiving notification from the
installation, for a total elapsed time of either 13 or 18 minutes. This time standard was
incorporated into on-post/off-post agreements and program guidance, and drove specifications
for other parts of the program such as equipment acquisition, software design and exercise
evaluation standards.

The time standard was defined in very precise terms. The Army clock started when a
“responsible person” (likely someone working with chemical munitions in the storage area, but
possibly also an observer such as a security guard) recognized that a toxic chemical release had
occurred or might be imminent and could safely report it. (In other words, the standard

 147

acknowledged that at the scene of an accident or incident with deadly chemicals and the potential
for explosions, there might be priority actions that must be taken to protect those present before
someone would be in a position to safely call in the emergency.) The Army clock stopped when
Army protective action recommendations were received by off-post warning points. The off-post
clock started upon receipt of the Army protective action recommendations and stopped when the
instructional message to the public had been completed.150

The standard also carefully defined the information that must be provided (a) from the accident
or incident scene to the on-post emergency operations center (EOC); (b) from the on-post EOC
to the off-post warning points; and (c) to the public in the affected area. The call from the scene
was to include the caller’s identity and location, the location of the release, the type of chemical
and approximate quantity involved, the mode of release (fire/explosion/leak), and any
information about injuries. (These were termed “essential elements of information.”) The
notification from the on-post EOC to the off-post warning points was to include the nature of the
release, the name of the chemical involved, the predominant wind direction, the areas predicted
to be affected, and protective action recommendations for all affected areas (i.e., whether to
evacuate or shelter-in-place, possibly depending on specific location). Off-post warning points
were to provide the public with protective action instructions, which could differ from what the
Army installation had recommended per the judgment of off-post authorities.

Developing Technical Capabilities to Support the Goal

New physical systems were needed to carry out the alert and notification process. On the Army
installation, all persons who worked in Army chemical storage and disposal facilities, including
security guards, were equipped to communicate directly with the installation EOC, which was
staffed 24/7. Radios were the primary tools for communicating this information. A system of
meteorological towers was operated at and near Army chemical storage facilities to provide local
weather information upon which to make hazard predictions. Output from these towers was fed
directly to the plume modeling tool. Hazard analysts used computers to perform plume modeling
to determine where dangerous concentrations of chemical agent might occur and which
protective actions would be best for persons in the downwind area.

Systems acquired to support the notification process (both on-post and off-post) included outdoor
siren/public address (PA) systems and tone-alert radios for residences, schools, businesses, and
other buildings. Outdoor warning devices were usually 360-degree electronic sirens with voice
messaging capability and were positioned to cover all frequently occupied areas, including not
only residential areas but also commercial, industrial, and recreational areas within the warning
zone. Indoor warning devices were installed or offered to each regularly occupied building.
These indoor and outdoor devices were supplemented with other mechanisms as appropriate,
such as EAS broadcast messages, pagers, phone calls (reverse-911 systems), crawl messages on
cable television, mobile PA systems, route alerting, and/or text-display highway signs.

150
 It should be noted that at some sites, the off-post community opted to simply let the Army activate public
 warning and notification systems and issue instructions to the public directly in order to save time. This
 activation method would be documented in a memorandum of agreement.

 148

The alert and notification systems included the means to alert and notify special populations,
such as providing public notifications in languages other than English where there were
significant non-English-speaking populations, and making mechanisms available to sensory-
impaired individuals (e.g., vibration devices, flashing lights, and teletype devices for the hearing
impaired) to allow them to receive emergency instructions in a timely manner.

Each system required regular maintenance, testing, and occasional upgrades. As the CSEP
Program ended at particular installations, the equipment was reallocated or repurposed; in some
cases, the cost of operation and maintenance meant that local siren/PA systems had to be shut
down.

Identifying All of the Steps in the Warning Process

As with any aspect of emergency planning, the process begins with analyzing the sequence of
steps and events that must take place if the public is to be protected. The response process begins
with awareness of an event – the first sign that something is wrong. For weapons-handling
accidents, that would most likely occur out in the storage area or in the disposal facility as some
operation goes awry. The accident would be apparent to those working in the area. Alternatively,
the first sign could come from a sensor reading or alarm, observation of smoke, or some other
indicator. The next step would be for information about the event to be transmitted to a central
duty station. The chemical storage installations maintained an EOC that was staffed 24/7.

The next several steps would then occur at the installation EOC: (a) a protective action
recommendation (PAR) would be generated based on information available at the time and the
output of the plume model; (b) a protective action decision (PAD) would be made for affected
areas of the installation; (c) processes would be started for warning people on the installation;
and (d) notification and a PAR would be provided to off-post warning points.151

The final steps for warning off-post populations fell under the purview of off-post authorities.
After receiving notification of the event and the PAR from the Army installation, off-post
officials would make a PAD for off-post affected areas (which might differ from the Army’s
PAR as noted earlier) and activate processes for alerting the public and providing protective
action instructions. Generally, that would mean activating outdoor siren systems, indoor warning
systems (tone-alert radios), EAS, and whatever other systems were available.

Developing Human Capabilities to Perform Alert and Notification

The human portion of the system included individual capabilities, provided through training and
practice, plus a package of synchronized plans, procedures, protocols, and agreements.

In the event of a sudden chemical agent release, close-in areas likely would not have time to
evacuate and would have to shelter in place (SIP), while areas further out might have time to
evacuate before plume arrival. Upwind areas might be at little risk. Thus, different areas would
need different instructions. The groundwork was laid for this at the planning stage by dividing

151
 Note that these are just the actions pertaining to alert and notification; many other response measures would also
 be simultaneously initiated from the installation EOC.

 149

the at-risk community into sub-areas called zones. The pre-defined zones allowed instructions to
be tailored for each zone individually. Zones were designed to be easily described in a short
message, by reference to familiar landmarks and boundaries.

Agreements were developed between the installation and surrounding communities outlining the
procedures and protocols for emergency communications – who would call whom using what
system and providing what information. In some cases, arrangements were made to allow
installation personnel to activate warning systems and provide instructions directly to affected
populations off-post in order to save crucial minutes.

Extensive public information campaigns were carried out to familiarize the local community
with the hazard, how they would be notified in an emergency, and what they might be called
upon to do in response.

All persons who worked in Army chemical storage and disposal facilities, including security
guards, were trained to recognize situations and conditions that indicate chemical agents might
be outside of engineering controls. They were also trained on what information to report, and to
whom, in the event of an accident.

Each workday before operations began, facility managers prepared a work plan that considered
the maximum credible event that could occur as a result of scheduled operations, and the
potential for that event to affect the population in nearby communities. This work plan was
shared with emergency managers in nearby communities to provide a baseline for possible
emergency response for that day.

Upon receipt of a report of a chemical accident from the field, responders in the installation EOC
were trained to immediately assess the significance of the event, determine the potential impact
on all areas of the facility, and alert and notify the facility population of protective actions they
must take. (This involved siren activation with pre-recorded messages, and activation of
telephonic and pager systems.) These responders would simultaneously determine the impact on
nearby communities and (if appropriate) provide a verbal warning, information, and protective
action recommendations to community warning points. This communication was done verbally
via a dedicated telephonic hotline with radio back-up, followed by confirmation over an
automated electronic system that shared plume modeling results (AEGLs and concentration
models) and recommended protective action recommendations. This information could also be
shared with the warning points via fax. The plume modeling system was developed and tested
specifically for CSEPP.152

The minimum information that must be sent to the community warning points included a
definitive statement about whether the event was classified as a Community Event (unprotected
persons in the community were at risk of chemical effects), the identity of the chemical released,
the current predominant wind direction, the areas in the community (response zones) at risk, and
a recommended initial protective action (evacuate or shelter-in-place) for each response zone.

152
 It was termed WebPuff®. See https://iem.com/webpuff-improving-protection-through-science-innovation-and-a-
 web-2-0-approach/, accessed February 2024, and descriptions in Volume I of this document.

 150

Information sent to the community warning points followed an agreed-upon protocol and format
(common form with fill-in-the-blanks in sequence, acknowledgment of receipt).

Community warning points might be a 911 Center or a county Operations Center when the latter
was operational. In either case, warning point operators were trained to take reports of a
chemical accident from the Army facility on the dedicated hot line, consider the information
(especially the recommended protective action) and current conditions in the community, decide
on the appropriate protective action for each response zone affected, and activate community
alert and notification systems. Notification messages were pre-scripted to save time and to make
sure they were consistent with public education programs. If the community protective action
decision differed from the recommendation made by the Army, the Army installation was to be
told immediately so that Army response personnel could consider this new direction when
making subsequent recommendations.

Workers at the accident site would keep the installation EOC operators updated about conditions
as the response evolved, including any additional releases. Response staff in the EOC would
track meteorological conditions to constantly reassess the threat to all populations that might be
impacted by these new conditions. If conditions changed such that protective actions needed to
be revised (e.g., wind shift), new information would be disseminated, and new alert and
notification system messages would be broadcast with the same priority and sense of urgency as
the initial alert and notifications announcements.

As soon as initial alerts and notifications were completed, staff in the installation EOC were
trained to begin assessing when and how to end shelter-in-place in all zones where a population
was instructed to take any initial protective action (both evacuate or shelter-in-place). This
assessment would focus on the best time to end shelter-in-place to avoid fatalities to the
maximum extent practicable. Recommended times to end shelter-in-place had to be passed to
community warning points in sufficient time for community authorities to consider the
recommendations and broadcast this direction on alert and notification systems in a timely
manner. A function to generate recommended exit-shelter times was built into the plume analysis
software.

Educating the Population at Risk

For this process to be most effective, the population at-risk should have some prior exposure to
information about the potential for an accident, how they would be notified, and what their
protective action options might be. They should also be familiar with the language that would be
used in the notification announcements, which in any case should be simple and unambiguous.
For example, they should know the protective action zone identification for places where they
reside and work, be knowledgeable of evacuation routes and receptions centers, and be prepared
to shelter-in-place if evacuation was not appropriate or possible. CSEPP used a variety of
methods to disseminate this information to at-risk populations.

 151

Conducting Drills and Exercises to Confirm Capabilities

The systems, procedures, and personnel involved in the alert and notification process were tested
through a spectrum of drills and exercises. These ranged from daily tests of communication
systems; through weekly, monthly, or quarterly system tests; up to an annual full-function
exercise. The annual exercise, based on an accident scenario, aimed to test all aspects of the alert
and notification process just short of actually providing protective action instructions to the
public. Sometimes a test message was broadcast to the public as part of the exercise. Independent
evaluators assessed whether the instructions issued were appropriate given the scenario situation
and whether they were produced and disseminated in a timely manner. Mechanisms were in
place to track and correct any significant errors noted by evaluators.

Establishing a Continuous Cycle of Maintenance, Testing, and Improvement

Attaining and maintaining a state of readiness for this crucial and time-sensitive function
required continuous effort. Apart from ordinary equipment testing and skill maintenance, staff
turnover created a need for training and exercising, and technologies changed over time.
Integrating new technologies into systems and procedures required effort but could add
capabilities and improve public safety.

4.7.5 Potential Application

The systems and capabilities developed for CSEPP are most clearly applicable to other fixed
facility toxic hazards – any military, commercial, or industrial facility that stores or uses large
quantities of potentially lethal toxic chemicals. Such facilities, and the communities that host
them, should be capable of immediately alerting and notifying all persons who work or live in or
near the facility. Generally, such communities will not have access to funding resources like
those in CSEPP, but elements of the CSEPP approach – a stepwise analysis of the process,
specific plans and procedures, inter-organizational agreements, and so on – may still provide a
model for building capabilities. In addition, more and more public-warning capabilities may
become within reach of local communities as standards and practices evolve under Integrated
Public Alert and Warning System (IPAWS). In a broader sense, the lessons of CSEPP may be
applicable to any situation warranting prompt public warning, such as severe weather scenarios,
flash floods, or hazmat transportation accidents.

4.7.6 References

U.S. Army and FEMA (Federal Emergency Management Agency), 2019, CSEPP Program
Guidebook, March, available at
https://portalfiles.blob.core.usgovcloudapi.net/training/2019_Program_Guidebook_FINAL.pdf,

 152

4.8 IPAWS Testing and Development153
The Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program (CSEPP) featured a well-funded,
cooperative relationship between federal, state, and local jurisdictions for emergency
preparedness in communities hosting Army chemical weapons storage sites. This made CSEPP
an ideal test bed for the Integrated Public Alert and Warning System (IPAWS).

4.8.1 Summary

IPAWS is a system to provide emergency alerts, warnings, and notifications (AWN) to the
public through a variety of media. Messages to the public can be initiated at the national, state, or
local level and distributed through various channels including broadcast media, mobile
telephones, and weather radios. Authorized by a presidential executive order and federal
legislation, the Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA) and other agencies worked to
create this enhanced public warning capability and make it available to state, local, tribal, and
territorial (SLTT) authorities. Developing the legal and regulatory systems, technical protocols,
software, user interface, and skills to make IPAWS practical was a challenging task requiring
years to implement. Rollout of these capabilities to local governments was a years-long process
and continues as communities and methods evolve.

CSEPP built a robust set of local and state capabilities for emergency response, along with
contacts and relationships with FEMA. CSEPP thus was a logical test bed for implementing the
new capabilities offered by IPAWS. CSEPP was an early adopter of IPAWS and developed
planning, guidance, training, and testing to implement the system at the local level in CSEPP
communities. CSEPP IPAWS testing yielded a number of lessons learned to address technical
and user interface complexities.

IPAWS implementation guidance developed by CSEPP provided a suite of useful tools to assist
local jurisdictions in assessing, planning, developing, and implementing IPAWS to enhance local
emergency public AWN.

4.8.2 Background

IPAWS is a system to provide emergency AWN to the public through a variety of media.
Messages to the public can be initiated at the national, state, or local level and can be distributed
through broadcast, dish, and cable networks for radio and television, weather radios, and mobile
telephones. IPAWS enables authorized federal and SLTT alerting authorities to send a Wireless
Emergency Alert (WEA) to mobile devices such as cell phones; an Emergency Alert System
(EAS) alert to media platforms, such as radios and television; and an alert message through the
National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA) system to weather radios, all using a
single origination system.

This powerful capability to issue public warnings and instructions was developed through close
coordination between multiple agencies, establishing information technology protocols and
153

 153

practices that provide interoperability among multiple systems. It was an inherently complex
problem with technical, practical, and legal aspects. Development took years, and involved
legislation, interagency and intergovernmental coordination through committees and working
groups, and interaction with companies and associations representing mobile
telecommunications, media distribution, and software development. Testing and guidance
development were important components of the implementation process. Access to IPAWS
(i.e., the ability to originate alerts) is regulated by the Federal Communications Commission
(FCC) and FEMA. FEMA operates IPAWS through the IPAWS Program Management Office
(PMO). As noted on a FEMA IPAWS webpage:

 IPAWS allows Alerting Authorities to write their own message using
 commercially available software that is Common Alerting Protocol (CAP)
 compliant. The message is then delivered to the Integrated Public Alert and
 Warning System, Open Platform for Emergency Networks (IPAWS OPEN),
 where it is authenticated and then delivered simultaneously through multiple
 communication pathways. Through IPAWS, one message is created to reach as
 many people as possible to save lives and protect property.154

Implementation of IPAWS was gradual, reflecting the complex nature of building a widespread
new technological capability with high importance and low tolerance for errors. FEMA began
the development process in 2006 in response to Executive Order 13407.155 The FEMA IPAWS
PMO was established in 2007, and IPAWS deployment began in 2011. Since then, IPAWS
participation has gradually grown from fewer than 100 alerting authorities in 2013 to more than
1,600 by 2024.156 Implementation of IPAWS at a given jurisdiction requires planning,
interagency agreements, acquisition of hardware and software, training, testing, and approval by
FEMA. Figure 4.8-1 illustrates the overall architecture of IPAWS.

 Figure 4.8-1 IPAWS Architecture157

CSEPP was established in the 1980s to bolster emergency preparedness in communities that
hosted U.S. Army facilities where chemical weapons were stored. The Army was destroying
these weapons as required by federal statute and treaty through the Chemical Stockpile Disposal
Program. But until the last chemical munition was destroyed, CSEPP provided a mechanism to
protect nearby communities in case of a chemical weapons accident.

Jointly administered by the Army and FEMA, CSEPP built well-established cooperative
relationships among federal, state, and local emergency management agencies. It bolstered local
capabilities through development of plans, facilities, staffing, and equipment, and through
implementation of extensive drill and exercise programs. These relationships and capabilities
made CSEPP a logical test bed for adoption of IPAWS.158

The CSEPP program promoted local implementation of IPAWS at two sites, the Kentucky
CSEPP community (associated with the Blue Grass Army Depot near Richmond) and the

157
 Source: CSEPP, 2019, Guide to Implementing the Integrated Public Alert and Warning System (IPAWS),
 Version 2, February, available at the CSEPP Portal, https://www.cseppportal.net, accessed December 2020.
158
 In addition, the immediate nature of the CSEPP hazard – most accident scenarios involve an explosion followed
 by a cloud of toxic gas drifting downwind – placed an emphasis on quick alert and notification to the public.
 Prompt public alerting and notification was always a component of CSEPP preparedness; IPAWS was a logical
 extension of this capability.

 155

Colorado CSEPP community (associated with the Pueblo Chemical Depot near Pueblo). The
CSEPP IPAWS implementation program began soon after the IPAWS program was established
and continued until the end of CSEPP. CSEPP established a working group to coordinate
IPAWS implementation, developed guidance, and assisted local communities with equipment
and software acquisition, training, testing, and exercising to ensure that IPAWS systems could be
effectively used for public warning in actual emergencies.

This lesson describes what was learned during CSEPP’s implementation of IPAWS. CSEPP
contributed significantly to the overall development of IPAWS and helped develop guidance and
standards that were then adopted nationally. Local CSEPP jurisdictions used their CSEPP-
developed IPAWS capabilities to respond to actual (non-chemical-weapons) emergencies.

4.8.3 Issue

How to effectively use IPAWS to enhance local alert and warning and provide a powerful,
flexible capability to warn the public about emergency situations requiring their attention.

4.8.4 Solution

Participation and Organization

CSEPP began following developments in IPAWS from the beginning and was actively
discussing how to fit it into the existing CSEPP public warning systems by 2012.159 CSEPP
formed an IPAWS working group in early 2013, with three subcommittees: Governance,
Messaging, and Technology. FEMA HQ also coordinated closely with the FEMA IPAWS PMO.
In 2014, these separate work groups were merged into the existing public affairs and automation
integrated process teams (IPTs). The working groups to follow through on IPAWS
implementation and daily operations for several more years.

The Governance subcommittee was tasked with establishing state and/or local alerting authority
to send messages to the public. The Messaging group developed message templates for various
types of alerts, and the Technology group discussed the technology associated with IPAWS
messaging and reviewed messaging templates for adherence to specifications. These groups met
fairly regularly throughout 2013, and then their roles were migrated to the regular IPT process.

The CSEPP IPAWS implementation process included:
 • CSEPP state and local jurisdictions (Colorado / Pueblo County; Kentucky / Madison,
 Lexington-Fayette, Garrard, Laurel, and Estill Counties),
 • Army Chemical Materials Activity (CMA),
 • FEMA CSEPP offices at HQ and Regions IV and VIII, and

159
 See, for example, CSEPP Automation Integrated Process Team minutes from its January 10–11, 2012, meeting.
 Accessed December 2020 on the CSEPP Portal, www.cseppportal.net.

 156

 • Contractors Integrated Emergency Management, Inc. (IEM), Argonne National
 Laboratory (Argonne), and Mission Critical Partners (MCP).

Testing and Guidance Development

The CSEPP IPAWS working group had two main accomplishments. First, the group conducted
an IPAWS test over two days in December 2014, using the IPAWS Lab housed at the
U.S. Department of Defense Joint Interoperability Test Command (JITC) test center in Indian
Head, Maryland. This test included message origination and distribution via IPAWS. Second, the
group incorporated lessons learned from the December 2014 test, along with other information,
to develop a CSEPP IPAWS Implementation Guide. The Guide was published in 2015 and
updated in 2019.160

Planning and Execution of December 2014 IPAWS Test

The IPAWS working group published a comprehensive report of the testing that was conducted
in December 2014.161 The report described the nearly two-year-long process of planning and
preparation that preceded the test. It included:
 • Development and revision of functional requirements, test guidelines, and a test plan.
 • Coordination with the FEMA IPAWS PMO and FEMA Technical Engineering Support
 (FTES).
 • Coordination with the FCC and the wireless communication industry association CTIA.
 Coordination was facilitated by MCP throughout the process.

A series of conference calls and meetings, discussions, and refinements of planning documents
and arrangements culminated in a two-day test session held December 8–9, 2014, at the JITC test
center in Maryland. The testing involved alert authority staff performing simulated alerting
procedures; test observers (serving as recipients of the simulated test messages); supporting
technical experts, primarily from the FEMA IPAWS PMO; equipment and software
corresponding to what the alerting authorities had at home; and the JITC IPAWS-Open Platform
for Emergency Networks (OPEN) Test Development Lab (TDL). TDL facilities and personnel
played a key role in conducting the test. The testing team collected data including log files,
photos, and video of the simulated messaging.

The testing process was described in the 2015 test report as follows:

 During CSEPP testing of IPAWS at the IPAWS Lab at JITC, the users began the
 testing being tentative and hesitant to enter messages. This was reinforced by the
 result of several of the first messages failing. As testing progressed, the users
 gained a better understanding of the use of their respective systems, which

160
 CSEPP, 2019, Guide to Implementing the Integrated Public Alert and Warning System (IPAWS), Version 2,
 February, available at the CSEPP Portal, accessed December 2020.
161
 CSEPP, 2015, Test Report: CSEPP Use of Integrated Public Alert and Warning System (IPAWS), April,
 available at https://portalfiles.blob.core.usgovcloudapi.net/training/CSEPP_ipaws_test_report_03apr2015.pdf,

 157

 resulted in more confidence in their systems. . . . During testing, a webinar was
 available to the participants. This webinar provided cameras focused on the
 wireless phones and the television screens. This allowed the users to see their
 messages as they were displayed on WEA-enabled cell phones and television
 screens (full screen and text crawl). While the cameras had to be switched back
 and forth for the users, the users that were able to use the webinar stated that [it]
 was helpful.162

Lessons Learned from Testing

The December 2014 test session at JITC brought to light a number of technical and procedural
issues, which led to failure of initial message deployments and constituted a learning curve for
users over the course of the two-day test session. A wealth of technical details affected the
ultimate performance of the system. Among the issues identified in the test report:
 • Time stamping of messages: a mismatch between the user system’s clock and the
 IPAWS-OPEN system’s clock led to failure of message transmission.
 • In EAS, the system would add a header at the beginning, but sometimes that made the
 message exceed the allowable text limit and it would just truncate the end, meaning the
 public would not get some of the instructions.
 • For the WEA, specifying a polygon (GIS target area for distribution of the message) was
 a source of errors. In one case, an error led to a polygon being defined that was a line, not
 an area. In another, an attempt to draw a distribution area freehand led to a polygon with
 too many circumference points for the system to handle.
 • Data in user message-status displays sometimes did not update until the user hit
 “refresh.”
 • Users were unable to cut and paste text into messages.
 • Users were unable to cancel or update a message.
 • The system did not allow bullet points.
 • Symbols used for bullet points such as “ – “ or “ > ” would be pronounced by voice-
 conversion programs as “dash” or “greater than.”
 • The format of WEA message displays would vary depending on the type of phone
 receiving the message in ways that could lead to confusion or muddle the message.
 • In some cases, messages had to be repeated for each distribution system.

The test report included several lessons learned based on the experience. Vendors of user
software were advised to:

162
 CSEPP, 2015, Test Report: CSEPP Use of Integrated Public Alert and Warning System (IPAWS), April,
 section 3.3, pp. 13-14, available at
 https://portalfiles.blob.core.usgovcloudapi.net/training/CSEPP_ipaws_test_report_03apr2015.pdf, accessed

 158

 • Synchronize time from a master clock or IPAWS-OPEN and not the user’s device to
 ensure the times attached to messages were valid.
 • Provide validation of polygons for IPAWS and the distribution channel before they were
 sent to IPAWS-OPEN.
 • Provide text validation to ensure that there were no improper or invisible characters in the
 message text.
 • Include a message cancel and/or update function.
 • Allow users to see the message status without having to refresh the page.
 • Eliminate the need to create the same message for each dissemination pathway.
 • Allow the user to retry a message, when posting a message failed with HTTP errors
 (e.g., 503 service temporarily unavailable).

In addition, the report emphasized the need for user-friendly instructions and manuals, job aids,
training, and testing. Vendors should have hands-on user initial and refresher training (in person
or webinar). “A user’s ability to practice and test the authoring tools will benefit the deployment
and operationalization of these systems. The more comfortable and confident users are with the
systems the more likely the systems will be used.”163 The report also recommended public
education to familiarize the local public with AWN systems.

User feedback from the test also pointed to the value of the webinar setup and the fact that users
could see live video of the ultimate appearance of their messages on mobile phones or simulated
television screens. This provided a complete, real-time, end-to-end picture for them of how the
system worked to distribute information and how the public would see the messages they sent
out.

Guidance Development

As noted above, the CSEPP IPAWS working group developed guidance for IPAWS
implementation based on the testing process and on all of the information and discussions that
went into planning and preparation for it. The initial guidance was completed in 2015, and an
updated version was published in February 2019.164 The guidance provided background on

As noted in the background section, IPAWS use is growing. More than 1,600 jurisdictions had
implemented IPAWS as of early 2024. As noted in a 2020 GAO report, “All states have at least

For those counties and other tribal, territorial, and local jurisdictions not yet part of the IPAWS
system, the CSEPP IPAWS Implementation Guide could be a helpful resource. The guide
provides useful background, an explanation of the potential benefits, and a suite of tools and
templates for planning, testing, and implementation. It complements the other guidance and tools
available from DHS CISA and the FEMA IPAWS PMO.

The CSEPP IPAWS test session in 2014 indicated that the systems at that time were valuable but
were not yet exactly “plug and play.” Although many of the specific issues and problems that
were encountered have likely been addressed since then, the more general take-home lessons are
still applicable:
 • Careful planning is necessary to address the FEMA IPAWS application process and
 ensure installation of a practical, functional system that enhances local capabilities.
 • Training and testing of users and systems, in conjunction with the vendor, are essential to
 success. A key aspect of testing is the ability for users to create and send messages and
 then monitor, in real time, their distribution and see the end-product public messages.
 • After the system is installed and certified, regular use is important to maintain skills.

The FEMA access approval process essentially requires these measures in order to obtain and
maintain authorization as an alerting authority.

It should be noted that as of January 2021, FEMA was operating the IPAWS test lab in Oxon
Hill, Maryland, renamed the IPAWS Technical Support Services Facility (TSSF). As described
on the FEMA webpage, the facility “is staffed 24/7 with subject-matter experts experienced in
emergency management, public safety communications, public works, and broadcasting, who
assist Alerting Authorities with IPAWS needs. The facility is equipped to provide technical
support with IPAWS activations, alert troubleshooting, WEA tests, monthly proficiency

 162

demonstrations, and other IPAWS-related requests.”170 Authorized alerting authorities must
conduct a monthly test to the Lab to maintain certification.
