4.9 Sampling and Analysis Planning171
An interagency group developed a framework and plan for how to assess the severity and extent
of residual contamination after a hazardous material release.

4.9.1 Summary

A group of local, state, and federal agency personnel and officials worked together over a 4-year
period to develop a detailed framework and plan for assessment of any potential release of
chemical warfare agent (CWA) from chemical munitions in storage. The process revealed issues
that might affect the process, and resources and knowledge that could be brought to bear on
those issues. The resulting documents – a framework and plan for sampling and analysis – can
serve as a resource for any assessment/cleanup of CWA, and more broadly for any high-profile
hazardous materials assessment resulting in air dispersal and potential deposition in the
surrounding area.

4.9.2 Background

The Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program (CSEPP) enhanced and integrated
hazardous materials preparedness on the part of Army chemical-storage installations and their
surrounding communities. The Kentucky installation, Blue Grass Army Depot (BGAD), had
both blister agent (mustard) and nerve agent, configured in a number of different types of
munitions. As the Army was destroying these munitions to eliminate the stockpile, there
remained a small possibility that an accident could release chemical agent, posing a hazard to on-
post workers and nearby residents. Through CSEPP, the Army, FEMA, and local and state
agencies in Kentucky regularly collaborated on planning, training, and exercises to maintain and
improve preparedness against a chemical weapons accident at BGAD.

Most of the CSEP Program focused on preparation for immediate, emergency-phase actions to
reduce the hazard, save lives, and preserve safety and health. However, as part of a
comprehensive strategy, the program also devoted resources to planning and preparedness for
recovery-phase actions that would be taken after an emergency. A key component of recovery
would be assessing residual hazard – where did the released chemical agent go, is it still there,
does it pose any residual health hazard, or can affected areas be released for unrestricted reentry
and use? During the emergency phase, computer dispersion modeling would be used to predict
those geographic areas at risk. An important component of the recovery-phase assessment
process would be to validate those predictions – confirming where the agent did and did not
travel. For areas that were affected by a release, a second question would be whether it posed a
continuing hazard or had dispersed or deteriorated to the point that the areas had become safe
again. To make these determinations, samples would be collected from the potentially affected
areas and analyzed in laboratories to look for chemical agent and/or its breakdown products.

The process after a chemical weapons accident was in many ways similar to any other hazardous
material incident resulting in air dispersal in terms of the types of resources and techniques that
171

 164

would be used to determine potentially affected areas and to collect and analyze samples.
However, given the unusual nature and toxicity of the chemical agents, the process of
determining whether and when an area is safe for reentry after an accident would be unusually
sensitive and subject to public scrutiny.

To provide a sound technical basis for such assessments, CSEPP promulgated a sampling and
analysis guide in 2008, the Final CSEPP Recovery Sampling and Analysis Plan (RSAP) Protocol
for Chemical Warfare Agent Accidents/Incidents. The 2008 protocol was state of the art at the
time, laying out a logical approach to determining areas impacted by a chemical agent release.
By 2015, however, a number of factors indicated it was time for an update. First, the number of
chemical storage facilities had decreased from the original eight to two, allowing an individual
focus on each remaining facility’s situation. Second, there were many new resources available to
plan a more efficient and effective sampling and analysis program. These included updated
regulations, guidance documents, plume models, sample design tools, field and analytical
methods, remote sensing technologies, and federal and state resources. The Kentucky CSEPP
community, noting these changes, decided to develop an updated sampling and analysis
framework tailored to their situation.

This lesson documents the process of developing the Kentucky-specific sampling and analysis
framework, and a subsequent process to develop a sampling and analysis implementation plan.

4.9.3 Issue

In light of current plans, policies, regulations, guidance, and available resources, the issue is how
the community can be best prepared to quickly and accurately assess the potential for residual
health hazards after a chemical weapons accident.

4.9.4 Solution

To address this issue, the Kentucky CSEPP community developed a Kentucky-specific recovery
sampling and analysis framework (RSAF) and sampling and analysis plan (SAP).

The initial working draft for the RSAF took approximately 18 months to develop through a
cooperative effort led by a Kentucky CSEPP community working group and supported by
subject matter experts from Argonne National Laboratory (Argonne).

The working group held four sessions to present and discuss issues and develop a path forward;
it then incorporated input from these sessions into an initial draft RSAF. The draft RSAF was
then reviewed in a series of conference calls and at a workshop held in Madison County,
Kentucky, in January 2018. The final RSAF, completed in November 2018, reflected the
judgment and conclusions of the working group as expressed in the review sessions, calls, and
workshop. It incorporated the unique aspects of BGAD’s storage operation, the specific chemical
agents present at BGAD, the environment and climate of the Blue Grass area, and the
community’s input and preferences.

 165

Upon completion of the RSAF, the working group developed the SAP to guide implementation
of the RSAF, including incident command structure and resources, plus accompanying support
functions such as security, medical surveillance, and public information. The final Sampling and
Analysis Plan was signed off in May 2019.

The following material details the RSAF and SAP and their development processes.

RSAF

Development Process

As with any emergency planning process, stakeholder involvement was key to developing a
useful product that had buy-in from the community.

Beginning in 2015, the Blue Grass CSEPP community began reviewing the need for an updated
and site-specific guide for recovery sampling and analysis. They determined that developing a
new, site-specific document was in order. The community established a recovery sampling and
analysis working group including representatives of local (county) emergency management, state
emergency management, state environmental and public health agencies, federal agencies (U.S.
Environmental Protection Agency [EPA] and FEMA), and the U.S. Army. Inclusion of these
stakeholders at all levels was essential to laying the foundations for interagency cooperation and
planning. It also was important to have participants with technical backgrounds, familiar with
hazardous material assessment technologies, together with emergency managers who were
familiar with the public pressure and information needs that would be present in a post-
emergency setting.

A contract support organization (Argonne) collected references and developed an initial draft
framework along with a list of issues for consideration by the stakeholders. A series of four
working group meetings were held over a 6-month period in 2016 and 2017 to discuss and
resolve different technical aspects of the framework, termed “big picture issues” (BPIs). More
than 30 BPIs in all were discussed, touching on topics such as dispersion modeling, action levels,
methods for selecting sampling points, sample screening processes, quality control, decision
support, analytical laboratory resources, and many other technical aspects of the assessment.

Based on the input collected during the BPI sessions, a working draft document was completed
and circulated to the working group for review. Then a workshop was held to go over the draft in
early 2018. The workshop identified 16 items for follow-up in terms of refining or adding to the
draft RSAF. These items were addressed in a revised draft and further reviewed by the working
group. In addition, the draft document was circulated to outside experts from the EPA and the
Army Public Health Center (APHC) for external review. The RSAF was finalized in late 2018.

 166

Technical and Tactical Considerations

Technical considerations for sampling and analysis of CWAs are similar to those for any other
hazardous material. The basic steps of modeling, selecting a grid for sampling, collection of
samples from various media (soil, vegetation, water), sample handling, laboratory analysis, and
documentation of results are all similar to other hazardous material assessments that are
performed frequently at chemical spill sites. However, the nature of the CWAs adds certain
complications. First, and probably most significant, is the acutely toxic nature of the chemical
agents. Many conventional hazardous substance removal or remedial operations involve
chemicals that pose a chronic hazard, rather than the acute hazard presented by CWA. The
possible presence of chemical agents affects many different aspects of site characterization —
everything from the personal protective equipment (PPE) used by field sampling teams to the
types of laboratories that are qualified to perform sample analyses. In addition, because the
chemical agents are surety materials, many different requirements apply, ranging from surety and
security to transportation of samples. Most sampling and analytical technologies for conventional
hazardous substance constituents also apply to the agents and degradation products. There are,
however, specialized procedures for air monitoring, sample screening, sampling, and analysis.
Also, hazard analysts would almost certainly be looking for very small concentrations (of the
warfare agents or their chemical breakdown products), and the technical limits of detection could
become a factor in the assessment.

In addition to the potential technical issues, there may be tactical complications with assessing a
CWA release. The assessment process would be taking place in a charged atmosphere of public
impact and scrutiny. The public (and by extension, elected officials and the emergency managers
who work for them) would want to be very sure that the affected area is safe, but at the same
time there would be pressure to get life back to normal as soon as possible. There could be
considerable tension between the conflicting goals of certainty, which requires a more extensive
and lengthy assessment process, and speed of recovery.

Contents of the RSAF

The final Recovery Sampling and Analysis Framework provided background information
including the governmental, legal, and regulatory context for recovery, and considered the
geography, infrastructure, demographics, and environment of the area. It then provided detailed
considerations for sampling and analysis including developing a conceptual site model, data
units, data quality strategies and objectives, sampling methods and design, available technical
resources, team organization, support functions, field and laboratory analytical methods, data
deliverables, decision support, and documentation/reporting requirements.

A concise post-event step-by-step outline provided guidance on what actions to take and in what
order after an actual or suspected release of chemical agent.

The RSAF was supplemented with references and appendices providing technical data on
CWAs, example data logging forms, and other job aids for recovery sampling and analysis.

 167

SAP

Development Process

During the RSAF development process, the Kentucky CSEPP community determined that in
addition to a detailed technical reference, emergency managers and public officials needed a
compact plan that would address the sampling and analysis implementation process, including
roles and responsibilities, command structure, resources, key decision points, and ancillary
functions such as medical support, security, and public information.

The working group developed a draft SAP to implement the RSAF. A high-level group of
community emergency managers and public officials then met for a week-long workshop in
Frankfort, Kentucky in December 2018 to review the draft and finalize content for the plan. The
group reviewed and revised the plan section by section, and developed appendices to the plan
with additional supporting information.

Contents

The SAP was developed to provide practical guidance for implementing the sampling and
analysis process. It addressed a number of situational and “envelope” issues that would need to
be resolved in order to accomplish the technically demanding process of assessing the hazard and
supporting decisions on reentry and reuse. Among other things, the SAP addressed:
 • Situation and assumptions – a summary of the context that would be expected in the
 aftermath of a chemical agent event.
 • Organization – including direction and control, support agencies, unified command, and
 incident command structure. Decisions regarding public protective actions would be the
 responsibility of local public officials. Hazard assessment to support such decisions
 would be led by the Army under the National Contingency Plan, with support and
 concurrence from many other federal, state, and local agencies and support contractors.
 Unified command and an integrated multi-agency incident command structure would
 help ensure coordination and efficiency of effort.
 • Concept of operations – including roles and responsibilities of the Army, EPA, and key
 state and local agencies. This section addressed priorities for monitoring and sampling
 and summarized the step-by-step process described in the RSAF for implementing the
 assessment. It also addressed timelines for implementation and the trade-off of time vs.
 certainty.
 • Resources – this section addressed the two key technical resources: sampling teams and
 analytical laboratories. It also addressed supporting logistics including staging,
 equipment, and supplies.
 • Control of hazard area – this section briefly addressed responsibilities for controlling
 access to affected areas. This would be determined at the time based on the best available
 information and estimates as to degree and location of any residual hazards.

 168

 • Exposure/ingestion pathway protection – this section addressed authorities and potential
 protective measures to prevent chemical agent exposure through ingestion of
 contaminated food or water.
 • Medical services – medical services during reentry and recovery might include managing
 human or animal remains, medical screening and other services to the public, and support
 to emergency workers who might be at risk of exposure to CWA in the environment.
 • Public information – joint public information operations would be established during the
 emergency phase of the response and would be continued during recovery operations.
 The public would be kept apprised of the progress of the assessment and associated issues
 of concern.

The SAP was supplemented with references, draft ICS forms, a table of anticipated public
information activities, a resource list, and an Execution Matrix172 that provided an overview of
anticipated actions by operational period.

4.9.5 Potential Application

The processes and documents summarized in this lesson learned have two types of potential
application. First, they illustrate some of the potential complications and issues associated with
assessment of a CWA release. The CSEPP-developed documents from the Kentucky community
provide a guide for systematic planning and execution of such an assessment.

During the Army’s storage and subsequent destruction of its stockpile of chemical weapons, no
release occurred that would require the sort of assessment contemplated here. However, there
have been a few instances of non-stockpile chemical weapons being found outside of controlled
areas that required an assessment of potential contamination followed by cleanup. Two examples
are the discovery of older (World War I–era) weapons at a construction site in Spring Valley,
District of Columbia, in 1993, and the accidental retrieval of discarded mustard-containing
munitions by a fishing vessel operating off Long Island, New York, in 2010. Discovery of other
discarded chemical munitions is possible in the future (both in the U.S. and in other parts of the
world). In addition, the guidance developed during this project could be applied in the event that
chemical agent is released in a terrorist attack, as happened in Tokyo in 1995.

Second, the discussion and documents developed for this project illustrate a thorough, systematic
process for assessing any sort of hazardous material air release and potential subsequent
deposition. The referenced documents indicate both the types of issues that may arise (e.g.,
statistical grid samples versus judgmental sampling locations; the inherent tension of certainty
versus timeliness) and the kinds of ancillary functions that should be considered and planned as
part of the overall effort (e.g., security, medical support, public information). Particularly in
high-profile situations where there is intense public interest, these types of considerations could
become very important to the success of assessment and subsequent cleanup work.

 See Section 4.2 of this document.

4.10 On-Scene Coordinator Training173
CSEPP developed training for Army installation personnel who would fill the position of On-
Scene Coordinator (OSC) in the event of a spill of chemical agent (or other hazardous substance)
on-post.

4.10.1 Summary

To meet federal and Army requirements, and to better serve chemical installations and their
communities, CSEPP developed training for installation personnel who would fill the position of
OSC in the event of a spill of chemical agent (or other hazardous substance) on-post. The OSC
would direct assessment and cleanup, if needed, of any such spill, in coordination with other
federal agencies and state and local jurisdictions.

4.10.2 Background

Army installations were responsible for preparedness and response to hazardous materials spills
that occur on-post as required by federal law and regulation. Congress established requirements
and structures for hazardous materials emergency preparedness and response through the
Comprehensive Environmental Response, Compensation, and Liability Act (CERCLA), which
applies to all federal agencies as well as the private sector. Regulations implementing CERCLA
and the Oil Pollution Act, issued by the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) and
U.S. Coast Guard (USCG), were commonly known as the National Contingency Plan (NCP).
The President, via executive order (EO) 12580, delegated responsibility for implementing
CERCLA and the NCP to the U.S. Department of Defense (DoD) and the U.S. Department of
Energy (DOE) for any releases or potential releases on or from a DoD or DOE facility or vessel.

Within DoD, responsibilities for spill contingency planning and response were delegated to the
constituent armed services, and within those services further delegated to component commands.
In the Army, individual installation commanders were primarily responsible for this function
(Figure 4.10-1 for the sequence of delegations). Commanders were responsible for filling
(directly or through delegation) the lead role in spill response, referred to as the on-scene
coordinator (OSC). The commander was responsible for designating an OSC.

The Army stored a particular type of hazardous material – chemical weapons – at certain Army
installations. Designed to incapacitate and kill on the battlefield, these chemicals were as toxic as
human ingenuity was able to devise. At the installations where they were stored, spill response
was particularly important174 and received considerable attention through a dedicated program.

173
 Lesson written in 2020.
174
 Originally, there were eight installations with chemical weapons storage in the continental U.S. Pursuant to
 treaty obligation, beginning in the late 1980s DoD destroyed these stockpiles through incineration and chemical
 neutralization. Of the original eight sites, by 2013 only two were left, and destruction was completed at the
 remaining sites by the end of 2023.

 171

 Figure 4.10-1 Delegation of Responsibility for Spill Contingency Planning and Response

The Army had an excellent record of storing and managing these toxic materials safely. Even
before CERCLA was enacted in 1980, the Army had a program tailored to ensure safe handling
of chemical weapons and respond to any accidents or incidents involving them, including
research, guidance, trained personnel, and specialized equipment for detecting and responding to
any leaks. The Army maintained these capabilities at each chemical weapons storage installation
and on a national level reporting to higher headquarters. The Army also coordinated with the
Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA) and state and local jurisdictions to enhance
emergency preparedness in the communities surrounding chemical-storage installations through
the Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program (CSEPP).

As the chemical stockpile was reduced through destruction, and as spill response planning
evolved as a discipline and capability, the Army moved to integrate chemical weapons-specific
response capabilities into its more general framework for hazardous materials response.
Guidance, standards, and training specific to handling of the chemical munitions was maintained,
but spill response was increasingly integrated into more generalized hazardous materials
response guidance and structures. As part of this process, the role of the OSC at chemical
installations acquired increased importance and attention. Among other things, Army CSEPP
leadership determined that designated OSCs should receive training specific to that role.

Initially, OSC training was obtained through USCG, which had already developed training for
USCG personnel (OSC is a career-track position at USCG). A number of chemical-installation
commanders and other personnel attended USCG training. However, the USCG course was not
ideally suited to Army OSCs as it was mainly oriented to maritime and waterway spill response
and was intended for personnel who would become full-time OSCs. In addition, access to the
USCG training was limited, and over time it became harder to get slots in the available courses.

 172

Eventually CSEPP leadership determined that an in-house, chemical installation-specific training
course would better serve those installations and their surrounding communities.

4.10.3 Issue

How to prepare installation commanders and other personnel who might fulfill or oversee the
position of OSC for those specific duties?

4.10.4 Solution

CSEPP personnel developed and refined a 5-day training course specifically tailored to the
execution of OSC duties at chemical installations. CSEPP borrowed from USCG and EPA
training to the extent possible, but also developed material specifically designed to serve the
needs of an Army installation OSC appointee. Someone in that position would not be regularly
executing OSC duties as part of their normal daily routine but might be called upon to do so in
the event of a spill. Therefore, they would need a basic grounding in OSC authorities, an
overview of the major responsibilities of an OSC, and guidance on how to obtain expert
assistance when needed.

Material was developed to familiarize trainees with the legal, regulatory, and doctrinal
background of an Army OSC role. For expertise on how to conduct spill response, course
designers sought out EPA and USCG personnel with deep experience in executing OSC duties.
They helped design course materials and taught those skills and knowledge. Course designers
also included instruction on public information aspects of the role, as OSCs would become the
public face of a cleanup and must be able to communicate information to the public as to the
processes, decisions, schedules, and actions being taken to ensure public safety and restore
affected areas.

The course was developed, implemented, and then refined based on input from the pilot offering.
Based on early input, course designers added more in the way of case studies and in-class
exercises to supplement the information presented through presentations and lectures.

Course content included:
 • Regulatory and statutory authorities,
 • National Response System,
 • Roles of the regional response teams (RRTs) and national response team (NRT),
 • Who is in charge? (roles of Army installation, other federal agencies, state and local
 authorities),
 • Incident assessment,
 • Site strategy model (building situational awareness and developing strategies to address
 the situation),
 • Documentation responsibilities,

 173

 • Funding, contracting, cost recovery, claims, and reimbursement,
 • Transitioning from removal to remediation,
 • Public information and communications, and
 • The OSC as a spokesperson.

The course described specific past cleanups having particular relevance, and includes a practical
exercise where trainees applied their knowledge to a specific spill scenario. Trainees developed
proposed actions, described how unified command would be set up, and suggested how
resources would be obtained to address the assessment and cleanup.

The CSEPP-developed OSC training course was offered four times and trained a total of
60 students, including commanders, deputy commanders, executive agents, environmental
officers, and operations officers. The students were mainly from chemical installations but also
included personnel from several other Army Materiel Command (AMC) and Joint Munitions
Command (JMC) installations, plus AMC and JMC headquarters. In addition, trainees included
six Navy personnel, three industry and two state environmental response personnel.

4.10.5 Potential Application

The OSC training course was developed for Army chemical installations where there was a
particular emphasis on hazardous material preparedness, and a program (CSEPP) that provided
support for planning, training, and exercises. It should be noted, however, that the need (and
requirements) for spill contingency preparedness extends beyond chemical installations to other
Army installations, DoD facilities, and federal facilities in general.

Army Regulation (AR) 200-1 (Environmental Protection and Enhancement), paragraph 11-4(b)
and DA Pam 525-27 (Army Emergency Management Program) paragraph 1-15.g(6) provided for
designation of an OSC to execute responsibilities under the NCP for each installation. AR 200-1
further specified that the OSC be “qualified” but did not define that term. Position-specific
training helped ensure that this position was filled by a qualified person and in a documented
fashion. The Army’s 80 or so substantial installations within the U.S. would be candidates for
this type of training.

Beyond the Army, as of this writing (January 2020) other DoD components along with DOE are
responsible for implementation of the NCP for spills on or from their facilities and vessels. Thus,
these departments and agencies should have qualified OSCs ready to step into that role if needed.
Standards and training for OSC qualification would be a component of meeting those
responsibilities.

4.11 Using a Distributed EOC175
When a pandemic created a need for social distancing, CSEPP staff adjusted operations using
information technology (IT) to allow close coordination of response operations without requiring
the physical proximity of staff.

4.11.1 Summary

When the Covid-19 pandemic struck the U.S., the emergency management community was
challenged to effectively respond, both to the pandemic and to all other types of emergencies and
disasters, while protecting the safety of response staff. One challenge was the use of Emergency
Operations Centers (EOCs) to coordinate response; staff in an EOC were at risk of disease
transmission due to working in close proximity. This lesson describes a process of planning and
executing adjustments to operations, using information technology to allow close coordination of
response operations while minimizing the risk to staff.

4.11.2 Background

During 2020–2022, the Covid-19 pandemic added an element of danger to any indoor gathering
of people. Initially, social distancing (physical separation and wearing masks) was the only way
of reducing transmission of the deadly disease. After widespread deployment of effective
vaccines, the consequences of exposure to the disease were reduced but not eliminated for most
people, but social distancing was still important to reduce transmission and avoid spread of the
disease to more vulnerable individuals.

The pandemic challenged emergency management agencies to respond effectively while limiting
risk to response staff. As noted by the Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA)
Administrator in May 2021, “For the past year, the emergency management community at all
levels of government—federal, state, local, tribal, and territorial—has faced historic challenges
as we adapted to performing our critical missions in the novel coronavirus (COVID-19)
pandemic environment.”176

Among many other issues, the pandemic made it problematic to gather emergency management
staff into EOCs. EOCs have long been relied upon to coordinate response operations during an
emergency. However, staff gathered in an EOC were at risk of disease transmission; it is difficult
to maintain social distancing in a crowded room. But the presence of the pandemic did not slow
the pace of natural and technological disasters requiring emergency response. Instead, the
pandemic necessitated emergency operations, and many EOCs were opened to coordinate
pandemic response.177
175
 Lesson written in 2022.
176
 FEMA, 2021, Covid-19 Pandemic Operational Guidance, All-Hazards Incident Response and Recovery, May,
 p. 1.
177
 See, for example, Ketchikan joint media release on Covid-19, accessed August 2025 at
 https://borough.ketchikan.ak.us/CivicAlerts.aspx?AID=138&ARC=240; activation of Malibu EOC for Covid
 response, https://www.malibucity.org/DocumentCenter/View/26271/Coronavirus-incident-malibu-EOC-weekly-
 update-42020. Pueblo County issued an emergency declaration on March 13, 2020. The 2021 Pueblo

 176

This lesson describes a process of preparing to manage emergency operations while minimizing
the need for physical gathering of staff. It emphasizes analyzing EOC functions and applying a
distributed teams concept to carry out those functions, supported by widely available, off-the-
shelf tools for communication and data sharing.

EOC Functions

Current research suggests that EOCs serve as coordination and support centers for incident site
responders and that the EOC staff make operational and strategic decisions in a high-stakes,
time-pressured, and resource-constrained environment.178 Incident management doctrine
describes EOC support functions as:
 • Collecting, analyzing, and sharing information;
 • Supporting resource needs and requests, including allocation and tracking;
 • Coordinating plans and determining current and future needs; and
 • Providing coordination and policy direction.179

Disaster operations research acknowledges six somewhat similar functions, viewing EOCs
broadly as communications, command and control, and coordination hubs:
 • Coordination. Actions directed at relating organization to one another and relating
 organization capabilities to disaster demands.
 • Policymaking. Tasks involving decision-making with regards to the overall response.
 • Operations. Tasks that specifically address disaster demands, not directed at coordination
 or other response needs.
 • Information gathering. Tasks directed at efforts to determine the nature and extent of
 disaster conditions.
 • Dispersal of public information. Tasks directed at informing the media and public.
 • Hosting visitors. Tasks necessary to handle convergence of VIPs and others on the
 EOC.180

 Community exercise AAR noted that the Colorado Department of Homeland Security and Emergency
 Management (CDHSEM) EOC had been open for some time to support Covid-19 response – at the start of the
 exercise the EOC was in its 431st operational period at Level 1. The Kentucky state EOC likewise was activated
 for Covid-19 response early on (March 2020) per an emergency declaration by the Governor. (See WHAS11,
 “What does it mean when the Governor declares a state of emergency?,” available at
 https://www.whas11.com/article/news/local/kentucky-state-of-emergency-meaning/417-4537cefd-27b9-41ac-
    882f-50895d26e5de, accessed December 2021.)

 Making in Emergency Operations Centres,” International Journal of Mass Emergencies and Disasters, 32(3),
    484–507, among others.

 University of Delaware Disaster Research Center. He derived the six EOC functions described here from more
 than 100 field studies conducted between 1963 and 1973.

Distributed Teams

Distributed teams are not new. O’Duinn181 suggests that distributed teams have existed for
centuries and have become more prevalent due to rapid changes in technology — land-line
telephones, fax machines, email, cell phones, instant messaging, and mass collaboration and
communication software — that allow people to communicate with others across geographic and
temporal separations. Distributed teams go by several familiar names: remote workforce, virtual
team, and even telecommuting. Distributed teams also appear in traditional work settings where
team members may work in adjacent offices, down the hall, or in different buildings from one
another.

What is a distributed team? Simply, it is a group of people separated by geographic location who
use combinations of telecommunications and information technologies to complete tasks.182
Being on a distributed team means that one may work alone or in the same location as some team
members, but away from other team members. Distributed teams may be all local, with some
members in other nearby offices or buildings, or at home; or may be more widely spread with
members in other jurisdictions or organizations across the country.

Using distributed teams potentially allows for more flexibility and a larger talent pool because
there are few geographic limitations and no need to wait for people to travel to a local EOC.

A common distributed-team concept for complex emergencies involves a central EOC with
satellite work centers in other locations. Administrative staff and organizational leadership might
be housed in the EOC, whereas technical and service work groups are in various other buildings.

There may also be a work team where everyone works in the same office, except for a few select
people working remotely because of their team role. These are the people regularly away as
investigators, trainers, inspectors, and such – those who only use the office as a touch point, if at
all, when doing their tasks.

Finally, and of particular interest during periods of enforced separation such as during COVID-
19 restrictions, team members may work in a no-office model where everyone works remotely. It
is the opposite of everyone at one location, but with the same level of cooperation through
communication and collaboration technologies.

These models can be mixed and matched as needed to best align with a given jurisdiction’s
needs, capabilities, and circumstances, with one or more central locations and some or all
emergency managers and support operating from individual remote locations (home or remote
office).

181
 O’Duinn, J., 2018, Distributed Teams: The Art and Practice of Working Together when Physically Apart,
 Middletown, Delaware: Release Mechanix, LLC.
182
 Bell, B. S., and S.W. J. Kozlowski, 2002, “A Typology of Virtual Teams: Implications for Effective
 Leadership,” Group and Organization Management, 27(1), 14-49.

 178

4.11.3 Issue

How can emergency response operations be effectively managed while minimizing the need for
physical gathering of management staff?

4.11.4 Solution

To start, it is important to acknowledge why physical EOCs and other nodes in the Incident
Command System (ICS) evolved: to facilitate communication and coordination among key
managers and teams. Being gathered in a room allows instant communication and information
sharing. A common operating picture can be developed through displays and periodic briefings
in the facility. And apart from formal briefings, it is enormously convenient to just step over to
someone else’s workstation to ask a question, share information, or give directions.

Planning and preparing for distributed operations involve:
 • Analyzing the organization’s system and team structure to identify the main streams of
 coordination and communication. These, in turn, suggest what operations and which
 personnel can participate remotely, and what are the key elements of coordination that
 may have to be retained in a traditional face-to-face format.
 • Identifying supporting technologies to allow effective coordination among physically
 separated teams and staff.
 • Ensuring that team members have the appropriate hardware and peripherals to support
 use of the chosen platforms. In addition, continual access to IT support should be
 provided to address any glitches or questions that arise as response staff use systems that
 may not be part of their daily routine.
 • Implementing and practicing use of the chosen platforms to identify and correct revealed
 issues.

These elements are described in further detail below. Note the descriptions are illustrated with
examples of current, common, off-the-shelf applications and how they might be used; these
should be treated as examples and do not represent an endorsement of any particular product.

Functional and Team Structure Analysis

Analyzing EOC core functions is a key part of planning for a distancing environment. The goal
is to support direction, control, and coordination for response to complex emergencies using
technology and distributed teams. What functions can successfully be performed remotely, and
what (and who) needs to be collocated in an operations center?

The incident command group (Commander; operations, planning, logistics, and
administration/finance coordination section chiefs; and special staff leaders) should meet and
identify which staff positions are so critical to decision-making that their face-to-face presence is
required in the EOC. They should draw on their collective experience and perhaps review plans
and job aids to search for instances where a group must collaborate very closely. Small

 179

functional teams in Operations or Planning might need to be collocated, although not necessarily
in the same place as each other or the command group.

Another factor to consider when determining who reports physically to EOCs is the operations
room’s size and floor plan. Office and conference room physical distancing constraints
(e.g., maintain six-foot separation, staff not facing each other at workstations during COVID-19
restrictions) may drive the maximum number of staff allowed in the facility — which might
mean that some critical staff will have to work from distant locations. The final consideration is
the collaboration and communications technologies available to EOC staff working from a
distance (discussed in the next section). An illustration of a distributed-team concept for
operations is presented in Table 4.11-1.

Planning for team structure and locations should also include the potential for outside aid. There
may be a need to accommodate personnel from mutual aid organizations or from state or federal
assistance. Can those personnel coordinate from their own locations or do they need to be
physically present in the host EOC?

Identify Supporting Technologies

Once the scene is set in terms of expected centralized and remote functions and personnel,
technologies should be evaluated to support that team structure. Selected technologies must
support both normal and distance-constrained operations. Finally, selected technologies must be
able to support an event’s entire life cycle from preparedness and initial response through
recovery.

The term “unified communications and collaboration” is often used in the IT industry to describe
a set of tools for organizational communication, housed or coordinated through a single IT
application. The general concept is to have an integrated platform to support a variety of
functions such as voice communications, conferencing, email, voice mail, instant messaging,
calendars, and scheduling. 183 There are at present multiple commercial packages and services
available that provide a combination of functions along those lines, including Zoom, WebEx, and
Adobe Connect, among others.

The full range of options should be explored. This lesson illustrates the technology evaluation
process by describing two potential options: Microsoft Teams and WebEOC. This discussion
does not constitute an endorsement of these particular products – rather, they are described to
illustrate some features that might be evaluated and compared to other available options.

Microsoft Teams. Microsoft (MS) Teams is a unified collaboration application that provides
instant messaging (chat), video calling, virtual meeting, screen sharing, document collaboration,
and document storage capabilities to users.

Through MS Teams, users have access to everyday productivity applications (Excel, PowerPoint,
Word), document storage (SharePoint, OneNote), integration with Outlook email, and newer

183
 See, for example, https://www.pgi.com/resources/articles/what-is-unified-communications-and-collaboration/.
 See also Wikipedia, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unified_communications.

 180

functions (Planner, Wiki, Shifts, Stream). Depending on the options selected, MS Teams may
also allow guest members and have additional functions such as MS Whiteboard and Teams Live
(a virtual moderated meeting function that allows up to 10,000 viewers).184

A brief summary of MS Teams features and their possible use in a distributed EOC is presented
in Table 4.11-2.

WebEOC. WebEOC is a commercial off-the-shelf (COTS), web-based crisis management
application designed to provide EOCs, field staff, and higher HQs with a common operating
picture (COP) of the event or incident to which they are responding. WebEOC was one of the
first web-enabled, COTS information management systems developed specifically for
emergency management. Typical of a web-based product, WebEOC makes crisis information
immediately and universally available to authorized users everywhere. WebEOC is widely used
by local, state, and federal emergency management organizations.

WebEOC uses boards (essentially, virtual status boards) and forms (e.g., ICS forms) to manage
and share real-time incident information among authorized users in a system and between linked
systems. Every board has two types of views: input and display. Data are entered and updated in
the input view and displayed in the display view. With WebEOC, users enter information
electronically into boards and forms, allowing everyone logged into WebEOC to see this
information instantly. WebEOC helps users build reports, request assistance, deploy resources,
store documents, and produce maps.185 The WebEOC website186 also claims integration with MS
Teams, which would add mass communications and collaboration capabilities that are not
inherent in the WebEOC application.

Ensure Team Members Have Necessary Hardware and Peripherals

Using mass collaboration and communication software to support distributed team interactions
requires good internet connections, PC microphones and speakers or headsets, and web cameras.

In most situations, team members should use web cameras, microphones, and speakers
associated with a desktop or laptop computer. Doing so avoids technology failures typically
associated with location-specific shared systems. Hardware and software are under the team
members’ control which, for the most part, removes technology-caused surprises. Thus, the same
software and same or similar hardware get used whether team members are joining the event
from their office, home, or a hotel room when traveling.

Most laptops come with integrated cameras, microphones, and speakers. It will be necessary to
coordinate use of these features with the organization’s cybersecurity personnel and policies. Are
any of these integrated systems inactivated by policy? It may be possible to negotiate their
activation because of critical mission requirements.

184
 Not all MS Teams deployments are the same. License type and internal controls within specific deployments
 determine what features are available to users.
185
 From the WebEOC User Guide, available at https://confluence.juvare.com/display/PKC/WebEOC.
186
 See https://www.juvare.com/webeoc/.

 181

If resources are available, from a functional standpoint it is always preferable to use external
(peripheral) hardware such as headsets, web cameras with integrated microphones, and external
speakers. Computers’ integrated microphones and speakers do not process crossover talk or
background noise well, whereas peripheral devices often provide noise filtering and speaker-
microphone echo controls. Simple and inexpensive earbud-style headphones work well; over-ear
headphone sets are better for long-duration (multiple-hours) wear. Similarly, peripheral web
cameras often offer improved video and come with camera-control software that allows for pan,
zoom, tilt, autofocus, and auto-white balance.

Ensure that Tech Support is Available during Emergency Response

As with any information-sharing system, there is a learning curve as users become familiar with
it. Integrating these systems into daily work activities will help staff become acclimated to the IT
environment and learn how the various functions are performed. However, in an emergency
response situation, it is likely that staff will be using the system in different ways, new situations
will be encountered, new staff brought in, and even new system issues arise as a result of much-
heavier-than-normal use. For all of these reasons, live tech support is an important component of
the distributed EOC concept. In the CSEP Program, the importance of having quickly available
tech support on hand was demonstrated and observed many times during exercises, even with a
more normal EOC staffing regime.

Implement and Practice

A concept for using MS Teams to operate a distributed EOC follows. Again, this particular
commercial option is used for illustrative purposes and is not an endorsement.
 1. Establish a Private Team for the EOC. Add all EOC staff as members of the private team.
 When establishing the team, set member and guest permissions.
 2. Set up channels for the Command Staff and each EOC section.
 a. When the team is established, the General channel is automatically included.
 Announcements to the entire team are made in this channel under the Posts tab.
 Similarly, references, forms, and other documents that should be available to the
 entire team would be uploaded under the Files tab. Use folders to group similar
 files, such as placing digital ICS forms in an “ICS Forms” file.
 b. Assign team members to the EOC command group or section channels. Channels
 provide the workspace for the individual coordination sections.
 c. Each channel has its own Posts, Files, and access to other Teams apps, allowing
 for internal section collaborations, tasking, and scheduling.
 3. Use Teams for day-to-day activities. For example, use Teams’ video-meeting and screen-
 sharing capabilities to conduct the Command Update Brief; use the Shifts app to have
 visibility on work crew and guard schedules. The goal is to make Teams part of the
 regular work routine and not something used only for special events – the more the
 application is used, the more skilled and secure the staff members become with its
 functions.

 182

 4. Conduct EOC operations during exercises using Teams. Determine which standard
 operating procedures or EOC process might need adaptation to reflect Teams capabilities.
 For example, determine what changes to the incident planning processes may be needed
 to accommodate use of Teams. Validate that sufficient bandwidth is available to conduct
 distributed EOC operations with Teams.
 5. Determine whether additional Teams features, such as Teams Meeting Rooms and Teams
 Whiteboard, are needed for distributed EOC functionality.

Application in the CSEP Program

In the CSEP Program, EOC staffing was adapted to minimize risk to staff both on-post and off-
post. Pueblo Chemical Depot (PCD) used a dispersed EOC concept as discussed in Argonne’s
“Using Distributed Management Systems for Direction, Control, and Coordination of Chemical
Accident or Incident Response Activities (CAIRA)” White Paper published in November 2020
(see 4.11.6, References).

Pueblo County declared a state of emergency as soon as the first Covid-19 case was detected in
the County, and the County went into emergency operations mode. Pueblo County Sheriff’s
Office Emergency Services Bureau (PCSOESB) staff came into work but maintained social
distance through a reduction of workstations in the EOC and dispersal to other offices, mostly in
the same building. Masks were worn indoors. Briefings were held through a remote conferencing
application (GoToMeeting) at first daily, then weekly, and then biweekly as the situation
stabilized. The briefings included representatives of health departments, schools, utilities,
hospitals, nursing homes, County commissioners, local law enforcement, and the Pueblo Mayor,
in addition to PCSOESB staff.187

During the 2021 Pueblo Community CSEPP exercise, the on-post PCD command group, hazard
analysts, and public affairs section were in the EOC. The security and planning sections were
dispersed to other buildings. Audio conference lines and MS Teams were used for
communications, briefings, coordination of operations, and situational awareness. At Pueblo
County, when the EOC was upgraded to a full Level 1 posture for the exercise, only the
Emergency Support Function (ESF) 8 coordinator and a geographic information systems (GIS)
specialist reported to the EOC; other support functions were executed from staff home offices.
Staff responding to the EOC received a temperature check and completed a health symptoms
questionnaire. Masks were worn in the EOC.188

Blue Grass Army Depot (BGAD) used a slightly different approach. Some EOC staff worked
from their normal location and had a liaison in the EOC. Other staff elements were dispersed into
offices in the EOC building. The number of people in the EOC was reduced by about 50%
because many of the sections had one person rather than having the “night shift” person present
for training.189

187
 Interview with Lisa Shorter, PCSOESB CSEPP Coordinator, December 21, 2021.
188
 U.S. Army and FEMA, 2021, Pueblo Community CSEPP Exercise 2021, Final AAR, July 7, pp. 3-24.
189
 Interview with Robert Sharp, Argonne National Laboratory, November 10, 2021.

 183

These measures increased the social distancing and safety of staff and allowed effective EOC
operations to continue under pandemic conditions.

4.11.5 Potential Application

Planning for remote operations should be considered for any jurisdiction or other organization
that may respond to complex emergencies. The Covid-19 pandemic forced emergency
management organizations to adapt to minimize risk to personnel and the public they serve.
Implementing a system such as described here should help minimize that risk through social
distancing, while preserving the effectiveness of response operations and coordination.

It should be noted that infectious disease episodes have become increasingly common in the
current connected world; before Covid-19, there were numerous flu epidemics and SARS. So
even after Covid-19 has faded as a public health menace, there will likely be more diseases of
concern that require social distancing. We can hope that the scientific methods and protocols that
allowed rapid vaccine development for Covid-19 will also be available for future pandemics, but
that is not certain.

The pandemic highlighted the advantage of being able to coordinate emergency operations
without bringing emergency managers into close proximity. But aside from disease concerns,
gathering a group of emergency managers in one place always has some drawbacks in terms of
travel, delay, and the sufficiency of facilities to support the required staffing level. The
emergency that occasioned the response – for example, a flood, wildfire, or severe weather –
may also make it difficult for response staff to travel to a central point in a timely fashion. And a
disaster may strain the capacity of local or state facilities to accommodate the number of staff
required to analyze, monitor, coordinate, and maintain the safety of response operations.
Organizations may find that technology-assisted remote coordination is a better, faster, cheaper
way to operate under a variety of circumstances.

4.12 Emergency Preparedness during Redevelopment Transition191
The Colorado Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program (CSEPP) community held a
series of meetings and tabletop exercises to work out how to maintain emergency readiness as
portions of the site were transferred to the reuse authority and leased to private parties.

4.12.1 Summary

During ongoing chemical weapon destruction operations, the Colorado CSEPP community was
preparing for the eventual transfer of Pueblo Chemical Depot (PCD) from the U.S. Army to
PuebloPlex, a redevelopment authority established by the State of Colorado and appointed to
privatize the 23,000 acres of PCD in Pueblo, Colorado.

Portions of PCD were being leased to civilian users while other portions were still being used for
chemical weapons storage and demilitarization. The transfer of ownership and civilian use of
portions of the facility in close proximity to military operations raised issues of authority,
responsibility, and coordination of response to potential emergency situations.

The Colorado CSEPP community held a series of three tabletop exercises (TTXs) from 2017–
2019 to explore these issues and determine follow-up measures to improve and coordinate
preparedness during the transition to PuebloPlex. The TTX scenarios were specifically designed
for PCD; however, the issues and information they developed could inform emergency
preparedness at any U.S. military installation.

4.12.2 Background

The determination was made under the Base Realignment and Closure (BRAC)192 process to
discontinue PCD as an Army installation once the chemical weapons storage and destruction
missions were completed. A state-sponsored redevelopment authority, PuebloPlex, was formed
and was already leasing portions of the installation while the weapons storage and destruction
missions were still ongoing. Figure 4.12-1 shows the locations of the parcels being transferred.

The areas being leased and transferred contained buildings and storage igloos. The igloos were
formerly used for storing munitions. Civilian uses included storage and light manufacturing. The
presence of numerous buildings, along with road and rail access, made the site attractive for
reuse. As of this writing (June 2020), the process of site cleanup and turnover to PuebloPlex was
still ongoing.

191
 Lesson written in 2020.
192
 Pueblo Chemical Depot was realigned in 1988 by Congress pursuant to the Base Realignment and Closure Act
      of 1988 (P.L. 100-526). See
      https://www.congress.gov/search?q=%7b%22source%22:%22legislation%22,%22search%22:%22cite:PL100-
      526%22%7d for text.

 189

Figure 4.12-1 PCD Parcel Transfers to PuebloPlex Redevelopment Authority

 190

During the ongoing chemical weapons storage and destruction missions at PCD, the presence of
civilian operations and people in close proximity (i.e., within the outer boundary of the post)
raised concerns over emergency preparedness and response. PCD maintained considerable
capabilities for emergency response including hazardous materials response, medical response,
and security. However, these capabilities were generally limited by the missions and in some
cases by law to response within the Depot only. Areas under civilian ownership and control fell
under state, county, and local purview for emergency response including law enforcement, fire,
medical, and emergency management. An on-post emergency in the Army-controlled areas, or in
the leased or transferred areas, could affect nearby areas and require close cooperation of on-post
and off-post response authorities in order to ensure a prompt, coordinated response.

The Depot and local authorities maintained memoranda of agreement (MOAs) and memoranda
of understanding (MOUs) to define roles, responsibilities and available assistance under
emergency conditions. However, on-post and off-post authorities felt that these agreements and
understandings should be reviewed in light of the transfer of use and ownership of portions of the
installation.

4.12.3 Issue

How to address and coordinate on-post and off-post emergency response capabilities, including
law enforcement, medical, hazardous material, and public information functions, during the
transition to civilian ownership and use of the facility.

4.12.4 Solution

To explore what issues might be raised and how they might be solved under various scenarios,
the Pueblo CSEPP community conducted a series of TTXs to explore implications of transfer of
the property for emergency planning and response. Transfer of ownership would place
responsibility for certain response functions with Pueblo County; however, close coordination
with PCD would still be needed due to the proximity of the remaining, federally owned portion
of the site; the availability of PCD response assets; and the fact that PCD would continue to
control access to some civilian-owned areas.

Objectives, format, participation, scenario, discussion, and conclusions of these exercises are
summarized briefly below. Each TTX included participation by both on-post and off-post
response authorities. The exercise format consisted of an initial plenary session to explain the
scenario and exercise format, followed by breakout sessions to address specific response topics,
and finally a second plenary session (hot wash) featuring reports from the breakout groups. All
sessions were supported by facilitators and note-takers to lead and capture discussion. A scenario
was developed for each exercise, designed to bring out specific issues for discussion as defined
in exercise objectives. Participants were provided with a situation manual (SitMan) beforehand
covering the scenario and objectives for the exercise, as well as an after-action report (AAR)
afterward summarizing the exercise results.

 191

Participation

Organizations were recruited for participation according to the scenario and objectives for each
exercise. Representatives of the Depot, Pueblo County, PuebloPlex, and FEMA participated in
each exercise. Some exercises also brought in other entities such as the Colorado State Police,
U.S. Army Materiel Command legal office, the Pueblo Chemical-Agent Destruction Pilot Plant
(PCAPP), the Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI), and the 48th Military Police Detachment
Criminal Investigation Division, Fort Carson.

Scenarios

Scenarios were developed to support discussion of law enforcement, boundaries, fire and
medical response, public information, and coordination between on-post and off-post agencies.

In the first exercise scenario, a methamphetamine lab was set up in a rented igloo in the
PuebloPlex area, with large quantities of hazardous chemicals. Pueblo County Sheriff’s officers
drove out to investigate and upon arriving at the igloo, noticed four individuals in the process of
moving containers of unknown chemicals into the igloo while also loading a vehicle with several
cardboard boxes. An exchange of gunfire led to one suspect injured at the scene, other suspects
fleeing in two vehicles, and a fire started at the meth lab igloo.

The fleeing suspects drive through the fence onto PCD, and later onto PCAPP, leading to further
crimes committed, injuries, and another fire. Responders from Depot Security and off-post law
enforcement (Pueblo County Sheriff’s Office [PCSO], Fort Carson military police, and FBI)
become involved, along with on- and off-post first responders (fire and medical) and emergency
managers.

The second exercise used the same scenario as the first, but advanced by six hours to focus on
follow-on actions, investigations, and cleanup rather than initial response.

The third exercise used a different scenario, in which individuals attempted to steal chemical
munitions from PCD, winding up off-post in the PuebloPlex area. That exercise focused on
recapture/recovery efforts including coordination between on-post and off-post law enforcement
agencies and securing and handling the recaptured weapons.

Objectives and Core Capabilities

Objectives and core capabilities (as identified in the National Preparedness Goal) were identified
for each exercise and are summarized in Table 4.12-1.

Conclusions & Items for Follow-up

The TTX series provided an opportunity for multiple jurisdictions and agencies to discuss
response procedures and potential issues arising from handover of portions of the installation to
civilian ownership and use. While the TTX discussions led to agreement and answers to some

 193

questions, some issues remained unresolved and new questions arose. Conclusions and issues for
follow-up that emerged from the three TTXs are summarized below. (This is a selection; refer to
the original AARs for the complete list.)

Responsibilities and Coordination

 • Responsibilities. Primary responsibility for law enforcement and investigation, fire,
 medical, hazardous material, and any other emergency response fell to the County for an
 incident located within County jurisdiction. PuebloPlex was responsible for cleanup of
 any hazmat spill in the parcel as the facility owner.
 • Army response assets. PCD had response assets nearby and would have provided
 assistance according to existing agreements, similar to any off-post situation and within
 the legal limits of its authority.
 • Coordinated response. Unified command was to be established to coordinate multi-
 jurisdictional response. Use of liaisons, for example, deploying an under-sheriff or a
 public affairs officer to the PCD Emergency Operations Center (EOC), would enhance
 coordination of a response at PuebloPlex.
 • Event notification. Participants acknowledged the value of enhancing pre-set notification
 lists and protocols between PuebloPlex, PCD, and PCAPP. The Integrated Public Alert
 and Warning System (IPAWS) was noted as a tool that could be used to alert those within
 a specified radius of the nearest cell tower, as was the effort to sign up PuebloPlex
 tenants to receive alerts via PCD’s existing At-Hoc system. The off-post transport of
 chemical agent material (CAM) off-post would be considered a chemical event and a
 community emergency. PCD would make appropriate notifications to the County EOC
 and produce a hazard estimate and protective action recommendation (PAR).
 • Communications. The on- and off-post emergency response organizations were using
 different communication systems. Improvements to inter-operability were to be made
 through communication drills and the provision of radios from Pueblo County to the PCD
 security forces. Procedures that did not rely on technology were also considered to
 enhance coordination, such as physically pairing on- and off-post law enforcement
 officers, or using a gate as a command center. The value of “professional courtesy”
 notifications of events of interest between on- and off-post agencies with similar missions
 was noted.
 • Incident command. Initial Incident Command would be led by PCD’s Security and Law
 Enforcement Division (SLED) until such time that the PCSO arrived at the scene; then a
 Unified Command would be implemented with PCD SLED being the lead.

Army Authorities for Off-post Response

 • Army law enforcement authority. PCD SLED guards were authorized to pursue
 individuals off-post to recapture/recover CAM. Securing the CAM was to be the first
 priority. Use-of-force rules allowed appropriate force to be used to prevent loss of the
 CAM.

 194

 • If additional guard(s) were available to pursue suspects, they were to use the minimum
 amount of force necessary to apprehend the suspects. Fleeing suspects pursued and
 captured by guards off-post could be detained but must be turned over to the county
 sheriff’s officers as soon as possible.
 • Secure the scene. When CAM may have been stolen, the first responding person,
 normally a security guard, had the authority to secure the scene until civil authorities
 arrive. Once local law enforcement arrived, they were to work with Army personnel to
 ensure that the CAM was secured until retrieved by the Army.
 • On-Post Ambulances. PCAPP and PCD ambulances were not licensed by the State to
 operate off-post, so an MOA would have to be activated prior to their deployment to
 PuebloPlex. In an active-shooter situation or similar law enforcement-led response, EMS
 resources were to activate but not deploy to a response scene until they received
 clearance.

Public Information

 • Joint information system. The public would become aware of the incident almost
 immediately and rapid public information response would be essential. A large degree of
 public and media interest would be expected. A joint information system (JIS) would be
 implemented to coordinate messaging among responding organizations. The County
 would be the lead for information about off-post events, and the Depot would be the lead
 for information about on-post events.
 • High-level interest. Media would approach the Depot and want briefings, interviews, and
 video from the scene of the incident. High-level interest and presence (e.g. Governor,
 general officer) would be expected.

Issues for Follow-up

Participants identified the need to address the following questions:
 • Review whether the Depot should continue to control access to the parcel. Does the
 Depot want/need that? Could fencing be configured to provide an alternate entrance to
 the parcel, other than through the Depot gate? Would that be advantageous?
 • If the Depot restricts access to the rented facilities for whatever reason, what liability
 might be incurred by the Army?
 • What is the nature of the easement(s) that the Depot will have for the parcel?
 • The leases for rented facilities in the public-access area should be reviewed regarding:
 (a) conditions for access, to address personal credentialing and the possibility of closure
 due to Depot security considerations; (b) permitted uses in the rented facilities;
 (c) County access to rented facilities for inspections. Does Colorado landlord/tenant law
 affect these?
 • What are the exact limits of law enforcement authority for Depot security officers? They
 are likely to be the closest in any emergency event within the parcel; also, there may be a

 195

 crisis situation where nearby Sheriff’s officers are in danger and SLED officers would
 want to render assistance. Are there circumstances or agreements that would enable them
 to use force offpost to save lives?

4.12.5 Potential Application

The scenarios in this series of exercises were specific to Pueblo Chemical Depot, where highly
toxic chemical weapons were stored in a restricted area that was in close proximity to areas being
opened to public access. However, many of the issues and concerns discussed apply to other
military facilities. Limits on law enforcement authority, coordination between on-post and off-
post authorities, memoranda of agreement for cooperation, access for emergency vehicles and
personnel, and coordination of public information are applicable to any U.S. military installation.
Any military installation may suffer an emergency or assault that raises issues similar to the ones
discussed in this series of exercises; thus, planning, preparation, and coordination with off-post
federal, state, and local authorities on these issues are important.

 197

4.13 Assessing and Exercising Emergency Communications
 Resilience193
The Colorado CSEPP community undertook a series of activities to assess vulnerabilities and
enhance the resilience of emergency communications.

4.13.1 Summary

The Colorado CSEPP community undertook a series of activities in 2015 and 2016 to enhance
the resilience of emergency communications. There were three phases. First, key emergency
communication systems and their underlying infrastructure were identified, and their
vulnerabilities were analyzed. Second, a workshop was held to establish a common foundation of
understanding between emergency managers, information technology (IT) staff, and
communications service providers regarding emergency communications. A planning working
group was convened to discuss ways to minimize the impact of any communication system
disruptions. Third, a tabletop exercise was held to test existing plans, capabilities, and
agreements against a variety of scenarios involving communication system breakdowns.

Participants in these activities came away with a better understanding of the infrastructure
underlying emergency communication systems, resources available to quickly repair those
systems, potential vulnerabilities, and the key role of interagency cooperation in working around
a communication system outage when it occurs.

4.13.2 Background

Experience suggests that emergency communications can be disrupted by damage to the
supporting infrastructure, possibly when it is most needed. A storm, fire, or other event can
affect physical infrastructure such as cell towers, microwave antennas, fiber-optic cables, or
power supplies that are essential for local communications networks. A local communications
outage can disrupt key information flow to and among emergency services. There are numerous
examples of such disruptions. Policymakers at the federal level, particularly at the
U.S. Department of Homeland Security (DHS), have recognized this issue and have developed
planning recommendations and programs to address it.

Examples of Disruption

In May 1998, a fire in a telephone switching center near Chicago disrupted telephone
communications on a large scale, including at the two Chicago airports, O’Hare and Midway.
Thousands of customers experienced outages of up to a few days, and local system disruptions
continued for weeks. Recognizing that landline service to 9-1-1 centers was unavailable, several
suburbs sent police officers and firefighters with radios to selected locations to receive reports of
fires or other emergencies in person.194

193
 Lesson written in 2018.
194
 See University of Illinois webpage, “Significant Illinois Fires: Illinois Bell Telephone fire” available at
 https://guides.library.illinois.edu/c.php?g=416856&p=2842669, accessed August 2025.

 198

In 2001, emergency communications in New York were severely disrupted on 9/11 due to loss of
communications equipment located in and on the Trade Center buildings.195

In June 2012, a long-lived derecho196 caused extensive damage in several Midwestern and Mid-
Atlantic states. Damaging power grids and communication networks, the storm affected service
at 77 public service answering points (PSAPs), of which at least 17 lost service completely,
cutting off access to 9-1-1 for more than 2 million residents.197 Figure 4.13-1 shows the path of
the storm and areas affected.

                          Figure 4.13-1 Path of Derecho, June 29–30, 2012198

Of the PSAPs affected by the derecho, some lost all functions while others experienced partial
losses only. Some were able to receive calls but did not have normal access to information about
the caller’s location. Most of the service outages were the result of loss of power rather than
direct damage to communications infrastructure.

 Management,” Crit Care; 5(6): 318–320, available at http://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC137379/,
 accessed January 2018.

 Weather event featuring a long-lived squall line of thunderstorms that produce damaging winds.
