 199

In August 2015, cellular telephone customers of all four major carriers had no service for several
hours in parts of Kentucky, Tennessee, Alabama, and Georgia. A spokesman for the Kentucky
Division of Emergency Management attributed the outage to a cut in a fiber-optic cable
belonging to AT&T along the Kentucky-Tennessee border.199

As of this writing (2018), outages are common enough to have routine trackers on the Web.
Information and maps for AT&T, Verizon, and Comcast outages can be obtained online.200

Emergency Communications Resilience in DHS Policy

The DHS and the Federal Communications Commission (FCC) have recognized the importance
of resilient emergency communications and have active programs to promote flexible and robust
communications for responders.

The DHS National Emergency Communications Plan (NECP), first published in 2008 and
updated in 2014, is intended “to improve the key communications capabilities of emergency
responders at all levels of government—notably the policies, governance structures, planning,
and protocols that enable them to communicate and share information under all
circumstances.”201 Exhibit 1 of the NECP lays out the vision, goals, and objectives of the plan;
Objective 4.3 is to “Strengthen resilience, security, and continuity of communications throughout
response operations.”202 To achieve this objective, the NECP recommends that PSAPs and
Public Safety Communications Centers (PSCCs) have continuity-of-operations plans to support
their ability to maintain 9-1-1 services and dispatch communications during incidents. Such
plans should include succession and backup procedures for major systems such as computer-
aided dispatch, radio, and power supply. In addition, they should have measures in place to
quickly restore service, such as priority arrangements with local service providers.203

4.13.3 Issue

What is the possibility that a construction accident, storm, fire, act of sabotage, or other adverse
event could take out crucial communication links that serve local emergency services? What
would be the impact to public safety? And how can agencies avoid, prepare for and respond to
such an event?

4.13.4 Solution

These issues were investigated and addressed in a series of activities conducted by the Colorado
CSEPP community.

199
 David Goldman, 2015, “Why a massive cell phone outage hit the Southeast,” CNNMoney, August 5, available at
 http://money.cnn.com/2015/08/05/technology/cell-phone-outage/, accessed January 2018.
200
 Visit downtector.com or http://patch.com/us/across-america/comcast-outages-reported-nationwide, accessed
    January 2018.
201
 DHS, 2014, National Emergency Communications Plan, p. V, available at https://www.dhs.gov/national-
 emergency-communications-plan, accessed January 2018.
202
    Ibid., p. 6.
203
    Ibid., pp. 40–41.

 200

The primary organizations within the Colorado CSEPP community were the Army’s Pueblo
Chemical Depot (PCD), the FEMA Region VIII office in Denver, Pueblo County, and the State
of Colorado. Representatives of each of these organizations formed a committee to plan and
execute a series of activities to assess and test emergency communications resiliency in the
Pueblo area over a roughly yearlong period from mid-2015 to mid-2016. Technical support was
provided by Argonne National Laboratory.

Key Systems

The Pueblo CSEPP community used a variety of systems and methods for emergency
communications. One very important system was WebPuff, a private online system that was
designed for and installed in CSEPP communities. In the event of a chemical agent release,
WebPuff software would be used to model the downwind concentrations of toxic material, and
then generate both a graphical depiction of areas expected to be affected and a set of
recommendations for protective actions (shelter-in-place or evacuate) in particular downwind
zones. WebPuff was installed on computers at the Army depot and in emergency operations
centers serving Pueblo County and the State of Colorado. It was a continuously accessible online
environment used to share information and provide a common operating picture for both Army
personnel and off-post local officials. In addition to the hazard analysis aspect, it served as a
communication interface for emergency personnel at the Army installation and in the nearby
community. WebPuff was incorporated into local procedures for warning off-post officials and
ensuring public protection in the event of a chemical-agent accident.204

Other systems used in the Pueblo CSEPP community included a hotline for on-post/off-post
communications; landline telephones; email; paging and dispatch systems; radio
communications channels used by law enforcement, fire, and emergency medical services; and
systems involved in delivering public warnings such as sirens, tone-alert radios, and the
Emergency Alert System (EAS).

These systems provided capabilities considered crucial to effective response in the event of a
chemical-agent release. In that scenario, time would be of the essence. The Army Depot would
be the first to know of the event, and would quickly provide notification to off-post authorities,
including a hazard analysis and recommended protective actions. The CSEPP standard for
accomplishing this warning at Pueblo was within 10 minutes. Other activities relating to public
warning, activation of response personnel, notifications to schools and hospitals, and other
notifications would also need to be accomplished quickly.

All of these capabilities in turn depended on local communications infrastructure including fiber-
optic networks, server rooms in county and municipal buildings, towers with microwave and
radio repeaters, and other equipment located on rooftops, in underground vaults, or strung on
poles. It is sometimes underappreciated how disaster response relies on communications, and
how these communications in turn rely on physical infrastructure and equipment that can be
damaged by storms, flooding, excavation, or deliberate malfeasance such as sabotage or
cyberattack.

204
 For further information on WebPuff, see https://iem.com/modeling-simulation/webpuff, accessed January 2018.

 201

Main Activities

The project had three main components or phases: assessment of vulnerabilities, workshop, and
tabletop exercise. They are detailed below.

Vulnerability Assessment

A vulnerability assessment was conducted in order to determine risks: (a) which local systems
were critical for carrying local emergency management notifications and traffic, and (b) the
potential vulnerabilities of those systems. The assessment was valuable in its own right to point
out possible soft spots and suggest ways to increase protection or redundancy for
communications assets. It also provided a sound basis for the subsequent components of the
activity, the workshop and tabletop exercise.

The vulnerability assessment covered five systems and buildings. An expert infrastructure
analyst visited the facilities and met with local IT staff and a vendor representative. For each
facility, the assessment included a description of its relevant communications assets, the
functions those assets perform, threats to and vulnerabilities of the assets, consequences if the
assets were lost, restoration processes to recover those assets, and dependencies (if any) on
electricity and water supply. The analysis also included options for consideration to increase
resilience. Options include both physical improvements (e.g., installation of redundant power
supply, fencing of key assets) and non-physical improvements such as updated plans, system
assessments, stress tests, personnel training, exercises, and interagency agreements.

The assessment produced a description of the local systems supporting emergency
communication. These included two looped fiber-optic networks, a Wide Area Network (WAN)
and a Municipal Area Network (MAN), including their routes and key nodes. Key nodes
included server rooms in several buildings. The systems description also included systems for
converting between analog and digital signals (for voice communications), data storage facilities,
radio systems and antennas, and microwave transmitters/receivers located on rooftops and
towers. Some components of the system were owned by governmental entities, and some were
leased from local telecommunications companies. Similarly, resources for identifying and fixing
communications system outages were a mix of in-house agency personnel and outside vendors.

The assessment considered risk factors of various kinds, including both accidental and deliberate
damage. Cables that run along poles above ground have certain risks from traffic and weather
that underground cables do not. Underground cables, on the other hand, are vulnerable to
damage from digging. Looped systems have built-in redundancy in the sense that generally they
are not vulnerable to a single cut; signals can simply travel the other way around the loop to
reach their destinations, and the system has equipment that monitors continuity and reroutes the
signals automatically if there is a break.

Key locations such as server rooms were evaluated for security, fire protection, and the presence
of backup power supply and cooling systems. Another consideration was where communication
cables enter a given facility; there might be redundancy in the sense that two separate cables

 202

serve the facility, but if they were located next to each other then they might both be damaged by
the same adverse event (i.e., they were subject to common-mode failure).

Personnel in charge of the systems were interviewed to obtain information about system
monitoring, redundancy, and protocols for response and repair in the event of a disruption.

The assessment produced a report that described the critical systems supporting emergency
communications, identified potential vulnerabilities, and proposed options for consideration to
increase protection and resilience of these systems. Knowledge gained from the assessment
provided a basis for subsequent activities, including development of scenarios for the exercise.
The report was provided to key agency IT staff responsible for maintaining these systems but
otherwise was kept confidential in order to protect the information about system vulnerabilities.

Workshop

A workshop was held in December 2015 after several months of planning. The main purpose of
the workshop was to provide a common base of knowledge among emergency managers, IT
support staff, telecommunications vendors, and others with regard to the infrastructure behind
emergency communications and how it was used by emergency officials to transmit key
information among themselves and to the public. The workshop also served to put
communication breakdowns “on the radar screen” as something that the emergency management
community should explicitly prepare for, rather than assuming that all systems will always be
available. Identifying communication alternatives, redundancies, backups, and work-arounds was
an objective of the workshop.

A good mix of participants was key to the workshop’s success. A total of 42 participants
included key staff from the U.S. Army (from PCD and its parent office, the Chemical Materials
Activity), FEMA (regional and national offices), the County and City of Pueblo, the State of
Colorado, the National Weather Service (operator of EAS), three different private
communications service providers that operate in the area, and local colleges and universities.

The participants heard presentations describing CSEPP and outlining the different systems
supporting emergency communications in the area. Presenters included representatives of the
Army, Pueblo County, and the communications service providers. The second part of the
workshop was a facilitated discussion among the participants. They discussed each major top-
level system in turn – the hotline that connected the Chemical Depot to the County and State, the
WebPuff system used to transmit hazard estimates, general internet access, videoconferencing,
and the public-warning systems (sirens and EAS). For each system, the group discussed how
they would know there was an outage, what backup systems were available, and whether the
backup systems were recorded in emergency plans and procedures.

 203

Tabletop Exercise

The last phase of this activity was a tabletop exercise (TTX) held in June 2016. Like the
workshop, it included a mix of participants including IT professionals, emergency planners,
emergency managers, and service providers (vendors). Overall, there were 45 participants,
broken up into four tables to foster small-group discussion. A lead controller presented a series
of brief scenarios for discussion by the groups. The scenarios were very simple, with just enough
information to provide a basis for discussion. They included a utility pole being knocked over at
one of the key facilities, a fire in a server room, a construction accident that severed cables, and
lastly an earthquake that caused numerous infrastructure failures as well as other types of
damage.

Participants were provided a list of the communication systems that would be affected by each
scenario and a set of specific questions to discuss. At each table, a controller moderated the
discussion, and afterwards a table representative briefed the whole group about the table’s
findings. A short whole-group discussion followed the table reports. The questions associated
with the first two scenarios (utility pole and server room fire) focused on communication and
information sharing; the third scenario (digging accident) focused on operations and procedures,
and the fourth scenario (earthquake) focused on prioritization and resiliency.

Note-takers documented the discussions at each table and during the whole-group sessions. An
after-action report summarizing the discussions was distributed to the participants after it was
reviewed and approved by the planning group.

Key Takeaways

Participants in the workshop and TTX were asked to fill out evaluation forms at the end of the
day. Based on comments collected from the evaluation forms and hotwash discussions among
the planning team, key benefits were identified:

Situational awareness and familiarity. An important aspect of the workshop and TTX portions of
the activity was participation of personnel from diverse organizations and disciplines. Many of
the emergency managers had limited awareness of the infrastructure underlying crucial
communication systems. Similarly, the IT managers and vendors who supplied connectivity did
not always have specific knowledge of the top-level functions their systems supported. It was
generally agreed that it was valuable for all sides to become acquainted with the big picture in
terms of how emergency communications take place and the potential vulnerabilities of the
systems they rely on. Regional providers of internet, cable, and telephone services generally have
emergency crews on call to promptly address outage situations.

Planning and coordination. CSEPP involved preparedness for a chemical weapons accident that
could occur quickly and have deadly consequences. As a result, time was of the essence in a
CSEPP response. To be effective, warnings and protective actions would have to take place
within minutes after an accident. This placed an emphasis on reliable and prompt communication
– key systems had to be available when needed – and on being prepared to respond quickly to
any disruption of communications. Planning, interagency agreements, and drills or exercises

 204

helped ensure that key information would get where it was needed in an emergency. In
particular, the activities brought to light issues such as:

 • Notification – if a system has an outage, is that immediately apparent? How will you
 know, and who do you notify?
 • Alternates – is there a satisfactory alternate way to get key information across so that
 protective action decisions can be made and warnings and other key information can be
 provided to the public?
 • The “handshake issue” – communication requires a sender and a receiver with the same
 expectations. If an alternate system is to be used, both sides have to know. Are alternate
 systems documented in plans and procedures? In memoranda of agreement (MOAs)?

An ongoing committee was formed to review and revise plans, procedures, and MOAs in order
to address these issues and ensure resilience of communications.

Identifying and addressing vulnerabilities. The vulnerability analysis portion of the activity
identified potential failure points and failure modes. Having studied and documented these
vulnerabilities, agency personnel were in a better position to address these vulnerabilities. In
some cases, the information was expected to bolster the case for obtaining the necessary
resources to address the issue, such as upgrades to facility security, fire protection, backup
power, or system redundancy.

4.13.5 Potential Application

Virtually any location or jurisdiction with emergency response duties and resources relies on
critical telecommunications systems. Activation and coordination of a response and key
functions such as public warning rely on electronic communications; those communications in
turn rely on an increasingly complicated set of critical systems.

Diversity of communication methods is a great advantage for emergency services, but also leads
to an increasingly complex system architecture. A local 9-1-1 center is more powerful for being
able to take calls from wireline, cellular, and voice over internet protocol (VoIP) phones and to
display the caller’s name and location. However, this increased functionality depends on
switching centers and routers to translate and transfer information (Figure 4.13-2). In addition,
emergency information may be collected and disseminated through email, websites, and social
media such as Twitter and Facebook. These in turn require connectivity with the larger Internet.

 205

 Figure 4.13-2 System Architecture Supporting 9-1-1 Center205

Anyone who has experienced a temporary outage of internet service, telephone, cable, or satellite
service will recognize the potential for failure of those systems. Widespread failures that affected
emergency response capabilities have been documented in a number of instances.

On the other hand, today’s communication infrastructure is typically robust and interconnected.
The Internet is a flexible system whereby packets of information are transmitted through a
network and can be rerouted around an obstacle; this happens constantly, routinely, and
invisibly. Local high-speed data systems often are looped, so that a single break (say, a cut to a
trunk optical fiber cable) has little effect because the data can be re-routed in the other direction.
Also, when one system such as landline telephone service is down, other communication
methods such as mobile phones, email, and VoIP may still be operational.

To ensure reliable communication access to emergency services, the first step is taking a hard
look at the different communication methods that might be used in an emergency, and
identifying the underlying systems, providers, and infrastructure. Once these are identified, they
can be reviewed for vulnerabilities. Are there single points of failure, systems that have no
backup? Are there key facilities where damage would cause a major disruption? Do
communications capabilities depend on other systems, such as electric power, that are
themselves vulnerable?

Based on identified vulnerabilities, strategies for fixes and workarounds can be developed.
Emergency personnel must know how an outage will be identified and who to call when there is
an outage. For each critical communications mode, an alternative should be identified to use if

205
 Federal Communications Commission, Public Safety and Homeland Security Bureau, January 2013, “Impact of
 the June 2012 Derecho on Communications Networks and Services,” p. 25. PSTN refers to Public Switched
 Telephone Network. Available at https://www.fcc.gov/document/derecho-report-and-recommendations, accessed
      January 2018.

 206

that particular system is unavailable; it is important to communicate and coordinate with local
response partners to address alternate communication methods and develop procedures and inter-
agency agreements for switching to those alternates. Where feasible, response authorities should
consider measures to reduce vulnerabilities such as installing fire suppression equipment,
acquiring backup power, protecting key outdoor equipment, and enhancing security for key
areas.

Last but not least, it is important to establish relationships between EM personnel and IT
personnel and with communications service vendors. Meetings, workshops, written agreements,
joint planning, drills, and exercises will raise awareness of the issue and help lay the groundwork
for quick resolution of communications outages in future.

4.14 Wet Bulb Globe Temperature Model206
Argonne CSEPP staff developed and implemented a novel method to model Wet Bulb Globe
Temperature (WBGT) using available meteorological data. WBGT is used to protect workers in
heavy personal protective equipment (PPE) from heat stress, and previous methods to obtain it
were cumbersome and error-prone. The novel approach improved worker safety and is widely
adaptable to similar work situations.

4.14.1 Summary

The Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program (CSEPP) required accurate and
continuous WBGT estimates to protect workers involved in chemical munitions disposal from
heat-related injuries. Traditional WBGT measurements posed logistical challenges at U.S. Army
depots, prompting the development of a novel modeling approach. This effort culminated in the
Liljegren et al. paper,207 which introduced a physics-based model to calculate WBGT using
standard meteorological data. The model achieved high accuracy (less than 1°C error) and
eliminated the need for specialized, labor-intensive equipment that was prone to user error,
offering a practical solution for CSEPP and broader applications. This lesson summarizes the
background, issue, solution, potential applications, real-world adoption, and impact of this work.

4.14.2 Background

CSEPP was established to enhance emergency preparedness and response at U.S. Army
installations storing chemical weapons, ensuring the safety of both military personnel and
surrounding communities during stockpile disposal.208 Workers at these sites, such as those at the
Anniston Army Depot and Tooele Chemical Agent Disposal Facility, often wore protective
clothing (e.g., mission-oriented protective posture [MOPP], level 4), increasing their
susceptibility to heat stress.209 WBGT, a heat stress index integrating air temperature, humidity,
wind speed, and solar radiation, was critical for assessing environmental conditions and guiding
safety protocols.210 The U.S. Army and the Occupational Safety and Health Administration
(OSHA) relied on WBGT to set activity thresholds, but direct measurement required specialized
instruments (e.g., wet bulb, globe, and dry bulb thermometers), which were labor intensive and
subject to significant user error.211

206
 Lesson written in 2025. The model described in this lesson was published in the Journal of Occupational and
 Environmental Hygiene in 2008 (see next footnote).
207
 Liljegren, James C., Richard A. Carhart, Philip Lawday, Stephen Tschopp, and Robert Sharp, 2008, “Modeling
 the Wet Bulb Globe Temperature Using Standard Meteorological Measurements,” Journal of Occupational and
    Environmental Hygiene, vol. 5, no. 10: 645–55. https://doi.org/10.1080/15459620802310770.
208
 U.S. Army Chemical Materials Agency, Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program Overview,
 Aberdeen Proving Ground, Maryland: U.S. Army, 2008.
209
 Liljegren, et al., “Modeling the Wet Bulb Globe Temperature,” 645.
210
 Budd, G. M., 2008, “Wet-Bulb Globe Temperature (WBGT)—Its History and Its Limitations,” Journal of
    Science and Medicine in Sport, vol. 11, no. 1: 20–32. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jsams.2007.07.003.
211
 Occupational Safety and Health Administration, 2017, Technical Manual: Heat Stress, Washington, D.C.:
 OSHA.

 208

Prior to 2008, WBGT approximations existed, but they often lacked precision or required site-
specific calibration, limiting their utility for CSEPP’s diverse geographic settings.212 The
U.S. Army Chemical Materials Agency, under Contract MIPR 7D22 CM7007, tasked Argonne
National Laboratory with developing a reliable, scalable method to estimate WBGT using
readily available weather data, aligning with CSEPP’s mission to enhance worker safety. 213

4.14.3 Issue

The primary issue for CSEPP was the inability to obtain continuous, accurate WBGT
measurements at chemical weapons depots without deploying error-prone and labor-intensive
equipment. Workers face heightened heat stress risks due to protective gear, with WBGT
adjustments of +5.6°C for easy work and +11.1°C for moderate-to-hard work in MOPP level 4
attire.214 Direct WBGT measurement involved natural wet bulb and globe temperature sensors,
which were sensitive to environmental variability and difficult to maintain in real-time
operations across sites like Pine Bluff Arsenal and Umatilla Chemical Depot.215 Existing
simplified WBGT models, such as the simplified WBGT (sWBGT), overestimated heat stress in
humid conditions, potentially leading to overly conservative safety measures, while others
required inputs unavailable from standard weather stations.216 This gap threatened CSEPP’s
ability to balance worker safety with operational efficiency.

4.14.4 Solution

Argonne National Laboratory developed a physics-based model to calculate WBGT using
standard meteorological inputs—air temperature, relative humidity, wind speed, and solar
radiation—available from weather stations at Army chemical depots.217 Published in the Journal
of Occupational and Environmental Hygiene in 2008, the model comprised sub-models for
natural wet bulb and globe temperatures, grounded in heat and mass transfer principles.218 Unlike
prior approximations, it required no site-specific parameters, enhancing its generalizability.
Validation against direct WBGT measurements at multiple depots showed an accuracy of better
than 1°C, meeting CSEPP’s need for precision.219

The Liljegren model iteratively solved energy balance equations for wet bulb and globe
temperatures, accounting for convective and radiative heat transfer.220 Implemented in C and
FORTRAN, it was integrated into the MetView software at depot operations centers, providing

212
 Lemke, Bruno, and Tord Kjellstrom, 2012, “Calculating Workplace WBGT from Meteorological Data: A Tool
 for Climate Change Assessment,” Industrial Health 50, no. 4: 267–78. https://doi.org/10.2486/indhealth.ms1352.
213
 Liljegren, et al., “Modeling the Wet Bulb Globe Temperature,” p. 645.
214
    Ibid., p. 647.
215
    Ibid., p. 649.
216
 Kong, Qiaohong, and Matthew Huber, 2022, “Explicit Calculations of Wet-Bulb Globe Temperature Compared
 with Approximations and Why it Matters for Labor Productivity,” Earth’s Future, vol. 10, no. 3:
    e2021EF002334.
217
 Liljegren, et al., “Modeling the Wet Bulb Globe Temperature,” p. 645.
218
    Ibid., pp. 650–52.
219
    Ibid., p. 654.
220
    Ibid., p. 651.

 209

real-time WBGT estimates and heat stress alerts.221 This solution eliminated the need for
specialized sensors, reduced maintenance costs, and enabled CSEPP to monitor heat stress
continuously across all sites.

4.14.5 Potential Application

The Liljegren model offered immediate potential beyond CSEPP. Its reliance on standard
meteorological data made it adaptable to other military contexts, such as training exercises or
overseas deployments, where heat stress monitoring is critical.222 Civilian applications include
occupational safety in industries like construction, agriculture, and manufacturing, where
workers face heat exposure but lack WBGT instrumentation.223 The model’s accuracy and
flexibility also suggested utility in climate change research, enabling retrospective WBGT
analysis using historical weather data to assess future heat stress trends.224 Its physics-based
approach promised improved reliability over simplified approximations, potentially informing
public health and emergency planning in heat-vulnerable regions.

The Liljegren method has seen widespread adoption and impact across policy and science since
publication in 2008:
 • U.S. Policy. The U.S. Army has validated its use in operational settings, with studies
 confirming its superiority over other models (e.g., Matthew, et al.) for heat injury risk
 assessment.225 The National Institute for Occupational Safety and Health (NIOSH) lists
 WBGT as an administrative control for heat stress.226 OSHA goes on to specifically
 recommend the Liljegren method as a resource to develop an effective heat illness
 prevention program in support of NIOSH227and also references the Argonne National
 Laboratory WBGT Utility program.228
 • International Policy. Internationally, the Liljegren method supports WBGT calculations
 in climate change assessments, the recommended heat risk metric by the International
 Organization for Standardization (ISO) for occupational heat stress standards (ISO
 7243:2017).229 Countries like Australia and Bangladesh have adopted it to map future

 5 Emergency Public Information

CSEPP has long emphasized the importance of effective emergency public information. The first
four lessons in this section address efforts to improve emergency public information functions
through training and use of media role-players (mock media) in exercises. The fifth lesson
describes how the program integrated social media play into drills and exercises. The last lesson
summarizes the most commonly identified emergency public information issues based on a
survey of exercises held from 2004 to 2008.

5.1 Emergency Public Information Staff Training233
A directed training program can improve the emergency public information function. Three areas
in particular are recommended for training: spokesperson skills, coordinating multi-agency
response through a Joint Information System / Joint Information Center (JIS/JIC), and
incorporating new communication technologies into a public information program.

5.1.1 Summary

The CSEP Program developed effective training to improve the performance of public
information officers (PIOs), public affairs officers (PAOs), and other public officials who might
be called upon to carry out emergency public information functions. The training included three
courses of note: spokesperson training, use of joint information systems (JIS) and joint
information centers (JICs), and use of emerging digital communications technologies. These
courses and the concepts behind them improved emergency public information for the CSEP
Program and for other emergency functions carried out by CSEPP jurisdictions. They became a
key component of FEMA public information training. Training of PIOs and other personnel and
officials who may be involved in emergency public information can benefit any emergency
management organization.

5.1.2 Background

As CSEPP jurisdictions added staff early in the program, hiring was overseen by the individual
jurisdictions and agencies. In some cases, PIOs were hired without any experience in public
affairs or specific education that prepared them for the position. Other PIOs entered the program
from previous public relations or media backgrounds. The program quickly prioritized training a
diverse PIO workforce in basic communication practices and the specific skills required for risk
communication, crisis communication, and emergency public information.

5.1.3 Issue

A diverse PIO workforce needed training in basic communication practices and the specific
skills required to communicate effectively during a crisis. On a broader level, emergency

233

 213

responders and public officials needed training on the importance of (and how to provide) the
public and the news media with timely, accurate, and complete emergency public information.

5.1.4 Solution

CSEPP managers determined that it would be necessary to develop and deliver a variety of
public affairs and emergency public information training courses for emergency managers,
elected officials, and public information/affairs officers.234 The training focused on spokesperson
skills, on coordinating multi-agency response through a JIS/JIC, and more recently on
incorporating new communication technologies into a public information program.

Spokesperson Training

Spokesperson training was developed for two distinct audiences: policymakers and PIO/PAOs.
Policymakers included military commanders, public officials, and emergency management
directors, all of whom might be called upon to speak to the public during an emergency response.
The PIO/PAO audience included those at the federal, state, and local level.

The spokesperson training focused on understanding the spokesperson’s role and honing skills to
carry it out effectively. The understanding component included what information the public
wants and needs in an emergency; knowledge of how to communicate to diverse cultures and
people with special needs; the role of a PIO/PAO; and the news media. Understanding the news
media means understanding their goals, expectations, and deadlines, plus the differing needs
among television, radio, print, and internet media. Understanding the needs and expectations of
the news media lays the groundwork for effective communication.

The skills component of the training addressed topics such as presenting a news briefing,
conducting an interview (or prepping someone else for an interview), and handling media at the
scene of an incident. It included hands-on practice and critiques in small groups.

JIS/JIC Training

JIS/JIC training was one component of implementing the JIS/JIC concept. Disaster experience
has shown that when different organizations provide inconsistent messages, the net effect may be
confusing for the public, and the news media will focus on the inconsistencies as a story. A joint
information system or JIS is designed to avoid inconsistencies by allowing the involved response
organizations to share and compare information that is destined for public release. A JIS can be
implemented remotely via email, telephone, and other networking and communication
technologies and/or through face-to-face interactions among PIO/PAOs at a joint information
center (JIC).

234
 A note about terminology: the CSEP Program included both Army and civilian response organizations. In the
 Army, personnel specializing in communication with the public were referred to as “public affairs officers” or
 PAOs. On the civilian side, the equivalent position was usually referred to as a “public information officer” or
 PIO. Both PAO and PIO were used in CSEPP and in this lesson learned.

 214

A JIC is a physical facility used to facilitate implementation of a JIS and provide a unified
location for news media to obtain information. In CSEPP, JIC facilities were designated for each
site and were demonstrated during exercises, including demonstration of joint news conferences.

A related concept is the Virtual JIC, which allows remote support to response actions. In a
Virtual JIC, distant elements of the responding organizations (e.g., other Army installations or
headquarters offices) and mutual-aid organizations can become components of the public
information effort through information technology and close coordination.

The JIS/JIC concept starts with coordinated community-wide planning and is then incorporated
into each organization’s plans, procedures, and training. JIS/JIC training focuses on coordination
among organizations to provide a consistent, unified message to the public through in-person
interactions at a JIC and/or virtual interactions through information technology. Training for JIC
personnel covers the purpose of a JIC, the functions performed there, and the staffing,
equipment, and facility layout needed to make a JIC function effectively.

CSEPP JIS/JIC training was conducted in a multi-day workshop format and, like the
spokesperson training, included hands-on components. The hands-on parts included breakout
activities and a mini-exercise based on a scenario that tested the emergency information response
capabilities of participating organizations. Experienced controllers guided the exercise play
while “mock media” simulated real-world media. Mock media covered the scenario situation as
if it were a real news event by gathering information and producing print and broadcast stories
from what they learned.235 This allowed participants the opportunity to react in a controlled
environment where their decisions could be assessed. The training activity concluded with a
discussion of how the JIS/JIC functioned, issue identification, why the mock media produced the
stories they did, how player performance influenced media coverage and other lessons learned
directly from JIC staff.

CSEPP was widely credited for its early adoption and expansion of JIS/JIC concepts, particularly
as they relate to ICS and NIMS. The JIC organizational model pioneered by CSEPP was adopted
by DHS/FEMA and published nationally in FEMA 517: Basic Guidance for Public Information
Officers (Nov. 2007), which was used widely for national-level public affairs training.

New Communication Technologies Training

In recognition of the growing importance of evolving digital communication technologies
including social media, the New Communication Technologies Workshop was designed to
provide instruction and hands-on training in the use of the latest technologies and platforms. It
included how to use the latest communication channels to relay information about potential risks,
inform people about protective actions, gather facts, and identify misinformation. Specific topics
included social media tools for PIOs/PAOs, how to monitor the new media, what people look for
on your website, and custom digital mapping, among others. The intended audience for this
course included the usual PIOs/PAOs and emergency managers, plus the IT staff who support
emergency response agencies.

235
 For further information on how mock media were used in CSEPP exercises, see Lesson 5.4.

 215

As CSEPP matured and emergency managers increasingly viewed chemical agents as part of a
broader “all hazards” focus, it became common to see a wide range of community members
taking part in CSEPP-sponsored training, further strengthening local preparedness and
augmenting the number of trained PIOs and PAOs available to respond to a chemical agent
emergency. Over the life of the CSEP Program, hundreds of training courses were presented on-
site in local communities to thousands of public affairs practitioners, enhancing their knowledge
of key public affairs techniques and improving their ability to execute them.

In addition to the training offered directly by CSEPP, PIOs from CSEPP communities regularly
attended FEMA-sponsored public information courses locally and at the Emergency
Management Institute in Emmitsburg, Maryland. In 2009 and 2010, CSEPP PIOs participated in
curriculum review groups and pilot tested local-level PIO courses for FEMA, with many of the
advances developed through CSEPP introduced to the FEMA curriculum. CSEPP public affairs
training also spurred development of similar training in other federal emergency management
programs, such as those run by the U.S. Department of Energy and the U.S. Department of
Homeland Security.

5.1.5 Potential Application

The value of emergency public information-related training extends to any organization with
emergency management responsibilities and applies to any type of emergency.

Guidebook, 2nd Ed, available at https://portalfiles.blob.core.usgovcloudapi.net/training/CSEPP

5.2 Crisis Communication Training in a Pandemic236
The Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program (CSEPP) continued crisis
communications during the Covid-19 pandemic through a variety of adaptations to training
format, content, and delivery.

5.2.1 Summary

Effective public communications is a key element of successful emergency management – key to
public protection, and also to the public’s perception of the response. Training in turn is an
important element of growing and maintaining capabilities for risk and crisis communication.
However, constraints on funding, travel, and especially on time can make it difficult for
extremely busy public information officers (PIOs), public affairs officers (PAOs), emergency
management directors, and public officials to obtain this training.

In 2020, the Covid-19 pandemic magnified the difficulties of conducting training. Travel became
difficult to impossible, gathering people in a room became unwise, and the demands on
emergency managers and public information officers were multiplied. To maintain a risk and
crisis communication training program, CSEPP had to adapt and re-think several aspects of the
training, including length of programs, subject matter, and format, to allow training to be
conducted remotely and to provide useful material in a form that PIOs and other public officials
could efficiently access.

This lesson learned summarizes CSEPP’s efforts to adapt public affairs training to the “new
realities” of 2020. While in-person training will always have certain advantages, these
adaptations demonstrated what was possible without it and may have a lasting impact moving
forward.

5.2.2 Background

CSEPP was established in the 1980s to bolster emergency preparedness in communities that host
U.S. Army facilities where chemical weapons are stored. The Army destroyed these weapons, as
required by treaty, over the course of a 37-year period. But until the last chemical munition was
destroyed, CSEPP provided a mechanism to protect nearby communities in the unlikely event of
a chemical weapons accident. The U.S. Army Chemical Materials Activity (CMA) and the
Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA) Technological Hazards Division (THD)
jointly administered CSEPP, in cooperation with and in service to relevant state and local
authorities.

CSEPP strengthened local and state emergency preparedness capabilities through improvements
to staffing, facilities, equipment, planning, training, and drills and exercises. The program
emphasized, among other things, the importance of crisis communications as a component of
emergency preparedness and response. The ability to effectively communicate with the public in
an emergency, particularly a fast-moving emergency such as a chemical weapons accident, can
236

 217

motivate the public’s response and thus can save lives. Crisis communications skills and
capabilities built to serve CSEPP easily translate to other emergency situations as well, such as
conventional hazardous materials incidents, wildfire, or weather emergencies.

CSEPP built a suite of training programs to enhance state and local crisis communications. The
training programs were directed to PIOs and PAOs and to emergency management leaders and
public officials who might serve as spokespersons during an emergency response. They covered
a variety of topics including effective communication at news conferences, interaction with
media, public education, and use of social media as a communication tool. Argonne National
Laboratory (Argonne) staff played a substantial role in the development and execution of these
programs, working in conjunction with CMA, FEMA THD, and state and local jurisdictions.
Argonne’s Public Affairs Science and Technology (PAST) Fusion Cell was responsible for risk
and crisis communication training developed for CSEPP and other programs.237

Prior to 2020, virtually all CSEPP risk and crisis communication training was conducted in-
person – either at a local sponsor’s location or hosted on-site at Argonne. As events unfolded in
February and March 2020, it became clear that there would be long-term obstacles to holding
classroom training sessions and to having instructors or trainees travel to attend them due to the
risk of Covid-19 infection. In addition, many potential trainees were caught up with pandemic
response duties and would not have time or energy to spare for training sessions. All in-person
training courses scheduled for the Blue Grass and Pueblo CSEPP communities during the 2020
calendar year were cancelled.

Toward the end of March 2020, the PAST Fusion Cell in collaboration with sponsors at CMA
and FEMA THD began to brainstorm about how to adapt the risk and crisis communications
training program to fit the new realities and meet new needs that had arisen due to the pandemic.
New virtual and “on demand” training formats, along with a broader range of training topics,
were identified and developed in order to support preparedness in the federal, state, and local
CSEPP communities.

This lesson addresses the adjustments that were made to the CSEPP risk and crisis
communications training program in 2020. Many of these changes and enhancements influenced
later CSEPP training programs could be applied to future crisis communication training in other
contexts.

5.2.3 Issue

How to conduct risk and crisis communications training during a pandemic in ways that are both
safe and effective, with particular emphasis on overcoming the pedagogical challenges posed by
the pandemic situation.

237
 See https://pastfusion.anl.gov/.

 218

5.2.4 Solution

Adapting to the pandemic required rethinking several aspects of risk and crisis communications
training simultaneously. It was not simply a matter of taking existing training and converting it to
a new distance-learning format, although even that was nontrivial and had many considerations,
as described below. The training program also had to be adapted to serve the community in
different ways than before. The training audience – primarily PIOs/PAOs and spokespersons –
were very busy with pandemic response on top of their “normal” load of emergencies, and
needed short-format training that could be taken without a large time block commitment. In
addition, the pandemic was affecting not just training but also how public affairs activities were
being conducted, with an increased emphasis on social media and on conducting remote
interviews and news conferences. These new ways of doing business required new skills and
knowledge, in turn requiring new training topics and approaches. Key elements of the adapted
program are detailed below.

Webinars and Short Videos

The training program produced a series of webinars, interviews, and short informative “how to”
videos addressing specific elements of risk and crisis communications. The shorter format
(ranging from five minutes to about an hour) and video format, delivered online, meant that busy
communications officers and public officials could tune in to receive training on their own
schedule, during a break or lunch period or after hours, without compromising their primary
work commitments. The goals were to sharpen skills or introduce relevant aspects of operating in
the current pandemic environment rather than more comprehensive “starting from scratch”
training. Many of the topics addressed derived from specific requests from the CSEPP
community over the course of conference calls and other communications.

The short briefings and webinars included three series: Flash Briefings, Sharpen your Skills, and
Wednesday Webinars. Below is a summary description of these efforts from March 2020 to
February 2021, as well as a comprehensive listing of the topics presented in each series:

Flash Briefings

These podcast-style interviews featured risk and crisis communication experts and other
professionals from various fields addressing current topical issues. They ranged between 5 and
15 minutes in length. Written content summaries and videos were available online.238 Topics
included:
 • Emerging technology to help PIOs with their information management and sharing.
 • More effective messaging with diversity, equity, and inclusion as part of a
 communications strategy.
 • Current state of online (mis) and (dis) information.
 • Best practices to identify and battle the burnout factor.
 • Tips and tricks to teach and communicate online.

238
 See the Argonne PAST Fusion Cell webpage, available at https://pastfusion.anl.gov/category/flashbriefings/.
 Note that these are the products of CSEPP and other sponsoring programs.

 219

 • New study on coronavirus television news coverage.
 • Using the Resilience Analysis and Planning Tool (RAPT) for crisis messaging.
 • Help for PIOs to legitimate organizations in the not-for-profit world.
 • Tailoring crisis messaging to various cultural communities.
 • The “why” of boosting online video messaging and the “how” to do it.
 • Best practices to keep the public engaged and mentally healthy in a crisis.
 • Unleashing the professional communicators in the coronavirus response.
 • Why the news media may not have been reporting jurisdictions’ coronavirus messages.
 • Must-have best practices for online coronavirus messaging.
 • Keeping it real about the coronavirus: facts vs fiction.

Sharpen Your Skills

These “how to” videos addressed topics focusing on producing live videos for online distribution
and interactions with social media. They ranged from 3 to 7 minutes in length. All were available
on demand for streaming online.239
 • Lights, Camera, Action…LIVE.
 • Effective Livestreaming.
 • Social Media Listening/Monitoring.
 • Social Media During Emergencies.
 • Social Media Landscape.
 • Protecting Your Agency from Social Media Trolls.
 • Message Maps.

Wednesday Webinars

These lunchtime webinars featured subject matter experts from across the communication and
public information landscape. The expert delivered a presentation, followed by a question-and-
answer (Q/A) segment, about an hour altogether. They were held in real time but recorded
sessions, presentations, and associated handouts were made available online.240
 • Improving Communication and Outreach for Vulnerable Populations.
 • Understanding False Information in the Digital Era.
 • How GIS Can Inform Your Public Messaging.
 • Livestreaming Video in an Emergency.
 • Video Teleconferencing Showdown.
 • Trolls and Their Toll – on YOU!

239
 See the Argonne PAST Fusion Cell webpage, https://pastfusion.anl.gov/sharpenyourskills/.
240
 See the Argonne PAST Fusion Cell webpage, https://pastfusion.anl.gov/wednesday-webinars/.

 220

Remote Classroom Sessions

Another component of training in the new environment was to adapt existing training, formerly
delivered in person, for delivery via distance learning. That involved attention to the format of
the training as well as the mechanism of delivery, to allow effective interaction between
instructor and trainees. One could not simply videotape an instructor delivering a PowerPoint
presentation – both the presentation and the delivery approach had to be adapted to engage
trainees’ attention and encourage interaction. This required some experimentation and research
regarding the various platforms available for distance learning.

It should be noted that although adaptation to distance learning required extra time and attention
to the details of training delivery, it did offer advantages as well. First, travel time and cost were
eliminated. Second, to some degree it made it easier for trainees to commit, since they did not
need to travel to a different location or be absent from their offices for an extended period. Third
and perhaps most important, it meant that class rosters could be filled by personnel from various
locations and organizations. A full class roster is desirable to maximize the efficiency of
instruction (i.e., make full use of the instructors’ time) and in some cases to provide a critical
mass of trainees, especially when part of the class involved breaking into smaller groups for
participatory activities (i.e., to have enough trainees to form multiple groups). It could sometimes
be difficult to recruit enough trainees from one location to fill out a roster; but if trainees were
participating remotely, the net can be cast more widely. CSEPP training sessions were assembled
with trainees from both remaining chemical stockpile locations (Pueblo, Colorado, and
Richmond, Kentucky) as well as Army and FEMA regional and headquarters personnel from
other locations. Having such a diverse student body offered other advantages as well in terms of
networking and sharing of diverse experiences during the class sessions.

Adapting the Format

As noted, in a distance learning environment, it is not sufficient to simply show existing
presentations remotely or by video. When remote, the instructor does not have the advantage of
being able to gauge students’ interest level and comprehension through personal observation –
the “read” one gets in a classroom setting. To compensate, a few strategies were used:
 • Breaking up lectures. Lecture presentations were livened up with embedded video and
 other breaks to avoid attention fatigue.
 • Shortening instruction time. Even with modifications to how the course was presented in
 a remote environment, it was also necessary to shorten the overall amount of instruction
 time. Whereas an in-person session could reasonably run for a full workday (with
 appropriate breaks), experience showed that the demands of virtual learning were such
 that 2- to 4-hour blocks of instruction per day were most effective.
 • Building in interaction. Short assignments or Q/A sessions were interspersed to
 encourage or almost force interaction among trainees and with the instructor. This
 interaction was key to the training experience to bring out students’ questions and allow
 the instructor to adjust to the audience.

 221

 • Doing a dry run ahead of time. Instructors adapting to a remote-learning environment
 benefited by doing a dry run of their presentation and planned interactions ahead of time
 to iron out any technical glitches and ensure that handoffs and transitions from one
 section or activity to the next could be handled smoothly.

Supporting Equipment and Software

While the requirements for delivering training via distance learning were not trivial, for the most
part, the CSEPP classes could be conducted using resources already in place. Trainees needed a
computer with a camera and microphone (or for some sessions, even a smartphone would
suffice) and internet connectivity. In general, the means for delivering remote training were the
same as for holding a virtual meeting – any platform that supported the ability to converse and to
show slides and video to the group would suffice. Trainees already had the means and were
familiar with the platforms through day-to-day use.

If available, certain platform features beyond presentation and conversation could be useful. For
example, the ability for trainees to ask questions in a sidebar chat or by a “raised hand” indicator
helped with interaction. A polling feature could also be useful for interaction – for example, the
instructor could query the group on who has used a given social media platform or visited a
certain website. In addition, the ability to form subgroups among the participants helped support
breakout sessions. Training sessions often involved a plenary, lecture-oriented session followed
by breakout sessions where trainees worked in small groups to develop assigned materials, then
reported back to the whole group. Side-by-side comparisons of multiple platforms helped
identify which one best met identified training needs. Doing a dry run, as mentioned above, with
the instructor and one or two trainee representatives also helped ensure that all of the needed
features and functions were available and working.

5.2.5 Potential Application

The Covid-19 pandemic forced a new approach to risk and crisis communication training. While
in-person training has significant advantages and a comfort level for both instructors and
trainees, adapting to the pandemic situation led CSEPP to test and evaluate other approaches that
will likely endure past the end of the pandemic, and which can be extended to other types of
emergency management programs.241

Most jurisdictions with dedicated emergency management personnel will already have the
wherewithal to execute remote training – essentially, computers with an internet connection and
meetings software. Across the country, millions of school children used similar resources to
attend school virtually during the worst of the pandemic.

By lowering costs, reducing the time commitment, and making it easier to fill a class roster,
virtual training can lower the threshold for participation and, in a sense, make training more
available to local jurisdictions. Emergency personnel can watch short, informative videos

241
 Per an interview with Karen Jolley Nikol, Chief, Public Affairs, U.S. Army Chemical Materials Activity, the use
 of remote-format public affairs training was expected to continue after pandemic restrictions ceased.

 222

whenever they have a few minutes or an hour available. Longer, remote training sessions need to
be scheduled and generally mean arranging simultaneous availability for a group of people.
However, the group can be at diverse locations, and the participants need not travel to a central
location to take part. The downside is that participants at their normal duty stations may be more
easily distracted by the normal run of phone calls, messages, and general interruptions in the
work environment.

For any type of remote training, instructors must adapt materials to fit the format and may need
to master some new skills to do so. Details such as lighting, camera angle, and background can
enhance or detract from the training message. For longer sessions, it is necessary to break up
lectures, adding interest with videos, and build in exercises and Q/A sessions to stimulate
interaction with trainees. Working with experts such as reporters, video production staff, or other
online presentation experts can be very useful for instructors, along with doing a dry run of
training ahead of time.

5.2.6 References

This lesson was developed primarily from interviews with Karen Nikol, lead PAO for the Army
CSEPP Office, and with Argonne National Laboratory personnel involved in the CSEPP risk and
crisis communications program: Brett Hansard, Don Hanscom, and Eric Singer.

For further information, see the Argonne PAST Fusion Cell webpage, https://pastfusion.anl.gov/.

5.3 News Media Interview Training and Exercising242
Handling news media interviews is an important component of crisis communications for
emergency management spokespersons and public officials. CSEPP conducted training and
exercising to ensure that those speaking about an emergency response could use interviews to
convey information effectively and instill confidence in the response effort.

5.3.1 Summary

Disasters and emergency situations affecting the public will attract intense interest from the news
media, and media reporting will serve as a conduit for information to the public. Emergency
managers must be prepared to carry out effective public communications through the medium of
news media. The exchange between reporter and spokesperson helps shape a story; influence the
public; and provide important information to targeted audiences before, during, and after an
emergency.

The Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program (CSEPP) worked with public
information/public affairs officers (PIOs/PAOs), government officials, leadership, and subject
matter experts who might be called upon to interact with news media during an emergency
response. The goal was to prepare participants to work confidently with reporters and make their
points more effectively. This was accomplished through training courses and through realistic
simulations of media interaction during exercises.

This lesson learned summarizes some of the processes and information conveyed through
CSEPP crisis communications training and exercises to prepare emergency management
personnel for news media interaction during an emergency response.

The CSEP Program specifically addressed hazardous materials incidents involving U.S. Army-
owned chemical weapons and associated facilities. However, being prepared for news media
interaction has value for response to any type of emergency that might attract media interest.

5.3.2 Background

Conducting effective crisis communications is an essential part of any emergency response,
particularly for events that attract public and media interest such as wildfires, hazardous
materials incidents, severe weather events, and terrorism or mass shooting events. The public
will learn about the emergency and the response through a variety of means, including direct
warnings and instructions from public officials (e.g., the emergency alert system), social media,
and through reporting by media news outlets on television, radio, print media, and online.

For such events, effective communication to the public through media reporting is an important
element of public safety. It also will significantly affect the public’s perception of the emergency
response – and the public is well served when they perceive that the response is timely and
professionally managed.
242

 224

In CSEPP, the assumption was that any emergency event involving chemical weapons would
attract intense public and media interest, and it was important that responders be prepared to
answer questions accurately and completely. Training and realistic exercising of crisis
communications was therefore an essential component of preparedness. And an essential
component of preparing for public and media interest was knowing how to handle interviews by
journalists.

CSEPP assisted Army, Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA), and state and local
emergency responders in preparing for media interviews (and more generally, for media
response and reporting) through training and through simulated interviews and reporting during
exercises. Training and exercising opportunities were offered to PIOs/PAOs, emergency
managers, subject matter experts, and public officials who might communicate with the media
during an emergency response.

5.3.3 Issue

How to prepare PIOs/PAOs and emergency management spokespersons to effectively
communicate to the public through media interviews.

5.3.4 Solution

As with all aspects of emergency response, preparation for effective crisis communications
begins with planning. It is important to recognize that an emergency situation can attract
significant media interest, and consider in advance how to deal with numerous media calls and
requests for interviews. Responsibility for speaking to the media should be allocated, and those
personnel should be prepared through appropriate training and practice in drills and exercises. In
CSEPP, PIOs/PAOs and spokespersons had the opportunity to receive specialized training and to
participate in annual exercises that incorporated simulations of media interviews and news
conferences. The exercises sought to reproduce, as realistically as possible, the pressure and
environment that could accompany interactions with news media. Training and exercise
processes are detailed below.

While the CSEPP exercise program always incorporated elements of distance learning and
remote interviews, those aspects of media interaction were greatly magnified in 2020 due to the
Covid-19 pandemic and requirements for social distancing. The program adapted to the new
situation by increasing its emphasis on skills and capabilities associated with conducting
interviews remotely.

Training

CSEPP provided training specific to crisis communications for on-post (Army) PAOs, post
commanders, off-post PIOs, emergency directors, public officials, and others who might be
called upon to speak to the media or public in an emergency. All of the trainees were in
government service and generally had positions with responsibilities in communications,
emergency management, or executive direction. However, in most cases crisis communications
was only one of their many day-to-day duties. Some had experience in communicating during

 225

actual emergencies, others not. The purpose of the training (as well as exercising, described
below) was to provide expertise and hone skills for this important aspect of their duties in a safe
environment where mistakes could be made and lessons learned without consequences to public
safety. Through practice in training sessions, spokespersons could build a level of skill and
confidence to be an effective communicator during an actual emergency. Many trainees found it
valuable to repeat the training as a refresher, gaining more experience and additional information
each time.

Key points in training for media interviews include:
 • Having a “message map” – a few key points to convey during an interview. In an
 emergency situation, typically the public wants to know what happened (source and
 nature of the emergency), how does it affect them (e.g., how do they stay safe, will
 schools be open tomorrow), and what is being done in response.
 • Staying on point through an interview or briefing. A reporter may ask questions that
 address topics other than the current crisis and response; for example, asking who is at
 fault for a hazardous material spill. Spokespersons should be prepared for such questions
 and be able to address them directly – “there will be an investigation of all the
 circumstances” – but return to a topic that can be productively addressed – “but for now,
 the priority is ensuring public safety and limiting damage.” Specific techniques can be
 learned to “bridge” back to the speaker’s message map.
 • Time and content management. Spokespersons should be aware of the types of interviews
 and how they differ in terms of time available and content desired. Radio and TV
 interviews generally will be time-limited due to the nature of the medium; they will
 receive only a few minutes of air time. Print media interviews may be longer and more
 in-depth since the format allows for longer articles and more detail.

As remote or virtual interviews became more common, CSEPP training emphasized preparing
for interviews from the home or office. Key points for success in this format include:
 • Remote interviews generally can be carried out with equipment and software that the
 spokesperson already has; a computer or smartphone with internet connectivity is about
 all that is needed. Their built-in cameras and microphones are sufficient.
 • It is worth taking a few minutes to “stage” the interview. Minimize audio or visual
 distractions that might interfere with conveying the desired message. Care should be
 taken with camera angle and lighting in video interviews, so that the spokesperson can be
 seen clearly and looks natural. Background visual or audio distractions should be
 minimized to the extent feasible.
 • The spokesperson’s message map can be displayed on their computer for quick reference.
 Even a few key words on a smartphone can be helpful as a reminder.

Exercising

In CSEPP exercises, exercise planners and controllers devoted considerable time and effort to
providing a realistic and challenging simulation of the news media environment in a major

 226

emergency. Broadcast (television and radio), print coverage, and social media discussion of the
emergency were simulated through secure online outlets. Experienced journalists and media
trainers played the role of inquisitive reporters requesting interviews and attending news
briefings.

To provide a realistic media simulation, crisis communications staff from Argonne National
Laboratory set up a simulated news media environment under the auspices of the Exercise
Training Network (ETN) and the Exercise News Network (ENN), which were accessed through
a secure, password-protected website. Exercise participants received login information ahead of
time. The website contained simulated news reports, including print stories as well as audio and
video from interviews conducted during the exercise, all in real time (Figures 5.3-1 and 5.3-2).
Exercise news conferences and briefings were carried “live.” The intent was to simulate the
appearance and content of a real-world news outlet that would cover an important local or
national breaking news event.

Reporter role-players (termed “mock media”)243 requested information from exercise participants
through phone calls and interviews and participated in news briefings. Mock media reporters
represented different fictional news outlets and produced real-time stories based on their
reporting, which exercise participants were expected to monitor and respond to, as they would in
an actual event (Figure 5.3-3). CSEPP mock media were personnel with a background and
experience in actual news reporting, and they questioned exercise participants as one would
expect reporters to do: by posing challenging questions.

 Figure 5.3-1 ETN Website Home Page

243
 See Lesson 5.4 for additional detail on use of mock media in exercises.

 227

  Figure 5.3-2 ETN Print, Radio, and Television Stories

 Figure 5.3-3 Example ENN Story

 228

In terms of advance preparation, Argonne staff worked with exercise participants to provide
login information to the ETN website and ensure they could access the information posted on it.
Exercise participants received a general briefing about the presence of mock media and that there
would be inquiries and requests for interviews; however, in the interest of realism they were not
provided with any further information as to how many calls there would be, what questions
would be asked, or any other aspects of media play during the exercise.

The ETN network built a capability for remote video interviews through a “virtual studio”
complete with lower thirds that identified the spokesperson and reporter (Figure 5.3-4) and the
ability to intersperse video clips during the interview. During exercises, mock media reporters
conducted live remote interviews with exercise participants using video calls or virtual meeting
software; the interviews were streamed live to the ETN website (with a video file also posted
afterward). During the Covid-19 pandemic, as social distancing forced real-world news media to
move to this type of interview format, so did ETN.

In terms of exercise security, limiting ETN access to those with login permission ensured that
information relating to the scenario was not “leaked” to the wider public where it could be
mistaken for an actual emergency. In addition, exercise labeling was used throughout to ensure
that the information posted could not be mistaken for an actual event.

 Figure 5.3-4 Example CSEPP Exercise Video – Live Remote Interview

5.3.5 Potential Application

Any large-scale or unusual emergency situation is likely to attract significant media interest. In
the age of internet connectivity and social media, news of such an event travels more or less

 229

instantaneously on many networks, from almost anywhere in the world. Reporters may travel to
the scene, but increasingly they do not need to do so in order to contact and interview personnel
responsible for emergency response. The threshold has been lowered; emergency managers and
PIOs/PAOs should expect and be prepared for news media inquiries in any emergency situation.

Training is a key step in preparing for news media interviews. Various training options are
available. While reading and lectures can be valuable, training should ideally include a vigorous
hands-on (in-person or remote) practice session in which trainees undergo a simulated interview
to gain live practice in a realistic way. The courses and lessons developed by CSEPP can be
applied in virtually any jurisdiction that has an emergency response capability.

Exercises offer an opportunity to reinforce training and practice techniques. CSEPP-developed
processes for media interaction during exercises can be applied in any functional or full-scale
exercise. The ETN and ENN sites and associated resources have been used for dozens of non-
CSEPP exercises on behalf of multiple federal sponsors, including the U.S. Department of
Energy and the U.S. Department of Homeland Security, and covering a wide range of scenarios,
from natural disasters to radiological incidents to acts of terror (Figure 5.3-5).

If resources are not available to take on the full range of mock media play used by CSEPP,
selected components can be implemented. A range of options are available. For example, a news
briefing conducted in the absence of an audience is better than no briefing at all; a step better
would be a news conference with organizational employees or journalism students playing the
roles of reporters; and better yet would be a news conference with actual reporters or
experienced crisis communications trainers playing the roles of reporters. Similarly, a live or
remote interview can be arranged using anyone as an interviewer, but an experienced reporter or
trainer will enhance the value by making it a more realistic and challenging interaction.

 Figure 5.3-5 Example ENN non-CSEPP Application
