5.4 Use of Mock Media in Exercises244
Use of trained personnel to play the role of media representatives at exercises improves the
challenge and value of the exercise for spokespersons and other personnel involved in
emergency public information.

5.4.1 Summary

Disasters of any kind attract significant attention from news media. An emergency at an Army
chemical storage installation would be certain to draw significant media attention. Therefore, risk
communication functions were incorporated into preparation for a chemical event and were
demonstrated during CSEPP exercises. In order to provide the best training and most realistic
play, controllers played the role of news media reporters – mock media – during these exercises.
They attended news conferences, interviewed exercise players in the field, and made calls to
participants from remote locations. They produced news stories including television reports,
newspaper articles, radio spots, blog entries, and social media messages; exercise participants got
to experience how their actions and statements were represented in the media, mimicking a real
event.

The success of the mock media program in CSEPP could be attributed to organizational buy-in
and funding; careful planning; active control during exercises; and the use of professional,
experienced staff.

Potential application for mock media is wide given that any emergency response requires
effective crisis communication, and use of mock media is an important training tool for public
information staff and spokespersons. Use of mock media can be scaled appropriately to the size
of the exercise.

5.4.2 Background

Disasters of any kind are often big news events. Local media are quickly drawn to the story and,
if significant, regional, national, and even international media will begin reporting, both on-scene
and remotely.

An emergency at an Army chemical storage installation would be certain to draw significant
media attention. In addition to the potential health hazard, any incident involving chemical
weapons would have unique characteristics as a potential news story. The chemicals were
produced as weapons of war and were stored under conditions of high security, evoking a certain
air of mystery and possibly fear. Significant resources were invested in emergency preparedness
activities which, while providing the public with preparedness awareness, also highlighted the
proximity of these dangerous materials.

244
 Lesson written in 2012. An article based on this lesson was published in the Journal of Emergency Management,
      March 2014.

 232

Experience bore out this expectation. Various minor occurrences at chemical installations, none
of which involved danger to the public, nevertheless received extensive media coverage. Two
examples: in 2002, reports of a possible intruder at the Deseret Chemical Deport were covered
by CNN245 and the Associated Press and reported on as far away as New Zealand.246 In 2009, the
accidental sounding of emergency alarms at Umatilla Chemical Depot was covered widely in
local and national media.247

The CSEP Program recognized the potential for media interest and the importance of clear
communications to the public. Requirements and guidance for emergency public information
were incorporated from the beginning of the program, including demonstration and evaluation of
the public information function. In designing its program, CSEPP drew and expanded upon the
practices that had been established in the earlier Radiological Emergency Preparedness Program
(REPP) for communities surrounding nuclear power plants.

In an emergency, public affairs and public information officers (PAOs/PIOs)248 would be
expected to manage information about the event in a variety of ways, typically set out in
organizational plans and procedures. They would develop information for the public in the form
of news releases, public statements, news conferences, website posts, and social media messages.
They would monitor information circulating in traditional media and in social media to
determine what information the public is receiving and to assess how the event and response
efforts are being perceived, and they would address rumors and correct misinformation. In
addition to disseminating information directly, PAOs/PIOs would also be responsible for
preparing executive officials for their role as spokespersons. All of these functions are
demonstrated during CSEPP exercises.

5.4.3 Issue

Exercises create an artificial reality to enable players to respond “as if” a disaster has occurred.
Without meaningful role play depicting the media, players do not have the opportunity to interact
with reporters as they would in a real event, nor appreciate the intense media scrutiny they would
be under. The phones don’t ring, and news conferences proceed without difficult questions. In a
quiet environment, both the training value and the capabilities-assessment value of the exercise
are dramatically reduced.

245
 “Intruder Spotted at Army Chemical depot,” Sept. 5, 2002, https://www.cbsnews.com/news/intruder-spotted-at-
 army-depot/. Accessed August 2025.
246
 “Intruder at Chemical Depot,” Sept. 6, 2002, TV New Zealand, http://tvnz.co.nz/content/129667, accessed in
 2011.
247
 Haight, A., 2009, “Ooops: Alarm at Umatilla Chemical Depot Accidentally Activated,” Oregon Environmental
 News, November 11, available at
 http://www.oregonlive.com/environment/index.ssf/2009/11/ooops_alarm_at_umatilla_chemic.html.
248
 Army installations use the term Public Affairs Officer for personnel tasked with communicating to the public;
 off-post jurisdictions generally use the term Public Information Officer.

 233

5.4.4 Solution

Exercise control staff can be used to enhance exercise realism by portraying reporters. Such staff
are referred to generally as “mock media.” Mock media help demonstrate the crisis
communication function in the same way that smoke generators are used to enhance realism for
accident-scene responders and moulage is used to simulate injuries for medical responders.

Mock media were used extensively in CSEPP. Described below are some of the ways they were
used, as well as keys to successful use of the mock media in exercises.

CSEPP Mock Media Exercise Role

For CSEPP exercises, mock media were divided into two teams, field and SimCell.249 Field
teams:
 • Traveled to key exercise locations such as the Army installation, offsite response
 agencies (counties, states, forward command posts), hospitals, and the Joint Information
 Center (JIC) in an effort to interview key participants, often on camera, and document
 response activities.
 • Produced news stories, based on material produced by players and on player interactions,
 using a variety of media – print, television, radio, websites, and social media. Television-
 news-style stand-ups and anchor news reports about the emergency were videotaped and
 viewed by exercise participants as part of play during the course of the exercise to show
 how the response efforts were being reported.
 • Participated in news conferences and briefings at the JIC and elsewhere, asking questions
 of participants and producing follow-up news stories as appropriate.
 • Edited draft stories in a studio maintained by a member of the mock media; all stories
 were then uploaded to a password-protected website specifically created for the purpose.
 The Exercise News Network (ENN) website was accessible to players and would
 typically be viewed by PAO/PIO staff assigned to media monitoring.
 • Some field team members might function remotely to gather news and write stories, just
 like a reporter operating from a distant newsroom.

SimCell mock media:

 • Injected scripted information requests into play – directed to particular organizations or
 players at particular times, to elicit information about the events and, sometimes, to
 “drive” play. SimCell controllers would not normally write stories but might provide
 information to field reporters to include in their reports.

249
 SimCell is short for Simulation Cell, an exercise control technique in which a group of exercise controllers role-
 play (portraying members of the public and agencies, officials, and organizations that are not participating in the
 exercise) from a centralized location, interacting with players via telephone, email, or social media. For more
 details on using a SimCell to enhance an exercise, see lesson 3.5.

 234

 • Modified the pre-scripted injects to players, as appropriate, based on mock media reports
 or other information developed during the exercise.
 • Occasionally injected unscripted or “ad hoc” messages into play when the situation
 warranted (and in coordination with the exercise co-directors).

Immediately following the exercise, mock media field and SimCell team members gathered to
review and analyze the day’s events and player responses. An informal report was prepared for
the exercise evaluation team summarizing the mock media team’s findings – positive or negative
– for each of the participating jurisdictions. While mock media staff were not part of the formal
evaluation process, their input served as an important part of the evaluation process.

Keys to Successful Mock Media Implementation

Successful use of mock media in the CSEP Program required careful planning, preparation, buy-
in from exercise participants, and explicit ground rules.

 • Buy-in. The news media are critical elements of an emergency public information
 program, helping responders deliver important health and safety messages to the public.
 Mock media controllers require players – primarily PIOs and PAOs as well as other
 spokespersons – to respond as they would in a real event, practicing and allowing for role
 play in a safe environment. As with a real-world response, mock media reporters will
 expect prompt and accurate responses to phoned questions; produce stories that must be
 reviewed for accuracy; show up at field locations, often requesting on-camera interviews;
 and ask difficult and challenging questions at every opportunity. At a minimum, they add
 to the number of tasks that exercise players must perform; beyond that, they may expose
 shortcomings in the capabilities of response organizations. CSEPP embraced these
 challenges and incorporated them into program policy and guidance. Exercise planners
 integrated mock media play in the Master Scenario Event Lists (MSELs) and extent-of-
 play agreements (XPAs), and PIOs and spokespersons expected to interact with mock
 media. In CSEPP, extensive resources were devoted to support development and
 implementation of the mock media program, including new techniques such as live
 webcasting of exercise news conferences and creating the Exercise News Network as an
 exercise surrogate for actual news outlets.
 • Pre-exercise planning. In the months before an exercise, the mock media team leader
 assisted the exercise planning team in developing messages to be injected into player
 activities and in identifying locations for mock media field team visits based on the
 particular site and exercise scenario. Immediately before the exercise, they scouted the
 area to be sure the field teams could efficiently cover the day’s key news events. (The
 limited timeframe for the exercises made it necessary to pre-plan certain items, such as
 transportation routes, to avoid wasting valuable exercise time.)
 • Live control during the exercise. Mock media monitored site and community activities
 and supplied other team members with information, including background information
 and news stories about local conditions, and information about key players to help assure
 as much realism as possible during the exercise.

 235

 • Staff continuity. The CSEPP mock media program was able to recruit and maintain a
 skilled staff of professionals, including former journalists, public affairs/public
 information officers, videographers, web designers, and others with media skills to
 perform various exercise-related functions. Staff turnover was low, allowing for
 continuity and strengthening of the team over time as they gained experience.
 • Input to evaluation. At CSEPP exercises, exercise control and evaluation functions were
 largely separated; that is, there was a separate staff of evaluators in addition to
 controllers. Mock media controllers in the field typically interacted with exercise players
 while evaluators observed and took notes. However, mock media controllers also
 provided input to evaluators. SimCell mock media took notes on the responses they
 received when interacting with participants, which were shared with evaluators, along
 with the mock media’s informal assessment of those interactions.

Adaptation to New Media

The media environment changed dramatically over the more than 30 years that CSEPP exercises
were conducted, primarily with the diminishment of legacy media such as newspapers, broadcast
TV and radio, and the growth of internet-based news and social media. While traditional media
remained important, the mock media program was adapted to encompass a wide range of digital
media such as blogs, news organizations’ websites, and social media including Facebook and
Twitter (later X). Argonne staff posted stories to the ENN exercise website, where exercise
participants could access information generated by mock media in real time.

5.4.5 Potential Application

Robust, expert crisis communication is an important aspect of disaster response. It contributes to
the effectiveness of response and also to the public’s opinion of the agencies involved. Any
small, moderate, or large-scale event will require effective crisis communication, regardless of
the hazard involved – flood, tornado, terrorist event, aviation disaster, hazmat, nuclear power
plant accident, etc. A report on the public information response to the Three Mile Island nuclear
power plant accident in 1979 said it well: “The accident at Three Mile Island became a test of the
ability of reporters to deal with technological complexity and factual uncertainty while working
under enormous pressure. It was, equally, a test of the ability of Met Ed and NRC public
information officials to inform the public under crisis conditions.”250 Exercise designers learned
much from this event and thus incorporated crisis communications, with mock media serving as
reporters, to simulate reality as closely as possible.

Mock media play can be scaled to the size of the exercise. For large-scale exercises such as
REPP and national-level homeland security exercises, given the investment involved in the
exercise, it makes sense to fully incorporate professional-level mock media into exercise
planning and execution to ensure that emergency public information programs are sufficiently
prepared to respond to the media “surge” that would follow a large-scale incident. For smaller
exercises at a state or local level, the same concept can be applied on a lesser scale, where even a

250
 Staff report to the President’s Commission on the Accident at Three Mile Island: Report of the Public’s Right to
 Information Task Force, Oct. 1979, https://catalog.hathitrust.org/Record/000043901, p. 1.

 236

handful of mock media reporters can push PIOs/PAOs beyond their comfort zones. It is also
possible to mount an effective mock media operation using only remote resources (mock
reporters participating from off-site locations) whereby travel costs are avoided. While such an
approach does limit the range of interactions (e.g., no in-person interviews), it can still provide a
strong test of public affairs capabilities. A small on-site mock media team could also be
augmented by available local resources. For example, there may be agency employees with a
media background, or journalism students from a nearby university. In such instances, it is
critical that the various mock media players be integrated into a single cohesive “news team,” to
include some pre-exercise training of the novices so that all media role players are able to work
in sync and avoid errors that can throw an exercise off course. (In this sense, the difference
between “real world” and “exercise” play is clear, as an actual emergency event may feature
scores of independent-acting reporters, whereas a training exercise by its nature requires a certain
degree of coordination to ensure that goals and objectives are met.)

(accessed February 2024).

5.5 Integrating Social Media into Drills and Exercises251
As social media became an important source of information for the public, CSEPP incorporated
social media play into drills and exercises.

5.5.1 Summary

In recent years, social media has become an increasingly important and effective tool for
agencies to use in communicating with the public and news media, particularly during an
emergency response. The challenge, however, has been in designing a way to safely and
realistically test social media plans and procedures in preparedness drills and exercises. Using
active agency accounts that are viewable by the general public is problematic, because scenario
details would likely alarm or confuse followers. On the other hand, proprietary software
programs that simulate social media are costly and do not offer the realism necessary for a robust
test of capabilities.252

A third option was pioneered by the Chemical Stockpile Emergency Preparedness Program
(CSEPP) – using actual social media platforms in a closed and protected environment, so that all
the information stayed in the exercise realm and was only visible to approved participants. The
benefits of this approach included unparalleled realism, universal accessibility, low cost, and
valuable hands-on experience with real-world social media tools. This approach did require a
strong commitment to coordination during the exercise planning phase, and a close working
relationship between the exercise management team and participating public affairs partners both
on- and off-site.

CSEPP communities implemented this approach during yearly full-scale CSEPP exercises and in
smaller-scale jurisdictional exercises and drills, with assistance from the Argonne National
Laboratory Public Affairs Science and Technology (PAST) Fusion Cell team.

5.5.2 Background

The communication of timely and accurate information to people potentially at risk from a
hazardous event is at the heart of an effective emergency response. Whether the information is
focused on saving lives, protecting property, or calming fears, messages that are clear, concise,
and instructive will increase the public’s understanding and allow people to take practical steps
to protect themselves before, during, and after a crisis. Conversely, the failure to provide such
information often results in misinformation and speculation that may unnecessarily alarm people
or cause them to act in ways that actually increase their potential to experience harm. As was
noted in the aftermath of Hurricane Katrina, people suffer when information is neither timely nor
accurate, yet “achieving both timeliness and accuracy in an environment of 24/7 news and

251

252
 Incorporating Social Media Into Your Exercises #SMEM, by Kim Stephens, idisaster 2.0 blog post, June 4, 2015,
 https://idisaster.wordpress.com/. Accessed January 2021.

 238

information and ever-expanding media is difficult in the best of times; in the midst of chaos and
uncertainty, it can be an immense challenge.”253

In the early 21st century, social media and digital communication technologies have rapidly and
undeniably changed the way information is monitored, collected, and shared, resulting in a
dramatic increase in the number of channels – each with advantages and disadvantages – that are
available to communicate emergency messages. It is clear that the emergence of social media
and digital communication are redefining the roles of government and media. The Boston
Marathon bombing in 2013 dramatically highlighted the importance of social media “as a key
communications tool to keep the media and frightened citizens accurately informed about what
was going on.”254 Yet the underlying goal (and challenge) of effective crisis communication
remains essentially the same: getting the right information to the right people at the right time so
they can make the right decisions.

Social media allows users to exchange information about topics of interest in real-time. Social
media is distinct from traditional media in that it enables real-time collaboration, sharing, and
discussion by anyone with internet access. Social media continues to have a major impact on the
emergency management community. It shapes how crises are communicated and how response
is coordinated. It provides new and accessible communication platforms that offer the
opportunity to reach more people with fewer filters than ever before. Additionally, social media
technology provides a mechanism for agencies to quickly and directly relay critical information
in a time of crisis to the public, the media, and each other. It is a proven and invaluable resource
for emergency management across the world.

Social media technologies are the standard for rapid information dissemination and can enhance
and amplify an agency’s efforts to reduce the impact of emergencies and disasters on life and
personal property. Social networks can be used as notification systems, in recovery efforts, as
emergency information gathering, and as information repositories. As the way people gather,
create, and share information evolves, emergency managers must continuously examine the ways
in which they engage stakeholders and disseminate and monitor information. New technologies
are constantly in flux, creating challenges for how emergency professionals do their jobs.
Training and practice are essential to ensure that emergency public information teams can
efficiently manage online information in a disaster situation.

5.5.3 Issue

As outlined in Homeland Security Exercise and Evaluation Program (HSEEP) guidance:
“Exercises play a vital role in preparedness. A well-designed exercise provides a low-risk
environment to familiarize personnel with roles and responsibilities; foster meaningful

253
 First Informers in the Disaster Zone: The Lessons of Katrina, Communications and Society Program, p. viii, by
 Albert L. May, The Aspen Institute, Washington, D.C., 2006, available at
 https://www.aspeninstitute.org/publications/first-informers-disaster-zone-lessons-katrina/, accessed August
 2025.
254
 Social Media: Big Lessons from the Boston Marathon Bombing, Government Technology Magazine, by Tod
 Newcombe, September 24, 2014, available at http://www.govtech.com/public-safety/Social-Media-Big-Lessons-
 from-the-Boston-Marathon-Bombing.html, accessed January 2021.

 239

interaction and communication across jurisdictions/organizations; assess and validate plans,
policies, procedures, and capabilities; and identify strengths and areas for improvement.”255 With
the advent and growing importance of social media as an emergency preparedness and response
tool, social media plans, policies, and procedures must be realistically tested in a robust exercise
environment, but without causing public alarm or confusion.

For CSEPP, the exercise environment had to be adaptable to facilitate exercises of various scales,
including special-purpose drills, Army-centric Chemical Accident/Incident Response Assistance
(CAIRA) exercises, and full-scale CSEPP exercises involving multiple jurisdictions and
scenarios that included widespread impact on the public.

5.5.4 Solution

As with all elements of an exercise, the use of social media must occur in a safe, controlled
environment. Standard CSEPP practice included precautions to ensure that exercise play was not
mistaken for a real-world situation – for example, by saying “exercise message” at the beginning
and end of phone calls, marking “EXERCISE” on written products such as news releases, and
using different warning siren sounds to indicate a drill or exercise. The solution pioneered by
CSEPP for social media play continued this practice: it used live, real-world social media
platforms that were set up in such a way that all the information stayed in the exercise realm and
was only visible to approved participants. Special exercise-specific accounts were set up to
contain exercise communications.

Once these exercise-specific accounts were established, they served as stand-ins for the social
media sites that agencies operated in the real-world, and players could use them for training and
exercises in the same way they would in an actual event—to share information, gather
intelligence, monitor the media, engage with the public, etc. This approach was repeatedly tested
in a range of exercise environments, from small stand-alone drills and exercises to large full-
scale exercises with numerous players participating from many agencies and jurisdictions. The
Pueblo and Blue Grass CSEPP communities employed this approach in their annual exercises for
the last several years of the program.

Setting Up Social Media Exercise Accounts

The world of social media can change dramatically in relatively short periods of time. The most
popular sites today may be replaced by new and different sites tomorrow. The virtual social
media exercising in CSEPP focused on the two social media platforms that were most frequently
used for emergency public information at the time – Twitter (X) and Facebook. As other
platforms gain relevance within emergency management, they can also be exercised following
this same process. Additionally, in some instances users can practice and gain experience with
these sites alongside applicable complementary tools, such as Hootsuite.

255
 Homeland Security Exercise and Evaluation Program (HSEEP), U.S. Department of Homeland Security,
 January 2020, p. V., available at https://www.fema.gov/emergency-managers/national-
 preparedness/exercises/hseep, accessed February 2024.

 240

To establish this environment for a CSEPP exercise, each agency was responsible for setting up
its own individual exercise accounts, which were then linked with other participating exercise
accounts to form a “closed loop” network. Argonne staff worked with public affairs officers at
Army installations and local and state emergency management agencies to help create these
exercise-exclusive accounts. In addition to participating agencies, a Simulation Cell (SimCell)
and a Mock Media team simulated social media input from news media and the public through
separate exercise accounts. Special “observer” accounts were also created to provide access for
controllers and evaluators.

Once set up, the accounts remained active and available for all future exercise play. Two
additional general control measures were employed:
 1. Ensuring that exercise accounts were not linked with personal or real-world agency
 accounts; this was the best way to ensure that exercise play stayed, as intended, within
 the exercise realm.
 2. Designating a single team member to create and manage the agency accounts; this person
 was responsible for authorizing use of the accounts for training and exercises and for
 providing general oversight.

Specific protective steps were necessary when setting up exercise accounts on social media
platforms, depending on the unique privacy measures built into each site. For example, in
Twitter there were multiple “Settings” options, the most important of which was to “Protect my
Tweets” so that only those who had been personally approved could see them (Figure 5.5-1).
With this option chosen, a “lock” icon would always be visible next to the account name. This
indicated that the tweets were protected. Users were advised to follow accounts with the lock
icon only and allow only lock-icon accounts to follow them.

 Figure 5.5-1 Screenshots from Twitter showing how to “Protect my Tweets”

 241

Likewise for Facebook, there were multiple options to review and select under “settings,” the
most important of which was to set the status so that only approved “friends” could see the posts.
Under “privacy” users would be asked, “Who can see my stuff?” Again, users were advised to
establish connections (by requesting or accepting friends) only from similarly protected accounts
(Figure 5.5-2).

 Figure 5.5-2 Screenshots from Facebook showing “Who can see my stuff?”

When the initial set-up was complete, the pages were customized further. A photo representing
the agency was included in the profile; and for another level of protection, the following
disclaimer (or some variation) was added on the page:

 —EXERCISE—EXERCISE—EXERCISE—
 This site is for exercise and training purposes only. All events are simulated.
 Do not publish or broadcast as a real event.

Once the social media exercise accounts were established, they were ready to be deployed. Users
ensured that all identified participant accounts were linked together. For Twitter, this meant the
owner’s exercise account and the other participant accounts were both “following” and
“follower.” For Facebook, this meant the owner’s exercise page and the other participant pages
were all “friends.” When using the accounts for exercise play, users were urged to include the
phrase “This is an Exercise” (or some variation) at the beginning of each message to provide an
additional layer of security (Figure 5.5-3).

 242

 Figure 5.5-3 Screenshots from Social Media Accounts Showing
 “Exercise and Training” Disclaimers and Protections

Social Media Exercise Play

Prior to using these accounts in a formal drill or exercise, a dry-run was scheduled in advance,
with all participants logging into their accounts and joining a conference call or webinar to
discuss what they were seeing on the screen. This was an opportunity to make a few test
Facebook posts and Twitter tweets to determine if any final adjustments were needed, prior to
commencing actual exercise play.

During exercises, CSEPP used a SimCell to simulate social media inputs from the public and the
news media to create a more dynamic and realistic exercise environment. This could include pre-
scripted injects based on the scenario, as well as real-time, ad-hoc interactions based on player
actions (Figure 5.5-4).256

256
 See Lesson 5.4, Use of Mock Media in Exercises, and Lesson 3.5, Using a SimCell for Exercise Realism, for
 additional detail on CSEPP SimCell practices.

 Figure 5.5-4 Screenshots from Social Media Exercise Accounts Showing
 Posts and Interactions among Players

5.5.5 Potential Application

FEMA has defined a set of 32 core capabilities that form the basis for effective emergency
response. The Public Information and Warning core capability reads as follows: “Deliver
coordinated, prompt, reliable, and actionable information to the whole community through the
use of clear, consistent, accessible, and culturally and linguistically appropriate methods to
effectively relay information regarding any threat or hazard, as well as the actions being taken
and the assistance being made available, as appropriate.”257 Effective use of social media is
central to meeting the Public Information and Warning core capability and is thus a necessary
component of response exercises.

The established technique for virtual social media exercising can be applied in any exercise
environment, from small, stand-alone drills and functional exercises that focus only on
emergency public information to large, full-scale exercises with multiple players participating
from across agencies and jurisdictions. The technique is equally adaptable to tabletop exercises
and distance exercises, where players may be participating from various remote locations.
Observations and data collection derived by evaluators from monitoring social media activity can
be seamlessly included in the exercise evaluation process.

257
 FEMA webpage, “Mission Areas and Core Capabilities,” https://www.fema.gov/emergency-managers/national-
 preparedness/mission-core-capabilities, accessed August 2025.

 244

CSEPP successfully employed virtual social media exercising in its annual full-scale exercises
from 2009 on, as well as in training, regular drills, and functional exercises among public affairs
staff. Similarly, the U.S. Department of Energy’s National Nuclear Security Administration has
employed virtual social media exercising as a standard part of its exercise program at multiple
sites and facilities around the country.

5.6 Emergency Public Information Issues Identified during
 Exercises258
Five years’ worth of CSEPP exercise reports were surveyed to extract issues relating to
emergency public information. The survey found four themes among the identified issues:
Emergency Alert System (EAS) messages and news releases; coordination of information among
different jurisdictions; internal communications; and information provided by call takers.

5.6.1 Summary

This lesson is based on a review of hundreds of observations relating to emergency public
information, made during dozens of CSEPP exercises. Five years’ worth of exercise reports were
surveyed for each of seven CSEPP sites. Evaluator comments pertaining to emergency public
information were gathered and analyzed. Four themes were identified among the issues
identified: EAS messages and news releases; coordination of information among different
jurisdictions; internal communications; and information provided by call takers. These themes
are described, with suggested solutions provided for each area.

Potential applications for this information include virtually any disaster response organization or
situation. Many of the suggested solutions involve refinement of training and procedures or other
measures that may be carried out in-house or externally at modest cost.

5.6.2 Background

CSEPP exercises were held yearly at each site. Each exercise included demonstration of several
emergency response functions, including provision of information to the public. In the CSEPP
exercise evaluation system, emergency public information was identified as an “emergency
response outcome” (ERO) to be demonstrated by the Army installation and each involved state
and local jurisdiction. The emergency public information ERO included several sub-elements
including dissemination of public health and safety information to the public, dissemination of
public health and safety information to the media, operating a joint information system (JIS), and
activating/operating a joint information center (JIC). In exercises during the study period,
response personnel simulated broadcast of EAS messages to the public, developed press releases,
held news conferences, and interacted with “mock media” (exercise controllers simulating media
personnel from local, regional, and national print, radio, and television organizations) both
in person and over the phone. They also interacted with controllers simulating members of the
public calling with questions and concerns. In short, emergency public information play was
very robust and involved many modes of communicating emergency information to the media
and public.

An independent team of evaluators attended the exercise and observed exercise play. After the
exercise, an after-action report was generated summarizing the exercise demonstrations and
listing observations, strengths, and areas for improvement that the evaluators had identified. The
258
 Lesson written in 2012. An article based on this lesson was published in the Journal of Homeland Security and
 Emergency Management, January 2014.

 247

after-action report was distributed to each of the jurisdictions involved in the exercise. A
collection of such exercise reports was archived electronically on the CSEPP Portal, a website
serving the CSEPP community.

For this lesson, a sampling of exercise reports was examined and the issues (areas for
improvement) pertaining to emergency public information were compiled. Table 5.6-1
summarizes the reports that were examined:

 Table 5.6-1 CSEPP Exercise Reports Surveyed
 Installation Exercise Reports
                         Anniston                  2004–2008
                         Blue Grass                2004–2008
                         Deseret                   2004–2008
                         Newport                   2004–2008
                         Pine Bluff                2005–2009
                         Pueblo                    2004–2008
                         Umatilla                  2004–2008

A total of 35 reports were surveyed, representing five years’ worth of annual exercises at each of
seven CSEPP sites. In all, 325 emergency public information issues were identified by evaluators
in these 35 exercises, or just over 9 issues per exercise on average. The totals were relatively
consistent from year to year, ranging from 56 to 75.

The issues were analyzed to determine themes. Four major themes were identified and are
described below, along with suggested solutions.

It should be noted that the issues referenced in this lesson do NOT imply that these sites were
poorly prepared or riddled with problems. All in all, these exercises were considered to be
successful demonstrations of response capability. Numerous issues were identified due to the
robust scale of effort that went into the exercises and the exacting evaluation process; each
exercise involved hundreds of participants and dozens of evaluators.

5.6.3 Issue

A detailed compilation of the identified issues is presented in Table 5.6-2. Analysis of the issues
led to the identification of four major themes:

EAS messages and news releases. A total of 51 issues were identified relating to EAS messages
and news releases. Due to the nature of the hazard in CSEPP, program guidance called for public
warnings to go out to the public within a few minutes, including detailed protective action
instructions that were customized for different geographic areas surrounding the installation.
Most, but not all, jurisdictions had pre-scripted messages ready to go, covering the many

 249

different possible permutations of geographic areas and protective actions. In an emergency (or
an exercise) the task became choosing the correct pre-scripted message, and perhaps modifying it
further to fit the particular circumstances of this event. A considerable number of particulars
often need to be included, such as particular evacuation routes to use, location of reception
centers, and recommended times to exit shelter-in-place. Unsurprisingly, the development and
dissemination of these complicated instructions under extreme time pressure resulted in issues
such as missing information, incorrect protective action recommendations, and conflicts between
recommendations. Problems noted by evaluators included wording regarding sheltering and
ending shelter (14 issues); evacuation (12 issues); missing, inadequate, or misused pre-scripted
messages (15 issues); and flaws in the description of the emergency situation (10 issues).

In addition, a number of issues were identified pertaining to news releases. Most concerned the
timeliness and frequency of news releases. News releases were either delayed or there were
simply not enough of them to keep up with exercise developments. Some issues were also noted
with respect to the content of news releases being incomplete or inaccurate.

Coordination of information among different jurisdictions or locations. Issues in this category
included items such as:
 • Releasing information to the public or media without coordinating with other
 organizations.
 • Failing to notify other organizations of actions taken (e.g., protective actions).
 • Breakdowns in sharing information within an organization, e.g., information not fed from
 the Emergency Operations Center (EOC) to the PIO at the JIC.

Internal information flow. Internal information flow issues arose where information in the hands
of some members of the emergency response organization did not get to other members of the
organization who needed it. PIO and PAO staff often did not have the latest and most complete
and accurate information to provide to the public and media when they received inquiries. Most
often, this was ascribed to a lack of internal briefings within an EOC; either the briefings were
too infrequent, or key public information staff were not in attendance. Other information flow
issues included problems with status boards and staff not sharing important information such as
errors noted in media stories (so that public information efforts could be directed at correcting
the misinformation in subsequent briefings or news releases).

Information provided by call takers. Each organization had staff devoted to fielding telephone
calls from the media and the public. As noted earlier, exercise controllers facilitated this play by
calling in questions, posing as members of the media or the public. In the sample examined, a
total of 28 issues were identified regarding this function. Some were ascribed to the internal
information flow issue noted above; call takers did not have all of the information they could
have used. A second group of issues were ascribed to training of the call takers; in some cases,
their answers were compromised by not being familiar with chemical agent terminology,
symptoms of exposure, or response-related information such as protective action zones,
evacuation routes, or reception center locations. A third group of issues related to calls being
mishandled; calls went unanswered, were dropped, or bounced back and forth between

 250

personnel. For example, in some cases calls were transferred to the JIC before that facility was
opened.

5.6.4 Solution

Each issue identified in a CSEPP exercise after-action report (AAR) came with a suggestion as
to how to solve or avoid it going forward. The suggested solutions were examined, and themes
were identified corresponding to the identified issue themes.

EAS messages and news releases. For EAS messages, one solution involved trying to increase
the time available for development and review by anticipating upcoming changes. In CSEPP
exercises (and presumably in a real chemical event, were one to occur), very little time was
available for the first public warning, as the initiating accident generally occurred without any
advance notice. However, subsequent warnings might be issued with somewhat less time
pressure as more information was learned about the emergency or something else changed
(e.g., a wind shift). Anticipating these developments might allow staff to spend a little more time
checking and refining the message.

Another solution, pertaining to both EAS messages and other news releases, is to build in a more
robust quality assurance (QA) procedure to ensure accuracy and completeness. One simple and
quick way to do that is to read the draft message aloud in the EOC. That allows many staff to
hear it and catch any problems. It also serves as a mini-briefing and makes staff aware of what
information is being disseminated to the media and public.

Coordination of information among different jurisdictions or locations. Solutions in this area
mainly focused on strengthening procedures and training regarding coordination of information.
In particular, a constant flow of information between a PIO or PAO at the JIC and his or her
“home EOC” is important. That can be implemented via any number of communication modes
such as telephone, email, texting, or social media. In addition, strengthening the procedures for
interaction and exchange among county PIOs was recommended, because the actions of one
county may affect another – for example, evacuation of residents from one county might trigger
the opening of shelters in a host county.

Internal information flow. Increasing the frequency of internal briefings was often recommended
as a way to promote internal communication. In fact, the later exercises in the period studied
tended to implement this recommendation. In addition, in several exercises, public information
staff conducted a pre-briefing of all spokespersons prior to a news conference to “get everyone
on the same page” and help iron out differences of opinion and discrepancies in data concerning
numbers of injuries, evacuees, etc. Also, procedures and training should emphasize conducting
internal briefings when there is important new information such as a change in protective actions
or after a news conference that divulges important new information.

Another theme in solutions to this issue involved more effective use of technology to promote
internal communication, such as, for example, making sure that electronic status boards were
kept up to date, had complete and accurate information, and were visible to all (either as a
display or on individual workstations). Ensuring effective use of technology can involve ironing

 251

out seemingly minor technical glitches, such as the board display not automatically refreshing
when new information is posted.

Information provided by call takers. Many of the solutions identified above would also solve
problems in this area by improving the call takers’ access to information. For example,
improvements to internal briefings and use of status boards (or other internal communication
modes) should help place key information in the hands of call takers.

Training was also identified as a key factor to ensure that call takers are familiar with their role
and with the sometimes complicated terminology and response information involved in chemical
agent response. In addition, call takers should be trained on how to handle questions when they
do not know the answer, which will happen sometimes – referring the call to another staff
member or returning a call after obtaining the needed information. Again, later exercises in the
study period saw more of these techniques being demonstrated.

Other measures suggested in this area include:
 • Attention to call handling, including procedures for forwarding calls, using voice mail,
 prompt call-backs, etc.
 • Effective monitoring of media stories (and social media traffic) on the emergency. This
 can provide useful feedback to public information staff on how the event and response
 are being reported, including quickly identifying misinformation that may be present. It
 can also be performed remotely through mutual aid agreements or by augmentees.
 • Adequate staffing and equipment. At about half of the exercises surveyed, there were
 shortages of call taker staff and workstations.

5.6.5 Potential Application

All of the external support and internal capabilities solutions identified above were employed by
one, a few, or many CSEPP communities. Some of the issues and solutions might have
application to a relatively narrow set of communities; for example, use of pre-scripted, zone-
based EAS messages may be limited to communities in the radiological emergency preparedness
program (REPP). However, most of the issues and solutions identified in this lesson should be
applicable to any community with an emergency operations plan and public information officers.
Prompt and effective public warning, internal communications, media monitoring, news releases,
and the like are important in virtually any disaster response situation.

Communities in the CSEPP program enjoyed staffing and resources beyond those available to
many similarly situated communities. However, many of the solutions recommended above may
be addressed without extravagant outlays.
 • Solutions involving training and sharpening of procedures can be carried out in-house.
 • Internal communications such as briefings and effective use of electronic
 communications can be carried out at minimal cost.

 252

 • For some functions, capabilities can be augmented through mutual aid agreements. For
 example, someone at a nearby jurisdiction can help monitor media coverage of an
 emergency and report erroneous or incomplete information.
 • Emergency management software (or general project management software that can be
 adapted to emergency management use) are increasingly available.
